Commentaries on the Twelve Davidic Psalms

Latin source: Ambrose's explanations of twelve psalms (Psalms 1, 35–40, 43, 45, 47, 48 and 61 in the Latin numbering), preached at Milan and gathered after his death in 397; the explanation of Psalm 43 breaks off unfinished. Translated from the critical text of M. Petschenig, Corpus Scriptorum Ecclesiasticorum Latinorum 64 (Vienna and Leipzig, 1919), pp. 3–397, verified against the page images and collated with the Benedictine edition as reprinted in Migne, PL 14, with the edition's misprints corrected. The psalm under comment and all other Scripture are translated as Ambrose quotes them, from his own Old Latin text; italics mark the psalm verses and the other Scripture he quotes. Psalms keep the Latin (LXX) numbering, with the Hebrew number in parentheses where it differs. Greek words are kept as printed, and Latin words Ambrose discusses as words are kept with an English gloss in square brackets. Petschenig's supplements are read into the text, the words he brackets as spurious are omitted, and his critical apparatus is not translated; the footnotes are the translator's. This English translation is released into the public domain.

On Psalm 1

1. God has set before us, as the highest incentive to virtue, the delight of future blessedness; the devil, for his part, devised delight as a vehement spur to error. Adam, the first of the human race, gives proof of both designs. He was placed by the Lord God in the paradise of pleasure, to enjoy everlasting delight, so that his future offspring might be stirred to virtue — for it was not unknown to God that he would give place to error, and that a hope of salvation ought to be set before the rest, by which they might strive to be restored to the seat snatched away from the human race —; and the same Adam was deceived, through the form of the serpent, which prefigured the enticements of delight, by the persuasion of his wife. Having seized the occasion, then, the adversary wrought death for me through delight. And so what had been bestowed by divine grace for life became death for me, and the enemy won man's assent to the fall all the more easily in that he held out a semblance of nature. For his works delighted the Lord, the first beginnings of nature delighted him, and seeing them the Lord said: very good.

2. The angels praise the Lord, the powers of the heavens sing psalms to him, and before the very beginning of the world the Cherubim and Seraphim, with the sweetness of their melodious voice, say: holy, holy, holy. Countless thousands of angels stand by, and the elders and a great multitude, like the voice of many waters, sing together: alleluia. A more explicit account holds that the very axis of heaven turns with a certain sweetness of perpetual harmony, so that its sound is heard in the farthest parts of the earth, where certain secrets of nature lie. Nor does this seem foreign to the ways of nature, since a voice sent out rebounds from woods or mountains with more pleasing applause, and they give back with sweeter sound what they have received. In the very crags and stones, too, nature has found something to delight: in some the outlook delights, in others their use or their beauty. The very wild beasts and birds are soothed by the delight of a pleasanter place or of a more melodious voice; and to infants at the breast, too, severity is a terror and caresses a pleasure. Delight, therefore, is natural.

3. Hence also holy David, who perceived from where man had been cast down and by what fraud — for had he kept the grace of that eternal and heavenly delight infused into him by the Lord, and not lost it, captured by worldly enticements, he would never have undergone the injuries of so miserable a hardship —, and so, striving to repair and restore that delight, he established for us, through the office of singing psalms, a likeness of the heavenly way of life.

4. For although all divine Scripture breathes the grace of God, yet the book of Psalms is especially sweet, since Moses himself, who described the deeds of the ancestors in plain speech, yet when he had led the people of the fathers through the Red Sea amid memorable wonder, seeing King Pharaoh sunk with his forces, raised his genius to greater things, because he had achieved things greater than his own strength, and sang to the Lord a song of triumph. Miriam too, taking up a timbrel, exhorted the other women, saying: let us sing to the Lord, for he has been gloriously honoured; horse and rider he has cast into the sea. Moses himself also, when he had read the Law of the Lord, in order to fix its memory in the hearts of his hearers, spoke through a song, saying: hear, O heaven, and I will speak; let my speech be awaited like the rain, and let my words come down like the dew, like a shower upon the grass and like snow upon the hay.

5. God, then, delights to be not only praised by a song but also reconciled by it. Hence Moses too made use of a song above all at the time when he was calling heaven and earth to witness, so that the world might listen more eagerly to its salvation sounding in harmony with the strain of heavenly grace, and that, by the sweetness of a sacred delightfulness, the observance of the Law might grow forever into human minds. In fact, before the tables of the Law were confirmed by a song, they were broken and shattered through the indignation of Moses; but once they had been consecrated with such a seal, human anger had no place, because the sanctification of sacred sweetness shut it out. And so the song of the Lord came down from heaven like a softer dew, and watered the faith of men, as the grass is watered, with a kind of shower of spiritual grace. These two songs, therefore, in the books of Moses, like two eyes of the world and two lights of heaven, illuminate the whole body of his work.

6. But David was chosen by the Lord principally for this office, that what in others seems rare and stands out above the rest of the work should shine in him constant and continuous. We read one song in the book of Judges; the rest is run through in the manner of history, in which the deeds of the ancestors are set out. Isaiah wrote one song, by which to soothe the hearts of his readers; in the rest he thundered with the terrible trumpet of reproof. Not even his enemies, who persecuted him to death on account of his other sayings, could reproach him with the song. Daniel one, Habakkuk one; Solomon himself, the son of David, although he is said to have sung countless songs, yet left only one that the Church has received, the Song of Songs; he also wrote Proverbs. In the others, then, one may notice single songs.

7. History instructs, the Law teaches, prophecy announces, reproof chastises, moral teaching persuades; in the book of Psalms there is the profit of them all, and a kind of medicine for human salvation. Whoever reads it has the means to cure the wounds of his own passion with a special remedy. Whoever wishes to look into it finds, as in a common gymnasium of souls and a kind of stadium of virtues, different kinds of contests prepared, and may choose for himself the one for which he understands himself best fitted, so as to reach the crown more easily. If anyone is eager to review the deeds of the ancestors and wishes to imitate them, he receives the whole sequence of the fathers' history gathered within a single psalm, so as to acquire a treasury of memory in a compendium of reading. Things that are set out more briefly also seem easier. Of what depth is this too, that within a short space it has marked out the adversities that came from the offences and has added beneath them the prosperity of reconciliation, so that you might learn at once what harm the offence of unbelief did, what a ready faith conferred! If anyone searches out the force of the Law, which lies wholly in the bond of charity — for he who loves his neighbour has fulfilled the Law —, let him read in the psalms with what affection of love he alone exposed himself to grave dangers to drive away the reproach from the whole people; and in this he will recognize a glory of charity not unequal to the triumph of virtue. If anyone dreads the harshness of reproofs, let him hear one saying: O Lord, rebuke me not in your anger, nor reprove me in your fury, and let him learn how he ought to temper the censure of an angry judge. If anyone longs to know an example of patience, let him read in the psalms: if I have repaid those who rendered evils to me, and let him notice that he foresaw the precept of the Gospel by the Spirit and anticipated it by virtue. And so you have that proverbial saying, that a gentle man is the physician of the heart. If anyone, moreover, wishes to be fortified against the assaults of spiritual wickedness, what should he know he must do more than sing psalms? David, while still a youth, used to sing psalms and put to flight the evil spirit of Saul, which had been gripping him before.

8. But what shall I say of the power of prophecy? What others announced through riddles seems to have been promised to him alone, plainly and openly: that the Lord Jesus would be born of his seed, as the Lord said to him: of the fruit of your womb I will set upon your throne. In the psalms, then, Jesus is not only born for us, but also takes upon himself that saving Passion of the body, rests, rises again, ascends to heaven, sits at the right hand of the Father. What no man would have presumed to say, this prophet alone announced, and afterwards the Lord himself proclaimed in the Gospel.

9. Moreover, all writers have set in their writings examples of psalms or some sayings of those before them; the psalms have nothing but what is their own. What, then, is more pleasing than a psalm? Hence David himself says beautifully: praise, he says, the Lord, for a psalm is good; to our God let there be pleasant and seemly praise. And truly; for a psalm is the blessing of the people, the praise of God, the commendation of the congregation, the applause of all, the speech of everyone, the voice of the Church, the melodious confession of faith, a devotion full of authority, the gladness of liberty, the cry of joyfulness, the echo of gladness. It softens from anger, it releases from anxiety, it lifts up from grief; it is armour by night, instruction by day, a shield in fear, a feast in holiness, an image of tranquillity, a pledge of peace and concord, bringing forth, after the manner of a lyre, one song out of diverse and unequal voices. The rising of the day re-echoes a psalm, its setting resounds with a psalm.

The Apostle orders women to keep silence in the church; yet a psalm they do well to cry out. It is sweet to every age, it is fitting for either sex. Old men sing it, laying aside the stiffness of old age; sad veterans answer it in the joyfulness of their heart; young men sing it without reproach of wantonness; youths sing it together without the danger of a slippery age and the temptation of pleasure; young women themselves sing psalms without loss of matronly modesty, and little girls, without any lapse of bashfulness, with sober gravity, make a hymn to God in the sweetness of a modulated voice. Childhood longs to hold it fast, infancy rejoices to practise it, though it shrinks from learning other things; it is a kind of play of learning, of greater profit than when discipline is handed on in earnest. What labour there is in the church to obtain silence when the lessons are read! If one person talks, all make a din; when a psalm is read, it is itself the maker of its own silence: all speak and no one makes a din. Kings re-echo a psalm without the haughtiness of power; David rejoiced to be seen in this ministry; a psalm is sung by emperors, shouted with jubilation by the peoples. Each one vies to cry out what profits all. A psalm is sung at home, rehearsed abroad; it is taken in without toil, kept with pleasure. A psalm joins those at variance, unites the discordant, reconciles the offended; for who would not forgive one with whom he has sent up a single voice to God? It is plainly a great bond of unity for the whole number of the people to come together into one choir. The strings of the lyre are unequal, but the symphony is one. On very few strings the fingers of the artist often go astray, but in the people the Spirit, the artist, does not know how to go astray. A psalm is the commerce of nightly work, the wage of daily rest, the instruction of beginners, the strengthening of the perfect, the ministry of angels, the heavenly warfare, the spiritual sacrifice. Even rocks answer a psalm; a psalm is sung, and even stony hearts are softened; we see the very hard weep, the merciless bend.

10. In a psalm teaching vies with grace; it is sung at once for delight and learned for instruction. For more violent precepts do not last; but what you have taken in with sweetness, once it has been poured into the heart, is not wont to slip away. What is there that does not meet you as you read the psalms? In them I read a song for the beloved and am inflamed with the desire of holy charity; in them I recognize the winepresses of the divine mystery, in them the grace of revelations; I review the testimonies of the resurrection, the gifts of the promise; in them I learn to avoid sin, I unlearn blushing at penitence for my offences. So great a king, so great a prophet has challenged me by his example, that I should strive either to lessen a sin committed or to beware of one not committed.

11. What, then, is a psalm but the instrument of the virtues, which the venerable prophet, striking it with the plectrum of the Holy Spirit, made to resound on earth with the sweetness of heavenly sound? At the same time, when on strings and chords, that is, on dead remains, modulating the distinctions of diverse sounds, he directed the song of divine praise towards heavenly things, he certainly taught us that first we must die to sin, and only then, in this body, are the diverse works of the virtues to be distinguished, by which the grace of our devotion may reach the Lord, so that no lust of earthly vices might creep in upon those occupied with attention to heavenly things, and at the same time the mind might shine with the sweetness of heavenly grace. Rightly, then, the Lord, praising the minister of so great an office, says: I have found David according to my heart.

12. They say, too, that the more skilled among those who sing to the lyre sing inwardly, as the stories relate of Aspendius the lyre-player, and that the principles of the melodies and certain closures of the rhythms are in the upper part of the psaltery. David therefore taught us that we must sing inwardly, sing psalms inwardly, as Paul too sang, saying: I will pray with the spirit, I will pray also with the mind; I will sing psalms with the spirit, I will sing psalms also with the mind, and that we must shape our life and actions with our gaze on the things above, lest the delight of sweetness should arouse the passions of the body, by which our soul is not redeemed but weighed down, since the holy prophet has recorded that he sings psalms for the redemption of his soul, saying: I will sing psalms to you, O God, on the lyre, Holy One of Israel. My lips shall rejoice when I sing to you, and my soul, which you have redeemed. But now let us enter upon the beginning of this psalm which has been set before us.

13. Blessed, he says, is the man who has not walked in the counsel of the ungodly. How apt, how timely a beginning! For just as those who have undertaken to put on some solemn contest are accustomed to set out a prize and to vaunt the nobility of the crown, so that those who are to compete may assemble with greater zeal and contend with more eager effort, so our Lord Jesus has set forth the glory of the heavenly kingdom, the grace of perpetual rest, the blessedness of eternal life, as incentives to human virtue. An emperor too, when he goes forth to war, promises the soldier a donative and promotions in rank in the military units, so that the hope of gain may steal away the toils and hide the fear of danger. Like the herald, then, of a great emperor, holy David exhorts the soldiers, calls the athletes, declares the prize, saying: blessed is the man who has not walked in the counsel of the ungodly. He began from the prize, to lighten the weight of the coming contest; he put the reward first, so that each one, leaping in his heart over the anxieties and toils of present things, might strive with eager haste to earn the blessedness of things to come. "Blessed", he says, "is the man". What more could be given to man than that beyond which nothing greater could be ascribed to God by apostolic authority? For blessed God has been called, and the only mighty one, and the King of kings, and the Lord of lords. He who alone is mighty, he the King of kings, he the Lord of lords, yet does not go beyond the power of blessedness; he has granted us a common share in that title which was deemed worthy of divine honour.

14. Let us consider now for what reason he said "blessed is the man" and not rather "blessed are men", since both sexes are called to grace. Did he exclude women from a share in blessedness because he named the man alone blessed? Far from it, since God did not exclude women from a share in creation either because he made the man first. For God said: let us make man according to our image and according to our likeness, and God made, he says, man; to the image of God he made him, male and female he made them. In the human being [homo] both are signified; in the man [uir] the sex is expressed. But just as both are included when one says human being, so when man is named, the woman too is understood whose husband [uir] he is. Indeed, she shall be called, he says, woman, because she was taken from her husband. There is this too: those whose nature is the same certainly cannot have separate works, and those whose work is equal certainly have an equal reward. In saying man, then, Scripture did not pass over the partner of the union, since in saying human being it did not keep silent about the partner in nature. And so, just as there we read that the human being was made, and yet, though the nature is one, we cannot deny that the principal sex was created first, so here too, when we read man, we recognize from the principal portion the sex as well that is its near equal. Equal, therefore, are the pursuits of virtue, because equal is the prerogative of creation. But why should you discuss sex, where what is demanded of you are contests not of the body but of the soul, which has no sex? Do not, then, distinguish the honour or divide the reward there, where sex is not distinguished. Yet it is not unwise if he is called first to the exercise who was later in the fall. She who began badly ought to follow, not to go before; or at least after the experience let her be more modest. Eve wrongly passed over the order of nature: she ought to have waited for the one who went before her. The serpent cunningly began from the later one; therefore the prophet turns back to the earlier, him, indeed, who would not have fallen had he not followed the later.

15. In fact, he called us back from the fall before he called us forth to the palm of victory. Blessed, he says, is the man who has not walked in the counsel of the ungodly. See where you are called blessed, O man: not in riches, not in powers and honours, not in nobility of birth or in comeliness and beauty, not in health of body, in which there is nothing good by nature; indeed, not only do they easily change into their opposites, but they even lend their service to fault for him who does not know how to use them. For who is righteous because of money, who humble in positions of power, who merciful because of nobility, who chaste because of comeliness? These things are more enticing to sin than fruitful for progress in virtue.

16. What, next, does it mean that he preferred to say has not walked and has not stood as of the past, when he could have said: "blessed is the man who does not walk in the counsel of the ungodly, and does not stand in the way of sinners, and does not sit in the chair of pestilence"? See the teaching: for he who is not ungodly or a sinner is not at once blessed, because of the uncertainty of the end. For it was not written idly: praise no one before death. So while anyone is in this life he cannot be praised with a definitive commendation, since he may still lie open to error; but he who has closed his life without offence is rightly reckoned under the name of blessed, as one who enjoys the company of the blessed.

17. But perhaps you may say: "For what reason, then, did he say elsewhere: blessed is he who understands concerning the needy and the poor? For he did not call blessed him who has understood, but him who understands." It is because those who do good find in the work itself also that by which they are approved, obtaining their reward in the very work, since a good deed is the fruit of a blessed conscience. But those who refrain from evil are not at once blessed if they have avoided fault once or twice, but only if they have been able to escape the contagion of fault through the whole time of their life.

18. Now this occurs to us: why he preferred to call blessed not the one who has performed some duty of devotion, but the one who has refrained from the counsel of the ungodly. For he who has fulfilled the office of virtue seems more worthy of praise than he who has escaped sin, since neither an ox nor a horse nor a stone is wont to be in sin or to sit in the chair of pestilence. But those things do not have the fruit of blessedness which do not have the sense of virtue; for how do they attain the reward of the Law which do not have the disposition to follow the Law? So I see that the sentence set before us concerns rational beings, that is, us. And for us the beginning of good things is abstinence from sins, since we read: turn aside from evil and do good. For this is the order of discipline, that you should strive from lower things to more perfect ones, lest you be terrified by the mass of greater things, you who ought to be drawn on by a beginning of lighter ones. For Scripture teaches us that the ascent of devotion is like a ladder, on which holy Jacob, a man of exercise, saw the angels of the Lord ascending and descending; he has been set before us so that through him we may learn that we must advance a step of virtue little by little, and that thus we can strive from the lowest things to the highest if we proceed by small advances towards the things that seem higher than human nature. Keep this ladder always set before you. Do not fear, O man, to climb these steps of discipline. The first step is near the earth, the next is like the first. So by equal steps one climbs to the summit. Do not disdain, O man, that first step as if it were the meanest. That first ascent separates you from the earth; you tread on air once you have lifted your foot from the ground. Placed in virtue, you have left the earth by rising; you leave the earth if you turn aside from fault. The beginning of progress towards virtue, then, is to abstain from sin.

19. And that we may know that this discipline belongs to teaching, hear the Law saying: you shall not commit adultery, you shall not commit murder; for these precepts seemed suited to the imperfect. In fact, the Lord Jesus himself, knowing that the man who asked him by what works he might attain eternal life was imperfect, answered him: you shall not commit adultery, you shall not commit murder, you shall not commit theft, and so on. Then, when he said that he had done all these things, he added the more perfect things, saying: sell all that you have and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven, and he taught that there is so great a distance between turning aside from evils and imitating good things that the man who judged the former easy for himself refused the latter. But because he had not climbed even the former, he could not fit himself for the latter; for if he had loved his neighbour, he could have kept from refusing the poor the help of his patrimony. And therefore climb the first step of the Law, so that you may be able to reach the heavenly summit of the Gospel. Hence I think that the holy prophet, as one placed under the Law and following the manner of the Law in the first psalm, in his own person rather announces what is to be avoided than sets forth what is to be followed, whereas in Psalm 40 (41), which is spoken in the person of the Savior, there is rather an exhortation to virtue than a restraint of error; for it speaks of the Savior's Passion. And therefore the dispenser of the Gospel too, as we may judge when we have heard him say: blessed are the merciful, has crowned mercy both in the psalm of his own Passion and in the Gospel alike. But let us now set about the explanation of the psalm and look into its prophetic power.

20. Blessed, he says, is the man who has not walked in the counsel of the ungodly, and has not stood in the way of sinners, and has not sat in the chair of pestilence. We can say that there are three kinds of sins, which we think are expressed here — of thought, of deed, of persistence — and that he is signified as blessed who has not even thought what is evil. For how can he be blessed who is to be convicted on the day of judgement by his own thoughts accusing him? Even if he has deceived man, fled from a witness, escaped an accuser, yet he will not be able to avoid himself as his own accuser, whom he ought most to fear, since he will have in him both the accuser and the guilty man confessing. Blessed, therefore, is he who has not thought what is evil, and has not committed sin — for sometimes we offend even without thought; for in much speaking we do not escape sin — and has not persisted in sin. Or thus: he who has not thought what belonged to error, or has not stood in that thought, or at least has not persisted in those thoughts which he perceived to be full of error. But whether these fit rightly, the reader will judge. For he who has once thought evil ought neither to stand in it nor to persist in it. But even if he did not persist, he could not be blessed, because he stood in what he wickedly thought; even if he did not stand, yet whether, by the very fact that he thought evil, he has the fruit of blessedness, he must look for some lenient interpreter. Further, since no one can say that he has a clean heart, let it be granted that a thought is pardonable: is the doing of sins pardonable too? Further, if standing is pardonable, is it also full of blessedness because it does not persist in the crime? Then, how could he who has not thought wicked things stand in sin or persist in it? Granting that he is blessed who has rightly kept these three, the order is surely different. For the first is that he should not persist in sin, the second that he should not have stood in it, the third that he should not have thought what belonged to error. For he who has not persisted could still have stood; he who has not stood could have thought; but he who has not even thought is truly blessed.

21. Hence I thought that another tradition too should not be passed over, by which we may maintain that the three steps are set in right order: that he who wishes to be blessed should not walk in the counsel of the ungodly, that is, should not walk in their thoughts; next, should not stand in the way of sinners; third, should not sit in the chair of pestilence. You, then, who have become a Christian for the Church, or you who are making your way to grace, abstain from the counsels of the ungodly, so that you may say: destroy not my soul with the ungodly, O God, nor my life with men of blood, and do not think the things that are ungodly. What are these but the things devised to the injury of God? For devotion is first towards God, second towards parents. And indeed the enemy often sows various things in our minds, and therefore the prophet cautiously judged that thoughts, rather than unforeseen suggestions, are to be held for a crime. You have abstained, then, from the counsels of the ungodly; rightly, yet you are not at once blessed. Beware also lest you have stood in the way of sinners.

22. With what careful weighing the words of divine Scripture are set down! For since we are all under sin, it does not demand of you what is beyond nature, that you should commit no sin — for not even an infant of one day is without sin — but that you should not remain in sin in a kind of long-standing station. Not all are ungodly: therefore you are called back from all the thoughts and conspiracies of the ungodly; but all are sinners: therefore you are admonished to cease sinning. If you have been liable to the lapses of a younger age, the advance of maturity ought to amend you. So do not walk into graver things, do not stand in lighter ones. You have this too, said by the Lord in Isaiah: go out from Babylon, fleeing from the Chaldeans, that is: even if you have entered into the confusion of vices, go out. You ought not to have entered; but you went in, the law of the flesh compelling you and taking you captive in the law of sin. Go out, depart even if late, strip yourself of a heavy servitude. You could not help entering into sin through frailty; it is granted to you to go out of sin through sobriety. Go out, then, from Babylon, fleeing from the Chaldeans. Babylon is confusion, which does not keep the order of the virtues; for it is with a mind confused by enticements that we offend. The Chaldeans are those who search out the courses of the stars with the zeal of a vain superstition and sow the errors of ungodly paganism. Flee from them, lest they capture you, lest they bind you in the heavy snare of captivity. Abraham was a Chaldean, but he fled from the Chaldeans, and that before the Law; you have been born already under the Law: flee from the ungodly. He spurned the inheritance of his fathers so as to possess that of faith; you, abandon the succession of the body, acquire the inheritance of devotion.

23. But if you have not stood in sin, not even so are you blessed: you still have something you ought to be without. Many are the enticements, many the turnings aside from virtue; grievous are the incitements of pleasures, grievous the tinder of avarice, the desire for power, the ambition for honour, which, as with a kind of poison, defile the minds of men and, with a kind of pestilent wasting of vices, their souls. This is the chair of pestilence, on which the scribes and Pharisees sat, who lay heavy burdens on men, but themselves will not move them with a finger. These chairs the Savior cast out of the temple: the chairs of those who boasted of their honour and sought the first places among dignities, of those who used the priesthood or the primacy of honour for gain, of those who, indulging the gullet, by no means kept the guard owed to continence. This is true pestilence; in fact, the sons of Eli were sons of pestilence. In this seat, as it were, of vices Scripture forbids us to bend back our neck and to recline the strength of our whole body. Mark, then, the proper meanings.

24. Blessed is he who has not stood in the way of sinners and has not sat in the chair of pestilence either. That the course of this life is called a way is not in doubt, since Scripture itself says: in this way in which I was walking they hid a snare for me, and: agree with your adversary quickly, while you are with him on the way. For as long as we run the course of this life, we have a path along which we walk daily until we reach the end; even if we do not seem to go bodily, we advance. For just as those sleeping on ships are driven by the winds into port, and, though at rest they have no sense of sailing, yet the course urges them to the end and drives them on unawares, so, as the span of our life flows away, each one is led to his own end by a hidden course. Hence it is said: rise, you who sleep — for you sleep and your time walks on — and see that, while you sleep long, time does not pass by. Therefore, even if you sleep, let your heart keep watch, let your heart take no holiday; if your heart is not idle, your times are not idle. You are on the way, O man: walk, that you may arrive, lest night overtake you on the way, lest the day of life be spent before you hasten the progress of virtue. You are a wayfarer of this life: all things pass, all things fall behind you, all things you see on this way and pass by. You saw the loveliness of the trees, the greenness of the grass, the clearness of the springs and whatever of this kind delights the eyes; it pleased you to look on: it delighted you to attend a little; while you attend, you have passed by. Again, while you walk, you have come upon a rocky and broken road, the hollows of cliffs, the steeps of mountains, the thickets of woods: you were wearied a little; again you passed by. Such is this life, whose prosperities do not last and whose adversities are not long. So, since you are placed as it were on a way, let neither favourable things lift you up nor adverse things break you, nor pleasing things delay you nor sad things detain you. Hasten always to the end, hasten that you may arrive; but choose your way before you run.

25. There are two ways, one of the righteous, the other of sinners, one of equity, the other of iniquity, of which the prophet said: and see if there is a way of iniquity in me. Not only is our life a way, but our very life is a way either of virtue or of iniquity. Beware, then, lest avarice set its footsteps in you and you become a way of crime; lest wickedness, lest lust do so and you become a way of iniquity, worn down by shameful deeds journeying along it. You may choose whom to follow, either the righteous or the unrighteous. The way of the righteous is narrower, that of the unrighteous broader; the one, of sobriety, narrower, the other, of drunkenness, broader, so that it can hold those who reel; this one has the enticements of this world, that one has the rewards of things to come. In the one the fruit is more at hand, in the other the hope is slower; for things that are sweet do not put off into a long expectation, but offer a present enjoyment, whereas what is serious is sought with toil, what is scarcely grasped by blessed thought, what eye has not seen nor ear heard, which God has prepared for those who love him. We find it hard to believe what we do not see, and therefore the soul is in turmoil and carries its thoughts this way and that like a kind of eyes. Then various appearances of things meet it and pour themselves over it; if it attends to eternal things, it chooses virtue; if to present things, it prefers pleasure. Grievous and unequal is the contest against the delights of present things. Here there is liberty for the desires; there the injury of servitude, to do what you do not wish and to abstain from what you have desired; here a banquet, there a fast; here intemperance of joys, there perseverance of tears; here dancing, there prayer; here sweet songs, there grievous groans. It is indeed written: the heart of the wise is in the house of mourning, and the heart of fools in the house of feasting. It is better to hear the rebuke of a wise man than a man hearing the song of fools; but few hear these things, fewer follow them. The sweetness of sin, which flows in at the present moment and fattens the disposition of the hearer, attracts men more than the sadness of virtue, which wraps the hope of faith in a kind of bark of bitter toil. Blessed, therefore, and admirable is he who, placed in the choice of such ways, has not been bent by the enticements of pleasure so as to set his step upon shoals and crooked places; to him it is not said: you who have forsaken the straight paths by walking into ways of darkness.

26. We have learned, then, what the way of sinners is, in which the prophet warns us that we must not stand; and Ecclesiastes too teaches this, who says: do not stand in an evil word, that is: do not persist in evil words, and likewise in deeds that deserve condemnation. How one must stand in good things the same holy prophet enjoins, saying: our feet were standing in your courts, O Jerusalem. In Jerusalem we must stand, from Babylon we must flee. And to Moses it is said: but you, stand with me, to him who fled from Egypt and, standing with the Lord, persevered. And in the Gospel those who stood until the eleventh hour received a reward equal to those who worked, and the virgins who had stood until the coming of the bridegroom were found worthy to go in together to the wedding, while those who went away and came back afterwards are shut out by the authority of the Lord's sentence. We have learned, then, not to stand in the way of sinners, but to stand in the office of virtue, because it is written: but you stand by faith.

27. Now let us consider what this means: and has not sat in the chair of pestilence. And indeed we state first that it is not simple sitting on a seat of this kind, such as is in common use, that is blamed — for what reproach could it carry to make it a fault? — but since the eyes of the Lord are always upon the faithful of the earth, we ought to stand as though placed in the sight of the emperor and set in some ministry. A soldier on campaign stands, he does not sit; a soldier in arms does not lean back, but rouses and raises himself. And therefore it is said to the soldiers of Christ: behold now, bless the Lord, all you servants of the Lord, who stand in the house of the Lord. But iniquity, on the contrary, sits on a talent of lead, because, fixed in sin, it cannot separate itself from it. For those grown old in error and zealously clinging to shameful deeds are said to sit, who will not rise up nor listen to him who says: rise, after you have sat. In fact, this same prophet says elsewhere: princes sat and spoke against me. Or are we ignorant that the inveterate habit of sinning has such force that it shuts out nature, which, though it can be healed to health, yet, once the passions have been strengthened by time, is found incurable? Let us not, then, remain in vices, but let each one, even if he has been in them, leap away from fault, as it is written of the harlot: do not fix your eye on her, but leap away, do not linger; leap away before the years of youth find you. But, what is graver, very many have not blushed at the luxury of the body even with whitening grey hair, and have prolonged a stained life to the time of old age; for the plague of fault, conceived in the inmost bowels, is embittered as time goes on. Beware, then, of the counsels of the ungodly; let counsels of this kind not penetrate your mind, lest it be said of you: will anyone bind fire in his bosom, and not burn his garments? For he who has once kindled in the bosom of his mind the flame of a burning shameful deed will quickly burn the garments of his body. And as fire leaping onto stubble clings and remains until it consumes all it has seized, so even a tiny spark of sin, if it has been roused by some tinder of vices, rouses a great fire. Do not, then, stand in sin. Lastly, you have set your footstep over the abyss of fault: quickly take your foot away, lest the pollution rise above your sole and, deceived by an easier slip, you settle down upon the mud.

28. The first vices, then, must be guarded against, lest they creep on afterwards into many graver ones. For just as those who roll about in mud are the more befouled the more they wallow, so he who has once spattered himself with the mud of wickedness, unless he quickly leaps out of it, draws over himself ever more heavily, day by day of a muddy way of life, the filth of his disgrace. And so, from the heavy stench of that foul and shameful abyss a kind of pestilence of souls is contracted, and, the breath of wholesome thoughts being corrupted, the pitiable plague of seething passions rages. Hence a deadly poison pours itself into minds, hence illness creeps upon bodies, sicknesses upon souls. For an evil sickness is the sickness of error, the sickness of avarice, the sickness of insatiable desire. These are riches, as Ecclesiastes says: there is an evil sickness which I have seen under the sun: riches kept to the harm of him who possesses them. Tell me, O Ecclesiastes, for what cause this sickness is evil. He will answer: because greedy hope devours many. The voracity of desire is insatiable; he who covets silver does not know how to be sated with silver. Riches distend, they do not fill. Even if one has been sated with riches, there is no one, he says, who lets him sleep. And indeed all his days are in darkness and mourning and much wrath and sickness and anger. For how can he sleep who keeps anxious guard over gold, who dreads losses, who ponders gains, who reckons interest, who counts his storehouses? Evil, then, is the sickness that takes away the good rest of the mind. An evil sickness is luxury, lust, concupiscence, worldly delight, ambition, which quickly corrupts the health of sobriety. Lastly, all the corruption of this world is pestilence. Do not, then, touch it, do not defile yourselves with it: it is a plague, it contaminates; it is a disease, it pollutes. Do not taste the things which all tend to corruption by their very use, as the Apostle said, who also cries elsewhere: the root of all evils is avarice. It rouses diseases, it implants pains; in fact, those who have sought after it have entangled themselves in many pains. This is the pestilence which often makes men neither hot nor cold but, what is worse than both the hot and the cold, lukewarm, so that those whom he has rejected for their grievous sins are vomited out of the mouth of the Lord Jesus. This is what rouses the sicknesses not of a few but of all. Every head in pain, every heart in sadness. From the feet even to the head are the ulcers of sinners. Every head is in pain when those who are reckoned wise are tormented by the pain of avarice. For the sense of the wise man is in his head; and this can also be referred to those who hold the first place in the Church. Every heart is in sadness when we savour the things of the flesh and bury the keen sight of our heart in bodily pleasures. Hence the Lord says of such men that he has given them a heart of flesh.

29. From the feet to the head the dread plague of diseases creeps, when they suffer from contagion, when they suffer along with others, if anyone is cheated of the fulfilment of his lust, has not been able to storm a widow's chastity or to seize a field, and the sick all pass their sicknesses on to one another. How often old men groan that they cannot drink for long! How often they grieve that they have ceased to go whoring, though they still have the will to whore! How often, amid the tales of drunkards, virtues are a reproach, sins a praise, uprightness a mockery, continence a laughing-stock, mercy a folly! These are the diseases that pass their evils on to many; corruption spreads from a few to all. They sit in assemblies disparaging the sober, belching their surfeit; they sit in taverns vying in drunkenness, and among them a harlot full of wine, smiling on this one, scorching that one, and inflaming all with the heat of lust. If a modest man passes by, he blushes and is picked at by each of them; if a shameless one, he is praised by the mouth of all, and like a disease he passes into the souls of each; for he who is celebrated for shameful deeds draws very many to the imitation of error. And so, while they imitate another's sin, they make the evil their own. Do not sit among those whom the holy prophet fled; imitate him, fleeing indeed, not sitting, who says: I have not sat in the council of evildoers, and with those who do iniquitous things I will not enter. For what reason you fled them, David, explain to us; point out these parties, so that we too may be able to flee them, lest we be infected with their contagion. They have been corrupted, he says, and have become abominable; there is none who does goodness, there is not even one. Generally, then, this can be referred to all the shameful, and then specially to the mockers of the good, whom Aquila called χλευαστὰς, because these are truly diseases, who by mocking the good work very much error in souls and corrupt minds. How many things he has named from which the blessed man ought to abstain! And he adds still others.

30. But his will has been in the Law of the Lord, and in his Law he will meditate day and night, that is: blessed is he who does these things with counsel, reason, prudence. For even a little child can abstain from the things mentioned, not through virtue but through inability and ignorance of offending; this can suit even an irrational beast, which has no power of counsel, no sense of error. This, then, is the fourth point, or what follows, in which the definition of the blessed man is distinguished from the beast: that the wise man is subject to the Law by will, not by necessity. For it makes the greatest difference, since in will there is the fruit of reward, in necessity the compliance of a stewardship. For so the Apostle taught us, saying: if I do this willingly, I have a reward; if unwillingly, a stewardship has been entrusted to me. And the fitting order is that first you love the Law, second that you meditate on it. He who loves fulfils the commandments of the Law from his will; he who fears observes them unwillingly. This method of teaching the righteousness of God we have received in the Law as well; for so it is written: hear, O Israel: the Lord your God is one God; and you shall love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul and with all your strength. And these words which I command you today shall be in your heart and in your soul; and you shall show them to your sons and speak of them sitting, and on a journey, and at rest, and when awake; and you shall bind them for a sign on your hand, and they shall be as ornaments before your eyes; and you shall write them on the thresholds in your dwellings and on your doors. And below: and now, Israel, what does the Lord your God ask of you but that you love the Lord your God and walk in all his ways? Wisdom too says: desire, therefore, my words, love them, and you shall have discipline. Bright is wisdom and never fading, and is easily seen by those who love her and is found by those who seek her. Rightly, then, we are taught by the holy prophet to have our will in the Law, our meditation according to the Law. Will in the Law lies not only in zeal but also in deed: first comes will, second operation. In fact, the Lord too, to him who said: if you will, you can make me clean, answered: I will; be made clean, to show that will ought to go before our works. In fact, the Law seeks the willing, because the law of the Lord is irreproachable, converting the soul, and no one is converted except he who is converted by his will. And the willing man hides and steals away the sense of toil.

31. And therefore he meditates on the Law day and night; in which not so much a continuous application to reading is required as the disposition to keep the Law; for he meditates fully who is a law to himself, attending to the work of the Law written in his heart. Aquila, however, put only the day, not the night as well; which he put not so much in disagreement with the others as with a different reference, since he who meditates on the Law is always in the light and has no night. For he whose works shine certainly cannot walk in darkness, because his righteousness shines forth like the light. Let our life, then, meditate on the Law; let our conduct meditate on it, let our action meditate on it, let our understanding of the heavenly mysteries meditate on it. For the Law is a pattern and shadow of heavenly things, a shadow of good things to come. Let him who recognizes in the Gospel what he believes in the Law meditate in darkness and by day, that is, when placed both in adversity and in prosperity; for the Law commands that we love the Lord. He who loves ought in every state of his to keep the disposition of a lasting charity. A father loves his son, loves him even when he rebukes him, loves him even when he beats him with a rod; he who indeed spares his rod hates his son. The Lord too chastises us and loves us. And so even when we commit what deserves coercion, he still loves, who even takes back the one who offended; and the Lord chastises every son whom he receives. You too, when you are chastised, love, because you are chastised so that you may be received. For what great thing is it if you love the Lord your God at a time when all things abound for you, when you enjoy what you have wished, when you rejoice in honours, wealth, children? Even to a man from whom we have received a benefit we are wont to return thanks. In fact, when Job was praised by the heavenly judgement, the devil replied this: it was no wonder if he was grateful to God, when such abundance of prosperity was at his disposal; but it would be proved if he lost all those things and still performed the office of a devout worshipper. This, then, is the first virtue: that you be not broken by adversities nor lifted up by prosperity. This the Law teaches you: that in affliction you should not relax your effort nor put on despair, and when already victorious should not say: my power and my strength have bestowed these things on me, but should know that all things are to be ascribed to the divine mercy.

32. Isaiah cries: he who is in distress shall not be confounded until the time. Drink this first. What is "drink this first"? Let us set aside the mystical sense and pursue the moral sense which the letter teaches. Because of their grievous errors he says that grievous crushings and vexations of the people will come, and these had to come first, so that mercy might follow. Drink first, then, tribulation; for through many tribulations we must enter the kingdom of God. Drink, that the sense of tribulation may be poured into the inmost parts of your breast; drink, with the disposition of a patient man, the pain of one who mourns; for when you have turned back and groaned, then you will reconcile the offended Lord to yourself. Drink this first, then, that you may be in pain and distress; gladness quickly pours in error. The people, grown fat and gross, rose up to play and departed from the Lord. It profits you to have a contrite heart; drink this first, that your sacrifice may be accepted by the Lord. Let the Apostle teach you what "drink this first" is, that is, the cup of tribulation; for tribulation works patience. There cannot be patience unless there has first been tribulation; tribulation, he says, works patience, and patience proof, and proof hope; and hope does not confound. First drink tribulation, so that afterwards the cups of so many virtues may be served to you. And that you may know that tribulation is drunk, you heard the prophet say today: you have made us drink the wine of compunction, and further on he says: and you will give them drink in tears by measure. He asks for drink by measure, not beyond measure, lest they be unable to bear it. In fact, with how good a disposition he prayed for this he proved by his own example, who mingled his drink with weeping, so as to incline the Lord's mercy towards himself.

33. Drink this first, then, that you may drink the second too — for this is the time for us to bring in the mystical sense —: drink first the Old Testament, that you may drink the New Testament too. Unless you have drunk the first, you will not be able to drink the second. Drink the first, that you may soothe your thirst; drink the second, that you may draw your fill of drinking. In the Old Testament there is compunction, in the New gladness. See how the Lord has met the arts of the devil on behalf of his little servants. The devil deceived one by the food of fraud, so as to ensnare all in the one; but Jesus redeemed all by the food of salvation, so as to restore in all that one too who had been deceived. The devil devised the golden cup of Babylon, so that whoever drank more might thirst more, and, because the drink could not please, he enticed them to drink by the value of the gold. He pledged them in his own wine, for which he sought the support of the metal; but the Lord Jesus poured water from the rock, and all drank. Those who drank in the type were satisfied; those who drank in the truth were made drunk. Good is the drunkenness that pours in gladness and does not bring confusion; good the drunkenness that steadies the gait of a sober mind; good the drunkenness that waters the gift of eternal life. Drink, then, this cup, of which the prophet said: and your inebriating cup, how excellent it is! And let it not move you that the cup of Babylon is golden, since you too drink the cup of wisdom, which is more precious than gold and silver. Drink, then, both cups, of the Old and of the New Testament, because in both you drink Christ. Drink Christ, because he is the vine; drink Christ, because he is the rock that gushed out water; drink Christ, because he is the fountain of life; drink Christ, because he is the river whose stream gladdens the city of God; drink Christ, because he is peace; drink Christ, because rivers of living water shall flow from his belly; drink Christ, that you may drink the blood by which you have been redeemed; drink Christ, that you may drink his words: his word is the Old Testament, his word is the New Testament. Divine Scripture is drunk and divine Scripture is devoured when the juice of the eternal Word descends into the veins of the mind and the powers of the soul; in fact, man does not live by bread alone, but by every word of God. Drink this word, but drink it in its order: first in the Old Testament; make haste, that you may drink it in the New Testament too. And as though he himself were hastening, he says: Galilee of the Gentiles and parts of Judaea, people who were walking in darkness, see a great light; you who dwell in the region of death, a light will shine upon you. Drink quickly, then, that a great light may shine upon you: a light not daily, not of the day, not of the sun, not of the moon, but that light which shuts out the shadow of death. For those who are in the shadow of death certainly cannot see the light of the sun or of the day. And as if you were asking whence comes so great a splendour, so great a grace, he answers: for a child is born to us, a son is given to us: a child, because he has sprung from a virgin; a son, because, born of God, he is the author of so great a light. A child is born to us, to us who believe, not to the Jews who have not believed; to us, not to heretics; to us, not to Manichaeans. To us he is born, because the Word was made flesh and dwelt in us; to us he is born who took flesh from a virgin, because as man he was born of Mary. Flesh is born to us, the Word is given; what is ours was born among us, what is above us was given to us.

34. We have digressed enough, we think, but not idly, so as to teach that even in tribulations we ought to love the Lord and not depart from him, since tribulation often follows upon gladness and gladness upon tribulation. In fact, he who is not broken by tribulation and follows the Law is blessed.

35. And he shall be like the tree that has been planted beside the courses of waters, which shall give its fruit in its season, and its leaf shall not fall; and all things whatsoever he does shall prosper. What is this blessedness which is compared to the wood of a tree, unless we understand the tree of life in paradise, that place of the blessed, brought forth from the earth in the midst of the other trees? For among many trees which were beautiful to look upon and good for food, the earth brought forth this tree too, and it was in the midst of paradise, so that the other trees might flourish by its greenness. What do we say this tree is but that through which salvation has come to us? And rightly did the earth bring it forth, because a virgin bore him, she who was earth according to the Author's sentence spoken against him: earth you are, and into earth you shall go. Beautifully, too, it is read as standing in the midst of the other trees, he who was in the midst of the apostles as they learned, or because he was in the midst of the mind and heart, as he himself says: there stands in the midst of you one whom you do not know; and elsewhere he says: but I am in the midst of you. In fact, Solomon too said of him: he is a tree of life to all who lay hold of him. He who is blessed, then, will be an imitator of the Lord Jesus, who is the tree of life, the tree of wisdom, planted in the womb of a virgin by the will of the Father, by whom he is planted to remain forever, so that he might give fruit in his season. For this planting could not wither, which had the abundance of spiritual grace abiding in itself. In fact, Jesus, full of the Holy Spirit, returned from the Jordan. These are the courses of waters of which he speaks in the Gospel: rivers of living water shall flow from his belly. Now this he said of the Spirit, which those who were to believe in him were beginning to receive.

36. There is also another tradition: that there are waters from which Jeremiah forbids us to drink, saying: what have you to do with the way of Egypt, to drink the water of Gihon? There is also the river Tigris, flowing past the Assyrians; there is also the Euphrates, stretching into Babylonia; there is also the Pishon, which in Latin interpretation is called "change of mouth", encircling the land of Havilah, where there is gold, and the gold of that land is good, and the carbuncle stone and the leek-green stone. Rightly is the mouth changed there, so that faith in promises is not kept, but there is deceit in the mouth, where there is good gold; for avarice breaks faith and does not keep simplicity of words, and precious ornaments too change the mind and spirit, so that there is one thing in the breast, another in speech. In those regions of rivers, then, were the Jews, led away captive into Egypt and to the Assyrians and to the Babylonians, where they sat by the rivers of Babylon and wept over the hardship of their calamity, and, as this same prophet testifies, there they hung up their instruments and laid aside all gladness, where they endured graver things. In fact, the remaining ten tribes were led away to the Assyrians, but the two, Judah and Benjamin, as guilty of a graver shameful deed, passed into Babylonia. For a priest's daughter who is an adulteress is punished more severely than other women, because she has discoloured the grace of the priestly race with foul disgrace.

37. As they, then, were in very grievous temptations, so our Savior exposed himself to many temptations, so as to pass over none of the contests of our temptation. He is rightly said, then, to be planted beside the courses of these rivers, not in the courses themselves, so that you may understand him to be bordering on them, not submerged. His first encounter was with flesh and blood. In fact, he says: Father, take this cup away from me; but not what I will, but what you will. There was the temptation of riches set before him, when the enemy offered him all the kingdoms of the earth if the Lord would fall down and worship him. You have the courses of two rivers, Gihon and Pishon. He had a wrestling against the princes of the world; there was in the Passion itself a contest against the tempters, whom the Hebrew interpretation calls Persians, who brought false testimonies, against those who said: let him come down now from the cross, and we believe him, things which the devil suggested. You have the rivers Tigris and Euphrates.

38. That our contest is fourfold the Apostle too comprised, saying: for our wrestling is not against flesh and blood, but against principalities and powers, against the rulers of this world and of darkness, against the spiritual forces of wickedness which are in the heavenly places. These are the rivers that go out from paradise. Hence I think that he who longs to return to paradise must cross these courses of waters. This the same holy prophet expressed not idly, showing that he who has undergone all temptations may hope that the rest of paradise is now owed to him. And so he says thus: my eyes have gone down courses of waters. For as there is a fiery sword at the entrance of paradise, so that he who returns may come back through fire, may burn his sins, may prove his gold, so he who returns returns through these courses, and rightly the saints say: we have passed through fire and water. Of these courses Isaiah too says: if you pass through water, the rivers shall not shut you in. What rivers? Hear David saying of those who were hastening to paradise: perhaps our soul would have passed through the unbearable water.

39. Some take of these four temptations this verse too: you shall walk upon the asp and the basilisk, and you shall trample the lion and the dragon, so that he walked in the Incarnation, trampled in the Passion, or tramples in the handing over of the kingdom, which he will hand over to the Father when he has made void every principality. Beautifully, too, Aquila said τὸ μεταπεφυτευμένον, that is, transplanted, because he was first planted in the virgin, afterwards transplanted into paradise, as he said to the thief: amen I say to you, today you shall be with me in paradise.

40. This tree, then, will give its fruit in its season. Earthly trees are said not to give fruit but to bear it; but the tree of life and of wisdom gives fruit, that is, bestows and grants it. Again this objection occurs: "if it is the tree of wisdom, why will it give in its season and not always?", lest perhaps it be grievous for us to think this of Christ. But you who have read that the Lord will set a faithful and prudent steward over his household, to give them their measure of wheat in season — in season, mark, not always —, certainly ought not to be troubled. Wisdom can always give fruit, but, because it is wisdom, it must give wisely, dispense prudently, whenever we either deserve or are able to receive the full measure. As here the last season yields fruit, so there too it will give good fruit to the needy, so that we may be able to take and keep a share in its resurrection. Now we cannot; in a vicious world we do not sustain it; for here there is corruption. And care had to be taken lest we corrupt the good fruits which the tree of life would give, because now we are corrupt, but there incorrupt, when the dead too, he says, shall rise incorrupt, and we shall be changed. For this corruptible must put on incorruption, and this mortal must put on immortality. What good, then, was it for one about to die to receive what death had power to take away from him? Wisdom, then, knows to whom and when it ought to give, wisdom which does not lose even the leaf of its tree. And therefore let us consider what the fruit of wisdom is, what the leaf.

41. The fruit is within; the leaf is that by which the fruit is defended either from the scorching sun or from the cold. The fruit seems to be faith, peace, excellence of teaching, the pursuit of true knowledge, the understanding of the mysteries. These fruits a good life guards; a bad one, even if it has received them, loses them. But to the sinner God has said: why do you recount my righteous acts? In the mystical is the fruit, in the moral the leaf, in view of the contemplation of the heavenly mysteries. For virtues without faith are leaves: they seem to be green, but they cannot profit; they are tossed by the wind, because they have no foundation. How many Gentiles have mercy, have sobriety, but have no fruit, because they do not have faith! The leaves quickly fall when the wind has blown. And some Jews have chastity, much assiduity and diligence in reading, but they are without fruit; they are tossed about like leaves. These perhaps are the leaves which the Savior found on that fig tree, but he did not find fruit.

42. The mystical things save and free from death, whereas the moral things are ornaments of comeliness, not aids to redemption. That the mystical surpass the moral the Lord himself also teaches in his Gospel, speaking of Mary, who, sitting at the feet of the Lord, was hearing his word, while Martha was busy about serving and complained that her own sister did not help her in the service of the table: Martha, Martha, Mary has chosen for herself the best part, which shall not be taken from her. If she who was serving Christ at table was not compared with her who longed to hear the word, what worker shall we be able to compare with one zealous for eternal knowledge — yet so that neither faith be lacking to the work of the one, nor, as with Mary, work to the knowledge of the other, lest either the leaves be without fruit or the fruit be left uncovered, without its natural protections, and lie open to injury?

43. We can also understand this: that Adam and Eve covered themselves with leaves when they sought bodily garments, because the resurrection of the Lord's body was being prophesied, since the flesh, which had formerly been wont to perish like leaves, was not going to perish in Christ and in all the righteous who, according to the Gospel, would shape their life and actions in imitation of the tree of wisdom born of a virgin. For the Greek said thus: ὃ τὸν καρπὸν αὐτοῦ δώσει, which can be referred to the blessed man — μακάριος καρπὸν δώσει in Greek —; but in Latin it is said "qui" [who], so that it runs: "who, being blessed, will give fruit" in his resurrection, when he can give it perpetually. It can also be taken thus: ὃ τὸν καρπὸν αὐτοῦ: ὑπὲρ ξύλου, so that it refers to the tree, all of whose deeds will prosper. In this there is clear testimony that it was said of the Savior; for whose deeds but his own can all be praised and have prosperous outcomes? But Aquila says they shall be directed, which seems to be referable also to any man whom the Lord directs with heavenly favour; for by the Lord, indeed, are the steps of a man directed, and David the prophet asked that his prayer be directed in the sight of God. But the Lord himself too directed his works, so that they were bent by no winding of error.

44. There follows: Not so the ungodly, not so, but like the dust which the wind casts forth from the face of the earth, that is: not so as the blessed man, who is blessed because he has not walked in the counsel of the ungodly, because he has not stood in sin, has not sat in the council of the pestilent who wish to pervert those who either believe devoutly or live devoutly; who has had his will in the Law and will meditate in it, or, as Aquila put it, will resound in the Law, so that his life may resound with the precepts of the Law and his conduct echo them, like those whose sound has gone out into all the earth. Perhaps here the sound of men's teaching goes forth, but there, where it will be granted to see face to face, a kind of fuller expression of the word seems to come about. Not, then, as this blessed man who has done these things, who will be like a planted tree, all of whose deeds will prosper, so too will the ungodly man be. Therefore he repeated it — either he who wrote it or he who added it afterwards, as some think — so that the repeated sentence might be the more confirmed, by saying: not so the ungodly, not so; they will be like dust. For they are earthly, and as dust is cast forth from the face of the wind, so these too will be cast forth and scattered by the Holy Spirit, who is wont to breathe like the south wind upon the fertile and fruitful earth of a wholesome soul.

45. To this wind it is said in the Song of Songs: come, O south wind, so that by the mildness of a softer air the fields of our heart may be loosened, which, bound by the winter frost, refused their lap to the seeds to be received. It is good for us that this wind should breathe, to bring safely into port the ships of Tarshish that carry to the true Solomon the things that seem needful for his temple. But this wind breathes only then, if that heavy wind, the north wind, has ceased to blow. Therefore either the Church or the devout soul says: arise, O north wind, and come, O south wind, that is: you, north wind, withdraw; and come, south wind, breathe upon my garden, so as not to shake off the flowers but to preserve them. The soul full of devotion, then, has a garden, or is itself a garden that bears fruits; the soul that lies open to ungodliness has dust, which is barren of fruit. The Lord indeed made it fruitful, but it gathered for itself the dust of ungodliness.

46. Why do you boast, O you who are full of ungodliness? Is it because you are powerful in honours and abound in riches? Do you not notice that you are dust, and that you will be dispersed and scattered? I saw, he says, the ungodly man exalted beyond measure and lifted up above the cedars of Lebanon, and I passed by, and behold, he was not. Why do you glory because many servants throng about you, many friends guard your sides, very many horses follow you, whose pedigree you would recount to us, and as it were the lineage of their ancestors? You display your riches because you feed your companions at banquets. Would that you fed the needy, would that you fed not the ministers of jests but the helpers of your prayers! You boast that people give way to you at once as you come forth, and men avoid you as they would a wild beast or a brute. "Are these things not something?" Do you not hear that all these things pass away like a shadow? What good are the consular purple-bordered robes or the triumphal palm-embroidered robes shining with gold? You will go out naked; no one there will recognize a consul. What do countless possessions profit? They are public, not yours; today you hold them, tomorrow another. When you have gone out, another comes in; scarcely have you moved your foot, another has stepped in. How many were there before you, how many will be masters there after you! And do you think this is private? Whom have riches ever redeemed from death, nay rather, whom have riches not driven to death? Whom has his wealth called back from the underworld, whom has power excused from punishment? Ungodliness is dust, as the power of the ungodly is dust; it brings darkness, it cannot give salvation. As soon as a vehement wind has begun to blow, it scatters it and dissolves it, troubles the air, lays bare the ground; like dust it is cast forth, like smoke it fails, like wax it melts.

47. Hence many have raised no small question, whether divine Scripture seems to assert that nature perishes, especially since elsewhere it says: I will crush them like dust before the face of the wind, and like the mud of the streets I will wipe them out, and elsewhere: behold, all the adversaries shall be confounded and shall be ashamed; for they shall be as though they were not. First, then, I ask whether they think ungodliness is according to nature or contrary to nature. If they assert that it is according to nature, it is certain that their opinion errs. Then let them say whether sin befits nature or not. But it is certain that to sin is to deviate from what is according to nature. What, then, is so absurd as for them to say that to be ungodly seems a lesser thing than to be a sinner, when the bitterest of all is whatever is seen to be done to the injury of God? But if acting ungodly is not contrary to nature, then living ungodly according to nature should neither be counted as sin nor be judged worthy of reproach; for no one is reproached who acts according to nature. Hence it is concluded that ungodliness is contrary to nature. How, then, does Scripture bring in, together with the destruction of a habit, συναπολουμένας φύσεις, that is, natures that will perish, when ungodliness is certainly not natural but contrary to nature? For what you do not have you do not lose, nor can that substance perish which did not exist. For dust too, when stirred, is changed either into the substance of water or into air or into fire, that is: it often seems to pass into another nature. It does not, then, fail into nothing, but passes into something else.

48. What, then, prevents him too who is crushed like dust by the power and reason of the word from being, not scattered into nothing, but changed for the better, so that from an earthly man he becomes a spiritual one, and from being wiped out like the mud of the streets in such a way that whatever seems rough and filthy is taken away and whatever is smooth and clean remains? And as for what he says of the adversaries of Jerusalem: they are as those who are not, he could certainly have said: "they will not be". But since he said: they shall be confounded and shall be ashamed, you surely understand that they will be, in substance and in the progress of conversion, but they will not be as adversaries, which they were. And so through the failing of their wickedness they will not be; they will be, changed by faith and devotion. In fact, elsewhere too it is said in the person of the sinner: and I will bear the anger of God, because I have sinned, until he justifies my judgement; for God, desiring to convert the sinner, punishes him and burns him in order to cleanse him. Hence he says: and he will lead me out into the light. For fire too burns wax: it melts so as to be purified; and we are proved through fire; and smoke, once all its material has been consumed, is purged away and does not pass beyond its nature. And so the soul too, purged of every stain, fails into what is, not into what is not, as Balaam says: let my soul die in the soul of the righteous, so that its fall and a certain habit of wickedness might die, but it might pass into the conduct and life of the righteous; for God wills all things to be saved. Hence Solomon too said: God did not make death, nor does he rejoice in the destruction of the living. He made the soul that it might be; he created man for incorruption, whom he made to his own image; but men, deviating from the gift of nature, made themselves liable to death, so that, as earthly, they are corrupted. But God drives them through tribulations to penitence, so that through penitence that accidental evil of wickedness may be burned and consumed and may perish, and that place of the soul which was the possession of accidental ungodliness may lie open to receive virtue and grace. And it is certain that the nature of the soul is precious, which, made to the likeness of God, admits the reception of every virtue, inasmuch as it has not been defrauded of a share in the knowledge from above.

49. Now we think there remains what is on the lips of many: for what reason God, without whose will not even the cheapest sparrow falls and with whom the hairs of the head are numbered, said through Isaiah: if all the nations have been reckoned as a drop from a pitcher and as the tipping of a scale, and shall be reckoned as spittle. Will all the nations, then, perish just as a drop from a pitcher and as spittle perishes and serves no use? But you who know that our God did not hold the nations as nothing to such a degree, he who said to Abraham: in you all the nations shall be blessed, and spoke through David to the Son, saying: I will give you the nations for your inheritance, and further on: all the nations shall serve him; you who have read that he offered his Son for every nation, to save sinners: you ought to consider in this place the force of the divine sentence. For in contemplation of the heavenly creatures, which are many — such as the heaven, which is manifold greater than the earth, so that by most the earth has been reckoned a point in comparison with the heaven; angels, archangels, principalities and powers, thrones, dominions, thousands and ten thousands and innumerable thousands —, in contemplation, then, of these, the nations have been reckoned as a drop from a pitcher to which belongs all the fullness. From that heavenly fullness, then, the nations have been reckoned as a falling drop. For how could they seem very great, when the earth itself, on which the nations are, is the smallest portion of the world, and the nations are, among all the things God has made, like the slight dip of a scale? At the same time, learn through this scale that God founded all things with justice, and that in the nations themselves there is by nature something in which there would seem to be even a slight weight of justice, and that the spittle itself comes forth as an inner portion, as it were, out of the totality of the whole body.

50. Here, then, it is rather mercy that is proclaimed, since he who came to seek what had perished did not despise that drop as worthless, but lifted up the tipping of the scale, and deigned to give that spittle the substance of a good body, that is: to gather all the nations into the one body of the Church. And among the nations Israel too can be numbered, by no means without a share in his divine justice, since Moses himself said of Israel: behold a wise people, and a great nation holding fast to discipline. And that you may know that the goodness of God has overflowed beyond our merit, the Apostle, interpreting this prophetic passage, says: God has shut up all things in unbelief, that he may have mercy on all, and added: O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God! How unsearchable are his judgements, and how untraceable his ways! For who has known the mind of the Lord? Or who has been his counsellor? Or who has first given to him, and it shall be repaid him? And the prophet, setting this passage first, says: if all the nations have been reckoned as a drop from a pitcher, and the rest, so that you may recognize that this understanding agrees with the Apostle's interpretation.

51. But what is more evident that he does not seem to bring in natures that perish than that the holy prophet himself added: for the ungodly do not rise again in the judgement? For he did not say: "they do not rise again", but he says: "they rise again indeed, but not in the judgement". And he who rises again certainly exists and abides; but because he has not believed in Christ, he has already been judged, and therefore he does not come into judgement, he for whom the punishment of a judgement already completed is waiting. And of the resurrection there are very many testimonies of the Scriptures, which we did not pass over in the books of the Consolation and of the Resurrection. But concerning what he says: the ungodly do not rise again in the judgement, according to the Gospel the sentence is settled: not all will be judged. The Apostle, however, says: for we must all stand before the judgement seat of Christ, so that each one may receive what is proper to the body, according as he has done, whether good or evil. Many think these are contradictory and do not notice that the Savior spoke of the unbelieving and ungodly, who have not believed in the Lord Jesus. For he says thus: he who believes in me is not judged; but he who does not believe has already been judged, because he has not believed in the name of the only-begotten Son of God. And this is the judgement, that the light has come into this world, and men loved darkness rather than the light; for their works were evil. It is clear, then, of whom it was said, that is: of those who have not believed in the Lord Jesus. The Apostle too, although he said "all", certainly spoke of those who have believed, but are to render an account of their actions on the day of judgement.
