In fact, he himself says elsewhere that the testimony of our conscience will be revealed at the day of judgement, when our thoughts will either accuse one another, as it is written, or defend, and the secrets of the heart will lie open. And what is more evident than what he says elsewhere: we shall all indeed rise again, but we shall not all be changed? For the righteous will be changed into incorruption, the truth of the body remaining. Daniel too says: the judgement sat, and the books were opened. He shows, then, that there will be a judgement, especially since the same prophet says elsewhere: many who sit in the mound of the earth shall rise up, these to eternal life and these to reproach and eternal confusion; and those who understand shall shine as the brightness of the heaven, and of the righteous many shall shine like the stars.

52. What is this judgement of judges sitting, and what are the books opened, but our consciences, like books containing the series of our sins? And yet it is mean even to think of this as though it were like a human judgement. Christ's judgement is another thing, where the conscience betrays itself, which cannot lie hidden from the judge of hidden things, where thoughts shine before him who used to say to men while they were still thinking: why do you think evil things in your hearts? When he said it to the Jews, he said it to all, so that no one should think that what he did could be hidden, so that no one should think that, shut in by walls, he could escape the witness of his hidden error. And therefore the Evangelist too testifies, saying: but Jesus knew their thoughts. How, then, does he say: the books were opened? Not, surely, books written in ink, but with the traces of offences and the defilement of shameful deeds. The book of your conscience will be opened, the book of your heart will be opened, our fault will be read out. There is a book there where there is a tablet; rather, there are books inscribed where there are tablets inscribed, which the apostolic teaching inscribes with the Holy Spirit, as we read where Paul says: you are our letter, inscribed not with ink but with the Spirit of the living God, not on tablets of stone but on fleshly tablets of the heart. There are, then, tablets of the heart inscribed by the Holy Spirit; these will be read out; if you have done well, the writing will remain. See that you do not take away the grace of the Holy Spirit, see that you do not erase it and write your shameful deeds in ink, lest the day of judgement come and the judge say: "let the books be read out, let the tablets of his deeds be read out", and say to you: "I wrote your tablets; why did you erase my letters? I too wrote my gifts; how did you erase my favours and write your disgraces? Did you not read that I write? Did I not say to you through the mouth of my prophet: my tongue is the pen of a scribe writing swiftly? And the judgement belongs to the Word".

53. Many, then, of those sleeping in the mound of the earth will rise, those who have done well to the resurrection of life, those who have done evil to the resurrection of judgement. What Daniel says: to eternal life, this the Savior called to the resurrection of life; likewise what Daniel says: to reproach and confusion, the Savior said: to the resurrection of judgement. Hence it is not expedient for us to come to judgement, and it is not expedient not to come, lest either we seem condemned beforehand or, in this stain of vices, we undergo the weight of so great a judgement. The prophet asks that the Lord may not enter into judgement with his servant; how much more ought we to fear the judgement of the Lord! Suppose that the Lord in his mercy will pardon: how much will be brought to light that I believed lay hidden! What shame, what confusion will there be, when I, who professed to teach others, am myself caught in the very thing for which I rebuked others!

54. And therefore, since both the Savior has set down two kinds of resurrection and so has John in the Apocalypse, saying: blessed is he who has a part in the first resurrection — for these come to grace without judgement, while those who do not come to the first resurrection but are reserved for the second will be burned until they fulfil the times between the first and the second resurrection, or, if they have not fulfilled them, will remain longer in punishment —, therefore, then, let us pray that we may be found worthy to have a part in the first resurrection. There are those who rose again at the Passion of Christ, and they are plainly blessed, who received the grace of Christ and heard his voice, of which it is written: the hour has come when the dead shall hear the voice of the Son of God, and those who hear shall live, and: they entered into the holy city. I think this signifies rather that Jerusalem above than this one which he left, which he accused, because into this one they entered with their feet, but into that one above by their merits.

55. But let us beware of this too, that while placed here we rise from the mound of the earth. There are those who, while living, are surrounded by a tomb and filled with the dead, whose throat is a tomb, as they speak words not of life but of death. If here we rise again from the dead, there too we shall rise again; if here we have not been dry bones, but have received the dew of the word, the moisture of the Holy Spirit, there too we shall live; if here Jesus raises us with his loud voice, as he raised Lazarus too, and through his disciples loosens us from the bonds of death and leads us into Bethany, where Lazarus was, that is, into the house of obedience, and admits us to his banquet, there too we shall feast with him always, and there we shall recline with him, and there the ointment will be fragrant for us whose pouring out the traitor alone lamented.

56. Therefore the ungodly do not rise again in the judgement, that is: into the portion of those who are to undergo judgement, nor do sinners rise in the council of the righteous. You see that the ungodly rise and do not rise in the judgement, since sinners too, though they do not rise in the council of the righteous, yet rise again in the judgement. Hence it appears that those who have believed well and carried out their faith in works as well are not themselves judged, but rise in the council of the righteous; while sinners, who cannot rise among the righteous, will rise in the judgement. You have two orders; there remains a third, that of the ungodly, who, since they have not believed, have already been judged, and therefore do not rise in the judgement, but to punishment; for they loved, indeed, darkness rather than light, and therefore their judgement is punishment, and perhaps the punishment of darkness. And it could indeed be understood that those who have evil works though they believe in Christ, wishing indeed to live rightly but overcome by the enticements of sins, loved darkness more than light, that is: they loved both, but darkness more. But since he had spoken beforehand of those who have not believed, I think it must be taken thus: that they loved darkness and not light; for Christ is the light. As for those, then, who have not believed the light, it is absurd that they should be believed to have loved even a little the light which they did not know; for they have not known, indeed, nor have they understood; they walk in darkness, as it is written.

57. There follows: for the Lord knows the way of the righteous, and the road of the ungodly shall perish. Notice the sense: the ungodly do not rise again in the judgement, for the Lord knows the way of the righteous. He certainly knows the ways of those whose steps he directs; for there are steps of men which are directed by the Lord. The ways of a man too are directed by the Lord. The Lord knows these ways which are straight, which tend to that life of him who says: I am the way and the truth and the life. This is the good way, but the way of the world is crooked; he does not deign to know that way. For he recognizes those who are his own, who do his works; but to those who work iniquity the Lord says: depart from me, all you workers of iniquity; I do not know you. Not through ignorance, but because they are unworthy of the knowledge of God, they are not known.

58. Beautifully he says: and the road of the ungodly shall perish. The Latin made a separation, so as to say "iter" [road], and as it were distinguished road from way; but the Greek said "uiam" [way] in both places. Yet the Latin did not do so idly, since the Lord too said I am the way; he did not say "I am the road". And he said that the road of the ungodly perishes, not the ungodly; he preserves their substance, so that if they are converted they will lose only the road of ungodliness, which neither was from the beginning nor will be. What is accidental, then, perishes; what is substantial remains. And the ungodly perish in the way in which it is said: the soul that sins, it shall die, so that they die by the sting of sin, not by the dissolution of their whole substance.

On Psalm 35 (36)

1. Being about to say in the following psalm what the form of the righteous man is, the prophet first portrayed the form of the unrighteous man. For we cannot know what the form of righteousness is unless we know what the likeness of iniquity is; for they are opposed and contrary to one another. For in the one is the simple bearing of nature, in the other the deceitful craftiness of wickedness; the one displays the images of the virtues, the other holds out the fabrications of the vices. That this order is fitting, reason itself teaches; and, if testimonies are to be taken from opponents, the teachers of philosophy themselves have admitted it, in following it. Who would doubt that David, whom not only Plato's master but not even Plato's grandfathers' grandfathers could have seen, was far earlier than the times of Plato, since the one lived at the beginning of the kingdom of the Jews, which was prolonged through countless courses of years, the other after the times of the captivity, by which the kingdom of his nation had already been dissolved? Therefore he himself, who holds the primacy over all in worldly wisdom, when he said that the form of righteousness could not be grasped unless they had first thought that the chain of unrighteousness should be examined, brought forward the example that those who wish to seek gold first smear themselves with mud. So righteousness is gold, iniquity is mud. That this has been said by our own writers also is not in doubt, since it is written: I will blot them out like the mud of the streets; and truly mud, because it defiles those who have come near it. Let us flee unrighteousness, then, lest we be defiled with the filth of mud, and lest not our outer foot but, what is more grievous, our mind be sunk in its whirlpool. Philosophy indeed says that it seeks gold, but it turns over mud, since it seeks divinity in statues; but the rod breaks the vessels of the potter. We seek gold with which to cleanse our bodies, carrying about the dying of Jesus Christ in our body, that the life of Jesus Christ also may be manifested in our body. Good gold is the blood of Christ, rich for the price, flowing freely to wash away every sin. We have spoken of the order; let us consider the title.

2. Unto the end, he says, for the servant of the Lord, a psalm of David. In the whole body of the Psalter this is the only title by which he testifies that he wrote a psalm "for the servant of the Lord". Who is this servant of the Lord? Let us see what the course of the psalm signifies, to whom this psalm was spoken. Let us hear whom he addresses: Lord, he says, your mercy is in heaven, and your truth reaches to the clouds; your righteousness is like the mountains of God, your judgements like a great deep. To whom is this said but to the Son of God? For these things belong not to human but to divine power. But what does it mean that the title declared this psalm to be spoken "for the servant of the Lord", when in a later title he preferred to name the prayer of Moses, the man of God, rather than "the servant of the Lord"? What the plan of the prophet was, then, I do not understand, or rather the plan of the Lord the Savior himself, who spoke in the prophet. Truly his judgements are like a great deep, that he should reserve the honour for the servant and ascribe to himself the condition of servitude. I seem to myself to understand; I seem to have drawn something of spiritual grace. This psalm, as we said, was written against unrighteousness, and therefore the unrighteousness of the unbelieving weighs the more heavily, who refused to receive Christ Jesus the Lord, when for their sake he had taken on servitude. For what is so unjust as to have been ungrateful for such great benefits, that those to whom he was giving salvation should shed his blood? Indeed elsewhere, when he was speaking of his Passion, to make the odium of the ungodliness of the Jews more bitter, he speaks thus: Do not turn away your face from your servant; because I am afflicted, hear me speedily. I am poor and in sorrow.

3. Come, Paul, interpret for us by what reason the Lord of heaven and earth called himself poor, what this poverty is. You certainly called him rich; how is the same one rich and poor? Let us hear the testimony of Paul: You know, he says, the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, that for your sakes he became poor, though he was rich, that you through his want might be made rich. Come you too, Isaiah, interpret for us how Christ called himself sorrowing, who was wont to heal the pains of wounds. The physician came to cure the sick: what had he himself to grieve over? Let us hear the testimony of Isaiah too: For us, he says, he grieves, and we reckoned him to be in sorrows. He expressed the feeling of a physician, who, even if he himself had no wound to grieve over, yet suffered with the wounded. This, then, is what the Lord reproaches the Jews with in the psalm: "I am poor for your sake, I grieve for you, and you laid ungodly hands on me, saying: Let us take away the righteous man, because he is useless to us. Let us put wood into his bread". Well does he say bread for his flesh; he brought nourishment, they in return for the benefit paid back punishment. It is no wonder, then, if they hunger who denied themselves the nourishment of eternal life. Beautifully too they joined these two: Let us put wood into his bread; the Jews did not know what they were saying, and they spoke a mystery. The cross of Christ has given paradise back to us; this is the wood which the Lord pointed out to Adam, saying of the tree of life, which was in the midst of paradise, that it was to be eaten, but of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, that it was not to be eaten. Adam went astray, he did not keep the commandments, he tasted what was forbidden; through the tree we began to hunger, until the flesh received its nourishment. Therefore the Lord in Christ joined flesh and wood, that the ancient hunger might cease and the grace of life be restored. Blessed is the wood of the Lord, which crucified the sins of all; blessed the flesh of the Lord, which ministered food to all!

4. Concerning the poverty and the sorrow of the Lord, then, we have brought forward suitable testimonies, as I think, of the saints, of whom the one saw and spoke, the other was chosen that he might speak. Let us bring forward again, then, the testimonies of these same approved men concerning the servitude of the Lord, or rather his own testimonies, which he himself spoke about himself in both of them. Let us hear, then, what he says: Thus says the Lord, who formed me from the womb as his servant, to gather Jacob and Israel to him. We observe, then, that he took on the form of servitude for the gathering of the people. Hear, O Jew: you both make an insult out of grace and turn to unbelief the very thing on account of which you ought rather to have believed. He came to call you, and you revile him. He said "a servant from the womb". Hear, Arian, from what womb; run back a little above: From the womb, he says, of my mother the Lord called my name. Let us hear what his name is, by which the Father called him: Behold, a virgin shall conceive in the womb and bear a son, and his name shall be called Emmanuel, which is interpreted: God with us. For what other name of Christ is there but "Son of God"? Take another. Gabriel, indeed, had also said to Joseph concerning Mary: She shall bear a son, and you shall call his name Jesus. Take the voice of God: And you, Bethlehem of Judaea, are not the least among the princes of Judah; for out of you shall come forth a prince who shall rule my people. Mark the mystery: from the womb of the virgin the same one came forth both servant and Lord, servant to work, Lord to command, that he might root the kingdom for God in the minds of men; both are one, not one from the Father and another from the virgin, but the same, who before the ages was from the Father, himself afterwards took flesh from the virgin. Therefore he is called both servant and Lord: servant for our sake, but by the unity of the divine substance God from God, Prince from Prince, equal from equal; for he did not beget one degenerate, in whom he declared that he was well pleased.

5. It is a great thing for you, he says, to be called my servant, that you may establish the tribes of Jacob. Everywhere he keeps the names of his dignity: a great God and a great servant; nor does he lose in the flesh the name of his greatness, of whose greatness there is no end. And rightly secure, when he was in the form of God, as the Apostle says, he did not think it robbery to be equal to God, but emptied himself, taking the form of a servant, made in the likeness of men; and found in appearance as a man, he humbled himself, becoming obedient unto death, even the death of the cross. This, then, is he who is equal as being the Son of God; this one took the form of a servant in the flesh; this one tasted death, whose greatness has no end, because the end of the Law is Christ, unto righteousness for everyone who believes, that we may all believe in him and adore him with inmost affection. Good is the servitude which made all free; good the servitude which acquired the name above every name; good the humility which brought it about that at his name every knee should bend, of things in heaven and on earth and under the earth, and every tongue confess that the Lord Jesus is in the glory of God the Father. Unto this end, then, this psalm is directed, that we too may be servants unto righteousness, not proud unto arrogance; for servitude is the wage of liberty, but arrogance is the wage of death. But now let us take up the course of the psalm.

6. The unrighteous man said, that he might sin against himself. What he said he did not express, and therefore I think it must be understood thus: that whatever the unrighteous man says is sin. For all unrighteousness is sin, since, where the instrument is faulty, how can the song not be faulty? The offspring of degenerate matter is discoloured. Whatever, then, the unrighteous man speaks is iniquity, which turns back upon its own author, like the offspring of the viper, which first tears open its own mother, as the common saying is; so he sins against himself. I think nothing more sublime has been said than this sentence; in those who claimed for themselves the wisdom of the world I have read nothing like it, I have learned nothing like it. Nor is it surprising, since they spoke by human talent, while this the Spirit of God poured in, the Spirit of truth: that the unrighteous man sins against himself, begets wounds for himself, stabs himself through. For as thorns spring up in the hand of the drunkard, as Scripture asserts, so too in the speech of the unrighteous man there spring up things that pierce the speaker. The unrighteous man speaks, and his inner conscience is wounded, because in every word that he speaks he is not free of fraud. For whose punishment is heavier than one's own, since he pays a penalty for every word? The serpent pours its venom into others, the unrighteous man into himself; for whatever he has poured out flows back upon him. The unrighteous man, then, is both useless to others and harmful to himself, but the life of the righteous man is fruitful to others and sweet to himself; for Solomon says: Son, if you are wise for yourself, you will be wise for your neighbours also; but if you turn out evil, you alone will drink up evils.

7. We observe, then, that righteousness is born for others rather than for itself; it looks to the common advantage, not its own, and counts another's good as its own gain. Blessed and glorious is righteousness, whose good profits all; for the most part it sets out from one and reaches all. Righteous was David, who spared his enemy and preferred to protect his innocence rather than his life, lest he should be avenged to the public harm and, by taking vengeance himself on the man who lay in wait for him, give others an example of attacking a prince, even in evil causes. Righteous was Abel, who thought that the firstfruits of his sheep, which the Lord had given him, should be offered to the Lord; and therefore he pleased God more, because he made no delay and proved his devotion. But this the ungodly man could not bear, the transgressor of righteousness, the root of iniquity, and therefore he killed his brother, because his brother's victim had been more approved by God than his own offering. But the one, slain, spoke to God in the voice of his blood; the other, living, was driven from the face of the Lord, and while punishment from the Lord was still held back, the consciousness of his sins tormented him. The wretch lay hidden, trembling and fearing, and while as yet no slayer was at hand, the very life of the unrighteous man tortured itself. He had received a sign, that no one should kill him, not that he might enjoy the sweetness of life, but that death might not take away his misery, so that he might suffer by fearing his slayer every day. He had indeed obtained that a slayer should be held back, but without any intermission he was himself his own executioner, himself his slayer.

8. What punishment is greater than to fear what you cannot avoid, and not to escape what you have feared? Hence David, splendidly expressing the most grievous dunning of a sinful conscience, says: For I acknowledge my iniquity, and my sin is ever against me. For the avenging image of our sin is spread before us and does not allow its defendant to be at rest; it brings on a wretched servitude and draws him into its chains, so that he cannot untie himself, since he willingly sold himself, though he was free not to take on the debt of sins and to keep the liberty of innocence. And so while we pray, sin spreads itself before us; when sleep has relaxed the senses of the body, sin returns. Always our error meets us like a wicked exactor, or like a shameless usurer dunning his debtor. Hence the Lord says: He who commits sin is the servant of sin. But the righteous man knows how to loosen the chains of his sins; he does not wait for an accuser but forestalls him, so that by confessing he lightens all his sin, lest the adversary have anything to charge him with. And therefore Scripture says to you: The righteous man is his own accuser at the beginning of his speech; for he snatches the voice from the adversary, and by the confession of his sins binds fast, as it were, certain teeth made ready for the prey of hostile accusation. Judas Iscariot said, so as to sin against himself — what did he say? —: Whomever I kiss, he it is; hold him. This he said, and by his own mouth drove himself into death. How many torturers must we think were in him, that he himself should bear down upon himself as the exactor of so grievous a punishment, and strangle himself with a noose!

9. The unrighteous man spoke and sinned against himself; the righteous man spoke: Behold, I the shepherd have done evil, and this flock, what has it done? and all his sin was forgiven. The righteous man spoke, then, and profited himself. Cain said: Am I my brother's keeper? and he lied to himself. Ananias said that he had brought the whole price of his sold land, and he lied to himself, who could have offered less without fraud. And therefore he says splendidly: And iniquity has lied to itself. For it lies first to itself when it lies to its own ruin; he sins against himself who robs himself of the sweetness of innocence. For what fruit is sweeter than purity and simplicity of heart, what food more pleasant than that on which the mind of one with a good conscience and the heart of the innocent feast? But iniquity weighs down the conscience as with a talent of lead, as Scripture testifies, and rightly David says: Like a heavy burden they have weighed heavy upon me, and Solomon: As an unripe grape to the teeth and smoke to the eyes, so is iniquity to all who use it. A heavy punishment, which hinders food, clouds the sight, and, what is more, pours a dark mist over the eyes of the inner mind, so that the unrighteous man cannot see what is true. And therefore he sins against himself who robs himself of the things that are precious.

10. The unrighteous man, then, speaks so as to sin against himself, but the righteous man speaks so as to profit both others and himself; the one speaks for destruction, the other speaks for salvation. Of the righteous and the faithful indeed it has been said: With the heart one believes unto righteousness, but with the mouth confession is made unto salvation. The tongue of the one wounds, but the tongue of the prudent heals. Hence, when David remonstrated with King Saul that, forgetful of the benefit of his safety, he was ungratefully pursuing to the death one by whom he saw that he had so often already been preserved, and was seeking the life of one who had exposed his own life to dangers for the king's safety, he said that the fruit of righteousness was not lost to him, since he had not slain the man who lay in wait for him when the Lord had delivered him into his hands. From the unrighteous iniquity came forth, from serpents venom was poured out; the venom and the weapons of iniquity have perished.

11. Therefore it is written: The unrighteous man said, that he might sin against himself. What did he say? I will set my throne above the clouds, and I will be like the Most High. The words have no effect, but they have sin; empty indeed is the pomp of boasting, but, guilty through the spirit of pride, he does not fear to violate the divine majesty with insult. For if any fear of God were in his disposition, he would not with rash daring have believed that he might carry out things full of guile in the sight of God, as though God, who is the searcher of the inmost parts, could not know hidden things. God beholds all things; there is nothing that escapes him, no one who deceives him; to him all things to come are present and things that lie hid are manifest. For if this sun of the world often sends its light into the closed dwellings of houses, how much more does God, supreme and eternal, search out and anticipate by his knowledge even the secret recesses of the human mind and every counsel of the angels! What else, then, does the unrighteous man do but seek for himself iniquity and hatred?

12. For so it is written: There is no fear of God before his eyes, for he has acted deceitfully in his sight, to find his iniquity and hatred. What is "to find"? Everyone, indeed, who seeks finds; so if evil is sought, it is sought in order to be found. But as he is praiseworthy who has found the good that he sought, so he is unpardonable who has laboured to find the evil that he had sought; we ought to spend our labour on good things, not on outrages and crimes. What, then, is it but the height of folly and madness to seek hatred, to find by evil arts iniquity, which no good man has sought?

13. Let us consider, then, how the wicked seek their iniquity and hatred. Herodias, we know, abounding in the wealth of royal power, was joined in lawful wedlock to the tetrarch Philip. She received Herod, who was on his way to Rome, as her husband's brother, by the right of hospitality and kinship, and, coupling her sins with him, not long after she left her husband and changed the rights of her partnership. Did not she seek iniquity and hatred, who deserted her marriage to find adultery? And because the holy Baptist steadfastly rebuked the unlawful nuptials, saying to Herod: "It is not lawful for you to have her as wife, who left her husband against the law and came together with her husband's brother as though by law", the adulteress was so stirred that she wished to kill him. But knowing that she could not easily bend Herod to the slaying of a righteous man, she devised a way to bring it about. It was Herod's birthday, which he was accustomed to celebrate with excessive luxury, as is the custom of most kings. She adorned her daughter, so that at the king's banquet the royal child should dance. And when she had pleased Herod, which ought to have displeased an uncle, he offered her to ask whatever reward she wished for the shame of her dancing. She consulted her mother, at whose prompting she demanded the head of the Baptist. Herod, overcome, because he had bound himself by an oath to give whatever his own niece asked, ordered John to be killed and his head brought. When it was brought he gave it to the girl, and she carried it to her mother. It is true, then, that iniquity and hatred are sought out, since that woman drew out by the depraved arts of a devised fraud what she could not obtain by an easy request.

14. What shall I say of Judas the traitor, who sought avarice and found sacrilege, saying: What will you give me, and I will deliver him to you?, and, not sated with the spoils of the poor, by sowing crimes reached the fullness of the most grievous crimes, mingling the holy sacraments of love with outrages, when he said: Whomever I kiss, he it is; hold him, so that truly it cannot be doubted that it was said of him: The words of his mouth are iniquity and deceit? He bore kisses on his lips, he poured venom into hearts; he was plotting bitter punishments, he brought a pledge of grace.

15. Well did the prophet cause it to be added concerning him: He would not understand, that he might do well; for he did not sin by nature but by will. Indeed, he was an apostle, he heard the divine commandments daily, he learned the heavenly mystery; he could have understood so as to do well, if he had wished. He saw paralytics healed, light poured back into the blind, the dead raised; ought he not to have understood that it was God who could do such things? But he would not understand, because, a covetous man and intent on money, he turned the vigour of his mind away from the knowledge of the divinity. For everyone who has gorged himself on outrages does not wish to understand what belongs to the heavenly commandments, lest he be called back from his vices. See him fleeing from understanding, who says: Darkness covers me and walls surround me; who knows whether the Lord sees? For he does not wish to seek and find that God sees and knows all things, lest he be drawn back from crime by fear. See King Ahab asking Naboth for his vineyard; recognize the man answering him: Far be it from me that I should give up the inheritance of my fathers. Ought he not to have understood that the man was answering justly? But he would not understand, that he might do well, and not seek what was another's. Ought he not to have understood that Elijah served the true God, when he saw the sky shut at the word of Elijah for three years and three months, and rains sent down at his prayers, so that all dry things were watered? But he would not understand, lest he should condemn unbelief and follow faith.

16. Moreover, he has meditated iniquity on his bed, where he ought to have sought the truth. For on our beds our sins ought to prick us, not be committed by our very sinning there, as the prophet says: What you say in your hearts, be pricked for it also on your beds.

17. He has stood, he says, in every way that is not good, but he did not hate malice. For not undeservedly does he meditate unjust things who stands in the way of error and loves malice, which he ought to hate. He said more in saying "has stood", as though he had long remained on the way of wickedness in a lasting station; for that man is blessed who has not stood in the way of sinners, not he who does not cease to stand in it. And therefore the first precepts of the holy prophet David are to flee ungodliness, not to stand in sin, to meditate in the Law, that you may understand what things are good, and distinguish the just from the unjust. In all things, then, the root of generation must be watched, lest a useless sap infect it from the beginning; and the vices of minds are much more grievous than those of young shrubs. We must beware above all, then, lest the habit of malice grow into the human character and every age that follows degenerate; for a tree, indeed, that is bad bears bad fruit. For if often we do what we do not wish and cannot avoid it, and do what we hate, bound either by some delight in outrage or by the stealthy approach of sin, how can we avoid what we love? We are bound even when unwilling; how can we not be held when willing? Paul scarcely freed himself from errors, wrestling in a daily struggle, so that, stripped of the chains of captivity, he might be saved by the grace of Christ; and do you think that by standing by your sins you can attain to the merit of the heavenly promises? The crown is sought by wrestling, not by wrestling against it; it is gained by zeal, not by opposition. Even for those who contend the support of the divine mercy must be asked, that the crown of righteousness may be bestowed for the merit of their labours.

18. Next let us consider what follows: Lord, he says, your mercy is in heaven, and your truth reaches to the clouds; your righteousness is like the mountains of God, your judgements like a great deep. Did not Paul follow this passage when he said: Unhappy man that I am! Who will deliver me from the body of this death? The grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord? For when the human mind is troubled and the hard and rough things of the contests weary us, the help of the Lord must be asked. And therefore, turning to the Lord, he calls upon him and implores him to stand by those who labour as their helper. Mercy, then, is to be sought from heaven, the truth of God gathered from the oracles of the prophets, who like clouds cover over the mysteries of divine knowledge. For God made darkness his hiding place, so that first you may receive the rain of mystic fruitfulness, and then, steeped in the heavenly dew, recognize the brightness of the light unlocked, so that you can say: Of his fullness we have received. For who can easily comprehend the secrets of God, whose righteousness is like the mountains of God, or, as Aquila has put it, like strong mountains, because his precepts are full of strong power? Hence the Apostle too, seeing that they are lofty, dares and says: O the height of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God! How unsearchable are his judgements and how untraceable his ways! For who has known the mind of the Lord? Or who has been his counsellor? So he related the height of the riches of wisdom to the height of the mountains; hear which mountains. For the Son of God himself is a great mountain, and therefore go up into this mountain, you who bring good tidings to Zion, that you may stand rooted and planted in Christ. There is a mountain as the wisdom of God, there is also a mountain as the righteousness of God, there is also a mountain as the knowledge of God, there is a mountain as sanctification, there is a mountain as redemption, there is a mountain as resurrection. Scripture has shown us these mountains, which said: But in him you are in Christ Jesus, who was made for us wisdom from God, and righteousness and sanctification and redemption; or else because the Son of God became an angel to angels, a prophet to prophets, he whose judgements are like a great deep. Hear what is good: Deep calls on deep at the voice of your cataracts, that is: the Scripture of the Old Testament summons the course of the New Testament to the consummation of sanctification and the fullness of grace, with a certain sound and outpouring of spiritual overflow.

19. Men and beasts you will save, O Lord, as you have multiplied your mercy, O God; but the sons of men hope under the protection of your wings; they shall be inebriated with the plenty of your house, and you will make them drink the torrent of your pleasure. What is "men and beasts"? Rational and irrational, surely. To the rational, judgements; to the irrational, mercy; the ones are ruled, the others are fed. And therefore he added: But the sons of men hope under the protection of your wings, that is: not the generation of vipers, but the sons of men, who live after the image and likeness of God, are placed not in pastures but at banquets; for some are in the place of pasture, others in the privilege of the sacraments; for the imperfect the juice of milk, for the perfect the table of refreshment, of which he said: You have prepared a table in my sight, on which is the living bread, that is, the Word of God; on which is the oil of sanctification, with which the head of the righteous man is made rich and the inner sense is strengthened, so that the oil of the sinner may be abolished; on which also is that inebriating cup, "how glorious" or "how mighty"; for the Greek said κράτιστον, either "powerful" or "strong" or "mighty"; mighty indeed, since by it outrages are washed away or blotted out. Good, then, is the inebriation of the cup of salvation. But there is another inebriation, from the overflow of the Scriptures, and there is yet another inebriation, through the infusion of the Holy Spirit; indeed those who spoke in different tongues in the Acts of the Apostles seemed to the hearers to be full of new wine. The house, then, is the Church; the plenty of the house, the overflow of graces; the torrent of pleasure, the Holy Spirit.

20. Nor let the name or the nature of a torrent offend you, in that it is sometimes drier or thinner, so that its streams are wont to cease. This one of ours ceased too; the torrent of spiritual outpouring once ceased among the people of the Jews. Why do I say "the torrent ceased", when the sea has ceased, as today's reading taught us, the Lord saying: Behold, by my rebuke I will make the sea desolate and make the rivers a desert, and their fish shall dry up, the fish, surely, of the Jews, through the dryness of their unbelief, so that they cannot swim out? The torrent ceased, then, because of the sacrileges of the synagogue, because that people was dry in faith, barren in works, captive to sins. For what should flow for those who did not drink but defiled the sacred channels, since they denied the fountain itself? A good torrent, which ceased for them that it might abound for us and turn away the thirst of human captivity, like a torrent in the south. Hence Scripture also says: Arise, north wind, and come, south wind, which is wont to blow through the young trees of paradise.

21. We have stated the sense; let us also consider the wording. Why did he say you will make them drink the torrent of pleasure, and not "torrente" [with the torrent], as if "de torrente" [from the torrent], unless because he wished to express the eagerness of the drinkers, as though they wished to drink up the torrent itself, if they could? This is perhaps the torrent of pleasure, the fountain which we read of in Genesis, which waters paradise and is divided into four rivers that go round the whole earth. From this fountain too the spiritual virtues flow forth: prudence, temperance, fortitude, justice. A good fountain of grace, and of the same splendour and nature as that river too, of which it is said further on: The rush of the river gladdens the city of God.

22. Most fittingly, then, he went on: For with you is the fountain of life, and in your light we shall see light. Extend your mercy to those who know you, and your righteousness to those who are upright of heart. For after the recalling of the heavenly benefits, thanks are rightly rendered to their author, our Lord Jesus Christ, who, the watering fountain of eternal life, came down to earth to water the drynesses of our heart. He is the brightness of the glory of God the Father and the image of his substance, and therefore in his true light, which enlightens every man coming into this world, we shall see, he says, the Father, for God is light. Rightly also it is said: In your light we shall see light, according to that saying: He who sees me sees my Father also. With you, then, O fountain of life, we shall see the Father present. For as you were with the Father in the beginning, God the Word, so the Father, who is in you, is always with you; for he is with him in whom he also is. And the coming of the Lord the Savior is prophesied, who, being to come to earth, was to say: I and the Father are one, that is: we are one light, as we are one name; through the unity of light and of name we both are one, nay, the Trinity is one in the unity of substance, but with the distinction of each person. Trinity signifies the distinction of the persons, unity the power. It can also be said to the Father: For with you is the fountain of life, that is, he from whom life proceeded, which was the Word, and which always was, for this reason, that it was with you. All things were made through him and in him, and he is the life of all things, and he has manifested you to us, that he may enlighten the hearts of men unto the knowledge of your majesty.

23. Extend, then, your mercy to those who know you. A prerogative is extended, so that those whom the merit of knowing you supports may deserve mercy. Indeed, further on we recognize that zeal for knowledge was a support even to persons of lower rank, the Lord saying: I will be mindful of Rahab and Babylon, to those who know me, that is: I will be mindful of that harlot Rahab and of that confusion among those who know me, or among them themselves, because that harlot recognized me, whom the chosen people did not know. Among those, then, who have known me Rahab will be remembered, that she may obtain the worthy reward of faith. Hence we note that it was said in the Gospel also: Harlots and publicans shall go before you in the kingdom of heaven. But because we are weak in carrying out, though devoted in believing, extend your mercy to those who believe in you, so that our deeds also may accord with our devotion and faith, and the weakness of this body may not forsake the zeal of our mind, but in our very temptations and weaknesses we may glory, in which the Apostle gloried, saying: Most gladly, therefore, will I rather glory in my weaknesses, that the power of Christ may extend itself in me.

24. Most beautifully, then, he added: Let not the foot of pride come to me, and let not the hand of sinners move me. There fell those who work iniquity; they were thrust out and could not stand. Above, some take "the torrent of your pleasure" as fortitude in temptations, such as that of the martyrs, in whom it pleased God to display for himself the pleasure of a sweet and welcome contest. For if winters are taken for temptations — whence the Lord warns that we must pray that our flight be not in winter or on the sabbath, that is: either in temptation or in idleness; and in winter a torrent grows and is filled up and swells — a torrent surely can be taken for a heavier temptation, such as there is in persecutions. And therefore it is well called a torrent of pleasure, because where the sin of persecution superabounded, the grace of confession also superabounded.

25. See the power of the prophet's distinction. First he set forth the unrighteous man, who either in his heart or in his speech says that he may sin against himself, or sin in himself; for the Greek codex has ἐν αὑτῷ, that is "in himself", but not all of them. And perhaps for this reason, because the fool has said in his heart that there is no God, lest perhaps the unrighteous man too should speak within himself and sin within himself, although to deny God openly is the mark of a madman. But many profess unrighteousness as a matter of praise; for to plunder, to harm even those who have done no injury, to defraud: many think this glorious for themselves. So first he set forth the life of the unrighteous man, afterwards he joined to it the mysteries of divine knowledge, that, fearing God, we may turn aside from iniquity and unrighteousness. He added a prayer, that he may deign to free his righteous ones from the fellowship of the unrighteous, that in those also who are clean of heart the divine righteousness may extend itself like a loving watchwoman, lest the onset of sins, like that of enemies, steal upon us and capture us sleeping. Therefore we must always keep watch, the camp of the Lord must be fortified, because the enemy and adversary comes by night, when the senses are held by sleep and the body is distended with food; we must pray that the righteousness of God may extend itself in us, which makes those set in weakness stronger, so that each one of us may say: When I am weak, then I am powerful.

26. Then he also prays specially for himself, to teach us how we ought to pray to the Lord: Let not, he says, the foot of pride come to me, that is: let me not fall into pride. Indeed elsewhere too he makes mention of himself, saying: If I have not walked in great things. Here he prayed, there he fulfilled it; he would not have fulfilled it had he not prayed. Pride must be guarded against, which trips us up even in prosperity; indeed Adam fell more grievously in paradise than if he had fallen on earth. To fall from the heights is a precipice; on level ground it is called a slip. Therefore the foot of the proud man errs, because he does not hold the head; the eyes, indeed, of the wise man are in his head. It is no wonder, then, if the step errs where no eye is kept. Let the eye go before, that the foot may follow. How then will you go as a traveller in darkness? The foot quickly stumbles in the night, if the moon, as a kind of eye of the world, does not show the way. And you are in the night of this world; let the Church show you the way, let the sun of righteousness enlighten you from on high, so that you cannot fear a fall.

27. And because he had spoken of the foot of pride, he added: and let not the hand of sinners move me. For as the saints are members of Christ, so the ungodly are members of the devil. Let not the hand of sinners move me, that is: let not the acts of those who sin move me from my station of righteousness. For often, when we see sinners abounding in prosperous successes, we waver in our affection, and as if by a kind of hand of sinners we are torn from the root of virtue. We must beware, then, lest the hostile hand supplant those whom the divine hand has planted in the house of God. Yet beautifully does he say this who is beaten by persecutors. But those who are moved from their root, which had already clung to Christ, fall.

28. Hence he added: There fell those who work iniquity. What is "there"? Is it where pride is, where the hand of sinners is, or where they stood, where they had been planted before, that there they fell; or in the mire; or on the spot, as it were at that very time, in that very place? And it seems ambiguous. But elsewhere we are taught what "there" means, where it is said: There were pains as of a woman in travail. Good is the pain for one who is in labour with faith, by which Christ is formed and born in the Church. And elsewhere he says: I will be mindful of Rahab and Babylon, to those who know me; for indeed the foreigners and Tyre and the people of the Ethiopians, these were there, that is: where those are who know me, where are the foundations on the holy mountains, there are the foreigners too, because they too thought that they must believe in me. There, then, they fell where they ought rather to have stood, because Adam too fell in paradise, and Christ came for a fall and for a resurrection, that he might be the fall of the unbelieving, but the resurrection of the righteous and the faithful.

29. Next, they were thrust out, because unrighteous men could not stand in the holy place. Hence the Apostle also said: And you who stand, see that you do not fall, and surely he said it to one who stood not in body but in faith. We can also understand "there" of the future, as in: There shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth, and: Naked shall I go there. And how brief, yet how great, is the conclusion at the close! "Let me not be proud," he says, "so as to sin; let me not sin, lest I be moved; let me not be moved, lest I fall; let me not fall, lest I be thrust out, as Adam was thrust out of paradise, because in him first the foot of pride could not stand". Pride does not know how to stand, and if it has fallen, it does not know how to rise again. And therefore he spoke beautifully of the proud in earlier psalms also: Some in chariots and some on horses, but we shall be magnified in the name of our God — for this is stable, that untrustworthy — and he subjoined: They are bound and have fallen, but we have risen and are set upright. And in the book of the prophet Micah it is written: Do not rejoice proudly over me, my enemy, because I have fallen; but I shall rise again. For we have fallen in this world, but in Christ we have risen, to whom be honour, glory, power, perpetuity, both from the ages, and now, and always, and unto all the ages of ages. Amen.

On Psalm 36 (37)

1. All divine Scripture is either natural, or mystical, or moral: natural in Genesis, in which it is set forth how heaven, seas and lands were made and in what manner this world was established; mystical in Leviticus, in which the priestly mystery is contained; moral in Deuteronomy, in which human life is shaped according to the precept of the Law. Hence also the three books of Solomon seem to have been chosen out of very many: Ecclesiastes concerning natural things, the Song of Songs concerning mystical things, Proverbs concerning moral things.

2. But since the body of all the psalms is one, nothing in them is therefore divided and set apart; rather, as the occasion offered itself, the discipline of instruction of this kind has been carried on with nothing left out. For it most evidently contains the natural, by speaking of angels and powers, of the sun and moon, of the stars and the light, of the heaven of heavens and the water that is above the heavens: for he spoke, and they were made; he commanded, and they were created. He established them for ever and ever; he set a precept, and it shall not pass away. And he spoke of mystical things, when he wrote of hidden things and of the ointment of the sacred anointing and of the completion of the tabernacle. Their grace is manifold, since the Lord spoke in the prophets at sundry times and in divers manners. For he both foretold that the Son of God would come as the prince of priests, and the titles "for the winepresses" declared that he would suffer for us and would cleanse our sins with his blood, and the psalm "of the resurrection" expressed that he would rise again; and another title afterwards, in which "his land was restored", indicated also that the change of all things was to come through the sacraments of faith. And of moral things he wove together a great deal, and showed the various kinds of virtues, and gave precepts for living, by which he healed the sores of our errors, and renewed human ways, and changed our inmost affections, as Psalm 33 (34) teaches, which Saint Peter too fixed in his epistle as a remedy upon our hearts, and also this one, which has been set before us in today's reading. For these above the rest are found to be ethical, but this one, which is inscribed the thirty-sixth, is the more extensive. For although that one is very sweet and clear, this one is yet sweeter, because that one taught me to restrain my tongue from evil, this one to cease from anger and to leave off indignation; that one enjoined me to speak without guile, this one, which has shown the rewards of meekness, has trained me to be meek. What greater remedies are there for the human heart than meekness and simplicity, by which every pain of an injury received is relieved and every stain of crime is shut out?

3. To these remedies, then, let us put our hand, so that we may cure our own wounds, lest, if we wish to say that we can cure others, it be said to us: Physician, cure yourself. Therefore, as is customary not only among physicians but also among the common people, that few profess medicine, yet very many say that they know some of its remedies, or as the physicians' boys do, let us too carry a remedy to the needy with another's medicine. Let the rich man call in the master, the poor man the assistant. Let him who grasps the height of the perfect take from the master himself; let him who desires plainer things feed in the valley, as a little child who fears the river drinks from the brook, and one who dreads the deep swims along the shore. Beautifully, moreover, does holy David describe in the preceding psalm the life of the unrighteous man, here that of the righteous man; there the unrighteous man is set forth, here the righteous man is instructed; that one sins for himself, this one takes away even the sins of others. And therefore let us consider what the first remedy of righteousness is.

4. Do not, he says, act malignly among the malignant, nor be emulous of those who work iniquity. Let us first learn what it is to emulate, although the force of this word is less in Latin than in Greek; for we read of emulation both in a good sense and in a bad. Thus the Apostle says: It is good to be emulous in a good thing always, and above he himself said: They emulate you not well, but they wish to shut you out, that you may emulate them, and again he himself says: Emulate the better gifts, and to the Romans you have: I say then: Have they so stumbled that they should fall? God forbid; but their offence is salvation for the Gentiles, that they may emulate them, and below: For I say to you Gentiles: as long as I am the apostle of the Gentiles, I will make my ministry illustrious, if by any means I may rouse my flesh to emulation and save some of them, that is: I may stir my flesh; indeed the Greek has παραζηλώσω. For here too it has μὴ παραζήλου ἐν πονηρευομένοις and μηδὲ ζήλου τοὺς ποιοῦντας τὴν ἀνομίαν, that is: do not rouse the malignant to emulation, since they contend not about good but about evil.

5. What is it to rouse to emulation? Let us make it plain, if we can, by an example. There are certain incontinent women who tempt the hearts of other women's husbands. When they have subjected these to their own habit and disgrace, not content to keep their error secret, they are eager to flaunt their shameful deed before the men's wives, so as to rouse and inflame them to jealousy of themselves, seeking a kind of triumph from the pain and agitation of virtuous minds. These women, not bearing, out of a woman's emulation, the insult to their own marriage-bed, either dissolve the bonds of the wedlock once agreed upon or contend in daily strife; and out of emulation comes discord, out of dissension separation, by which the whole house is thrown into confusion. By the example, then, of a wanton woman, learn, you who read, that it is the part of the most wicked and detestable cunning to stir another's soul by a shameless emulation, and that zeal is one thing, parazeal another. For this is the perverse emulation that steals into the affection of good men, by which the Jew too went astray, who, straying from the true and good discipline of the Gospel, turned aside through emulation for the Law, as the Apostle says of himself, that according to emulation for the Law he persecuted the Church of Christ.

6. Therefore, although our God was frequently offended by the ungrateful dispositions and complaints of the Jews, yet he by no means abandoned the people once taken up and chosen for himself; but the synagogue, like a forward harlot, began to provoke him to the bitterness of emulation by mingling herself in the sacrileges of transgression. Thus they said to the priest Aaron: Make us gods, that we may worship, and they began to worship the head of a calf. Hence through the great song of Moses the Lord resounded in his mouth, saying: They have roused me to emulation with what is not God, they have provoked me with their idols; and I will rouse them to emulation with what is not a people, with a foolish nation I will provoke them. See how the Lord condemned the synagogue for its harlot's ways, so as to turn their own cunning back upon them, and that they might be struck by their own device, who with grievous sacrileges rejected the Lord their God, by whom they had been taken up, choosing for themselves gods to worship; while he, on the contrary, took to himself a Church out of the profane, whom the people of the Jews would groan to see preferred to themselves, without the Law, without grace, and so that their affection might be roused the more to emulation of them, the baser those were on whom the choice had fallen; for before, the people had nothing by which to be stirred, since it regarded itself alone as chosen by the Lord. But when it noticed that a people of strangers had been admitted from the nations, which claimed for itself the Law of the Lord, the oracles of the prophets, the New Testament of the Lord, then at last it began to be tormented with excessive feeling, after it knew that it had been rejected. Thus, if it sees the ceremonies of the Gentiles, it is not moved; if it hears of the progress of the Church, it is tortured with wretched envy. There is fulfilled, then, in Judaea: And I will rouse them to emulation with what is not a people.

7. There is added to the distress a heavier torment, that sinners from the Gentiles seem to be preferred to them, who do not even raise up the name of any people or nation. For every gathering of each region has been accustomed to claim a name for itself, as the Egyptians, Ethiopians, Syrians, Jews, Arabs bear the name of their province and their lands. We, gathered from diverse peoples, cannot take to ourselves the name of one people, and therefore, because we had no name on earth, we received one from heaven, that we should be called the people of Christ. But the Gentile thinks this folly, the Jew a reproach. True, then, is what is written, that God avenged the insult done to himself by seeking for himself a Church out of what is not a people, and by preferring it, out of a foolish nation, to that ancient and royal people. What the foolish nation is that has been preferred, hear him say: For God has chosen the foolish things of the world, to confound the wise, and again: If any man among you seems to be wise in this world, let him become a fool, that he may be wise. The Lord, then, repaid the provocation of emulation, he did not inflict it, so that it should be not in the form of imitation but in the punishment of iniquity.

8. Thus the Latin interpreter, wishing to make a distinction between the emulation of virtue and the emulation of offence, says: Do we emulate the Lord? that is: do we bring upon the Lord the offence of emulation, by eating of the things that have been offered to idols, as the Jews exasperated him by sacrificing to idols? But if among men the intention of rousing emulation gives offence, and frequently the person of the one exasperated is found to be the stronger, which has been accustomed to turn to the harm of the one who incited the emulation, what folly is it to provoke the divine majesty and to assail it with the stumbling-block of emulation, where there is no difficulty in vengeance!

9. We ought not, therefore, to supply the malignant with the goads of emulation against ourselves, who, even when not provoked, are roused by the stings of envy to do harm, as Cain killed his brother because the other's sacrifice was accepted rather than the one which he himself had thought fit to offer; in which it was not Abel who was emulous, but the envious Cain who pursued the favour of being preferred with a wicked fratricide. For Abel did not wish to overshadow his brother's sacrifice, but to keep the discipline of sacrifice, so that no sloth should delay the firstfruits, nor any usurpation violate them by diverting them to his own uses. Saul too attacked with snares the prophet David, the conqueror of the Philistines and the preserver of his own safety, who had been preferred to him in the talk of young girls; he made ready to kill him by casting a spear, and had almost shed the blood of his innocent son-in-law, had David not eluded the wound by bending his body aside. What malice was there, if the young girls said: Saul among thousands, David among ten thousands? But how fierce the envy of King Saul, who turned an error of speech to the destruction of an innocent man!

10. Nor, he says, be emulous of those who work iniquity. He has not repeated what he had said, but changed it. For it is one thing to emulate covertly, another to emulate; covert emulation has cunning, emulation simplicity. But yet even simplicity itself ought to be cautious and provident, so that it may know what is to be guarded against; for it was not idly said: Be cunning as serpents and simple as doves. The cunning, then, ought to be spiritual, such as keeps salvation and knows no guile; spiritual ought to be the simplicity. Scripture seems, moreover, to have made the distinction of covert emulation in another place too, when it says: And the zeal of Ephraim shall be taken away, and the enemies of Judah shall perish; Ephraim shall not be the rival of Judah, and Judah shall not vex Ephraim. But elsewhere it has: So you also, since you are emulators of spiritual things, seek to abound for the edification of the Church. He put emulators for the good and for imitators of a good thing, a rival for one who is spiteful and envious.

11. The first thing, then, is that you should not provoke the malignant to the malignity of emulation; the second, that you should not emulate those who work iniquity. For very often the devout, seeing that some have sought riches and attained honours by fraud and cunning, long with an unseemly emulation to follow their ways, so that they may attain riches and honours by a like art, or may circumvent young girls in contracting marriage. For what does it profit, when riches themselves and all the glory of this world quickly wither like grass and, like the green herbs, perish in the very beauty of their flower? All flesh is grass, and all the glory of man as the flower of grass. Do not, then, greatly strive after what cannot be lasting; do not be a rival and spiteful; do not be contentious and a combatant in zeal. Hence also Aquila said: Do not vie among the malignant, Symmachus: Do not contend. Do not be an imitator of iniquity and fraud either, but be an imitator of the apostolic teaching, of the prophetic grace and of the virtue of the saints, so that you may bear fruit and lay up a harvest of goodness, as Joseph did, who by a multitude of grain shut out the famine of a long barrenness, as Habakkuk did, who, carrying a midday meal to the reapers, was lifted up by an angel and took his way along a path through the air, and, restored to earth amid the fierce roaring of lions, served a sweet banquet to the devout prophet.

12. Rightly, then, does the prophet say: Hope in the Lord and do goodness, and inhabit the land, and you shall be fed in its riches. What is the land which he urges you to inhabit, but your soul, which you ought to cultivate well and frequently plough with spiritual ploughshares, lest it grow rough with long neglect? For a good husbandman works his field with daily labour and watchful care, and guards his own lands, lest the boar out of the wood lay them waste and the robber snatch away the ripe fruits. Plough, then, your land, so that when he comes who sows the Word, he may find your soul prepared, lest the seed fall upon the untilled places of your heart, and the birds of heaven come and carry off what has been sown. What the land is, then, hear him say: Behold, the sower went out to sow his field. And while he sows, some fell by the wayside, some upon the rock, some upon good ground. But that upon good ground, these are they who, hearing the word with a good heart, retain it and bring forth fruit through patience. Let your heart, then, be clean, let your soul be clean, so that you may be able to bring forth the fruit of goodness, that is, of spiritual grace. For goodness is the fruit of the Holy Spirit, as it is written: But the fruit of the Spirit is charity, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faith, modesty, continence. These are the fruits on whose riches we are fed and with whose abundance we are filled. In this land righteous Noah planted that fruitful vine, and drank of its fruit, and was loosened in his inmost heart, when the twofold posterity of his virtues clothed him with the praiseworthy covering of dutiful love. In another place also the holy prophet has shown us these riches, in which he was rich in Christ and abounded in all things that lead to eternal life. And in truth, who is richer than he who is rich in the Lord, so that he can say: I have delighted in the way of your testimonies as in all riches? For what could be lacking to a man who was enriched by heavenly oracles? And therefore he urges in this psalm that we should seek for ourselves that eternal treasure and take delight in acquiring it.

13. And therefore he says: Delight in the Lord, and he will give you the petitions of your heart. Why did he not say "your petitions", but "the petitions of your heart"? For the petitions of the outer man and of the inner are not shared, nor are all approved by Christ, because the law of the flesh frequently fights against the law of the mind. But those which belong to the inner man, who has been renewed in the spirit, those petitions the Lord, when he is asked, grants in effect. Hence also elsewhere he says: May the Lord grant you according to your heart and confirm all your petitions. He says "according to the heart", not "according to the appetite of the flesh", and may he confirm, he says, those petitions which come forth from the inmost heart, not those which are directed by the allurement and pleasures of this flesh.

14. Reveal your way to the Lord and hope in him, and he himself will do it; and he will bring forth your righteousness as the light, and your judgement as the noonday. By this one verse he has declared what kind of man you ought to be; for who reveals his way but he who confesses the inner things of his breast to God, the judge of his hidden things? Well does he say "reveal", that is: lay open your conscience, lest the shadow of this world or of the flesh weigh it down. For seeds that are breaking forth, if they have been overshadowed, grow thin; exposed to the sun, they grow fat. Why do I speak of seeds? The shade of the forest kills the young trees themselves, so that they do not raise themselves on high, and prevents them from spreading out their branching arms. Beautifully, moreover, did he say: Reveal your way to the Lord, because the nature of men, prone to sin, draws as it were a kind of veil over our mind, so that we do not confess our sins to the Lord, who is able to heal our wounds, so that a man blushes to ask for medicine with his own mouth, lest his disgraceful deeds be betrayed before men. The conscience, then, presses itself in within the man, although it cannot lie hidden, and puts off so long, until the wound festers, that it is uncovered no longer by the wholesomeness of faith but by the incurable foulness of the sore. Reveal, he says, your way to the Lord, that is: lay open your way, do not hide it, as Cain hid it, who desired to lie hidden; for everyone who does evil hates the light. David revealed his way, who said: I speak my works to the king. Lay open your mind, so that there may be nothing for which you fear to be reproached; Paul too revealed his, who ventured to say: I am conscious of nothing against myself. Let your conduct, let your life be such that your way may shine before your Father who is in heaven.

15. But since the whole human condition is subject to frailty, and it is not in our power to direct our course according to our will, therefore he says to you: "hope in the Lord, and he himself will do it", that is: so that he may lay open your way and not permit you to be such that you flee the light, while you fear to be betrayed, and love the darkness, so that you may hide your shameful deeds, saying: Darkness covers me; who knows whether the Most High sees? For how can the man who plots adultery not seek a night suited to his attempts, the man who contrives a falsehood not bring a witness to his fraud, the man who angles to corrupt a judge in order to crush an innocent man not search out the secret place of iniquity? The robber lurks in lonely places, he hides under the darkness of night, so that his crime may succeed. Iniquity, then, is darkness; God is light. And if you wish to hide your righteousness, God brings it out into the light and does not suffer the judgement to lie hidden by which you chose the things that are good and rejected the things that are wicked. And not only does he make your judgement shine, but shine as the noonday. When this sun pours itself out wholly into splendour, it is noonday; it was noonday when Joseph feasted with his brothers, not having avenged his injury, but having forgotten it.

16. Be subject to the Lord and entreat him. You are admonished not only to be subject to the Lord but also to entreat the Lord, so that you may be able to fulfil your zeal for subjection, as he also said above: Reveal your way to the Lord and hope in him. It befits you not only to reveal your way but also to hope in the Lord. And good subjection is not abject, not base, but glorious and sublime; for he is subject to God who does the will of the Lord. Thus, who does not know that the wisdom of the mind is more excellent than the wisdom of the flesh? Now the wisdom of the mind is subject to the law of God; the wisdom of the flesh is not subject, and the Apostle added: nor indeed can it be. Be subject, then, that is, next to Christ, so that you may fulfil the law. Thus Christ, by doing the will of the Father, fulfilled the law, and therefore he is the end of the law and the fullness of charity, because, loving the Father, he applied his whole effect to his will. Hence the Apostle said, by way of glory: But when all things have been subjected to him, then he himself also will be subject to him who subjected all things to himself, that God may be all in all, and he himself says of himself: Is not my soul subject to God? For from him is my salvation. Thus it was out of dutiful love, not out of weakness, that he was subject to his parents Joseph and Mary. But the greatest glory of Christ is that he pours himself into the hearts of all men and calls all back from the ungodliness of unbelief and from the disposition of heathenism, so as to make them subject to himself. But when he has subjected all things to himself, when the fullness of the Gentiles has entered and all Israel has been saved and in the whole world there is one body in Christ, then he himself also will be subject, offering his gift to God the Father, and as it were the prince of all priests and possessed of the heavenly altars, so that the faith of all may be the sacrifice. This subjection, then, belongs to loving-kindness, in that the Lord Jesus will be subject in the body, whose body and members we are. Be subject, then, O man, to Christ, that is: subject to the wisdom of God, subject to the Word, subject to righteousness, subject to power, because Christ is all these things. Let every man subject himself to God; for he teaches not one but all, that they should subject their heart, subject their soul, subject their flesh, so that God may be all and in all. He, then, is subject who is full of grace and takes up the yoke of Christ and carries out the commandments of the Lord vigorously and without hesitation; but he is without subjection who exalts himself in vain, puffed up by the sense of his own flesh, unused to devotion and turning aside from the observance of devout service, which by the very right of nature we owe to the eternal Author. Lastly, he who is without sin is subject to Christ, since he has been redeemed by the Lord; but he who is in sin cannot be called free, but a slave, whom the heavy chains of sin hold fast.

17. There follows: Do not be emulous of him who prospers in his way, working iniquity. Here he has clearly made known what he meant above: that the malignant are not to be roused to malignity by emulation, nor are those who work iniquity to be imitated. For it is not iniquity, but the prosperity that comes to those who do unjust things, that often stirs us to think that they should be imitated, so that we may arrive at their successes, saying: Behold, these are sinners, and abounding in the world they have obtained riches. And I said: Then in vain have I justified my heart and washed my hands among the innocent, and I have been scourged all the day. If, then, David was scourged, we ought to beware lest we too be scourged, and it be said to us that riches, honours, powers seem to have been allotted to this generation as the wandering and uncertain works of money and the gains of iniquity, not as any rewards of virtue, and therefore they come like a dream and, when you rise, they vanish with the dream. It is certain that athletes themselves are crowned when they have conquered, not before they conquer. Our wrestling is with the world; conquer the world first before you seek the crown. No one is crowned beforehand unless he has completed the contest. Do those who run the race receive the prize before they have run through the course? How many who were ahead fall at the finish and are cheated of the swiftness of their running! Are you more acceptable to God than Paul? He, the vessel of election, he, the teacher of the Gentiles, never dared to claim a crown for himself in this world. Thus hear him say: I have fought the good contest, I have finished the course, I have kept the faith; as for the rest, there is laid up for me the crown of righteousness, which the Lord will render to me on that day. That Paul who was caught up into the third heaven — who heard the things which it is not lawful for a man to speak, and does not know whether he was caught up in the body or without the body, as he himself has testified —, Paul, then, says that the crown is to be rendered to him on that day: do you insist that it be rendered to you here? Fulfil, then, the time of your contest. Not once only does the athlete wrestle, nor once only does the soldier fight, so as to be able to complete his service; and you too have a wrestling not with one passion only.

18. If you have escaped the passions above, anger comes next, and therefore Scripture says to you: Cease from anger and leave off indignation. There are many vices that creep upon the unwise. Anger is a grievous passion; very often it kindles a man against his will, and carries off into fury one who wishes to punish more mildly, so that he kills the man whom he had thought ought to be restrained. When roused, he often runs an innocent man through with the sword; very many have killed friends and brothers through indignation. Therefore the wise man says: Anger destroys even the wise. It is the saying of Solomon, that anger destroys not only any men whatever from the common sort but even the wise themselves. And David admonishes the wise man, saying: Cease from anger, lest, when you are kindled by it, it should not cease before its flame consumes you. Leave off, he says, indignation, that is: nature carries you away, feeling moves you, someone's fault or offence rouses you to be indignant; but not without end, so that you know no measure: leave it off, put an end to it, lest it drag you into sin. This is what he said above: Be angry and do not sin. For he does not exhort you to be angry, but for a time he gives way to the feeling, yet he gives a remedy, lest the force of the sore creep on too long. "Be angry", which he says, belongs to your passion. For a physician does not at once apply medicines to a sickness. If the pain is burning, he applies poultices so that the pain may abate; if the fever is raging, he waits for the time of the remedy, and he is accustomed to deny drink itself to the thirsty. He does not say: "Do not have a fever", when the heat of the sick man is burning, but he says: "Wait until the fever ceases, until the disturbance is dispersed". So also the prophet could not say to a man whose flesh is stirred, as by the various passions of diseases, so by those of disturbances: "You shall not be angry", but he says: "Cease from anger and leave off indignation, lest you sin; for anger is a grievous teacher of sin". Another physician too says: Let not the sun go down upon your wrath, lest, while you put it off long, he should come who is accustomed to rouse the heated body from sleep, and goad you, and put thoughts into you, and plunge himself into the secret places of your heart, saying: "Avenge your injury, recognize that you are a man; it belongs to womanly weakness not to take vengeance. So a slave was to despise you, a brother to deceive you, a friend to mock you, and you still do not avenge your insult? You must proscribe him, you must rise against him with the sword and console your pain by the death of your adversary. He was a man who killed his enemy; he is rightly praised, because he avenged himself so that no one else dared to inflict an injury on him". By these goads he is kindled the more, stirred the more, so that what is written is fulfilled: Anger destroys even the wise.

19. Do not, then, listen to him, do not emulate so as to act wickedly, since those who act wickedly shall be exterminated. Those are exterminated who have no roots, like green herbs or grass. Let the weak man eat herbs; but you, plant a vine in your field, set out a vineyard, and if Ahab comes to say to you: Give me your vineyard, that I may plant herbs for myself, do not consent to him, lest by your consent he sow perishable things and cut down eternal ones. Therefore Naboth is held among the saints, because he thought that the inheritance of his forefathers was not to be yielded even to the king, and preferred to be stoned rather than give up his vineyard to be plundered. The inheritance of the forefathers is the true faith. There arose Arians, supported by royal power, who thought that the temple of the Lord ought to be handed over to them, threatening bitter punishments. But far be it that, with a mind serving the Lord, the fear of punishment should weigh more than the pattern of devotion. Unbelief did not prevail, because faith stood firm. There is also a certain vineyard in the hearts of the faithful, of which Isaiah says: A vineyard has been made for the beloved on a fruitful horn.
