This vineyard the Lord has planted in our hearts, and therefore we read God saying: I planted you a fruitful vine, wholly true. Let no one, then, take away this vineyard from the field of our soul, because that vineyard is blessed. And therefore it was said of the saints: By the fruit of corn, wine and oil they were multiplied. It is good, then, to have within you winepresses overflowing with wine, so that wine may flow for you into your vessel from the vineyard of Sorek, that cup, the one which inebriates, how excellent it is. For Sorek is the vineyard of a new beginning, of a new equity. Hence it is also said to us: Sing to your God a new song; his beginning, magnify his name from the end of the earth. Let this vineyard, then, yield grapes, not iniquity. Therefore the vineyard of the Jews was abandoned, because it wrought iniquity, as it is written, and not judgement. Let us, then, bear fruit in Christ, so that we may be found worthy to be everlasting.

20. Those who wait for the Lord, indeed, shall possess the land, that land, surely, of the living. There is a certain heavenly land which bears fruit for the heavenly, of which he says: I believe that I shall see the good things of the Lord in the land of the living. This land, with much sweat of men, supplies food for the belly; that land of the Lord's good things bears fruit without any labour, and in it is the lasting possession of the righteous and the inheritance of the devout mind. And well does he say: But those who wait for the Lord shall possess the land by inheritance. For this is the land that does not pass away; for heaven and earth shall pass away, but the words of the Lord shall not pass away. And therefore neither can that intelligible land of paradise pass away, in which are those who keep the words of the Lord. In this land Adam was placed, that he might take the fruit of eternal life; but because he could not keep the words of the Lord, therefore he did not deserve to remain in the possession which he had received. But he who keeps the words of the Lord says with confidence: Waiting, I waited for the Lord, and he looked upon me. But since Adam did not wait for the Lord — for how did he wait, who fled back and feared to present himself? —, therefore neither did the Lord deign to see him; the eyes, indeed, of the Lord are upon the righteous. Him, however, he was so far unwilling to see that he sought him, saying: Adam, where are you? He who is sought is held as absent. It is faith that presents us to God, unbelief that makes the ungodly exiles. And so no one is absent from God except he who has made himself absent, and therefore he says: Be it done to you according to your faith; for he who does not know will not be known. Adam, then, as a sinner, could not keep his place; cast out of paradise, he was banished to a fortified village, that he might do penance. He received a respite, so as not to perish utterly at once, so that Eve might be saved through the bearing of children: the faith of holy Abel, the grace of the prophets, the posterity of the Church.

21. But he who will not be redeemed through these things and holds that he must persevere in sin, of him the prophecy says: And yet a little while, and the sinner shall not be, and you shall seek his place and shall not find it. For how will he be able to exist in the future, since the place of sin cannot be lasting? For this is the earth that opened its mouth to receive the blood of the innocent, and therefore in this earth is the place of sinners. The earth passes away; how will the sinner's place be able to be found? I think that God commanded the firmament to be between water and water for this reason, that he might separate sins from virtues, and that the upper water, which praises the Lord, might remain at rest from error, while the lower might be liable to sin. Thus that one sees God, this one does not see; the water which is above the heavens sees, that which is in the abyss does not see. Hence also it was said: The waters saw you, O God, the waters saw you and were afraid, the abysses were troubled; a multitude of the sound of waters. Rightly are the abysses troubled, over which there is unsightly darkness, and therefore they cannot have peace. Hence the legion of demons asked to be sent into the abyss, and cast itself headlong into the waves with a great tumult, so as to drown the herd of swine which it had found. And so sinners make for the abyss, where the gloom of darkness is.

22. But the meek shall possess the land and shall delight in the multitude of peace. Rightly do they possess the land in whom God himself rests, as he resounded through Isaiah in a divine oracle, saying: Upon whom shall I rest but upon the humble and quiet and the one who trembles at my words? Who are the meek, but those whom no goad of dissension harasses, whom anger does not disturb, nor cruelty exasperate, nor the madness of savagery inflame? And therefore, because, while placed in the body, they loved not wines, not banquets, not riches, but the peace of the Lord, in return for that delight in bodily pleasures, of which they thought they should deprive themselves in order to obtain eternal grace, they shall delight in the multitude of peace which our Lord Jesus bestowed on the human race in his days, as the prophecy, which has not lied, embraced it, declaring: In his days righteousness shall arise, and a multitude of peace, until the moon be taken away. What, then, is the peace by which the people of the whole Church has been multiplied, but that of which the Lord said: My peace I leave to you, my peace I give to you? He gave peace, who calmed the wars of souls.

23. There follows: The sinner will watch the righteous man and will gnash upon him with his teeth. But the Lord will laugh at him, since he foresees that his day will come. It is indeed the habit of one who is indignant and seething with anger to gnash with the teeth; but the heart of the malignant man also has its own teeth, which are accustomed not to resound but to tear. Snares, the devices of wickedness, are the teeth of the sinner. The sinner, then, lies in wait for the righteous man because he envies him; for the life of the righteous man reproves the sinner, whom, though silent, it condemns with greater authority than if it spoke with a loud voice. But the righteous man ought not to fear the gnashing of the sinner, because wickedness cannot be everlasting. Snares are for a time, but the defences of virtue are eternal. The death of the sinner dissolves all his power and fraud.

24. He adds more: Behold, he says, sinners have drawn the sword, they have bent their bow, to cast down the poor and the needy. What is the sinner's sword but the contrary of the sword of the Holy Spirit? Scripture has taught me of this sword, the Apostle has taught me, saying that we have the breastplate of righteousness and the shield of faith and the helmet of salvation and the sword of the Spirit, which is the word of God. The word of God, then, is the sword of the Holy Spirit; on the contrary, surely, the sword of the most wicked one is an evil word. With this the apostle Peter struck Ananias and Sapphira, as with a sort of blade of his speech; with this sword Paul snatched away the light of his eyes from Elymas, who opposed his discourses, and poured over him the night of blindness. Consider now for me sinners quarrelling and hurling bitter insolences and bringing forth the reproaches of accusations. If you hear them, will you not say: Sinners have drawn the sword, when foul speech is brought out of a wanton mouth as from a sheath, which ought to have been restrained and put away? Just as the word of God is called a sword, and so too is the sinner's speech, so also the bow which sinners bend is their mind, and the arrow which they shoot is poisoned speech. For as Christ, who is the Word of God, is an arrow, to whom it is said: I have set you as a chosen arrow, which is brought forth from the quiver of God, so there are arrows of the unbelieving, which, shot from a kind of bow of iniquity, wound the unwary innocent man, unless their fiery darts are repelled by the shield of faith, and therefore you ought to be watchful as a soldier in battle, because your fight is not only against flesh and blood, but also against the spiritual wickednesses themselves, which are not seen. Let arms mighty for God abound for you, so that you may easily draw out what weapons you wish, lest the enemy be able to crush you poor and unarmed. Be strong for God, rich for God, so that it may be said of you: The ransom of a man's soul is his riches. Abound in the treasure of wisdom, be rich in word and good works, so that you may be fortified. Flee the riches of the sinner, lest he find where he can wound you. Be merciful, so that you may remain unwounded, or may be able to heal yourself if you have been wounded. There is also the poor man whom the adversaries wish to wound, of that number of whom the Savior said: Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven.

25. And therefore let the spear of sinners enter, he says, into their own heart, and let their bows be broken, so that, just as the peace of those who bless returns to the servants of God from those who have not received it, so also the malice of sinners, by which they try to harm the righteous man, may return to their own destruction, so that they are slain by their own weapons and wounds; for often darts are flung back upon the very men who threw them. This happened also in the recent war, when unbelieving and sacrilegious men were attacking one who trusted in the Lord and were striving to go and snatch away his kingdom, threatening the churches of the Lord with savage persecutions: suddenly a wind arose which struck the shields out of the hands of the unbelievers and turned back the darts and missiles of all of them upon the army of the sinner. The enemy was not yet there, and already they could not bear the battle of the winds and were being laid low by their own spears, and, what is worse, those wounds of their bodies were not more grievous than those of their minds; for they failed in heart when they knew that God was fighting against them. And so they had gone out provoking, and from the quiver of their heart were bringing forth the poisoned darts of unbelief against the Christian people, but their ungodliness returned upon their own head. Thus they were broken up among themselves by their own unbelief, and the Lord scattered the snares prepared for the faithful, so that not only could they not harm the devout, but they were stripped of their own auxiliaries, and their own arms passed over to the enemy. How much better had they not brought forth the sword from the sheath, that is, had they by no means uttered ungodly words! For if each one is to render an account for an idle word, how much more will he expiate words of sacrilege with grievous punishments!

26. You have tried us with fire, says David. All, then, will be tried by fire. And Ezekiel says: Behold, the Lord almighty comes. And who will endure the day of his entrance? Or who will endure when he appears to us? For he himself will enter as the fire of a smelting furnace and as the trough of those who wash. And he will sit smelting and purifying as gold and silver, and he will purify the sons of Levi and will pour them out as gold and as silver, and they will be offering sacrifice to the Lord in equity. By fire, then, the sons of Levi will be purified, by fire Ezekiel, by fire Daniel. But these, although they will be tried through fire, will yet say: We have passed through fire and water. Others will remain in the fire. For the former the fire will fall like dew, as for the Hebrew children, who were thrown into the blaze of the burning furnace; but the avenging fire will burn up the ministers of ungodliness. Woe to me, if my work is burned and I suffer the loss of this labour! Although the Lord will save his servants, we shall be saved through faith, yet so saved as through fire; though we shall not be burned up, yet we shall be burned. How, however, some remain in the fire while others pass through, let divine Scripture teach us in another place. Surely, the people of the Egyptians was drowned in the Red Sea, but the people of the Hebrews passed over; Moses passed through, Pharaoh was cast headlong, since his grievous sins sank him. In that way will the sacrilegious be cast headlong into the lake of burning fire, who have hurled proud insults against God. Let us, then, while placed here, follow the pillar of fire, which may enlighten us while we are placed in this body and show us the way, so that in the future the cloud of night may cool us, whereby we may be able to relieve the fierce burnings.

27. But see what Scripture says: may the Lord, it says, break the bows of sinners. Yet he set his own bow in the cloud when the floods had ceased, so that calm might be poured back. Hence it is to be believed that the adversary and malignant one bends his bow so as to stir up tempests in quiet minds, to raise up blasts. Let us pray, then, that the Lord our God may break up the bow of malignity, and may stand by his poor and needy man, who through the fear of God does not know that riches are to be striven after, the estates of lesser men seized, widows stripped of the inheritances left by their forefathers.

28. Better is a little to the righteous man than the many riches of sinners. It is not, then, riches that are accused, but the riches of sinners, unless perhaps because the sinner said: All these things have been delivered to me, and to whom I will I give them; and then because riches rather inflame the torch of covetousness, and each man, while he desires greater things, does not turn aside from the byways of sinners. Hence also the Savior said: Make for yourselves friends of the mammon of iniquity. For that wealth belongs to iniquity which is in the devil's power, to be bestowed on whom he will. This too can be understood, that better is a little to the righteous man than the many riches of sinners, because there is a certain man rich in words, as are the philosophers of this world, disputing about sacrileges, about the motion of the stars, about the star of Jupiter and of Saturn, about the generations of men, about the worship of images, about geometry and dialectic. The philosophers, then, are rich in speech, needy in faith, without share in the truth. And there are very many simple priests of the Lord, poor in speech, sublime in abstinence and virtue. Those speak unbelief to many, these assert the faith in few words; those lose their own priests daily, this poor man acquires peoples for the Church and for the number of believers. He, then, who has heard these and seen the quality of their works will say: Better is a small portion to the righteous man than the many riches of sinners. From here Solomon derived that saying, which he set down as if his own: In much speaking you will not escape sin. Dialectic, then, flows with riches of words; devotion keeps the fear of God. Therefore this man, sparing in words, rich in spirit, would rather fear than fling about empty words about the truth. For fear is the discipline of wisdom; talkativeness is the shipwreck of innocence and virtue, and the incitement to falling and to guilt.

29. For the arms, he says, of sinners shall be broken, so that their actions may not be able to bring a hindrance to the righteous man, lest the rod of sinners be left upon the lot of the righteous. And Paul says: May God crush Satan under your feet. For if his arm is crushed, he himself will be crushed entirely, so that his devices may be trodden down like the venom of a serpent.

30. But the Lord confirms the righteous, when the adversary's arm has been broken; and therefore the righteous man says: And you have confirmed your hand upon me, and Job says: The hand of the Lord has touched me. The Lord, then, put forth his hand upon his servant, and crushed the hand and the arm of the sinner, which he had put forth upon him after receiving power. Thus the devil was deceived by his own speech, who said: Put forth your hand upon him, and let us see whether he does not bless you to your face; for he did not dare to say "curses", but left this to be understood. The Lord put forth his hand and Job was confirmed: he who was believed to curse began to bless; for when the divine hand touches the righteous, it heals, it does not wound. He put forth his hand, and every stain of the leper departed; he touched the eyes of the blind, and when blindness had been driven away, the light of their eyes shone again. Ask, then, always that you may be confirmed, since you too who stand, see that you do not fall. We ought to stand, so that we may be confirmed by the Lord. The world is slippery, we quickly fall; therefore let us ask that the Lord may deign to establish and confirm us.

31. Do not think that the Lord does not know your way; if you are righteous, he knows it. Believe the prophet when he says: The Lord knows the ways of the spotless, and their inheritance shall be spotless; they shall not be confounded in the evil time. He who knows Christ is known by the Lord; he knows the righteous, he does not know the unrighteous. Therefore he will say to the unrighteous: Depart from me, all you who work iniquity; I do not know you, that is: because you are unworthy of divine knowledge; I do not know you, because you yourselves chose not to know me. Your works do not know me, your deeds do not know me; even if you say that you know me, your sins refute you. Every sin is from the evil one, but he who does not sin abides in me; this John wrote. Why should I speak of the Lord, that he disdains to know the ungodly man? Paul disdains it, who said: If any among you is a prophet or spiritual, let him recognize what I write to you; but he who does not know will not be known; and elsewhere it was said thus: The Lord has known who are his. Let us, then, be the Lord's, so that the Lord may know us, and let everyone who names the name of the Lord depart from iniquity.

32. The Greek has: The Lord knows the days of the spotless; for there are days of Elijah, there are days of Nebuchadnezzar. Therefore the Gospel has: In the days of Elijah, when heaven was shut. It was night for the unbelieving, but for Elijah there was light; heaven was shut for the unbelieving, but for Elijah it lay open; there was famine for the unbelieving, but for Elijah plenty. For he could not hunger to whom heavenly things supplied food, nor did he hunger who himself fed others. The righteous man, then, is a day to himself in the darkness, because the light also shines in the darkness. And Joseph was in Egypt, and the noonday shone for him, as he said above. But to the sinner God said: Why do you declare my righteousnesses? Righteousness is light, because above it has: And he will bring forth your righteousness as the light. You have, then, something to shine in you, if you follow righteousness. The day shines bright for you, the night gives light to you, because for the faithful the night too shall be lit up as the day. The Lord, then, knows the days of the righteous man, because he himself enlightens every man coming into this world, that is, the one who lives according to the image and likeness of God, the one who knows himself to be his man, so that he flees the neighing lust of horses, the madness of wild beasts, the timidity of little hares, the cunning of foxes, the rapine of wolves; the man who acts as though he had come into this world from elsewhere, who has been born not of blood nor of the will of the flesh, but of God. You have come, then; do not stay behind, do not cling to earthly things. But that you may know that the days are good, hear him say: Abraham saw my day and was glad. He has a good day who knows the Son of God to be the good God and confesses him as Lord. And again he himself warns us that we ought to beware, because the days are evil. What days are evil? Those, surely, in which evil is known, which is from the evil one. Or perhaps the days of the world are evil, because the world is set in wickedness. But we read of an evil day even after the world: In the evil day the Lord will deliver him, that is, on the day of judgement, on account of the punishments of many. For it must be that the unrighteous are tormented and the righteous suffer with them, because the angels too rejoice when a single sinner is saved from death. They suffer with him, then, when he is punished, although we read elsewhere: The righteous man shall rejoice when he sees the vengeance on the ungodly, which I reserve for its own place, although you have often heard why he rejoices; but let us not thrust one task into another.

33. The Lord knows the days of the spotless, because his grace and fullness has mercy on spotless innocence; it does not have mercy on those who go astray. These have no day, because they flee the light; of them it was beautifully said: their days pass away like a shadow. The knowledge of God, then, is one of condescension, not of sight. His eyes are light; those on whom he looks he enlightens; and therefore his eyes are the day of the righteous.

34. Hence and their inheritance shall be for ever, because they sought eternal goods, not the gains of a temporal inheritance, and they will have nothing to blush for in the evil time, that is, of the heavenly judgement, and in the days of famine they shall be filled, for man does not live by bread alone, but by every word of God. But what man? I know a man in Christ fourteen years ago — whether in the body I do not know, or out of the body I do not know, God knows — such a one caught up even to the third heaven. That man, then, who is in Christ, who does not know himself to be in the flesh, who does not walk in the flesh but in the spirit, he who is caught up not only to heaven but even to the third heaven, and is caught up into paradise and hears words which it is not lawful for a man to speak, who glories not in his powers but in his weaknesses, he lives not by bread alone but by every word of God. For the Word of God is life, because the Word was made flesh. Hence the Evangelist said most excellently: What was made in him is life.

35. The Alexandrians, indeed, and the Egyptians read: All things were made through him, and without him was made nothing that was made, and, putting a pause in between, they add: In him is life. Let that division of the text stand for the faithful; I do not fear to read: What was made in him is life, and the Arian has nothing to lay hold of, because I do not consider his poisons, but recognize the custom of the sacred reading. For he did not say "the Word was made before every beginning", he did not say "the Word was made", but if you wish to hear what he did say: The Word, he says, was with God. He was with God, who worked with him, who ruled with him. He did not say "the Word was made", but he said: The Word was God. And God is not a thing made, but the Maker and Creator. Open your ears and hear: All things were made through him, and without him was made nothing. You learn that he is the Son, in whom is the fullness of divinity. Open your ears a little longer and hear him say: What was made in him is life. "In him", he says, "was made", not "the Word of God was made". Or if this moves you to slander, because he said "was made in him", do you also slander God the Father, because the Son of God said: But he who does the truth comes to the light, that his works may be made manifest, because they have been done in God, or because David said: I will confess to you, O Lord, since you have heard me and have been made salvation for me, that is: you have turned to me for salvation, you have worked for salvation? I could use other passages, but I do not want you to believe me, lest you think them arguments of ingenuity, not testimonies of truth.

36. Let the son of thunder himself, he who reclined on Christ's breast, he from whom the Lord did not keep his secrets silent, to whom Peter beckoned that he should ask the Lord, and he asked and the Lord revealed it, let him himself expound what he meant by what he said: What was made in him is life. Hear him, then, interpreting, for he was already guarding against your slanders, O Arian: That which was from the beginning, and which we have heard and have seen with our eyes, which we have looked upon and our hands have searched out concerning the Word of life, and the life appeared. It is, then, the flesh which appeared in Christ, or Christ in the flesh; he himself is our life in all things. His divinity is life, his eternity is life, his flesh is life, his passion is life. Hence also Jeremiah says: In his shadow we shall live. The shadow of the wings is the shadow of the cross, it is the shadow of the Passion. His death is life, his wound is life, his blood is life, his burial is life, his resurrection is the life of all. Do you wish to know how his death is life? We have been baptized into his death, that with him we may walk in newness of life. And he himself says: Amen, amen, I say to you, unless a grain of wheat falling into the ground dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it brings forth much fruit. He himself, the grain, was loosed in body and died for us, that he might bring forth much fruit in us. And so his death is the fruit of life. What was made in him, then, is life. Flesh was made in him: it is life; infancy was made in him: it is life; judgement was made in him: it is life; death was made in him: it is life; the remission of sins was made in him: it is life; a wound was made in him: it is life; mockery was made in him: it is life; division was made in him: it is life; burial was made in him: it is life; resurrection was made in him: it is life. See how many things were made in him, by which the restoration of our life was made, so that what had perished might be given back. Lastly, a sale was made in him: it is life; redemption was made in him: it is life. He was sold to death by Judas, bought by the Jews for death, so that we might be redeemed to life by his precious blood. This is the life which was made, this is the life which appeared, this is the life which we have seen, this is the life which was with the Father, because he himself who was in the beginning was himself born of the Virgin, so as to be life for those who were to die.

37. Let us question this very passage set before us. What is: a man in Christ? That is, made in Christ, in whom all things were made, whether thrones or dominions or principalities or powers: all things were created through him and in him, and he is before all, and all things hold together in him, that is, in his power. A man, then, in Christ, who has been made after his image and likeness; a man in Christ, who is wholly in Christ. For just as God the Father, through the unity and fullness of divinity, is wholly in the Son, and the Son in the Father, so, through intention and the affection of devotion — to use it by way of example, not of comparison — man is wholly in Christ; for he who cleaves to the Lord is one spirit. The man in Christ, then, is not that earthly one, not that one of sin, but the man of Christ. Why, then, does what was made in him is life trouble anyone, if man too, especially the inner man, was made in him, crucified in him, renewed in him, buried in him and buried together with him, raised again in him? Why does it trouble anyone, as I said, that it is written: What was made in him is life, when a man has said: In God we shall do valiantly? If you ask what the life is, if what has been made in him troubles you, take this: surely the life is the Church. She was made in him, in his side; in him Eve was raised up. And Eve is life, that is, that which was made, because Eve, who had perished, has been saved through the Church, that is, through the bearing of her children, as it is written, because a sober inheritance has amended the offence of the earlier one. Hence Paul himself too was caught up to life, who before was a persecutor unto death.

38. We have wandered rather far, in order to speak of that man who lives not by bread alone but by every word of God. Let us return to the psalm: and therefore the spotless will not be confounded in the day of judgement, and in the days of famine they shall be filled.

39. Failing like smoke, they shall fail. The Greek has ἐξέλιπον, that is, "they have failed". You see someone suddenly arrive at the insignia of power and honour; you judge him exalted. You see that he has success; do you not say of him: "Where has this man been honoured and exalted? He has failed"? Therefore the Greek has said more, because where someone is believed to be honoured and exalted, there he is forestalled by his own failing, so that you perceive the courses of rivers to have passed by sooner than they have come, and while you are waiting for the streams to come, they have flowed away while you are still expecting them. On the other hand, the humble and meek, while they are subjected by the rich and oppressed, have been exalted by their humility and have suddenly shone forth. And therefore Paul took pleasure in his weaknesses, not in his powers.

40. But let us consider, lest anyone think that he gloried in his revelations, and let us repeat the words, so that he may defend himself as the teacher of humility: I know, he says, a man in Christ fourteen years ago — whether in the body I do not know, or out of the body I do not know, God knows —, such a one caught up even to the third heaven. He says it was revealed to him fourteen years before, and yet he held back and kept down the revelation for so long, nor would he have told it unless he had judged it useful to us that he should tell it, so that we should not be lifted up by revelations. For if Paul did not glory in so great a grace, neither ought we to glory. He did not glory as a young man: would he glory as an old man? Then, he could not deny that he had been caught up even to the third heaven, and yet he testified that he did not know whether he had been caught up in the body or out of the body. He glories, then, not in knowledge but in ignorance, and proclaims God's grace towards himself. What belongs to knowledge he denies; what belongs to charity he confesses; for knowledge puffs up, but charity builds up. And again he said such a man had been caught up; how he had been caught up, whether in the body or out of the body, he declared to be uncertain to himself. See the balance of wisdom. He set up one man in Christ, and himself as another, who says: "I do not know". What is another's he raises up; what is his own he humbles. And he heard, he says, unspeakable words. He did not say "I heard", but he did not deny that another had heard. And so modestly he preferred to present himself as a witness rather than a seer, and shrank from seeming to be a judge of heavenly secrets. For to bear witness belongs to truth; not to shrink from the merit of a revelation belongs to one who glories. And therefore he says: Of such a one I will glory, but of myself I will not glory.

41. But what is it that he says the man heard, that man in Christ, which it is not lawful for a man to speak? How does this agree, that it was not lawful for a man to speak what it was lawful for a man to hear? If it is not lawful for the very man who heard to speak, how was it entrusted to him to hear what he was not permitted to speak? What is this distinction? If it was another man, one who was outward, how could a man come to know what it was not lawful for a man to hear? Hence it seems that it was not the grace of speaking that was lacking to that man who was in Christ — it was not lacking to him — but that place, time and merit were lacking to those who would hear. For he heard in heaven, and therefore it was judged not fitting that he should speak on earth what he had heard in heaven, since on the earth itself there is this distinction, that what is sung in one region is not sung in another, according to what is written: How shall we sing the Lord's song in a strange land? Thus the Hebrews did not sing in the land of captivity what they had been accustomed to sing in their own homeland. This is the land of captivity, the other is the land of liberty; this one of sin, that one of eternal rest; this [land] is of the valley, that one of heaven. In heaven, then, Paul now proclaims those things which he could not proclaim before on earth; for the secrets of wisdom are to be spoken among the perfect.

42. And yet what does the recounting of this revelation achieve, except to teach that one ought by no means to glory in revelations, but in weaknesses, because weakness is both the remedy for revelation and the exercise of virtue, whereas revelation is a slippery path to elation? For Paul himself, who was caught up into the third heaven, received a thorn of the flesh, lest he should be lifted up by the sublimity of the revelation. Weakness, then, came to his aid, lest grace should be turned into danger. Weakness, then, was more useful than grace. This same weakness is also the workshop of virtue, as the Lord testified, that virtue is perfected in weaknesses. Thus this very man after the revelation asked for the remedy of health and did not obtain it; but in weakness he did not seek a remedy, but finished the course and found the crown.

43. The sinner borrows, he says, and will not repay, but the righteous man has mercy and gives. This too befits the person of Paul, because the righteous man has mercy and gives. See him dividing out the words of the Lord, see him lending out the Lord's silver at interest. He received one mina, he returned two; he received two, he returned four; he received five, he returned ten. He did not tie up in a napkin what he had received, but paid it out to the money-changers, and what he had paid out he received back with interest. Thus he both freed the one who received from the greatest sin, lest the Lord's money should perish with him, and he himself earned to be set over ten cities. What the ten cities are, recognize in the epistles which he wrote, although the apostles are not held to a prescribed number, to whom it was said: Go through the whole world and preach the Gospel to every creature. This man, then, from Jerusalem through the East and Illyricum and Italy, dispensed the Lord's money. And lest anyone should think that he was lending out his own, he testified that the money was the Lord's, saying: But to those who are joined in matrimony I give charge, not I but the Lord, that the wife depart not from her husband, and elsewhere: Do you seek a proof of Christ who speaks in me? Thus the king himself, in whom there is a figure of the Lord, said this: You ought to have committed my money to the money-changers. "My", he says, not "your". The righteous man, then, has mercy and gives. How he gives, hear him say: I teach throughout all the churches. But the sinner borrows and does not repay. See the rich man borrowing and not returning, the poor man receiving and at once repaying, so as not to be in debt any longer. These are the moral things.

44. See now the mystical poor man, that is, the simple man who fears the name of God, hearing the word about chastity and repaying, hearing about mercy and repaying, hearing about gentleness and not being angry; but that rich man, arrogant and proud, hearing indeed, but casting the words of God behind him, hearing about the condemnation of luxury and indulging in luxury the more. Thus the Church repays what she has received; the synagogue does not repay. Recognize that the Church repays: They were yours, he says, and you gave them to me, and they have kept your word, and elsewhere: For the Father himself loves you, because you love me. The Church, then, has returned the money of charity which she received from the Lord, but the synagogue has not returned it. And therefore it is said of the Jews: If I had not come and spoken to them, they would not have sin; but now they have no excuse for their sin, because they certainly heard and did not believe. It has been proved, then, that the sinners borrowed and did not repay. The sinner, therefore, is always in need, but the righteous man, whose conscience is rich, abounds and gives. Therefore the Jews were in need in their riches — the rich were in need and went hungry —, but the Christians did not fail in the riches of their simplicity.

45. The righteous man, then, is good, and therefore Scripture added: For those who bless him shall possess the land, but those who curse him shall utterly perish. How does the righteous man possess the land, or what land, when Scripture says: Will you alone dwell upon the earth?, and elsewhere: Woe to those who dwell upon the earth, which belongs to rebuke and to cursing? Of what land, then, is the possession blessed? Not of this one, which is hidden in darkness and filled with bitternesses, but of that one which flows with honey and milk, that is, which has the grace of sweetness and the brightness of eternal light. Take the sweetness of good honey, nay, beyond honey: Pain and sorrow and groaning have fled away, because the grace of sweetness will shut out the bitterness of human frailty. And elsewhere: And he will wipe away every tear from their eyes, and death shall be no more, nor mourning. Take also the brightness of milk: And they shall not need a lamp or the light of the sun; for the Lord himself will give light upon them, and they shall reign for ages of ages.

46. There is also the mystical righteous man who has mercy and gives, who gave us all the words which he received from the Father, and remitted to us the debts of our sins, and paid his blood for our debts, so that we should not be under another's usury, but the good creditor might hold us in his own debt. There is also that sinner who gathered what he did not bring forth and borrowed what he did not hold, and will not restore what he received. Hear that the devil borrowed: To you, he says, I will give all this power and their glory, because they have been delivered to me, and to whom I will I give them, if you will fall down and worship me. Most wicked one, you received it for temptation, not for death, that is: you received it so that the servants of God might be tested, not that they might be destroyed; you received it so that God might be worshipped, not that he might be denied. You received the things of this world; why do you snatch away the things that are eternal? You received the things that are the world's; why do you wish to take away the things that are Christ's? Give those things to whom you will, we do not grudge them; why are the things that we desire grudged us by you? Do you wish to be worshipped, you who, more wicked than all, are unworthy even of slavery itself?

47. By the Lord the steps of a man shall be directed. The Greek said διαβήματα, that is, "crossings", and therefore it is said to you: If you pass through water, the rivers shall not shut you in. Pass on, then, do not halt, as a good traveller, when he comes to some marker of the road, does not stand still but passes on; you too are on the road as long as you are set on this course. If Paul had stood still, he would not have finished his course. See whether he does not say for this reason: And you who stand, see that you do not fall; for he who has passed on cannot fear that he may fall. I saw, he says, the ungodly man exalted above measure and lifted up above the cedars of Lebanon, and I passed by, and behold, he was not. Seeing, then, the ungodly man exalted above measure, he who did not stand still did not stumble; if he had stood still, he would have admired him and followed him, and he would have stumbled and fallen like the ungodly man. Therefore Moses, seeing the bush shining and not burning, says: Passing over, I will see this great sight. He who passes over from this world sees a great sight; he who loosens the chains of this bond by which we are bound to this body sees a great sight. Moses sees many wonders in Exodus; no other who is not in an exodus sees so many. The same Moses passed over; the people of the fathers too passed over, because it was going out of the land of captivity. And so their steps were directed by the Lord, for whom a pillar of fire shone by night, of cloud by day, so that neither the heat of the day should afflict them as they journeyed nor the darkness of night bring hindrance to the travellers.

48. And you, deserve it by your deeds and beg it by your prayers, that your steps may be directed by the Lord, lest your feet be moved, because it is written: But my feet were almost moved, my steps had well-nigh been poured out. You must beware also lest you abandon the straight path and the byways of crooked roads deceive you. Therefore it is said: Prepare the way for the Lord, make his paths straight. Let us, then, prepare the way for the Lord our God in our minds, let us make his paths straight, let us make straight the paths of our souls, lest we slip. Let not our steps be poured out like those of Lot's wife, who looked back behind her and could not hold her steps, but they were poured out when she was suddenly turned into salt. Let them not be poured out like those of the Egyptians, whose step the wave of the sea poured out. For the very Hebrews who were with Moses, because they sinned in the desert, the footsteps of their feet fell, so that they did not enter the land of the resurrection.

49. Is it not also beautifully said of these that their advances have been poured out, whose hopes have slipped, whose labour has been poured out in vain, whose vows have been forsaken? For consider that someone for some years had zeal for uprightness, the guarding of chastity, the love of a more careful life, the purpose of devout service, the duty of diligent observance, and that suddenly he was changed, left the monastery, bade farewell to fasts, renounced continence, indulges in delights, gives himself to luxury. Not long ago they went out of the monasteries, and now they are teachers of luxury, sowers of incontinence, inciters of wantonness, detractors of modesty. Would you not beautifully say of these: "their steps have been poured out", who repented of having directed them well? And so they have done penance of a new kind, for their virtues, and they do not do it for their offences. But these went out from us, says John, but they were not of us; for if they had been of us, they would have remained with us. These, then, have condemned their own way, to whom it is fitting to say: O you who have forsaken the straight paths by going away into the ways of darkness, and who rejoice in evils and are glad in evil overthrow, whose paths are crooked and their courses winding, like their slippery and winding author. Why have you begun to hate the straight way and deserted righteous counsel? The Lord did not direct you. But he whom the Lord directs, he will desire his way, as it is written, and will delight in his paths.

50. Both, however, can be understood — so ambiguous is it —: that he too who is directed by the Lord will desire the way of the Lord, because with him as guide every labour is lightened, all hindrances are removed, incentives are supplied; and that the Lord himself does not spurn, but gladly accepts, the way of the man whom he himself has directed to virtue. Beautifully, moreover, are the steps of a man said to be directed by the Lord, because to keep one's footstep without stumbling is not of him who wills, nor of him who runs, but of God who has mercy. For he who plants and he who waters are nothing, but God who gives the increase: to him alone the glory of virtues is rightly assigned.

51. Thus even the righteous man falls at times. But if he is righteous, when he falls he shall not be confounded. That he falls belongs to his condition; that he rises again, to righteousness, since God does not abandon the righteous man but confirms his hand. For what reason did he say not foot but hand, unless perhaps for this, that he who falls does not slip more by the foot, but the weak man is often deceived by a slippery place or stumbles against a rock? Here, however, understand the fall of the righteous man, that is, of the stronger. Thus, among the common people who wrestle, if one has put down a knee or has slipped with his foot, he is held as beaten. The athlete who has skill in wrestling and wrestles for the crown even puts down his knee on purpose, so that he may conquer; and if he has slipped he is not shut out, and if he is pressed down by one who is on top, yet supporting himself on his hands he contends lawfully, nor is the prize taken from him unless he has been thrown flat on his belly or stretched out in a lock of the arms. From this arise frequent contests, because the kinds of falls are many and unknown to most. For they properly call their falls ruin; for in Greek they are called πτώματα. Therefore, when he is gripped and pressed, he often rolls himself over, and he who was held to be underneath comes out on top, and as he rises throws down the one who was on top. This Scripture seems to signify when it says: You have turned over his whole bed in his weakness. And so it is said of him: "when the good athlete falls, he is not confounded". Very many, who trust in their art, even wish to be held, so that they may conquer the sooner. But even if he has been marked down, to use the very term, both the first time and the second, he is not shut out; he is allowed at times to renew the wrestling, and it often happens that he conquers and that he yields who had prevailed in the second bout. So, then, let the righteous man too, even if he has run into a stumbling-block and has fallen, nevertheless not abandon his zeal for devotion and faith; let him hold to sobriety, do penance, often renew himself. And therefore Peter asks: If my brother sins against me, how often shall I forgive him? Up to seven times? And the Lord answered: Not only seven times, but also seventy times seven. But that you may know that we are athletes, and are pushed, and some fall to ruin and very many are thrown down, hear him say: The Lord props up all who fall to ruin and sets upright all who have been thrown down. Hence David also says of himself, or he who spoke in the prophet: Pushed, I was overturned so that I should fall, and the Lord took me up; for Jesus did not fall, but was pushed. For since he himself turns back those who have been thrown down, how could he himself be thrown down? But Scripture testifies that the righteous man, when he has fallen, can rise again, when it says: Shall he who falls not add to rise again? Or does he who has turned away not return? Woe to those who turn away with a shameless turning away, says the Lord.

52. Would indeed that the athlete of Christ might be unable to be wrestled down and unconquerable, and glorious at every age and in every kind of virtue, as he says: But in all things we overcome because of him who loved us! What is "in all things"? There are athletes who are called boys, youths, men, that is, παῖδες, ἀγένειοι, ἄνδρες. Scripture too knows these ages among wrestlers, when David says: Do not turn away your face from your boy, and: I have been young and have grown old. Hence also John says: I write to you, boys, because you have known the Father. I write to you, young men, because you have overcome the evil one. I write to you, fathers, because you have known him who is from the beginning. And so, being older, writing to the brethren, he designates as mature those who are so by advance in faith and devotion. These, then, are ages of virtues, not of weakness; for the boy is not without virtue who has known the Father, the God of powers. From that boy come these boys, that boy who, before he knew how to call father or mother, received the power of Damascus and the spoils of Samaria. He himself is the boy who is born to us, a son is given to us, whose government is upon his shoulders. He himself taught us that boyhood is virtue, saying: Let them be, and do not forbid the children to come to me, and elsewhere: Amen, amen, I say to you, unless you are converted and become like this child, you shall not enter the kingdom of heaven. Even in strength of body itself boys very often overcome men. And certainly, if we consider the age of boyhood, a boy cannot either know all the practice or undergo the force of wrestling; yet we know that boys have frequently overcome those whom they could not carry; so far does vigour of mind shut out the weakness of age.

53. And as the boys are, so too are the youths, that is, spiritual. Scripture knows Paul as a young man already close to his conversion; it knows also Eutychus, a young man, who, while he clung intently to the discourses of Paul, overcome by sleep, fell from the third storey and rose again; it knows John too, a young man, reclining on Christ's breast, who was so strong that he did not fear persecution and overcame the evil one. This is the boy who left his natural father and followed that Father whom he knew to be eternal; as a young man, wrapped in a linen cloth, he followed the Lord at the time of the Passion, he who had left all his own things; when older, he knew that God the Word had always been and is in the very beginning, and he proved that he abides in himself.

54. Scripture has taught us of spiritual men too, when the prophet Agabus says: The man whose girdle this is, so shall the Jews bind in Jerusalem, and Festus says: There is a certain man left in custody by Felix. Before his passion Paul is called a young man; in his passion he is signified as a man, being already, his course finished, close to the crown.

55. We have come to know the ages contending for faith and devotion. Let us come to know also the kinds of the several contests. Let the tenor of Scripture teach us these too. In this worldly contest, then, there are some who wrestle in a certain simple and regular manner and contend only by holds of the body, and do not know how to strike: these are called wrestlers; others who mingle blows and the flinging of dust with the efforts of their limbs, every right of striking being permitted to them: these they call pammacharii, because they have against each other the licence of every kind of strife and fight; others who contend against each other with the gauntlets and are tormented with torn heads: these are called boxers.

56. All these contests the apostle Paul underwent, as he himself shows. And so elsewhere he says: For we have no πάλη against flesh and blood, but against princes and powers and — to be brief — against the spiritual wickednesses. In most Latin copies "wrestling-together" is found; in all the Greek, πάλη is written; for the πάλη of the Greeks is the wrestling of the Latins. And truly it is πάλη, because flesh and blood and the spiritual wickednesses are overcome by patience and continence of the body and by moderation of the mind. Anger, fury, slaughter are the arms of the devil. Elsewhere too he signifies that he was beaten with stripes above measure, saying: In stripes above measure, in deaths often, and that at Jerusalem he was struck with fists. There, when the Jews had at some point stopped striking Paul because the soldiers intervened, the high priest Ananias himself ordered those standing by to strike his mouth. To him the Apostle answered: God shall begin to strike you, you whitewashed wall; and do you sit judging me according to the law, and contrary to the law order me to be struck? He certainly knew how to strike back, who struck back the high priest with a heavier fist; for he himself was struck bodily, while he gave him back the word that he would be struck in the soul by Christ. In the first epistle to the Corinthians too he writes: I therefore so run, not as at an uncertainty; so I strive, not as beating the air. What in Latin he said "I strive", in Greek he says πυκτεύω. And truly, like a good boxer, he beat not the air, but the powers of the air and the heads of the Jews, because they did not have as their head the Lord Jesus, the head of all. And so in every contest the Apostle was proved, who also received the crown when his course was finished. Hence he himself also says: I can do all things in him who strengthens me.

57. Christ has also those who in their boyhood have overcome men mature in age, as Daniel, a boy, with strong spirit refuted those elders of the Jews and by the right of victory cast them down to death. He has others also who wrestled in their mother's womb before they were born. Thus Jacob supplanted his brother Esau and overcame the evil one, and so came out of his mother's womb as to show the token of victory and, as victor, to overturn the foot of the degenerate elder. Jeremiah was sanctified and proved in his mother's womb. John the Baptist, not yet born, knew that the president of the human contest and the rewarder of the wrestlers had come, and by leaping in his mother's womb earned the prize of a devout confession. Rightly was he already then designated for the crown, who before all had offered his name for the contest of Christ as a witness of faith and a herald of virtue. A good herald, who roused others to the contest, and truly a good herald, who cried out with so great a voice that at its sound the secrets of heaven resounded. Why say more? He moved the earth, he filled heaven. And therefore he received the name of voice, because a sacred voice went before the Word of God, as he himself taught us, saying: I am the voice of one crying in the desert. The prophet Isaiah said this; John confirmed that it had been said of himself. We have heard what the herald said: Prepare the way of the Lord, make his paths straight. This is the singular voice, and alone so resonant that it was heard by all, so sweet that it soothed the hearts of all. The Lord, then, confirmed his righteous athletes so that they might conquer; for he never deserts and abandons his own.

58. And therefore the prophet added: I have been young and have grown old. So the Latins; but some, following the Greeks, have: I have been young; for indeed I have grown old, and I have not seen the righteous man forsaken, nor his seed seeking bread. For one who wishes to understand it simply, the sense is plain: that in the time of his life David did not see a righteous man forsaken. But this both belongs to a short time and is incredible; for we see many righteous men in the world forsaken by men, when some of the powerful persecute them, and no one dares to approach them when he sees them placed in fear or under injury. Hence Job says: My brothers have departed, they have acknowledged strangers rather than me; my friends have become merciless, and those who knew me have forgotten my name. David too cries out that he has been not only forsaken but even attacked by friends and neighbours: My friends, he says, and my neighbours have drawn near against me. If, then, righteous men are found forsaken in this world, how did David say things contrary to himself, unless you understand that the righteous man, even if he is forsaken in the world, is yet not forsaken by the Lord? For Job, when he was on the dunghill, was not forsaken by the Lord, whom the Lord himself praised with his own voice in the council of the angels, whom he suffered to be tempted so that he might crown him; and he gave leave for temptation upon the body, but refused it upon the soul. Nor was David forsaken, to whom he gave a kingdom, so that he might rule over a chosen people, and whom, when he had been assailed in parricidal battles and driven from the borders of his kingdom, he restored by the right of victory. Jacob too, Elijah and Elisha and John the Baptist and the others, who went about wandering in goatskins in deserts and mountains and caves and holes of the earth, although they seem to have endured many mockeries and bitter punishments, yet were not forsaken. For when Jacob was fleeing his brother and wandering unaccompanied through the deserts, he fell asleep, and rising he saw there a multitude of the heavenly host, and said: This place is called the camps of the angels. It was not enough to call it a camp, but camps, such as would suit so great a multitude. You see, then, that he thought himself alone, and the camps of the heavenly army were following him, just as the rock, as it is written, followed our fathers in Moses' time, lest they should thirst in the desert. For since we have read in Exodus that, when the people were thirsting, Moses struck the rock with his rod and water flowed and the people of God drank, and again we have read in Leviticus or in Numbers that Moses once more touched the rock with his rod and water gushed out for the fathers to drink, the Apostle most excellently interpreted this, saying: But they drank from the rock that followed them, and the rock was Christ. Those complainers, then, those murmurers, those transgressors, the Lord nevertheless did not desert, but followed. What shall I say of Elijah and Elisha, whom, as they walked through the deserts, horses from heaven and a fiery chariot met? How was Elijah forsaken, who was invited by Christ? How forsaken on earth, who was caught up to heaven? How needy and naked and empty, who left to his disciple the inheritance of a twofold spirit, so that the heir, gifted with one sheepskin cloak, might stop rivers, call the Jordan back to its source, feed the armies of kings in the desert and supply drink to the thirsty? At his will drink flowed down from heaven, on earth the dead rose again. How was Elisha forsaken, who, when he was surrounded by the army of the Syrians, to be carried off captive to the king, when his servant Gehazi said: Lord, what shall we do?, said: Do not fear, for there are more with us than with them. And he said: Lord, open his eyes that he may see? And his eyes were opened, and he saw the mountain and all things round about filled with countless horses and heavenly legions. Paul too, who said that he had been tossed about in perils in the sea, in perils in the desert, yet himself testified, saying: If God is for us, who is against us? So he, thus forsaken and abandoned by men, abounds and overflows with God. For Elijah too was so abandoned that he said: Lord, take my soul, and yet he was so strong with God that he was terrible in power to kings themselves.

59. How could David as a young man grasp this? For a young man considers temporal things rather than eternal, because that age inclines to vices. But there are some who are old men in youth, and others who are young men in old age. There are, I say, men whose minds are reckoned by the gravity of old men, in whom the age of old age is a spotless life, in whom hoary prudence flourishes, such as was Jeremiah, who, when he spoke of himself as young in view of his age and excused himself because of his youth, since he seemed unequal to the office of preaching, the Lord said to him: Do not say: I am young, because you shall go to all to whom I shall send you. So he did not think him young whom he judged fit for the grace of prophecy. Such a young man, then, was David, who had been renewed and had flourished again, as he himself said, as an eagle's his youth. Thus, then, it must be understood: I have been young, but sanctified, but set as a prophet among the nations, one who could already know the sacraments of the heavenly kingdom and consider the true rewards of righteousness, but I have not seen the righteous man forsaken. Afterwards too, elsewhere, strengthened both by practice and by gift, I acquired increase of wisdom; the same grace seemed to me to remain about the righteous man.

60. We have explained, as we could, how he said: I have been young; but let us consider how he added: for indeed I have grown old. Youth is good, but old age better; for he who perseveres to the end, he shall be saved. Hence it was not said idly of the patriarch Abraham that he was nourished in a good old age. And John began to write the Gospel or the epistles as an old man. Since he shrank from writing himself apostle, he wrote himself the elder, nor was he esteemed the less, he to whom there came a kind of swan-like grace of old age. The very vessel of the Lord's election, writing to Philemon, says: Since you are such a one as Paul the old man, and now also a prisoner of Christ Jesus, I beseech you for my son Onesimus, whom I have begotten in chains. And so, wishing to pay him the greatest honour, he compared him to Paul the elder, not the young man, and he gloried in being an old man there where he was already held in chains. And see the difference: he was signified as a young man in the passion of another, as an old man in his own; there as a young man, because he was keeping the garments of the Jews, here as an old man, where he was putting off the cast skin of his body. Thus Peter too is called an old man there where there is the contest of the passion: When you were younger, he says, you girded yourself and walked where you wished; but when you have grown old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will gird you and lead you where you do not wish. And this he said signifying by what death he would glorify God. Before the contest he is called younger; in the contest, an old man, as one who has fulfilled the whole contest. This, then, is what David says, that warrior in youth, peacemaker in old age: I have been, he says, young, but I do not want you to think me still young; for indeed I have grown old, and I have not seen the righteous man forsaken, nor his seed seeking bread.

61. What is the seed of the righteous man? To Abraham were the promises spoken, and to his seed. He does not say "and to seeds", as of many, but as of one: "and to your seed", which is Christ. Hear the seed of the righteous man: My little children, of whom I am in labour until Christ be formed in you. And now, if anyone coming from the Gentiles hears our discourse and the Lord deigns to help, so that Christ is formed in his mind, whereby he may bear the offspring of faith, Christ will be his seed. And so Abraham was both the father of a line of descent according to the flesh, and he who first poured the word of the Lord into the hearts of the righteous. And therefore this seed of Abraham does not seek those loaves by which bodily nourishment is furnished, because it has the bread of righteousness which came down from heaven. For just as those are denied to be sons of Abraham who have not done the works of Abraham, so those are the seed of Abraham and are received among his sons who have accomplished his work. A true seed of Abraham was Elijah, to whom an angel furnished food, and in the strength of that food he walked through a course of forty days. The true seed of Abraham did not seek this nourishment of the body, he to whom loaves were brought from heaven; thus ravens served him his daily meal. He was so in need of everything that he had nothing of his own, and he was sent to bestow sustenance on others. A true seed was Daniel, to whom, when he was set among the lions, dishes through the air, filled with the reapers' meal, were carried by the prophet Habakkuk. There is, then, a bread of angels which man has eaten; for so it is written: Man ate the bread of angels. This is the bread of which David said: The Lord feeds me, and nothing shall be lacking to me. In a green place, there he has set me; beside the water of refreshment he has brought me up. Good David taught me the bread of angels, and he himself taught me the water of refreshment. This refreshment is spiritual, this refreshment is the rest of the inner mind. Good is the water that washes away sin, that cleanses inner things. What this water is, let us hear: If anyone, he says, thirsts, let him come to me and drink. He who believes in me, as Scripture says, out of his belly shall flow rivers of living water. Now this he said of the Spirit which those who were to believe in him were beginning to receive. The righteous man, then, lacks neither this bread nor the water of refreshment, he for whom the Holy Spirit is rest and refreshment.

62. But how will he be able to be in want who all the day has mercy and lends? What is it that the righteous man lends? Not bronze, not iron, not lead, but silver tried by fire. This he brings to the table of the Lord and divides among those who seek it; to one one mina, to another two, to another five he divides daily, and it never fails. But we have heard that lending at interest was censured in the Law, when Moses says: You shall not lend at interest. Lending is twofold, one of money, the other of grace. Divide the two, because it is written: If you offer rightly but do not divide rightly, you have sinned; be still. And you, divide rightly, lest you sin. Let Moses himself teach you to divide, who warned that one must divide rightly, so that you may follow what Abel did, who divided rightly, and flee what Cain was; he was a fratricide who did not know how to divide rightly. The Holy Spirit, then, divides the divisions, who works all things, dividing to each as he wills. He as he wills, you as you can, as you are able by your talent. Distinguish when the Law speaks of money, when of grace. For it said to you: You shall lend to the nations — that of which the Lord exacts interest — but you shall not borrow. There is the money of lending, and there is also grace, of which the Lord exacts interest; the money of lending holds bronze, grace confers faith. Lend, then, faith to the nations, so that grace may abound for you; but do not borrow as one in need, but lend as one rich and wealthy. Peter lent, Paul lent, John the Evangelist lent, or both of them, and were not in need, that is: they lent the money of Christ, they did not lend moneys at usury. Lend, then, and do not cease to lend. Do you hear what Scripture says: All the day he has mercy and lends? The righteous man lends by day, the avaricious man at night; for grace belongs to the light, but avarice is the usury of darkness. And therefore the seed of him who lends faith shall be in blessing, having indeed the reward of blessing.

63. Turn aside from evil and do good, and dwell for ages of ages. For the Lord loves judgement and will not forsake his saints; they shall be preserved for ever; but the unrighteous shall be punished, and the seed of the ungodly shall perish. The righteous shall possess the land by inheritance and shall dwell upon it for ever and ever. By these verses the prophet shows that we belong to the Lord, and that he himself is the judge of our thoughts and deeds, regarding the merits of each, who does nothing without judgement, but distinguishes evil and good acts by judgement. One must, then, turn aside from evil and do good. That you may not confuse good and evil, the first thing is that you should be free of crime, the next that you should bring forth the fruit of innocence, so that you may be everlasting. Do not think that your sins escape God; for so very many fall. He himself sees all things and condemns them, and exterminates the seed of the ungodly, the seed, surely, not of bodily generation, but of the inner mind, and the succession of ungodliness. For certainly Job is marked out by the testimony of Scripture as born of the seed of Esau. How, then, did the seed of Esau perish in Job, when he was so great a man that he foresaw the Lord who was to come upon earth and subdue the devil, the whale of this world? This, then, is what is sought, that malice should not be passed on to posterity. This Christ works through the sacrament of baptism, so that each one puts off what he was born and puts on what he was reborn, and becomes the heir not of a family but of grace, so that he may be able to acquire for himself a perpetual dwelling in the eternal habitation. And he who has been found worthy to dwell for ever and ever will himself also honour God for ages of ages.

64. When he has instructed the righteous man in moral disciplines, so as to teach you what kind of man he ought to be, or what the form of perfect righteousness is, he wishes his mind to rise to wisdom and to contemplations, and he rouses his attention, so that he may gaze with attentive heart on heavenly things, and turn over the divine oracles within himself, and shape his affection to those things which are pleasing to God; that he may be practised in the law, and that no commandments of the Lord may escape him, and that he may know the movements of the divine sacrament. Thus from here on see the holy prophet rising in his precepts: The mouth of the righteous man shall meditate wisdom, and his tongue shall speak judgement; the law of his God is in his heart. Nor will, he says, the Lord condemn him when he is judged. Wait for the Lord and keep his way, and he will exalt you, so that you may possess the land by inheritance; when sinners perish you shall see. I saw the ungodly man exalted above measure and lifted up beyond the cedars of Lebanon, and I passed by, and behold, he was not; and I sought him, and his place was not found. The things above are moral, these are intelligible. What is the mouth of the righteous man that shall meditate wisdom but that of the inner man? For there are two men in each one, one inner, the other outer. The inner thinks the things that belong to the mind, speaks the things that belong to the mind; the outer, those that belong to the body. Yet the Lord, at his coming, joined both together and made the two into one man, so that they should not fight each other with differing movements, but be united to each other by the unity of their wills. Therefore the mouth of the righteous man shall meditate wisdom. For now even the outer man of the righteous man himself has been carried over into the discipline of the inner man, and, conformed to his nature, performs his offices, so that the flesh meditates what belongs to the inner mind. But lest this seem incredible to you, hear him say that the Lord Jesus has transfigured the body of our lowliness, so that it might be made conformable to the body of his glory. Who would dare to say that the flesh without sin, the flesh taken from the Virgin, the flesh begotten by the Spirit of God coming upon Mary, which in nothing differed from the disciplines of wisdom, was without share in the virtues of the inner man and could not pass over into its uses, when it was beyond man that at its touch the sick were healed, light was poured back into the blind, the dead rose again? Fittingly, then, it is written: The mouth of the righteous man shall meditate wisdom, because the whole man is spiritual, not, surely, earthly. For as is the earthly, such also are the earthly, and as is the heavenly, such also are the heavenly. The earthly man, then, has gone, the heavenly has remained. And therefore the prophet, referring it to the times of redemption, said "shall meditate", although one of several has written "meditates", nor is it foreign to the sense, because to the spirit of the prophets future things stand for present. Others think that mouth is said for mind. Beautifully, moreover, did Solomon expound this to us, saying: The discourse of the righteous man is always wisdom, but the fool changes like the moon, that is: because he often varies and does not persevere in his opinion, and seems as it were in darkness to pour out light but not to hold it.

65. Let there, then, always be in our heart and mouth the meditation of wisdom, and let your tongue speak judgement; let the law of your God be in your heart. Therefore Scripture says to you: You shall speak of them sitting in the house, walking on the way, sleeping, rising up. Let us, then, speak the Lord Jesus, because he himself is wisdom, he himself is the word and the Word of God. For this too is written: Open your mouth to the word of God. He breathes him who sounds forth his discourses and meditates his words. Let us always speak him. When we speak of wisdom, it is he; when we speak of virtue, it is he; when we speak of righteousness, it is he; when we speak of peace, it is he; when we speak of truth and life and redemption, it is he. Open your mouth to the word of God, it is written. You open it, he speaks. Therefore David said: I will hear what the Lord speaks in me, and the Son of God himself says: Open your mouth, and I will fill it. But not all can receive the perfection of wisdom as Solomon did, not all as Daniel did; yet the spirit of wisdom is poured into all according to their capacity, but into all who are faithful. If you believe, you have the spirit of wisdom. Therefore the wise man says: I believed, therefore I have spoken. When you have believed, you will receive the grace of speaking. And he who believes is redeemed, and he who prays is redeemed, if he applies himself devoutly to prayers and is assiduous in prayer, if he anticipates the day, frequents the night, goes to meet the morning hours of the sun, so that he himself is enlightened by Christ before the earth is by the rising of the sun. And he who sings psalms is redeemed, and he who is pricked with compunction is redeemed.

66. Therefore meditate always, speak the things that are God's, sitting in the house. We can take the house as the Church; we can take the house as the interior within us, so that we speak within ourselves. Judges sit in Jerusalem; seats were set for judgement; a council of judges also sat, and the books were opened. The prophet says: And do all things with counsel. Judge of your deeds with counsel, drink wine with counsel, speak with counsel, so that you may escape sin, lest you fall into it through much speaking. Sitting, speak with yourself as one who judges. On the way speak, so that you may never be idle. You speak on the way if you speak in Christ, because Christ is the way. On the way speak to yourself, speak to Christ. Hear how you should speak to him: I will, he says, that men pray in every place, lifting up pure hands, without anger and disputing. Speak, O man, while sleeping, lest the sleep of death steal upon you. Hear how you should speak while sleeping: If I give sleep to my eyes and slumber to my eyelids, until I find a place for the Lord, a tabernacle for the God of Jacob. Overcome nature by diligence, shut out the sleep of the body. We cannot form our nature, we can form diligence. David was a man; he gave something to sleep too through the night, but he shut out sleep by washing his bed every night and watering his couch with tears. Therefore he was always mindful of his Lord upon his couch and meditated on him, so that he might anticipate the dawn and Christ might shine upon him in the darkness, saying: Rise, you who sleep. This grace is not in all, but diligence can be in very many. Do not, then, wait for Christ to rouse you, but rather rouse Christ. He rouses him who, while sleeping, thinks of him. If you rouse him, he too will rouse you from sleep, will raise you from death, saying to you: Arise from the dead. When, then, you rise up or rise again, speak him, so that you may fulfil what you are commanded. Hear how Christ rouses you. Your soul says: The voice of my brother knocks at the door, and Christ says: Open to me, my sister, my bride. Hear how you may awaken Christ. The soul says: I have adjured you, daughters of Jerusalem, if you should stir up and awaken charity. Charity is Christ. Hear also how you may speak while sleeping: I sleep, and my heart keeps watch. These things in the common sense; now raise your mind a little higher.

67. Speak sitting in the house, in this earthly house of this habitation which is dissolved, in which we are sojourning away from the Lord. For placed in this body, while we wish to be clothed over rather than stripped, we are exiles from Christ and groan heavily. Sitting in this, then, confess your sins, because you have sat and not stood, and have not said: Our feet were standing in the courts of Jerusalem. Speak rather, and do not hide your offences; speak sitting, so that you may hear him say: Rise, after you have sat, you who eat the bread of sorrow. Speak walking on the way, that is, as you take the course of this life. Speak here, lest there you be struck dumb, as he was struck dumb who did not at first believe the angel of Christ, but afterwards, when he had believed, received his voice. Speak sleeping and at rest, buried together in Christ, so that with him you may rise again in newness of life. Speak even when buried in the tomb, as the souls of the saints spoke, seeking vengeance on the author of death: How long, O Lord, holy and true, do you not judge and avenge our blood? Speak lastly when you rise, as he taught who said: I slept and rested and rose again, since the Lord will take me up. And there too, then, the mouth of the righteous man shall meditate wisdom, until he himself arrives at the more sublime secrets of the tabernacle, full of heavenly and sacred joy and gladness, as Scripture has taught us, when David says: These things I remembered, and I poured out my soul upon me, for I shall go into the place of the wonderful tabernacle, even to the house of God, with the voice of exultation and confession, the sound of one feasting. So both sitting in this bodily house, and having gone forth from here and walking on the way when we are caught up, if we deserve it, to meet Christ, and sleeping, we shall speak.

68. Take a third ἀναγωγήν, by which the soul of the righteous man raises itself. For a certain spiritual sleep is to be understood, of which Solomon says: For if you sit, you will sit without fear; if you sleep, you will rest sweetly; and you will not fear the terror that comes upon you, nor the onsets of the ungodly coming upon you. You will rest with sweetness and will not fear terror and the onsets of the ungodly coming upon you, if your tongue speaks the judgement of God and you always have it before your eyes, so that you leave no place for sin and know that the price of an offence will be paid by the requital of punishment. For he himself interpreted this in Psalm 90 (91), saying: You shall not be afraid of the terror of the night, and of the arrow that flies by day, of the business that walks about in darkness, of the onset and the noonday demon. A thousand shall fall at your side and ten thousand at your right hand, but it shall not come near you; yet you shall consider with your eyes and shall see the requital of sinners. For the righteous man, when he speaks the judgement of God, or when he himself speaks the things that are righteous and full of true judgement, does not speak with anger, not from the affliction of a grieving mind, not from pain, not from any passion, but he speaks from truth, he speaks from equity, so that he does not lean by feeling, but weighs what he speaks on a true balance; for he judges what things are to be said or not brought forth. Like this is that saying: The lips of the wise are bound by sense, because all things that they speak are seen to agree with true sense, and by the prudence of their sense they understand what they ought to speak or keep silent, so that they keep back what is to be kept silent by a sort of bar and fastening of the lips, while in those things which ought to be said the bonds of the lips are loosed. Rightly, then, did he set first: The mouth of the righteous man shall meditate wisdom; for long meditation will be able to have full judgement, which the righteous man will speak in due time, when he has seen face to face the things that are perfect; for now we cannot speak true things about the things we do not comprehend.
