Thus not even Paul presumed to speak, who, caught up into paradise, heard heavenly secrets, but, wishing not to err in what he said, he says: How inscrutable are the judgements of God, and his ways past searching out! But now, released from the body, perhaps he comprehends the kinds of the ways past searching out and his judgements, which are many like the abyss.

69. The form of righteousness is still being described and enlarged, when Scripture says: The law of his God is in his heart, and his steps shall not be supplanted. In the heart of the righteous man is the law of God. What law? Not the written, but the natural; for the righteous man, indeed, the law is not laid down, but for the unrighteous. In his heart is the law, not superficially, as on the lips of the Jews; for with the heart one believes unto righteousness. He who believes also speaks; but he who speaks does not forthwith believe. Thus the people did not believe of whom it was said: This people honours me with their lips, but their heart is far from me. If, then, the Gentiles, when they do the things that are of the law, are a law to themselves, knowing what they ought to follow or beware of, how much more does the faithful and righteous man, who lives according to the image and likeness of God, know how to discern things seemly and honourable, and to use a kind of lawful helm of natural wisdom, so that his footsteps may not be supplanted like those of Esau, whom his brother, on account of the greed of his gullet, supplanted so that he fell, and poured out his footsteps into death!

70. The more righteous anyone is, the more the enemy lies in wait for him, and therefore Scripture says: The sinner observes the righteous man and seeks to destroy him; but the Lord will not leave him in his hands, nor condemn him when he is judged. The sinner, then, seeing that the righteous man speaks wisdom with his mouth and meditates it in his heart, that he speaks judgement with his tongue, that he keeps the law of the Lord in his heart, tries to bring upon him the death of sin, but the Lord protects him. And therefore let us not fear the snares of the sinner, since God is for us. If, then, God is for us, who is against us? Therefore God will not forsake his righteous man nor condemn him when he holds judgement concerning him; for he is a true judge, and therefore righteousness cannot be endangered. Hence also Aquila said: Nor will he condemn him in judging him, Symmachus: Nor will he condemn him when the righteous man is judged.

71. But since the Seventy put it thus: when he is judged, we think that he had regard to this, that it is written: For the Lord himself will come into judgement. But into what judgement? Hear him say: Against you only have I sinned and done evil before you, that you may be justified in your words and may overcome when you are judged. For this reason, then, the Lord offers himself to be heard, that judgement may be passed on him, so that he may the more overcome. How he offers himself, hear: My people, what have I done to you, or in what have I grieved you, or how have I been troublesome to you? Answer me. For I brought you out of Egypt and freed you from the house of bondage, and elsewhere: I am, I am he who blots out your iniquities, and I will not remember them. But you, remember, and let us be judged.

72. Grievous is this judgement, when the Lord demands to be judged against man concerning himself. For what would you answer to him who bestowed all things upon you, set you over all, subjected to you the Egyptians to whom you had come as a guest, and afterwards drowned them in the sea when they pursued you? He destroyed and overthrew your enemies, he created and made you; he redeemed you with his blood, and do you hand yourself over to serve his enemy? He forgave you all your sins, and do you commit more grievous ones? He calls you: coming into judgement, what will you answer him, when, unless he forgives you again, you have perished? And therefore holy David, seeing that by this kind of moral dealing of God he was the more weighed down, flees from judgement and asks for mercy, saying: And do not enter into judgement with your servant, for in your sight no living man shall be justified. He confesses that he has been placed in darkness as one dead of the world. Cautiously he said as one dead of the world, not dead. For those who die in Christ are not dead of the world; but those are dead of the world who set their whole life, which they have lived in the world, in destruction and death. And therefore, as one in despair, he flees to the remedy, saying: Hear me quickly, O Lord, for my spirit has failed. For when the offer of such a judgement was set before him, in which truth was to be examined rather than mercy bestowed, he failed.

73. Yet the Lord is so loving that, although no living man is justified in his sight — for who, however righteous, is not liable to sin, or whose life is such that it is judged worthy of the likeness of God? —, certainly he who has been made after the image and likeness of God ought, as God is without sin, so likewise, being after his image, to be without sin. Of what punishment, then, is he worthy who has lost so great a grace of the Lord's workmanship and the likeness of the divine beauty? But because he is merciful, even if he submits himself to judgement, he yet does not judge the righteous man, but the unrighteous. He spares the righteous man as one who sins through the frailty of his condition; he punishes the unrighteous man as one who loathes the ungrateful. Then, even if you have many works of righteousness, do not be impatient and arrogant, as one who thinks that the rewards of righteousness are to be demanded for yourself in this world, or who complains that some adversities have befallen you undeservedly. For as long as you live, a contest is owed to you, not a reward.

74. Wait, then, for the Lord, whether as helper or as rewarder, and keep his ways. This is "to wait": to keep the commandments of him for whom you think you must wait. Even if you are crushed, even if you are afflicted, even if you fail through sickness, yet wait for the Lord; hope will not confound you.

75. The Lord will come and will exalt you, so that you may possess the land by inheritance. It is clear that the land is a higher one, not this earthly one in the valley, but that one of the eternal promise, in which whoever is set is exalted by the Lord.

76. But you shall see, when sinners perish. For then the reward of the righteous will come, when judgement has been passed on the merits of the ungodly. But sinners do not perish in one way only. They perish for you beforehand, if you do not admire their power, if you do not admire their riches, if it does not move you when you learn that they abound in honours, children, friends. For these things belong to the world, and the world lies under worldly malignity, and the prince of the world favours those who serve him, according to that saying: All things I will give you, if you will fall down and worship me. Why do you seek that these things be given to you, when the ungodly one himself who gives them cannot remain in them long? He himself also passes away: how can the things which he has bestowed not pass away? Do you only keep a steadfast mind, hold to righteousness, let no one move you, and you will see that the ungodly man is nothing.

77. Thus hear him say: I saw the ungodly man exalted above measure and lifted up above the cedars of Lebanon, and I passed by, and behold, he was not. I saw him in this world, I saw him with the eyes of the body, I saw him also boasting and exalting himself above measure with his words, thinking himself to be something when he is nothing. How does he exalt himself above measure? I will set, he says, my throne above the clouds, and I will be like the Most High. He exalts himself, so that you see him like the cedars of Lebanon exalted above measure. These are themselves lofty and stand on a very high mountain; rightly, then, are they exalted above measure; and exalted above measure seems the ungodly man who has set himself upon that mountain of which it is written: If you have faith like a grain of mustard seed, you would say to this mountain: Be taken up and be cast into the sea. "To this demon", he says, "by which this man whom you marvel to see healed had been invaded and oppressed. Therefore you have charity in Christ rather than power over the devil." You see the cedar exalted above measure on a very high mountain; but it is broken by the wind, it is burned up by fire, it is dissolved by age. So is the rich man in the world; he shines indeed with a certain splendour of worldly favour, like Lebanon, he leans on the power of the world, he exults in wealth and riches. He seems to you to be something, before you say: Passing over, I will see. For just as Moses in mind and spirit passed over material things and saw God, so you too, if you pass over from here, raising the footstep of your mind to the grace of God, will see that he is nothing who seemed to himself most powerful in this land. Therefore God the Word says to your soul, says to your mind: Come hither from Lebanon, O bride, come hither from Lebanon: you shall pass over and pass through. If you pass over worldly things, you will pass through to paradise. Hear one passing over: Remember me, O Lord, when you come into your kingdom; hear again one passing through: Amen, amen, I say to you, today you shall be with me in paradise.

78. What is: and behold, he was not? In the beginning, surely, was the Word, and he who sees the Word, who is a partaker of the Word, he too surely is, because the Word of God always is, and the speech which is from him is not "is" and "not", but "is", and it abides in him who follows God. But he who does not know the Word is not, because he does not cleave to him who said: I am who I am. But he who cleaves to him is one spirit; and where the Spirit is, there also is life. Therefore Scripture says to you of God: Who gives life to the dead and calls the things that are not as the things that are, that is: he calls the Gentiles, who were not, as his people, who was; for our fathers were, who were the chosen of God. And elsewhere, where it is said: The Lord has known who are his, he does not know those who are not; and Esther says: Do not give the sceptre of your kingdom to those who are not. Rightly, then, does the righteous man say: And I passed by, and behold, he was not, that is: I saw him exalted above measure in the world, before I considered that the world is nothing; but when I passed in mind above the world, I saw that he who before was exalted above measure in this world was no longer.

79. And you, if you are righteous, pass from present things to those that are to come, and present things will pass away for you. Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away. For the righteous man, then, all things pass away. The ungodly man passes away, he is like vanity, and his days pass away like a shadow. And not only do present things pass away for the righteous man, but also, when he has turned his attention to the things that are to come, these things already pass away. See how present things passed away for the righteous man. Certainly in the times of the prophet David there was the synagogue; the Church from the Gentiles was not; and yet he speaks of things to come as if present: Bless our God, O nations; then above he says: God has gone up with jubilation. He passed beyond the times of unbelief that were to come, not by age but by mind, and with the devout devotion of faith he celebrated the resurrection of the Lord, which was to come many ages later, saying: Sing psalms to our God, sing psalms; sing psalms to our King, sing psalms. As if he were present at the very sacraments of the nuptial union of Christ and the Church, so he dances and rejoices. He expressed also the cause of the celebration, saying: The Lord has reigned over all the earth. So if we pass by, those things too which are to come seem to us to have passed by, just as, if in sailing you pass by the land, you see it too passing by and as it were departing from you. Often sailors, especially in a storm, flee the land, and yet, while they flee it as they sail, the land itself rather seems to flee from the sailors. You too, if you flee this world, it also will flee you; if you pass by this land, it too passes you by. You too sail on this sea and are tossed in this world: flee the land; it has rocks, it has stones, as it is written: Cast the stones out of the way. If you hasten through this airy deep to the harbour of divine pleasure, you will see that all these things have passed by for you. For what difference is there whether they have passed by, or you judge that they have passed by for you?

80. There is also another ungodly man who seems exalted and lifted up above the cedars of Lebanon, and if you pass by, he will not be, nor will you find his place. See for me a Jewish scribe reciting the sequence of the old Scriptures, but not following it; you hear that he recites it with his lips, you admire him as learned. You ask what he understands; he answers according to the letter, he runs through the history. He seems to you exalted above measure and lifted up, if you consider the letter. Pass over to the spiritual understanding, because the law is spiritual; you see that he is nothing, and then you say: I saw the ungodly man exalted above measure, and I passed by, and behold, he was not; and I sought him, and his place was not found. I sought him where he ought to have been, where life is; I did not find him; he was not there where life was, because he was dead, and therefore neither was his place found. For what place has a dead man, who was not and never has been? For he who was in the letter was not; for the letter kills, but the spirit gives life. I sought him, then, so that I might find him; he did not offer himself to be found, that is: I wished to convert him, he would not offer himself to me to be converted. I did what belonged to one who seeks; he shrank from what ought to have belonged to one who returns. Thus the soul that wishes to be converted seeks the Word, and when it has found him it says: I held him and will not let him go, that is, the Son of God, the Word of God. But the unrighteous man, who would not be healed, I sought him and did not find him; and therefore do not imitate him.

81. Keep innocence and behold equity. He who keeps innocence beholds equity, since he who is clean sees God, whose rod is upright and whose righteousness is upright, so that he turns away from those who have turned aside to the pursuits of perversity. Good is the innocence which sees God. Thus others have put: Keep perfection; for he is perfect who does not know malice nor is acquainted with guile. Therefore, he says, keep perfection, for there are remnants for the peaceable man. The remnants of a man are spoken of from the body of one who has died, from his cast-off remains. They are called remnants because they are seen to survive after a man's death; for those things survive which are kept for the resurrection. For this corruptible must put on incorruption, and this mortal put on immortality. Rather, then, the remnants for a man are the hope of resurrection and the faith of conversion and the grace of love, since the ungodly do not rise again unto judgement, but the life of the righteous is known to God and is approved by the judgement of the Lord. To this place that saying beautifully fits: The remnants according to the election of grace have been saved.

82. But the unrighteous shall utterly perish together; the remnants of the ungodly shall perish. The remnants of the righteous are virtues; the remnants of the ungodly are wickedness and unbelief and sin. These will be blotted out, so that they are not.

83. But the salvation of the righteous is from the Lord, not from the world, not from an element; heaven and earth shall pass away. I do not entrust my salvation to heaven, because it too passes away, since it was said of the many heavens: They shall perish, but you remain. I entrust it to God alone, who remains, who can forgive sins, that he may be my protector in the time of tribulation, and may help me and free me and rescue me from sinners in the time of his judgement, and may save me, because I have hoped in him. In him alone have I hoped; for he does not wish us to serve both him and another. He who serves him alone is set free, since to him belong praise, glory, perpetuity, from the ages, and now, and always, and unto all the ages of ages. Amen.

On Psalm 37 (38)

1. I wrote two little books on penitence long ago, and I think I must write on it again: first, because it is useful to pray for the pardon of sins even daily; then, because in those two little books there is an exhortation to penitence, or the profit it brings if it is practised, but now it must be set out how penitence ought to be done; for it profits nothing to do it unless it is done as it ought to be. In this holy David has set before us a teaching to follow, and he seems to me to have tasted sin for this reason, that he might teach how sin could be abolished. For from what did medicine take its beginning, if not from a wound? While the first age of the human race teaches others what profited itself, and shows others how it healed its own wound, use made the art and sickness the teaching. For this is the first and strong school of medicine, which experience, not conjecture, formed. And so the first physicians were called empirics from experience, and from that school the others were derived, and from it they took a certain sap of their own power. But what is stronger for encouraging penitence than that the holy prophet David taught us to repent of our sins? For who would disdain to humble himself before God, when a king has humbled himself, or who would hesitate to afflict his soul, when so great a prophet has afflicted his, paying the price of tears for the redemption of sin? And although he has spread the precepts for doing penitence through the whole body of his writings — for which of his psalms is not a remedy for a sinning conscience, since in one place he has shown how he was thrust, in another how he was troubled, so that he might demonstrate not only how wounds are to be healed but also how they are to be guarded against, and teach us by his own example? And who would believe that he himself stands, where even the stronger has fallen? — yet in Psalm 37 (38) he has expressed a greater force of inward grief and of lamentable mourning.

2. Indeed at the outset the title itself reminds us that precepts of this kind are being shaped; for thus we read: for a remembrance, he says, concerning the sabbath day, a psalm of David. For the remembrance of sins is the full definition of penitence, so that each one may chastise his sins as with a kind of scourge of daily speech, and condemn the outrages he has committed. For so we are taught, as God says: Tell your iniquities, that you may be justified, and the Apostle says: with the mouth confession is made unto salvation. But perhaps it may trouble someone that he did not say "a remembrance of sins", but "concerning the sabbath day" or, as another version has it, "of the sabbaths". What, then, is the sabbath but the rest of things to come, the relief of things present, on which the Law commands that men's works keep holiday and burdens be laid down? What burdens are heavier than our sins? Let these be laid down on the days of the sabbaths, that is, in this time of this life. For every week is closed in seven days; in six days this world was formed; on the seventh day the Lord rested from his work, he who rests in our meekness, as he himself said: Upon whom shall I rest but upon the humble and meek? Release yourself, then, every sabbath, lift off the weight of error, so that you may deserve to come secure to the rest of that future sabbath; for if you carry heavy sins from here, you will have no rest there. Unburden your mind, therefore, cast off everything by which the conscience is weighed down and pressed, lest your flight be in winter or on the sabbath. This life is in labour, that one in rest. Let the consummation of this world find you lighter, lest, burdened with sins, you be unable to flee. Flee beforehand, so as to forestall it. He flees the world who is not held by the enticements of the world, who has released himself from every care of it. Flee also the wrath to come, that is, the day of judgement. He flees it who has repented; for so John the Baptist defined it, saying: Brood of vipers, who showed you to flee from the wrath to come? Bear therefore fruits worthy of penitence.

3. You have heard read today that John was baptizing in Aenon near Salim. Aenon is called "eye of punishments", Salim "he himself ascending"; this is what the interpretation holds. He, then, who chooses to be baptized foresees the punishments, and so takes refuge in the sacrament of baptism, to lay down every sin, lest he begin to be liable to punishments. And perhaps he who is baptized with the baptism of penitence foresees punishments; but he who is baptized in Christ looks to grace. The baptism of John, then, is the eye of punishments, the baptism of Christ the eye of graces. Yet, although John baptized in Aenon, he baptized near the one ascending; for this interpretation holds this too, that Salim is called "he himself ascending". He, then, who announced his coming was nearest to Christ. For the Son of Man, who descended from heaven, is himself also the one who ascended into heaven, that he might fill all things. But because as is the heavenly, such also are the heavenly, he too ascends into heaven who, laying aside earthly things, is buried together in Christ, so that with Christ he may rise from the death of sin to newness of life and to a share in the inheritance, so as to become, as it is written, an heir of God, a co-heir of Christ.

4. True medicine, then, is to do penitence, which was lawfully preached at the time when the physician came from heaven, one who would not exasperate wounds but heal them. A good physician, who taught how we should seek remedies in this earth of our body, when the flower of the healing herb rose up for us, he who from sin condemned sin in the flesh. The flesh, then, was made an antidote, which before was the poison of sin, because it was the enticement of sins. Hear how the flesh is an antidote: the Word of God was made flesh, put his hand into the hole of asps, emptied out the poison, took away sin, that is: from sin he condemned sin in the flesh.

5. And so that this may be considered more fully, let us go over the very passage of the Apostle. For thus it is written: God, sending his own Son in the likeness of the flesh of sin, also from sin condemned sin in the flesh, that the justification of the Law might be fulfilled in us, that we may walk not according to the flesh but according to the Spirit. The Son of God, then, came in the likeness of the flesh of sin; certainly he did not come in the likeness of flesh, he who took on true flesh, but in the likeness of the flesh of sin, that is, of sinful flesh; for by the fraud of the serpent, and infused with his poison, our flesh had been made a flesh of sin. After it was made liable to sin, it had become a flesh of death, because it was owed to death. The likeness of this flesh, already guilty, already prejudged, Christ took on in his own flesh, because, although he had taken on the natural substance of this flesh, he had nevertheless taken on no contagion, nor was he conceived in iniquities and born in sins, he who was born not of blood nor of the will of the flesh nor of the will of man, but of the Holy Spirit and the Virgin. What, then, is: from sin he condemned sin in the flesh? Is it from the likeness of the flesh of sin; or because Christ took on our sins not by practice but by their quality, not by crime but by the sacrament of his mercy, and he who himself did no sin was made sin for us; or rather because from this flesh of sin too, that is, this our liable and prejudged flesh, he condemned sin in the flesh, by teaching how, set in the flesh, we may walk not according to the flesh but according to the Spirit? For it is sin to walk according to the flesh, because he who conducts himself according to the wisdom of the flesh lives for luxury and pleasures and wantonness and the desires of the flesh; for the wisdom indeed of the flesh is an enemy to God, whence those who are in the flesh cannot please God. And therefore he condemns sin from sin who, lifting himself off from the vices of the flesh, lives according to the Spirit, wholly given to God and intent on the heavenly oracles; for this Christ came, to do not his own will, that is, the flesh's, but God's. If anyone, then, follows him, so as to do what pleases God and to curb the craving of his own flesh, he is an imitator of Christ.

6. We have expressed what we think; you who read, choose what to follow. There is no error where the disposition of devotion is simple and every sense is faithful. Christ condemned sin by taking on the likeness of the flesh of sin, so as to abolish the sins of our flesh. He condemned sin so as to crucify our sins in his own flesh, himself made sin for us, so that we, who were the captivity of death and the prey of the serpent, might be the righteousness of God in him. This taking on of sins, then, is of loving-kindness, not of crime. Through this sin the eternal God absolved us, he who did not spare his own Son and made him to be sin for us. Then, Christ condemned sin in the flesh from the very flesh of sin, which with a kind of judicial vigour and censorial authority hates the vices it had been used to love, and shudders at the unchastity which it used to think pleasure. It separates itself from wantonness, disowns its desires, renounces crimes and outrages, drives off avarice, which makes effeminate a certain manliness of the flesh. See for yourself that imitator of Christ, himself condemning sin from sin in the flesh: So I run, he says, not as at an uncertainty, so I wrestle, not as beating the air, but I chastise my body and bring it into servitude, lest, when I have preached to others, I myself become reprobate.

7. We have not, I think, digressed superfluously, because this too belonged to the medicine of man. Providing for this medicine, God, as he commanded this earth to bear the healthful juices of herbs and trees, by which the wounds of the flesh might be cured, so also filled the series of the divine Scriptures with healthful precepts, by which the infirmity of the soul might be healed. How grievous it is, then, that dumb animals know how to doctor themselves and seek out healthful herbs for their wounds and do not refuse to pick them, and the diligence of men does not seek them, though even a physician has been sent to them, to point out the seeds of healthful medicine in the old Scriptures and to bring new ones himself! Let us take examples from the earth itself, then, of how many things are healthful that are carelessly judged harmful.

8. The serpent nourishes poison, has harmful bites, wounds the flesh; but in its very poison too you will find an antidote, if you look for it. Indeed its flesh is burnt, and from its ash theriac is made, by which the force of poison is commonly blunted, so that it cannot harm. Not the earth, then, is at fault, which brings forth the serpent, but he who does not know how to beware of the serpent. The serpent itself can benefit you, if you understand, or rather if you do not spurn the physician's precepts. Learn that the one you think should be fled is to be imitated: Be, he says, shrewd as serpents and simple as doves. Guard your head like the serpent; even if you are torn in your whole body, you will revive. Your head is Christ, because he is the head of man; if you keep the faith, even if you die like the serpent, you too will rise again. Imitate the serpent, which puts off its outer self so that the inner may be renewed; imitate the serpent, which vomits out its poison. Blessed are you if you too vomit out your evil thoughts and lay down the poisons of your malice. It is a well-known story, and one strong with the authority of writers, that the serpent, approaching the waters, with a hiss pledges itself to union with a female snake; that she, consulting her own safety, promises the sought use of her union on this condition, if the serpent vomits out its poison; and that he, captivated, obeys her commands, lays down his arms, and by the custom of their own union puts his head into her mouth; and that, when the intimacy begins to grow warm, the female, whether by some violence of nature or by the heat of lust, tightens her mouth and presses bites upon the kisses of lust, and so the serpent's head is cut off.

9. Will you not recognize in the nature of the serpent a mystery of faith? That serpent of paradise first provoked the woman to the adultery of guilt. But when its poison was poured out into this world, the offspring of that woman, avenging the deception of its parent and the fraud of the serpent, stripped it of its arms and cut off its head. But if the poisons have prevailed, and sin, in which is the sting of death, has crept in, use the example of physicians, so that the serpent may die for you, even if late: you should crush its poison and break up its body, and know that it must be blended, mixed with healthful juices, so that it not only loses its force and venom but also becomes a remedy from poison. And that intelligible serpent dies for you, if you die to sin and your sins die to you. So its poison is crushed, if your deeds prick you; so all its force is abolished, if you cover the crime committed with good works and temper it with faithful confession. Hear how the power of the dragon itself is crushed: May God, he says, crush Satan under your feet. Crush first your heart, in which was the lair of the dragon, so that it may not find where it can dwell. Crush the flesh of the dragon; its flesh is our sins. Therefore God says to you: the nations feast on him, and below he says: dying runs ahead. And the flesh of his body cleaves to him. For as the saints are the body and members of Christ, so sinners, who do not desert sin but cleave to sin, are the body and members of the dragon. Therefore we feast on the body of Christ, but they feast on the body of the dragon. We who strive to cleave to Christ feast on the daily remission and pardon of sins; but they, who daily link sins to sins, feast on a continuation of outrages and crimes.

10. Crush, therefore, this flesh and sprinkle over it the contrition of your heart, then pour over all of it the water of the Jordan. For the Jordan is a descent and an ascent, since he who has descended into the sacred font also ascends, to seek the things that are above; for he who is baptized in Christ descends into the death of Christ and ascends into his resurrection. But if you have gone astray when already baptized, pour the water of tears, not lying but true, so that you may cry from the depth to the Lord your God and it may be said of you: A voice was heard in Ramah, weeping and much wailing; Rachel weeping for her sons, and she would not be consoled, because they are not. Rachel is the Church, in which the people of God is blessed. Let her weep for you, let her lament your sins and weep very much, so that she does not easily admit consolation, as those do who grieve very much. She would not be consoled for this reason, because you who were have ceased to be; but by this perseverance in grief and weeping let it be earned that she may say: my son was dead and has come to life again, he was lost and is found. Or perhaps Rachel would not be consoled for the sons of the Jews, because they are not, whom she had surely borne for this, that they might be. And therefore she did not admit consolation when the little ones were slain by Herod, because they did not believe in the coming of Jesus, filling up the measure of wickedness so as even to kill the little ones, so that among them the Son of the Virgin too might be killed. The Church of the Lord, then, would not be consoled for the Jews, because they are not; but in the Christians, who have been gathered from the nations, she feasts and rejoices, since those who were not have begun to be.

11. You have poured, then, the water of the Jordan, the water of grace; drink this first. You have poured the water of tears, the water of penitence; this is the second cup, so that you may restore the first. Drink water from your own vessels, drink water from your own weeping, so that you may say: and I mingled my drink with weeping. Your weeping, then, is your drink, and your tears are your food. My tears, he says, have been bread to me. If tears are loaves, you have from the sin of iniquity the food of conversion, according to what you read in the Gospel, that he to whom more is forgiven loves more. Whence we often see some who were negligent Christians before become more diligent after committing some sin, and commonly come out of it perfect through penitence. As, then, conversion comes from sin, so the remedy comes from poison. Hear this remedy: The righteous man at the beginning of his speech is his own accuser. Sin is poison, iniquity is poison; the confession of one's own crime is the remedy for the fall. And therefore it is truly a remedy from poison, if you tell your iniquities, that you may be justified.

12. But now let us take up the psalm, so that it may itself teach us how penitence is to be done. Thus, then, it begins:

13. Lord, do not rebuke me in your wrath, nor chasten me in your fury. He who repents must be ready to bear reproaches and to undergo injuries, and not be disturbed if anyone casts up to him the crime of his sin. For since he ought to accuse himself, how does he not bear another rebuking him? But if he ought not to fear being rebuked by man, how much less by the Lord his God, against whom we all sin even in the things that are hidden, especially since condemnation in things present is absolution in things to come; but those who are not condemned in this world will not be scourged with men. Woe to me, if it is said of me: "He has his reward"! If a good deed is condemned by this sentence, how much more is a crime weighed down! For if mercy boasted of in easy speech is cheated of eternal fruit, how much more is the punishment of iniquity deferred with the addition of bitter interest! He who repents, then, must offer himself to punishment, so that he may be punished here by the Lord and not be kept for eternal torments; nor should he wait for the time, but go to meet the divine indignation.

14. See that David's deeds agree with his prayers. He had offended the Lord, because he had ordered the people to be numbered. By confession he forestalled God's messenger, recalling that he had sinned greatly and had done very foolishly, not as if reminding one who had forgotten, but as if pressing one who delayed, so that he should not put off any longer the stirring of anger at the offence. I have sinned, he says, greatly, because I have done this thing; and now, Lord, blot out the iniquity of your servant. Hear one saying how sins are blotted out: Her sin is loosed, for she has received from the hand of the Lord her sins double. He does not ask, then, that his sin be wholly remitted to him, but that it be blotted out by a moderate payment, that is, that its memory be blotted out for the future. Indeed the Lord, as if addressed by the voice of one pressing him not to prolong the vengeance for the error committed, sent Nathan the prophet to him, and he came to the king and said to him: Choose what you wish to be done: three years of famine upon the land, or that you flee for three months from the face of your enemies, and they pursuing you, or that there be death in the land for three days. And now know and see what I am to answer him who sent me. See God teaching by what moderation his indignation is softened, if we do not wholly refuse the stripes, but ask that the punishment be of a kind that lightens it, not one that escapes it. He proposed three, so that he might choose what he thought more moderate. See also how he provokes us to penitence, so that we offer ourselves to injury, commanding him to choose, so that in the very punishment a certain privilege of choice may be kept, and he may soothe his guilty one with this word. And David said to Nathan: I am in straits in these three; but let me fall rather into the hands of the Lord, since his mercy is great, than into the hands of men. Did he ask that the punishment for his error be forgiven him? Had he done so, he would have been branded with impudence, as one ungrateful for the divine moderation. A modest confession greatly supports the guilty, and the punishment we cannot escape by defence we lighten by shame. He chose not what would be immune from the scourge, but what he judged more moderate, so as to commit himself to the gentleness of God, who knew how to pardon, rather than to the power of men, who often exceed the measure of vengeance. He has mercy, then, who does not know how to err; he does not have mercy who is a partaker of error.

15. Nor did his faith deceive holy David, but even in the offence itself he earned the grace of the divine compassion. For he who had proposed to exercise death in the land for three days did not let even one day pass, but at the hour of the midday meal he gladly granted pardon, and, to use the word of Scripture, he repented over the evil. See how Scripture exhorts you not to shun penitence either, if you follow God, whom you ought to follow. He did well to add "over the evil", because every vengeance seems harsh. Therefore the day of judgement too is called an evil day, from which he is blessed who is delivered by God, as it is written: In the evil day the Lord will deliver him. And the Lord, it says, told the angel to spare. But see that, when the Lord wishes to pardon, he gives the grace and the confidence to entreat. And David saw the angel striking and said: Behold, it is I. I have sinned, and I the shepherd have done evil; and these in this flock, what have they done? Let your hand be upon me and upon my father's house. If the Lord had commanded the angel to spare, how was the angel still striking, unless because the Lord, even though he wishes to pardon, wishes to be asked, and works so that he is asked? Nor would the man have seen the angel striking, had the Lord not revealed the angel to his eyes. Whence Elisha too says: Lord, open now the eyes of this boy, that he may see; and the eyes of his boy were opened, and he saw the mountain filled with horses and chariots round about the prophet. Nor let it trouble you that the person of a little servant is meaner than that of a prophet or a king, because the sight of horses and chariots too is meaner than the holiness of angels.

16. And perhaps to some who read carelessly it may seem harsh that David, that man humble of heart and meek, who spared his enemies, chose the death of the people rather than his own flight or famine upon the land. He avoided the famine of the people, because it is held heavier than death and all torments, and pestilence too follows it, which pollutes the sky and heaps up death. He did not ask for his own flight for this reason, because the prophet could intercede for the people, as was done, but the people could not intervene for the prophet. For it is written: If the people goes astray, the priest will pray for it. If the priest goes astray, who will ask for him? And yet, when he saw the people being slain, he offered himself to the striking angel, so that he himself might rather be struck for the people. That, not terrified even by the very sight of the dead, he offered himself to the sword, is more than if he had wished for the remission of the condition proposed. In the choice, then, he followed reason; in the grief, dutiful love.

17. But see the grace of the Lord, that he himself also turned aside from the condition proposed. Is it any crime of compassion that it threatens more and exacts less? He who keeps his promises in the reward of prizes retracts what he prescribed in the exacting of punishments. When he is angry, he puts off the guilty; when he has mercy, he hastens to absolve. He terrifies to correct, warns to amend, forestalls to pardon. Whence elsewhere too the prophet says of the Lord: The cup in the hand of the Lord is full of unmixed wine, mingled; nevertheless its dregs have not been emptied out. For terrifying the cup is full; for striking it is not emptied out. The cup was full when death for three days was ordered, but the mercy of God came to meet it and held the angel's hand before he emptied that cup. And that you may know that the cup is punishment or the sword, hear one saying: Take this cup of unmixed wine from my hand, and you shall make all the nations drink to whom I shall send you, and they shall vomit and go mad before the sword; and to Jerusalem it is said: You have drunk the cup of wrath from the hand of the Lord; for you have drunk the cup of ruin and emptied it, and there was none to console you. Jerusalem drank the measure, she who sinned beyond measure. The Church of the Christians does not know how to empty the cup of wrath, but she has emptied out ointment, she for whose sake Christ emptied himself, that it might be fragrant everywhere. From the hand of the Lord she received this gift, but the cup of death she does not know. I read of two cups, one of death, the other of life. Christ emptied the cup of death in his own blood and served a new cup, so that we may say: I will take the cup of salvation, the cup of the new testament, which is poured out for the remission of sins. This cup is poured out and its dregs are not found, because it cleanses every sin.

18. We have said how the Lord bends back his anger in avenging; let us say how in rewarding he forestalls our prayer, and teach it by an example. Hear that one of the two thieves saying to the Lord: Remember me, Lord, when you come into your kingdom. The Lord answered: Amen I say to you, today you will be with me in paradise. He was still asking that he remember him when he had come into the kingdom, and the Lord was already granting the heavenly kingdom. How swift a mercy! The prayer of the one asking is slower than the reward of the one rewarding.

19. That is why David kept to moderation and did not pray for remission, saying: Lord, do not rebuke me in your wrath, nor chasten me in your fury. Fury is called θυμὸς in Greek, and it is the onset of wrath. This, then, is what the Latin meant to express: "neither in wrath nor in the very onset of wrath rebuke me or chasten me". And chastening is instruction; in Greek it is called παίδευσις, of which it is said: Blessed is the man whom you instruct, Lord. For God is not open to passion, so as to be angry, since he is impassible; but because he avenges, he seems to be angry. It seems so to us, because we ourselves are accustomed to avenge with agitation. Yet men too are often found who, when they avenge, are not moved, but carry out vengeance and apply torments with the utmost patience. Why, then, should you wonder at it in God, if you recognize it at times in man? And that vengeance is wrath we said above, concerning the day of wrath. Indeed usage has it that we say of anyone punished that he has fallen under the wrath of the laws, not because the laws have wrath, but because he has fallen under the severity of the laws. So too elsewhere he says of the ten plagues of the Egyptians: He sent wrath upon them through evil angels, that is, vengeance; for the law does not know how to be angry, but the minister of the laws does. He, then, who is the author of the laws does not know how to be angry either, he whose will is to terrify, not to punish. Imitate, then, you emperors, the divine example, so that you may be more severe in establishing laws, merciful in exacting punishments. Let the severity of the laws restrain insolent audacity; let the mercy of princes withdraw the guilty from punishment. The prophet, then, recognizes his fault, sees his wounds, asks to be cured. He who wishes to be healed does not dread being rebuked; but he does not wish to be rebuked in fury, but in the word of God. The word of God is health; for so we read: He sent his word, and healed them. He does not wish to be instructed in wrath, but in teaching, as if you were to ask a physician not to cut your wound but to apply a medicine. He presses with the medicine, but does not cut; there is pain, but not beyond the measure of pain; it bites, but does not draw blood.

20. And he added: for your arrows are fixed in me. He seems to say the same as holy Job, but the two are different. For Job too says: For the arrows of the Lord are in my body, whose fury drinks my blood; when I begin to speak, they prick me. He complains of the wound of his body; this one laments the wounds of his soul. And perhaps this one does penitence for sin, while that one pleads the cause of human infirmity and, as a defender, so to speak, of our weakness, asks the help of the Creator as a remedy for his work. This one, then, prays, that one pleads as an advocate; and therefore this one's grief is more vehement, because the wounds of the soul are heavier than those of the flesh. That one says the arrows of the Lord are in his body, this one laments them as fixed; that one's blood is drunk, this one's is poured out; that one is pricked, this one is wounded; the hand of God touched that one, upon this one it has been made firm with heavy weight; this one bewails that his soul is filled with mockeries, that one that his body is filled with sores. And by the hand of God we understand the power of punishing. This hand scourged the king of the Egyptians for the wrong done to Abraham, for the attempt upon Sarah's chastity; this hand sank the chariots and horses and peoples of the Egyptians in the depth of the Red Sea; this hand weighed down the mind of King Saul, so that he hated the grace of his preserver, and for his transgression of the heavenly command, deserted by his allies, bereft too by the slaughter of his sons, conquered by his enemies — and no sight is more shameful for a king — he turned his own sword upon himself, lest he live as an old captive, surviving both his sons and his kingdom. David experienced this hand in himself and lamented in his children the incest of one and the parricide of the other; and, what is heavier for a devoted father than the ruin of his own safety, he wept at once for the disgrace of his offspring and for the downfall of dutiful love; for one of them was inflamed to incest out of a brother's affection, the other armed to parricide out of zeal for chastity. How the borders of the virtues mocked them in the gravest crimes! Would that either the one had not loved his sister or the other had not avenged her! At last he himself, driven from the borders of his country by his son, fled as an enemy the one he wished to have as his heir; he feared to conquer, lest he be conquered at the cost of his dutiful love.

21. But perhaps someone may say: how was the hand of God in parricide or incest, when that is the work of the enemy? Let us recognize, then, that even where the devil wounds, the arrows of the Lord are said to wound. For so we read that, when in the council of the angels the Lord, turning to the devil, praised holy Job his servant, so that the envious one and adversary of the human race might be burned — for the praise of a man of lower substance is the condemnation of him who was cast down from a higher state —, the devil answered that Job did not worship the Lord for nothing, since by God's nod he abounded in all things. But put forth, he says, your hand and touch all that he has; let us see whether he does not bless you to your face. And God gave the devil power to put forth his hand upon all that Job had. When this was done and holy Job remained in his fortitude, because Job was not moved either by the death of his sons or by the loss of his means, the Lord spoke again to the devil, mocking him, that he had scattered all that Job had had and killed his sons, yet had in no way been able to move him from the post of his virtue. And he answered: Whatever a man has he will give for his soul. But put forth, he says, your hand and touch his flesh and bones. And again he received power to put forth his hand upon his body, yet so as to guard his soul; and he covered holy Job with sores, when he had gone out from the Lord. We notice, then, that it is called the hand of the Lord where there is the temptation of man under the devil's assault, since Job too said that it was the hand of the Lord that had touched him, and said that the arrows of the pirates were the arrows of the Lord: and he delivered me, he says, into the hands of the unjust. So in his own case he settled the question: because, when the devil wounds, the arrows are the Lord's, who permitted the devil the power of wounding. Indeed, if you order your little servant to be beaten, is he not said to have been beaten by you, even if he is struck by another standing by? There is also this reason, that the Lord gives the tempter power for this purpose, that the disposition of men may be proved in temptations; persecution happens for this, that faith may shine, virtue excel, the inner mind be made manifest to all.

22. Temptation, then, penetrates the inner parts of man like an arrow, and like the sword of God which searches the inner things. And the sword is the word of God, strong and sharper than every sharpest sword. Of this sword hear one saying to Mary: And a sword shall pass through your own soul, that the thoughts of many hearts may be revealed. For by the word of God all things are revealed, in whose sight all things are naked and open. The soul itself is divided, the inner marrows and the thoughts are made manifest, nor, lastly, is there any creature, as Scripture says, that hides itself from his knowledge. Let us sell everything, then, so as to buy the Word, and let us store him up in our inmost parts.

23. Lastly, the devil himself testifies that a man gives all he has for his soul, and confesses that the price of an entire patrimony is not worthy of the redemption of one soul. Why do we spare a patrimony which even the devil himself judges cheap for salvation? I have said too little in saying "for salvation"; he testifies that it is little even for error. Indeed, All these, he says, I will give you, if you fall down and worship me, and he showed not only the riches of the world but also honours and kingdoms. If it costs so much that the devil be worshipped, how much must a Christian offer, to be raised up with Christ? But let us dismiss the devil like the goat of sending-away into the desert; for he is not a faithful upholder of the truth, even if at times he transforms himself into an angel of light.

24. The testimonies of the divine Scriptures abound for us, by which we are taught that there is nothing more precious in man than faith, that no patrimony is so great as to be compared with the price of our salvation and of our soul. By faith Abraham left his country and land and even the kin he saw, and followed him whom he did not see as though he beheld him; Moses too valued the price of his soul above all the riches of Egypt. Why do I speak of sublime men? Rahab the harlot, that foreigner from the world, yet thought that her soul should be redeemed not only by contempt of all she had but even by the perils of her life; she denied the spies of Jesus son of Nave [Joshua] to her fellow citizens when they sought them, and preferred to hide rather than betray men who were enemies of her country, yet envoys of faith. Not the threats of her fellow citizens, not the perils of wars, not the burning of her country, not the dangers of her own people terrified her. Learn, man, learn, Christian, how you ought to follow the true Jesus, when a woman despised all that was hers and followed Jesus in figure because of the likeness of the name. Whence Solomon finely said: A man's riches are the redemption of his soul. Redeem, then, your soul. Money is cheap, but it becomes precious through faith; it is cheap when it is hoarded, precious when it is dispersed; for thus it is written: He has dispersed, he has given to the poor, his righteousness remains for ever.

25. If, then, you are such that you can despise not only all that is yours, but even your very flesh, for righteousness, than which no possession can be richer — for a clean man is a precious possession —, even if the higher rivers have shut you in, you cross over; even if the Lord has given power to tempt you, he nevertheless commands the devil himself to guard your soul, according to what is written: that you may destroy the enemy and the defender; for he tempts as an adversary, defends as a servant. For it is written: and the unicorn will be willing to serve you. For he serves who does not carry out what he wishes of his own will, but of necessity obeys imperial commands against his will. See the depth of Christ, who turned back upon the devil the price of his malice. He forces us to do what we hate; for not what I will do I do, as the Apostle said, but what I hate, that I do. The Lord turned it back upon him, so that he too often does not do what he wills, but does what he hates; indeed he guards the soul which he wishes to storm. We condemn the corruption of the flesh and follow it, like that widow who made void the faith promised to her husband and afterwards wishes to marry, which before she fled. He is an enemy to the saints and is employed as their defender, so that he may be punished the more, in that he who desires to harm does not dare to harm. And how much more tolerable it is to love the virtues, even if you cannot fulfil them, than to hate the virtues, which you cannot harm!

26. There is no health, he says, in my flesh from the face of your wrath. Isaiah has explained this place for us: You were angry, and we sinned. Yet what is the face of the Lord's wrath? Is it perhaps this, that the eyes of the Lord are upon those who do evil? For if the eyes of the Lord are upon the righteous, how is the prophet made weak by the face of God's wrath? Whence consider this too, which David himself said later on: For you have rescued me from all tribulations, and your eye has looked upon my enemies. For as it looks upon the good deeds of the righteous, so it also detects the hidden things of sinners. Unless perhaps you refer this to Christ, who was rescued from all by whom he was being crushed, when he stripped himself from the people of the Jews, who crushed him with frequent sacrileges and daily impieties, and called his enemies to grace, those whom the eye of God saw and loved. Since, then, God is loving, there must be no despair at all; even if he is angry, he pardons; even if he strikes, he heals; even if he delivers the flesh to destruction, he saves the spirit. Do not fear, then, the infirmity of the flesh, because the saint, when he is weak, is more powerful.

27. But what is it that he says: there is no peace for my bones from the face of my sins? What are these bones? Are they of the soul or of the body? But the care for bodily pain would not be so great where the soul too is labouring, since it belongs to a saint to wish the flesh to be scourged for the sake of the soul, as Paul too scourged himself, lest his claim be rejected. There are certain bones of the inner man, just as there are his other members too, the eyes and nostrils of the mind, as Job said: the divine spirit which is in my nostrils. There are, then, bones too, by which a certain framework of charity is formed. Whence Adam too said of the partner of his charity and co-heir of the grace of life: This now is bone of my bones and flesh of my flesh; which the Apostle interpreted, saying: This sacrament is great, but I speak in Christ and in the Church. And who would doubt that the sacrament of Christ and the Church is not carnal but spiritual, since every good man, even in marriage itself, is held not by the beauty of the flesh but by that of virtue, and loves in his wife her character, not her use? Indeed, hear that he speaks not according to the flesh but according to inner virtue: My bone is not hidden, which you made in secret. It is virtue, then, not flesh, which knows the hidden things of God the Father.

28. There is, then, no peace for the virtues of the soul, when our sins rush together before our eyes and pour themselves over our minds. And this the chosen teacher of the Gentiles interpreted well in the second epistle to the Corinthians, saying: For even when we had come into Macedonia, our flesh had no rest, but we were afflicted in all things; fightings without, fears within. The sins of the Macedonians troubled him; how much more do each one's own sins disturb every one of us, so that there can be no rest for us! Our own guilt is a heavier adversary to us: it disquiets the idle, afflicts the healthy, saddens the glad, unsettles the calm, harries the gentle, rouses the sleeping. We are guilty without an accuser, we are tortured without a torturer, we are bound without chains, we are slaves without a seller. Whence Scripture too says: By your sins you have been sold. These, then, are the sins which are always against us, as the prophet said: They have sold us and they lord it over us. A slave who is sold goes out of his former servitude to pass to another master; we do not lay down the yoke of the former ones, and we are bowed under new sins.

29. Whence the saint groaned, saying: for my iniquities have risen above my head, as a heavy burden they have weighed heavily upon me, that is: my iniquities have passed over my head and tower above me, so as to press down my senses; for the eyes indeed of the wise man are in his head. And therefore Nabal was evil and hard, because malice and iniquity barred his senses. Whence he could not bear even the word of Abigail, but his heart hardened and he fell down as one sick. Or see whether this is not the head of which the Apostle says that he who is puffed up by the mind of the flesh does not hold it. And this head is Christ, because the head of every man is Christ. This is the head which through the joinings and bindings of the whole people grows to the increase of God, because in all of us Christ rises up through each of his members. So when our sins weigh heavy and we are pressed down to earthly things by a kind of leaden talent of iniquity, let us burst their bonds and cast away their yoke from us, so that we may lift the eyes of our mind and hear him saying: Come to me, all you who labour and are burdened, and I will refresh you. Indeed avarice burdened Egypt, money troubled the merchandise of the Ethiopians, as it is written: Egypt has laboured, and the merchandise of the Ethiopians, and the Sabeans, lofty men, will pass over to you. Egypt laboured before it knew the truth, but it labours no longer, now that it has passed over to Christ. The Sabean men, who before fled, follow willingly, because the bonds of charity hold them, which are stronger than adamant. Finely too that word of Isaiah fits this place: Every head in pain and every heart in sorrow. From the feet up to the head there is neither wound nor scar nor stroke with inflammation. For iniquity is inflamed when it lords it over our head, lifting itself up and occupying the place, so that Christ, the teacher of penitence, may not hold it. These iniquities have no small force, if you consider that man of iniquity who is to come according to the working of Satan in all power and signs and lying wonders and every seduction of iniquity, whom the Apostle has shown we must beware of, because he will receive the working of error, so that the faithful may be proved and the faithless judged.

30. Rightly, then, set under iniquities, and, what is worse, his own, he says that there have grown foul and have festered for him his scars from the face of his folly, because the remedy followed too slowly upon one who was lifting the burdens of iniquities. Yet health was restored to holy Job too, who scraped the pus of his sores, covered with sores from his feet to his head; and Lazarus the poor man, who lay at the rich man's gates while the dogs licked his sores, was carried away by the angels from the stench of his scars and placed in Abraham's bosom. There is, then, in holy David hope and a remedy of health, because the wounds of his sins smell not of ointment but of stench, and because he is afflicted and bowed down by them and does not delight in them. Look now at some wanton youth, notably lustful, who spends his life in debaucheries, lying, after the fashion of that rich man, in fine linen and purple and feasting splendidly every day, whose pavements swim with wine, whose ground is covered with flowers and strewn with fish-bones, whose dining-rooms are filled with the scents of various burnt incenses: how he thinks himself blessed and judges that he smells sweet, though he bears grievous and long-standing wounds of his soul and runs with corrupt blood, yet perceives no stench from his scar. He has nostrils blocked with mud and cannot say: the divine spirit which is in my nostrils. Therefore that rich man could not find the remedy of salvation; the poor man found it; in the end the one is in the underworld in torment, the other in rest.

31. Holy David the prophet, then, also found the remedy of perpetual salvation, he who confessed that the wounds of his soul stank to him and said that his own scars had festered for him from the face of his folly. But there is also a folly which, through the foolishness of preaching, brings salvation to those who believe. The prophet, then, already with the spirit of the Gospel, flees the wisdom of this world, by which God is not known, which covers its wounds and does not reveal them to the Lord. Better, then, the folly which has eyes, to see its sores, than the wisdom which has none. And therefore, admonished by the spiritual regard of his folly, so great a king declares himself afflicted with miseries, so that he may find the remedy of penitence, which Judas, who possessed a field from the wages of iniquity, could not find.

32. With miseries, he says, I am afflicted and bowed down unto the end; all the day I went about saddened. Unto what end does he say he was bowed down: unto the lawful end of penitence, or further, so that we may understand it mystically, unto Christ, who is the end of the Law, who suffered himself to be stoned, suffered his dead body to be wounded? But those wounds gave off no stench of penitence, but the fragrance of grace. Indeed no corruption of death, as from other men, but a fountain of eternal life sprang from his wound, as Scripture teaches us, saying: And water shall leap with delight from the fountains of salvation. Water, then, leapt from the wound, that we might drink salvation; all the sinners of the earth shall drink, that they may lay down their sins. Look at each point. Christ was afflicted with miseries, to make blessed the men who were set in miseries. Let no one call him miserable who is righteous, because he himself said: No one will make you miserable; he was bowed down, so that we might be raised up; he was saddened, to gladden us, according to what is written: For if I sadden you, who is there to gladden me but he who is saddened by me? He, then, who is saddened by the Lord Jesus Christ himself gladdens Christ and is himself gladdened by Christ. Therefore we too, then, recognize that satisfaction must be made by us not carelessly. Let us be bowed down unto the end, that is, bringing to Christ not only faith but also perseverance in our sufferings, and let us rejoice in our sufferings, as Christ too rejoiced in his sufferings. Those he took on for his little servants; let us undergo them for the Lord. This, then, is the end: that I may fill up, he says, what is lacking of the tribulations of Christ in my flesh for his body, which is the Church, of which I have been made a minister. We see what we must take on, we who take on the priestly ministry: that we ought to bear the sufferings of the body bravely not only for ourselves but also for the Church of the Lord. But David added the sufferings of the soul too.

33. Indeed he himself says: for my soul is filled with mockeries, and there is no health in my flesh. I am bent down and humbled exceedingly, I roared with the groaning of my heart. And before you is all my desire, and my groaning is not hidden from you. My heart is troubled, and my strength has forsaken me, and the light of my eyes is not with me. We notice, then, that the labour of the soul is greater than that of the flesh. Understand, then, with what mockeries the soul is filled, in that in temptations the devil seems to mock it and insult it as one labouring grievously. Symmachus, however, says "loins", in which are the seed-beds of human generation. Whence the wisdom of God commands you to have your loins girded, lest your chastity be loosened. Indeed, that you may know that in the loins are the stirrings of lust, which are mostly roused by the devil when he strives to scatter the members of Christ and make them members of a harlot, behold, he says, his strength is in his loins and his power in his womb. In the loins of the man is the seed, but in the womb of the woman. The devil, then, mocks in these, so as to bring about adultery, incest, fornications. And therefore he who fornicates sins against his own body, not outside the body: first because the flesh is weakened, which melts away in lusts and does not keep the restraints of chastity; then because the seeds, which ought to serve the procreation of offspring, work defilement of the body, not the fruit of posterity. Those things, then, are called mockeries, perhaps because in them the heat of the body often deludes without the fulfilment of intercourse. Whence the Seventy rightly spoke of mockeries of the soul, because the devil, mocking someone's disposition, strives to pour out his strength in vain, to empty his virtue, to loosen his fortitude. And therefore he says elsewhere: and my reins are loosened. In this the blessed Apostle also strengthens us, that we ought to fear neither the mockeries of the soul nor the infirmity of the flesh, because Christ is the power of both our soul and our body, he who cures the sick as a physician and strengthens the weak as the strength of all. Indeed, even if our outward man is corrupted, yet the inward is renewed from day to day.

34. Wearied, then, by these mockeries of the soul, holy David had bent himself down and humbled himself exceedingly, not so as to give place to the iniquities that set themselves above his head, as we have said, or to yield to the enemy; he teaches later on that one must not yield to him, saying: How much evil the enemy has wrought in your saints! And those who hated you boasted in the midst of your feast. They set up their signs as signs, and I did not know, as on the way above the summit. As in a wood of trees they have cut down its doors with axes. When my enemies, he says, were setting the signs of their iniquity above the summit — what is the summit but your head, where the senses are, where Christ is wisdom? — I did not know, that is, I did not acquiesce, I did not consent to them and join the verdict of my thought to theirs. Whence elsewhere too: I did not know an evil word, and the wicked who turned aside from me I did not know. Indeed, though Christ knows all things, he did not know sin; for he knows the things that are his, that is, the things that belong to the virtues, not those that belong to outrages. And therefore Scripture says to you: The Lord knew those who are his, but to the wicked he says: Depart from me, because I do not know you, and through Jeremiah he spoke, saying: As a lamb I was led to be sacrificed, and I did not know. How did he not know what he had foretold would come? But I did not know, he says, their thoughts, their malice; I do not wish to know the things that belong to blood; indeed I will not gather their assemblies from bloods. I blot out their iniquities and do not remember them; I do not wish to know the things I came to remit. I did not know the wood that is to burn, nor the things set in a heap of wood, whether straw or hay, so that the flame may consume them the more quickly as its fuel. I wish to know those things which remain, so that they may receive the fruit of their reward. I wish to know those things which have been built upon the foundation, so that they may be improved, not those on the way, where passers-by plunder them. I wish to know those things which belong to the vine, those sure trees which bear fruit, not those which, gathered into a bundle, are made ready for burning; that is: I am not the arbiter and partner of things that are perishable. Not, then, fellowship in some thing, but criminal fellowship is at fault.

35. And for what reason did he not know those who boast in unfruitful and fragile things? Because with evil works and the axes of their iniquity they overturn the entrances of the faithful soul, and with cruel deeds they split the vestibules of the devout mind, so that Christ cannot enter through them. But each one ought to guard his gates and doors, so that, when Christ has come and knocked, the ministering powers which go before and run ahead may say: Lift up your gates, you princes, and be lifted up, you eternal gates. Truly princes are those who have ruled themselves well, so that Christ may have entrance into their souls. Like this too is that saying of Solomon: If the spirit of him who has power rises up against you, do not leave your place, because care will soften great offences. For diligence and faith hold the higher place, unbelief the lower. Therefore let good diligence rise up to you, which shuts out the snares of the enemy and takes away sin, so that the malice of him who has power cannot harm. In the Songs1 Ecclesiastes saw this malice under the sun, that is, in this world; whence Scripture also called this place the one under the sun. He saw it, then, under the sun, not above the sun, where the peace of the angels is, or the holiness of the heavenly powers. Or perhaps he meant under the sun of iniquity; for the devil presides over malice, Christ over the virtues. And so, to overcome the spirit of him who had power, which he received for a time, David bent himself down in prayer, bending his neck like a circle, and humbled himself in his entreaties; for the prayer of one who humbles himself pierces the clouds and passes beyond the heights of the elements, so as to draw near to Christ.

36. And he roared with the groaning of his heart. He roars who utters only a groan, not words. This, then, is that glorious prayer, when the Spirit intercedes for us with unutterable groanings, as the vessel of election asserted. This is the groaning which God does not despise, he who does not despise the young servant bereft of his father, nor the widow, if she pours out her speech.

37. The Apostle, then, puts groaning before all things as the sign of the Holy Spirit, as we read.2 Let us, then, guard the doors of our soul, so that the entrance of our confession may not be split by the axes of much speaking. Let nothing insolent and proud come out of our mouth, and let us not lift the axe or the hammer, which do not enter into the church of the Lord. Shut the doors when you pray, so that the wicked spirit may not thrust himself in, to extort sin there where we wish to acquire the gain of devotion. He who prays thus, his groaning is not hidden from the Lord. Indeed the groaning of Saint Peter was both known and heard, when he wept most bitterly; surely it was not the tears that were bitter, but the affection that shed them. Even in unbelieving Ahab groaning would have found grace, had not his abiding envy heaped up the offence. For it is not a careless groaning that is commended, but one that has conversion; for it is written: If, being converted, you groan, you will be saved.

38. Let us also set all our desire before the Lord. What is desire? For I have read of the desire of a good thing: With desire I have desired to eat this pasch with you, and: The hart desires the fountains of waters. But I think that he who does penitence, who afflicts himself, ought to enumerate not his good things but the sins he has committed. Indeed, as I think, we are taught this by those verses in which he says: God, I will declare my life to you; you have set my tears in your sight. For he does not announce his life as innocent, where tears are shed, which usually undertake the embassy for sins. Unless perhaps we understand it thus: that, even if someone is innocent, he cannot be secure, since he has daily contests against most grievous enemies, and therefore, even where a glad conscience shines, the fight is yet tearful. In view of these verses, then, we can take it as concupiscence rather than desire; for the Greek has put ἐπιθυμίαν, and both concupiscence and desire are called ἐπιθυμία, but concupiscence is said of both good and evil: My soul has coveted and fainted for the courts of the Lord, for good, but in the Law otherwise: For I did not know concupiscence, unless the Law said: You shall not covet, surely for evil. Indeed the words that follow teach us that, taking occasion, sin through the commandment works every concupiscence in the disposition of man. Yet we can take it thus: "I set all my desire before you", that is, I make plain to you what I desire to obtain, so that it seems it should be referred to the series of petitions, not to a boasting of virtues; for boasting is insolent even in the blameless, entreaty praiseworthy even in a sinner.

39. The prophet also added this, that, since his heart was troubled, his strength had forsaken him. A great danger, if the heart is harried, with which we believe unto righteousness. But yet, as in great dangers of sickness, if pain is felt and the sense of it is expressed, a remedy of health is shown — for to feel pain is more tolerable than not to feel pain; for it is one thing, the mark of a quickening still remaining, if you feel pain, another, the sign of final death, if there is no sense of pain —, so the heart too shows signs of health when it recognizes the causes of its disturbance. Indeed David's heart was troubled against Nabal, a pestilent man, and he found grace. Abigail came to meet him, to soften the stirring of his indignation and to receive the sanctification of a blessing. But Nabal, with his hard and rigid heart, could not bear his wife's word, and stunned he grew stiff and fell into death.

40. But what strength forsook the prophet, that of the flesh or that of the mind? If that of the flesh, health is certainly not to be despaired of; for power is made perfect in infirmity. Of this a certain arbiter and fighter in the wrestling of mind and body discoursed at no small length, knowing how to temper the contest of each, so as to restrain the flesh, if he saw it was the stronger, lest it lead the mind captive in the law of sin. And so he held back the flesh, but joined to it the power of the mind; thus he deserved to reach the crown. So, as the prophet says, if the strength of the flesh forsook him, the strength of the mind prevailed. But if we take it that the strength of the mind forsook him, it is no wonder if in most grievous temptations, his heart troubled, a man thinks he is forsaken by the strength of his mind. Let him teach you himself, saying: My strength and my praise is the Lord. It was the Lord, then, he sought, and therefore, so as not to deserve to be forsaken, he thought he must seek him the more often, and wherever he wavered, he thought himself forsaken. So the apostles rouse the sleeping one, lest he sleep to them, not because they believed he had fallen asleep. So Elisha, to use the examples of earlier men: Where, he says, is the God of Elijah?, not that he thought him absent, but because he sought his presence in benefits. So Jeremiah followed him as a physician, saying: Heal me, Lord, and I shall be healed; save me, and I shall be saved. I have not laboured, following after you. So the Church sought him in the Song of Songs and asked the daughters of Jerusalem to rouse love; she sought to find him whom she held by her love and never felt absent. So, then, David too did not complain that he had been forsaken by Christ, but judged that with him present he could not be troubled.

41. Indeed, that you may know it is rather to be understood of Christ, he added: and the light of my eyes is not with me. What is the true light of all but Christ, of whom John says: He was the true light which enlightens every man coming into this world, because it is he who enlightens both the eyes of the body and the gaze of the mind? Let us ask, then, that he may always pour his light into us and always be with us, as he was with David. And therefore David dared to say: For with you is the fountain of life, and in your light we shall see light. He certainly, as a prophet, had seen the great light; for us let his lamp shine, so that we may not go astray; and the lamp is the word, as the true light is the Word which enlightens the whole world. Indeed, Your word, Lord, is a lamp to my feet; and that lamp the prophet too sought, not a lantern, but a lamp. John found the lamp and showed it, when the persecutor came with a lantern and pointed it out; for a lantern has a light enclosed, not free. The light of the Jews is like a lamp under a bushel or torches under a veil; it can be seen, but is not seen. But we, with unveiled face, behold the eternal glory of the Lord, so as to be re-formed into his image from glory to glory through the Holy Spirit. The crowd of persecutors, then, came with lanterns, and so their eyes could not see the enclosed light; it came with torches, which have more murky smoke than brightness in their light. Indeed it is the custom for the corpses of the dead to burn by torches. The Jews, then, who persecuted the author of salvation, were already bringing burnings upon themselves. They came with arms, signifying that they, who refused the peace of the Lord, would perish by arms at the hands of the Romans, to the destruction of the whole city and temple.

42. My friends and my neighbours drew near against me and stood, and my neighbours stood afar off; and those who sought my soul used violence, and those who sought out evils for me spoke vanity and meditated deceit all the day. But I, like one deaf, did not hear, and was like one dumb who does not open his mouth; and I became like a man who does not hear and has no rebukes in his mouth. Let those who discuss these things subtly see who they are. To me, especially in this first verse, the Lord's sentence seems to be the one to hold, that in temptations a man's enemies become even those of his own household. This, then, holy David confesses purely and sincerely and with grief. For it is true grief and the confession of the inner heart, when all the things are enumerated by which the secret entrails of the mind are pricked with most bitter affection and exasperated with household gall. The prophet, then, laments that he has been attacked by his friends and neighbours, by whom he surely ought not to have been attacked but helped.
