This surely agrees with the complaint of holy Job, because he too rebuked those three kings, comforters of evils, who brought greater contests upon him when they had come together to console him for friendship's sake; and this we surely must beware of. For consolation ought to be gentle, not harsh, so as rather to soften grief and calm the fever than to stir up agitation. Surely the medicine of the body itself may teach us, which is accustomed to apply softer medicines to inflamed wounds, by which to relieve pain. And so wounds are first fomented, afterwards cut, lest the very hardness offend and the incision exasperate the wound. How much more, then, it befits us to take care that, when we come to console, we do not speak lightly, not carelessly! For seven days Job was silent, his friends were silent, and they would not have spoken had not Job in his grief burst into speech. For you must seek where to begin, lest your consolation offend in the very speech; even silence itself is medicine, and he who is swift in speech wounds the more. Why do you wonder if he wounds another, when he is often wounded himself, because in much speaking he does not escape sin? Indeed, if the physician waits for the time of healing, so that the aids of medicine may be applied when the sicknesses have been digested, lest the sickness, still bitter and unripe, as they assert, resist the remedies of cure and be unable to feel their benefit, how much more it befits us to explore, so that the healing word may come forth from us opportunely, so as to seem not to kindle mourning but to soothe it! The force of grief presses, the heart of an afflicted woman is crushed, who has lost her husband or sons by an untimely death: why do you hurry, when she will not hear you unless her grief cools down? Often we have seen quarrels stirred up by consolations. You have come to grieve, not to quarrel. The very order of your speech must be sought, lest you incur sin before God while you desire to console a man; lest, when you say: "Hear these things", and many others which profit nothing, the answer come back to you: Hear, comforters of evils; lest in another's mourning you bring in the contest of garrulous disputation; lest you fail to come when you should, lest you come and your speech be too harsh. Indeed, let holy Job teach you what is said of such men: At once the most grievous temptations came upon me; they surrounded me, considering me. My brothers departed from me; they knew strangers rather than me; my friends have become merciless. This, then, is the natural sense of the holy prophet David too: he complains that he was attacked by his friends, abandoned by his neighbours.

43. But it would not be foreign even to a mystical sense of devotion to take it that he spoke of the angels, who spread their guard before those who fear the Lord, to rescue them from temptations which they cannot bear. How, then, do those stand afar off who have been assigned to help? But they do not separate themselves; rather, he who is pressed by temptations thinks those far away whom he wishes to be nearer to him, and supposes that they are holding back, while they await the time of helping at the nod of their commander, who has ordered his athlete to contend the longer, so that he might conquer the more gloriously. And this seems to fit better with what follows, because when the angels relaxed their protective watch, the enemies lay in wait, seeking to find in his soul something to harm it. For then power for a heavier temptation is permitted them, when a heavier fault of the soul is found. Whence you have in the book of Kingdoms that saying of King Ahab to Elijah, when the prophet rebuked him vehemently and announced death to him: Have you found, he says, me? And Elijah answered: I have found you, because you have done evil in the sight of the Lord. You see, then, that kings must not rashly be wronged, either by the prophets of God or by priests, if there are no graver sins for which they ought to be rebuked. But where the sins are graver, there it seems the priest should not spare, so that they may be corrected by just rebukes.

44. Yet David says in this place that they seem to have found nothing, and that therefore his enemies spoke vanity, since they found nothing that they could speak for the truth. "Or surely because, even if I have sinned, I was nevertheless cleansing my sins with the grief of penitence; in this they spoke with me in deceit, to confound me with reproach and call me back from conversion." And see whether he did not rather mean this: that they sought out evils of his, but, when they wished to accuse him, were forestalled, because he himself had laid bare his wounds, being his own accuser, and that therefore the force of their accusation was weakened, and they spoke things empty of truth, which could no longer harm one who had confessed his guilt.

45. "Shut out, then, from the odium of accusation, they used deceit, so as to rise up", he says, "against me and insult me, to goad me to some agitation; and I, seeing their deceit, like one deaf, did not hear". Consider the force of the word. He did not say "because I pretended not to hear what they said", but he said "I did not hear", and that by the intention of his mind he shut out the voice of the speaker and did not open his mouth, like one dumb. Blessed is he who can have such great virtue that, provoked, he is not angry, and, stirred, he will not avenge himself. Enemies do this to provoke to wrath: they curse, that we may curse; they accuse, that we may throw back the accusation; they revile, to rouse us to return the insult. Whence Peter finely set down of the Lord Jesus in his epistle that when he was cursed he did not curse, when he suffered he did not threaten. Desiring to shape the ordering of his life to his likeness and image, the righteous man, when accused, is silent; when hurt, forgives; when provoked, lets it pass and does not open his mouth, so as to imitate him who was led like a lamb to the sacrifice and does not open his mouth, and who, though he could have something to answer, prefers to be silent rather than speak. For the Lord Jesus himself truly was silent when he was accused, and when he was struck did not strike back. Indeed, when struck he answered: If I have spoken evil, bear witness of the evil; if well, why do you strike me? See how, as if truly weak and as one who could not avenge himself, he spoke with a kind of childlike affection. So then you too, if you have the means to refute your accuser, will do better to be silent, lest by refuting in return you betray your agitation; for it is better to let an injury pass than to avenge it when you refute. Good is the dumb man who does not know how to speak evil, from whose mouth crime does not know how to come out. This is the truly blessed dumb man, who, when he is silent, speaks within himself: "The Lord gives me a tongue of instruction, when I ought to speak a word." Such things Zechariah spoke within himself when he had become dumb; and truly, because speaking had not profited him, he became dumb so as not to speak, and was heard by Christ so that he might speak. Indeed he wrote what Christ would hear, and received back the voice which Christ granted, and added the grace which he did not have before, to prophesy him whose commands he had not believed before. Why do I speak of the Lord of all powers, when the woman Susanna, not troubled even by the infirmity of her sex, uttered not a word to those by whom she knew she had been consigned to the peril of death? She was accused and was silent, she was led to death and covered herself with silence, so as not to lay bare her modesty. Yet within herself she spoke to God, who heard her the more when she was silent; had she wished to speak, she would perhaps not have been heard.

46. And you too, then, who have resolved to make satisfaction for your offences to the Lord your God, purge yourself to him alone with your inner heart, look upon him who can wash away sins. He who has thought that you should be rebuked is helping you. Indeed, when David was being cursed and his captain Abishai wished to avenge his injury, David said to him: Let him alone, that he may curse, since the Lord has spoken to him, so that he may see my humiliation, and the Lord repay me good for this cursing. You see, then, that you are helped by those who revile you, so that the Lord may hear you and remit your sin. For since you ought yourself to be your own accuser, and to embitter your outrages and offer yourself to punishment, how can you wish to deny what is cast up to you? Penitence seeks patience; patience softens great offences. How are you angry with others, you who are yourself guilty before your own conscience? How are you stirred, when you ought to be an object of pity? He who is rebuked, and, what is more, by himself, ought to care for his own wounds, not wound another. No one heals himself by wounding another. Physician, heal yourself. If a physician, how much more ought a guilty man first to heal himself! You confess your sin and you profess yourself another's physician: even if what you throw back belongs to the truth, it does not belong to the time; for to the sinner indeed God said: Why do you recount my righteous deeds? You have claimed the right to rebuke from the Law, you who yourself have stood against the Law. Why do you lose the time for tears? Why, by disputing, do you either hear or speak vain words, when it is written for you: You shall not receive a vain report, when you have read in the Gospel that judgement must be undergone for every idle word? Even if another speaks, be silent; even if another reviles, stop your ear.

47. David conquered his adversaries by keeping silent, and, because he became as one dumb, he received his voice back, for, turned to the Lord, he spoke, saying: For in you, Lord, have I hoped; you will hear me, Lord my God. For I said: Lest at any time my enemies exult over me. Look at each point. David was silent, his enemies spoke; they provoked him so that he too might say something. They said: "Let us hear your voice"; he, silent, spoke within himself: "What need is there for them to hear these things, which cannot profit them? In you, Lord, have I hoped; to you alone I speak; hear me, you who can hearken. I have always asked of you that my enemies may not at any time exult over me, because, even if I have sinned, you forgive sin; even if I have fallen, you raise up, so that those who rejoice in others' sins may have nothing to exult in." For we who have sinned more have gained more, because your grace makes us more blessed than our innocence. We have this sense also in the book of Micah the prophet: Do not rejoice over me, my enemy, because I have fallen; but I shall rise again. The fall of infirmity is not grievous, if there is not also a resolve of the will not to rise from it. Have the will to rise: he is present who can make you rise again.

48. David, then, spoke in his heart, asking to be heard by the Lord and that his enemies might not exult over him, asking also that he might remain in the resolve of his conversion, lest, while the steps of his resolve, the feet, as it were, of his soul, are shaken, his adversaries should speak against him things proud and full of boasting, as those do who desire to insult. Yet, even if he is shaken as a man, he says he is ready for the scourges, so as to pay for his error at least by punishment. Even if the scourges of the Lord cease, he says that he is nevertheless scourged by his own grief, so that the fault, which good confession has forestalled, may find nothing to condemn. This, then, is what he says: and while my feet are shaken, they have spoken great things against me, because they are so ready for the mockeries of insult that at the shaking of my feet they had their boasting ready. Or surely thus: while my feet are shaken, thinking that I would fall, they have already spoken proud and boastful things.

49. But yet, because he himself said later on that his feet were almost moved, lest any scruple arise from this, consider that here we are taught the disposition of penitence, while there the opinion of that error is excluded, that the riches and more prosperous success of the wicked ought to move us. And a habit of expression of this kind is found in the divine Scriptures. For so elsewhere too: When the Lord turned back the captivity of Zion, we became as those consoled, although the saint wishes to be proved not in the display of words but in the power of the Spirit. The sense, then, must always be looked to by us, a sense which frequent translation, from Hebrew into Greek and from Greek into Latin, commonly weakens as well.

50. For all the foregoing, then, a fine remedy is that he is ready for the scourges and offers himself to the Lord, to bear whatever scourges have pleased God. I read indeed that holy David chose the kind of scourge which he would bear with equanimity; but he chose because the necessity of choosing one of three conditions was laid upon him. But where it is not commanded, the little servant of God is ready for everything, whether he undergoes bodily sickness or flight from the face of the enemy or the death of his sons, whom he does not fear to send on ahead, because he can receive them back undaunted. For he is aware that, if he has been punished here with temporal punishment, he can lighten the penalty of perpetual affliction in the future. He asks, then, that his petition be received and that he himself be chastised, so that he may be received; for the Lord chastises indeed every son whom he receives.

51. If you see your little servant confessing his own sins and offering himself to punishment of his own accord, you are moved, you pardon: and do you distrust the compassion of the Lord? The judge himself, who in very many cases may not refrain from the sword, because he serves the laws, can yet make a gift of the penalties; and do you still tremble at what you should ask of the Lord of the laws and author of mercy, whose will is law and who has the right of granting, if only you ask that your sins be granted to you, and do not consider your honours or blush before your friends, lest you seem to have bent your dignity? The friend of God, the prophet of God, the king chosen by God himself and anointed to the kingdom offered himself of his own accord to the scourges and did not blush; and do you blush? That bashfulness will not help you much when you come to the judgement of God; but you will repent of that shame when, set in the sight not only of men but also of angels and all the heavenly powers, you begin not to deny your own sins. How will you excuse yourself, when you have committed so much? You will plead the infirmity of your condition, because no one is without sin. It will be answered to you: "You ought, then, to have repented; I had given a remedy, why did you refuse it?" You will add your shame, that you blushed for your honours. He will say: "If you blushed for me before your friends, I too will blush for you before my Father who is in heaven. Learn that what is written is true, that there is a bashfulness that brings sin. David set his grief in his sight, that he might never forget it; David himself declared his iniquity; David thought of his sin, not of his riches; David did not blush to review his sins, so as not to blush for them when set in my judgement: and did you blush? My little servant Job would not be in rest today, if he had blushed before those three kings his friends, nor David himself, if he had blushed to confess his own offences. Since, then, he did not blush to open his sins to me, neither will I blush to unlock my secrets to him. And since each of them did not blush to put the price of his deeds in my power, to commit himself to my judgement and will, neither will I blush for such little servants, so as to call them friends who have been eager to do my will. And therefore, since they were before in sorrow, they are now in consolation; you, because you were in delights, are now in torments. A great chaos is between you, so that neither their grace can pass over to you nor your punishment to them." Do you hear, then, what David says? Hear, while you are allowed to correct and amend; if you amend here, you will rest there. Let not the sweet things of the world and the pleasant things of this age delight you; they commonly vomit up heavy gall. He who chose the death of the saints rather than the life of the wicked did not seek pleasant things when set in kingship.

52. My enemies, he says, live and have been made strong over me, and those who hate me unjustly have been multiplied; those who repay evil for good detracted from me, because I followed close after righteousness. But how much more illustrious is he who died daily, so as to give life to his people, and offered his body to the wounds of death, as he himself says: in deaths often! For an honourable death is the redemption even of a discoloured life, or the firm support of an innocent one, and the issue of death is in the hand of life; and therefore the Apostle preferred to die daily, so as to prove the merit of his life; for it is written: Do not praise a man in his life. For those who live die; those who die will rise again. He, then, is nearer to salvation who dies so as to rise again than he who lives so as to die. And who is it who dies daily but he who carries about the death of the Lord Jesus in his flesh, so that all sins may die to him? David's enemies were indeed made strong and multiplied in this world; but there is no adequate firm support except his who is made strong in Christ. Indeed those are made strong in the world who hate the righteous man unjustly; not, then, a just hatred, but an unjust one, since they hated unjustly.

53. But see the difference. Later on he says: who hate me gratuitously; here he has: who hate me unjustly. But there it is in the person of Christ, here in his own person; where he speaks in the person of Christ, he is hated gratuitously, where in his own, unjustly. For a man can be not open to some one wound — say, of injustice, intemperance, unchastity — but be open to other wounds; but in Christ there could be no cause by which he could receive the wound of any sin, immune as he was from guilt, untouched by offence and unstained by vice. But there are those who think that both psalms run their course in the person of Christ, who was making satisfaction to the Father for our sins. Here, then, he said that he was attacked against righteousness, there against grace.

54. And he does well to add, to prove that he was attacked unjustly: because I followed close after righteousness. How great the force of the word in the addition of this syllable, that he said he had "subsecutum" [followed close after] righteousness, not "secutum" [followed]! For he who follows close after is nearer than he who follows, as subpar [nearly equal] is nearer than inpar [unequal], and an heir's coming is named successio [succession] rather than accessio [accession].

55. And yet, although he follows close after righteousness, he thinks it belongs not to his own virtue but to heavenly grace, if he is not forsaken by Christ; and so he prays the more earnestly, saying: Do not forsake me, Lord; my God, do not depart from me, that is: men have forsaken me, my friends have attacked one dear to them, my neighbours have not drawn near. They fled me as one dead and abhorred me, because I desired to confess my sins to you, because I offered myself to your scourges to be wounded, because I chose the scars of wounds above the banquets of kings and the boasting of the powerful. Do you alone not forsake me; do you cleave to your little servant, you who raise up the needy from the earth and lift up the poor from the dunghill. Supported by your company, I shall reckon myself more thronged than if peoples were courting me. My scars have indeed festered, but still I desire the scars of your stripes, which are drawn over wounds once healed, so that afterwards no sore appears. Good are the scars of triumphal wounds, in which the victors of this earthly battle glory: how much more illustrious are the wounds which are seen to have been received for faith and the glory of your name! This is the scar which opens heaven, acquires the kingdom, finds immortality. This, then, brothers, is the blessed scar, since blessed are those who have washed their robes in their own blood. So the flesh of death began to be a robe of glory, the flesh in which Paul too, the chosen one of God, was once in peril, had he not asked to be freed from the body of this death, as we read.

56. And therefore let us who are in this body of death pray that that good physician, beloved of God, may not forsake us, he whom the patriarch David begged not to depart from him. Let us commit ourselves to him, ready to be cured with whatever medicine he wishes. No one tells the physician of his body how he is to be cured. The physician knows what medicines suit each wound, which sore's putrefaction must be cut away with the knife, lest it creep on to the destruction of the whole body. If the physician names the kind of medicine by which the sick man ought to be cured, and he is disdainful of it, the physician departs and leaves the sick man. See one who wishes to be cured acquiescing in the physician in every way; mark the order. First he opens his wounds to the physician and says: "Cure me, but, I ask, not in your wrath, because my infirmities do not bear a harsh medicine." The medicine of Christ is chastening; for the Lord chastens him whom he wishes to convert. Therefore Paul too says to the physician: Rebuke, entreat, reprove. He, then, does not refuse to be cured who asks to be rebuked, but he wishes the punishment to be lightened, so that he may not be rebuked in anger and chastened in the onset of wrath.

57. And see the progression. First he asks to be rebuked, afterwards, what is greater, to be chastened. Then he not only confesses his sins, but also numbers them and accuses them; for he does not wish his offences to lie hidden. For as fevers, when they are deep within, cannot be soothed, but when they break out, bring hope of ceasing, so the disease of sins burns more fiercely while it is covered, and if it is betrayed by confessions, it evaporates. And therefore the righteous man is his own accuser at the beginning of his speech, before the contagion of the sore creeps inward; for the memory of offences weighs down the conscience, unless medicine is sought. And if the physician delays, the sick man ought to offer himself, so as to be cut the sooner, as David offered himself to the scourges of the Lord, saying: "Repay me my sins double, provided you avenge them here; do not forsake me, do not turn your face away from me, do not disdain and shudder at the stench of my wounds. Job too, your little servant, had been struck with a sore from his feet to his head, and found the remedy of his health, although that wound was one of virtue, this one of error. The wounds stank, which physicians could not cure. You spoke, Lord, the mysteries of your sacraments, you made manifest the poisons of the serpent, and by the medicine of your word alone the wounds of your servant were cured, because you did not forsake him; and do not forsake me, Lord, do not depart from me. Men have forsaken me, because my wounds are foul to them, which I thought should be laid open to your loving-kindness. They say: Go out from us, because you are a sinner; depart, lest you pollute us. But you, Lord, cure and are not polluted, you help and are not contaminated, because you are the God of salvation, Lord, and your hand is accustomed not to destroy but to heal."

58. We have completed the psalm, together with the interpretation of a verse which the Greeks have, and some Latin codices, but not all. For before the next response there is the verse: and they cast me away like a dead man abhorred, that is, those who repaid me evil for good. But you, do not forsake me, Lord, do not depart from me — for this follows —, that is: do not you forsake me, you who are accustomed to cure a stinking dead man. Indeed we have this in the Gospel: For when he had come to the tomb of Lazarus and had said: Take away the stone, Martha said: He already stinks, for he has been four days. He answered her: Did I not tell you that if you believe you will see the glory of God? And he called Lazarus, and he came out whole. Let us too, then, believe, so that from our wounds we may acquire for ourselves the medicine of salvation and the glory to come.

59. In prayers, then, and supplications, with grief and tears, penitence must be carried out, so that we may deserve to see that glory of God. Nor let it trouble you that mourning, grief, maceration of the body are most grievous sufferings; even if they seem most grievous, yet such sufferings are not worthy of the glory that is to come, as the Apostle Paul is witness. Let it not irk us, then, to bear lighter things here, so that there we may obtain things full of praise and glory, carrying off perpetual rewards for temporal labours through the Lord Jesus, to whom be praise, honour, glory, perpetuity, from the ages, and now, and always, and unto all the ages of ages. Amen.

On Psalm 38 (39)

Title: Unto the end, for Jeduthun; a song to David himself.

1. In the preceding psalm the form of penitence was expressed; in this one that follows, the form of patience is marked out. This psalm David wrote and gave to be sung to Jeduthun, a man trained in the Levitical and priestly disciplines, who, most skilled in composing the series of psalms, used to lead the singing before the ark of the Lord. Since, then, it was not Jeduthun who wrote this psalm, but the prophet David, who gave it to Jeduthun, a man skilled in singing, to be sung, the title has therefore been inscribed thus. Indeed, in secular writings too there were some who wrote, and others who were accustomed to sing on the stage either songs or comedies or tragedies. And these indeed sought the Olympic crown, which is why their writings also bear witness at the end to an epinician hymn. But David, who did not seek these garlands of withering crowns, but with devout affection longed for that incorruptible and undefiled reward, the inheritance of heavenly things, wrote a hymn that was not an epinician of victory, but to him who imparted victory to those who believe in him, who is the end of all things that we ask with a devout mind. For whether you seek wisdom, or devote yourself to virtue, or to truth, or to the way and righteousness, or to the resurrection, in all things you must follow Christ, who is the power of God and wisdom, the truth, the way, righteousness, the resurrection. To whom, then, should you strive but to the perfection of all things and the sum of the virtues? And therefore he says to you: Come, follow me, that is, so that you may deserve to attain the consummation of the virtues. Therefore he who follows Christ ought to imitate him according to his ability, so that he may meditate within himself on his precepts and on the examples of his divine deeds.

2. And therefore David meditated within himself, because he kept in mind that Christ had spoken in him, so that, when some were troublesome to him and tried to provoke him to contention, he might put on patience and decline the zeal for disputing, from which empty clamour and quarrels frequently arise. And so, since many who envied his virtues and believed that he must be drawn away from his purpose of gentleness and from his disposition of humility frequently assailed holy David, and since above all the spiritual wickednesses in the heavenly places themselves, against which the righteous have a grievous wrestling, drove the ministers of their wickedness on to stir him up, the holy prophet resolved to keep silent, lest amid the frequent contests of those clamouring against him and the tumults of dispute some word of offence should slip from him. O silence, strong shield of circumspect defence! O most trustworthy foundation of steadfastness, on which, if anyone can stand, he cannot fear the slipperiness of speech! For many, though made firm even with a steady heart, have yet often fallen, careless, through the error of wavering speech. Seeing, therefore, that he was being tempted by snares of this kind and by the empty clamour of those contending with him, the prophet pondered silently within himself, imposing on himself a law of silence; and when he kept silent before those who insulted him and provoked him to say something — such as the contests and speeches of insolent men usually are: "Answer us, if you presume to be our equal; you see that you too are beaten and have nothing to reply" —, when he seemed almost driven to speak by their reproaches, he called himself back, so as to speak in his heart:

3. I said: I will guard my ways, that I may not offend with my tongue; I have set a guard to my mouth, while the sinner stands against me, that is: I resolved, I confirmed, I commanded myself, I spoke to my heart: "I will guard my ways". If I had said it to others, my word ought to stand; how much more steadfast ought that to be which I have appointed for myself! If anyone deceives a stranger, it is unseemly; if a neighbour, it is grievous; how much more grievous, then, if anyone thinks he must cheat himself of what he has promised himself, so that by his own judgement he is reckoned faithless to himself and contemptible! To whom, then, can he seem fit who is worthless to himself? Let us therefore hold to the constancy of our soul, lest our lips, hasty to speak, burst out into offence. Job too overcame his wound by silence and by keeping quiet surpassed his own endurance. The flesh is not well trusted; we would have lived, if Eve had kept silent. Therefore that first sin came forth from the voice, and that most wicked and cunning serpent tempted us first through the voice. And would that either Adam had been deaf or Eve had been dumb: he, so as not to hear his wife's voice; she, so as not to speak to her husband and, through the service of a slippery voice, pour the serpent's poisons into her husband! Cain too, although by his parricide he violated human nature and, ungodly, blotted out the rights of dutiful love, nevertheless through his voice added sacrilege to his crime, in denying to God the death of his murdered brother. Why should I speak of individuals? The very people chosen by the Lord, for whom Moses in silence opened the sea, ungrateful for heavenly benefits, murmured, and by this incurred the divine displeasure. Then it rejected Moses himself, the leader of that pathless and unaccustomed journey, and demanded of his brother Aaron that gods be made for it to worship.

4. Hence he too, considering such great dangers of the voice, said: I will guard my ways. What, then, are these ways of man which must be greatly guarded, lest they incur some graver offence? So great a prophet admonishes us to consider it more diligently; Solomon his son, the interpreter of his father's mind, admonishes us also to examine it more keenly, who wrote: Three things are impossible for me to understand, and a fourth which I do not know: the track of an eagle flying, and the way of a serpent on a rock, and the paths of a ship sailing on the sea, and the ways of a man in his youth. Three things, then, he said were impossible for him to understand; a fourth, which he, who held among men the seal of wisdom, did not know, namely the course of the ways of a man in his youth. Can the guarding of ways, then, be easy, when the knowledge of them is either inexplicable or difficult? Hence not without reason David himself offered no defence of the sins of his youth, but asked that they be forgotten: The sins, he says, of my youth and of my ignorance remember not. Not without reason, then, did Solomon say that he did not know what his father, in whom there was a greater grace of God, testified that he had been ignorant of. And perhaps he referred the sins of youth to the heat of the flesh, but those of ignorance to the slipping of words, which are mostly poured out not from our judgement but from a certain impulse and rush of speaking; and the prophet, knowing that this is dangerous, says: I believed, therefore I have spoken. Upon the foundation of virtue, then, the voice is safe; without a foundation it is slippery. Therefore, as with the sound of a trumpet, the chosen interpreter of divine Scripture rang out: With the heart one believes unto righteousness, with the mouth confession is made unto salvation. In believers faith is the principal thing; confession belongs to its carrying out.

5. Let us hear, then, how the holy prophet David resolved to guard his ways, or for what reason: that I may not, he says, offend with my tongue. If the prophet guards against this, do you not guard against it? If he in whom the grace of God spoke feared this, do you not fear, you who do not shun words of error and delight in theatrical talk? Do you not dread it, you for whom it is written in the Gospel that for every idle word you will render an account? If there is danger for an idle word, how much more for a criminal one! Not everything that is idle is also criminal, but everything that is not fruitful is dangerous and fit to be uprooted; for every tree that does not bear fruit is cut down and cast into the fire. When the mind is moved and stirred up by adversaries goading it, clamouring, mocking, accusing, a word of agitation slips out; therefore silence is both safe and seemly, for it keeps caution with dignity. For what are our lips but certain bonds of ours? Indeed, each one is bound by the bonds of his own lips. Therefore the wise man keeps silent in season and considers within himself at what time he ought to speak. Of this Solomon himself says excellently: The lips of the wise are bound with understanding. When, therefore, you see a wise man keeping silent while shameless men mock him, you will say: He has bound his lips with understanding, that is, he keeps silent prudently, so as not to be tied up in the bonds of his own lips. He has set a guard to his mouth, he has hedged his ears with thorns, he has put a bar to the doors of his mouth, he keeps the treasure of his heart and the silver of his speech, so as to bring it forth tested and refined when it is fitting, lest some thief or disturber first break into his heart and drag him captive to those to whom he may sell his crimes.

6. Judas was meditating on selling Christ, but the great grace of the Lord called him back, and certain soothing charms of fatherly loving-kindness, by which his fury was softened; nor would he have broken out into the crime, had not the adversary plunged himself into his heart, because he thought he was deceiving Christ. Do not doubt, then, when some are troublesome to you as you wish to keep righteousness, that they are ministers of that most wicked sinner who is the author of all crimes. David saw with prophetic eyes, he recognized his figure; therefore he kept silent, so as not to do the will of him who longs to throw the disposition into confusion. He held down his voice, he closed his gates with silence, patience was held before him as a shield in it, and he kept watch with the guard of silence, lest the enemy creep in, lest some wandering and careless word go out from the bars of his mouth. Therefore the patient man who restrains himself is stronger than he who takes cities. The righteous man is himself his own bar, himself his own ever-watchful guard.

7. When, therefore, he set this as a shield before himself, he spoke in his heart: I have set a guard to my mouth, while the sinner stands against me. The enemy is always present, because he always lies in wait. Even if you do not see him, he is present; even if you do not perceive him, he attacks. If you do not see, believe him who sees: he stands, he says, against me. He who sees his own sin sees also the sinner mocking him in the things he has done; but he who has foreseen the sinner can avoid sin. David saw the sinner standing against him when Shimei cursed and stoned his king and shouted: Go out, you man of blood. For he would not have been so headlong as neither to be moved by reverence for so great a king nor to fear his armed host, when one man of his host could have killed the reviler; but the devil had made him mad, driving him on to death. Therefore David fought against the author rather than pursuing the minister. For what great thing was it to take vengeance on a feeble man whom another was urging to fury, in whose killing the sinner would have achieved the effect of his will? The devil is conquered not by the sword but by the word of God; therefore let the tongue of man be silent, let the word of God speak. Indeed Christ too was silent when he stood before the judgement of Pilate, to give us a pattern of keeping silent, because the tongue of the body is easily led to error, prone to falling. But there are also those who speak while silent, like Moses, who stood astonished and was silent, to whom, as he kept silent, God said: Why do you cry to me? Like Susanna, who was silent in her dangers, and the Lord heard her. Therefore, even if the word of God is in you, you will both keep silent in the word of God and, silent, you will cry out, so that you may be heard by Christ.

8. Meditate, then, on the things that are Christ's, on which the prophet David meditated within himself, in whom Christ beforehand spoke the things he says in this psalm that he meditated within himself. For thus it is written: But I, when they were troublesome to me in anything, clothed myself with sackcloth and humbled my soul with fasting, and my prayer shall be turned back into my bosom. What is "in anything"? Because it is then above all that either an unseasonable word slips out or the affection is stirred, when we are in some grief or sadness, or the mind grows sick with indignation, or is hampered by some anxieties, or is taken up with disputes. Who, then, are troublesome? Understand it first of the author of sin himself and his angels, then of those who prefer to be his followers rather than Christ's. A wife is driven on, like holy Job's; a friend, as is made plain in that same book; a son, like Absalom; a slave, a freedman and a member of the household, because a man's enemies are even those of his own household, so that one by one they harass him. What remedy, then, against such sicknesses of minds but patience, but silence? And, what is more grievous, he provokes you more who has enjoyed your benefits, and among them he exasperates you more who owes you more, so that you are the more moved by the bitterness of an ungrateful man or by the indignity of some worthless person.

9. Therefore the righteous man, even if his friend is insolent to him, grieves for his friend, because his friend is slipping so, because he is driven on by the tempter. If he grieves for a friend, much more for a wife, for a son, if he finds them too insolent to him; and if a brother harasses him, as Esau did holy Jacob, he grieves and seeks to reconcile his brother to himself or to flee from his face, as Rebekah urged, lest his brother's wrath should find some occasion of doing harm; and if a neighbour or a member of the household does so, he grieves; and if an attendant or a freedman or a slave, he bears it patiently, and in all these things he keeps silent, so as not to answer the one by whom he sees the man driven on. Someone of the household reviles him, then: he grieves and is silent; a freedman reviles him: he is silent; a slave reviles him: he is silent. Thus is he overcome who spreads a snare for his soul with the bait of the voice. Be silent, then, you too, when an unworthy man reviles you; be silent when a slave, and above all your own, insults you, and say in your heart: I am silent, but you, do not be silent, O Lord, and add: do not depart from me, as the prophet David said, that is: do not leave me alone to those who attack me; for he is not alone to whom God is present. Take the testimony: Behold, he says, the hour comes that you will be scattered each to his own and will leave me alone; but I am not alone, because the Father is with me.

10. The righteous man, then, is silent before those who are insolent to him; the righteous man prays. Hear the righteous man praying: Instead of loving me they disparaged me, but I prayed. The righteous man blesses those who curse him, as the apostles did, as Paul affirms, saying: We are cursed and we bless. The righteous man loves his enemies. See how he is taught to ascend little by little, step by step, to the increase of the virtues, as the Lord says: Love your enemies. The righteous man prays for those who slander him, because the Lord commanded this too, adding: Do good to those who hate you, pray for those who slander and persecute you. He is righteous in every respect who keeps and observes these things: first, because he is silent and does not answer his enemies; next, because he prays, that is, he is not idle in his affection. And he who prays prays for good things, not for whatever might harm his enemy. It is more, then, to pray than to be silent. The righteous man blesses those who curse him. This is more than praying for himself; for, secure about himself, he blesses one whom he does not fear, or even one whom he feared before. The righteous man loves his enemy. It is more to love than to bless, because the love of the mind is greater than the graciousness of speech. The righteous man does good to him by whose hatred he has been attacked; the righteous man prays for those who slander him. Good is love and good is loving-kindness; both are of God and both are God, because love is God, as John said in his epistle. Therefore both are joined: Love your enemies and pray for those who slander and persecute you. Love is the fullness of the Law, but this too is the fullness of the Gospel, to pray for those who slander and persecute us, so that we ask pardon for their sins, just as holy Job was chosen by divine judgement, so that his prayer might abolish the offence of those kings who slandered the righteous man.

11. Therefore, if you are weak, pray, and if you are strong, pray; weak, you pray for yourself; strong, you pray for your enemy. Prayer is a good shield for weakness; you pray and the Lord protects you. It is a good shield also for one who triumphs, so that you defend your enemy whom you could strike, lest he be slain by someone and, what is greater, by Christ, who said: Vengeance is mine, I will repay. This is the prayer that is turned back into the bosom of him who prays, bringing back the fruit of the things he wished to obtain, so that the gifts he asked of the Lord return into his soul and mind, as this same prophet taught you later on, saying: So will I bless you in my life, and in your name I will lift up my hands. Let my soul be filled as with fat and fatness. The bosom of the soul is the secret place of its prayer, the inner chambers of the will, and certain innermost receptacles of the vows that return. Hence too the righteous are read to rest in Abraham's bosom, because those who have put on a faith conformed to his and have made their will the same as his in good works rest in his grace, in his rest, in his calm.

12. But perhaps someone may wonder, since the Lord said through the mouth of David that he humbled his soul with fasting, how David himself says later on: If I was not humbly minded, but exalted my soul. But let him consider that one thing refers to the wisdom of the mind, another to the humility of the heart. The wisdom of the mind is lofty, but the wisdom of the flesh ought not to be puffed up. He humbled his soul, then, with fasting, to teach that the swelling of our flesh must be chastised; the prophet exalted his soul through the sublime grace of God, since also he who exalts himself shall be humbled and he who humbles himself shall be exalted. And not only is the proud man beaten down and the humble man raised up by the due reward of merits, as must be understood first; but because the word of God is sharp on every side, like a sword which is sharp on three sides, I think it can be understood that both he who exalts himself knows how to humble himself, and he who knows how to humble himself knows also how to exalt himself. Indeed Paul shows it, saying: But I have learned to be sufficient in whatever state I am. I know both how to be humbled, I know also how to abound. So David too, by not thinking humbly of Christ, exalted his soul, which the Arians humble and cast down, and by exalting his soul through the vigour of his understanding and the grace of faith he represented the virtue of humility with devout affection.

13. Therefore, then, he said: I was dumb and was humbled and kept silent from good things, because he knew when he ought to humble himself, and knew when he ought to keep silent. He was dumb, so as not himself to clamour in quarrelsome contention with those who rebuked him; he was humbled, to cast down the swelling of the proud, or to teach them by his own example how they ought to humble themselves. He kept silent from good things, because a good conscience, which rests on its own testimony and is its own judge, needs no defence of words. And therefore the righteous man says: Who will contradict me, or who will resist me? And Paul says: For with me it is a very small thing to be judged by you or by man's day. Against slanderers and sinners the righteous man is content with his own judgement, but he reserves the judgement of his merits to Christ. And therefore he added: He who judges me is the Lord. Most beautifully he chose as judge him who is deceived by no fraud, so that neither do hidden things pass him by nor does the slip of a weak condition offend him, for he knows how to pardon frailty. For everything, then, silence is useful. If you acknowledge the sin, be silent, lest by denying it you make it greater; if you do not acknowledge it, be silent, secure of your innocence. Others' words cannot fasten on you a crime that your own conscience does not admit.

14. There follows: and my grief was renewed. Still placed on a lower step of virtue, he utters these words: that his grief was renewed by the casting up of old sins, which, blotted out, oblivion had seemed already to have covered over, or the compensation of good works had hidden. Indeed later on, as if stronger, he says: They saw me and shook their heads, because they threatened and could not harm; and he added: They will curse, and you will bless. And this he speaks as one stronger, heedless of injury or shame, since the blessing of the Lord abounded to him in place of all things. Therefore in this Psalm 38 (39), as if in a kind of meditation, yet not yet placed in the full perfection of virtue, he says that his grief has been renewed. For the strongest man does not know how to feel the hurt of a scar, nor can he who is stronger fear a wound. In the very contest of boxers, the stronger usually waits to be provoked by some blow of the weaker, so that, being struck, he may rise up the more fiercely; so strengthened is he by great training and endurance that, as though insensible to pain, he cannot feel the wound: struck, he laughs; provoked, he threatens. But the weaker, if he happens to take the striker's blow on the healed-over scar of an old wound, feels the pain renewed. So it is with one who has his former sins cast up at him, which he duly grieved over and wept for, so that by fitting satisfaction he drew a scar over the wound of his sins. If he is perfect, he keeps silent before his revilers, with no grief renewed in him, because, even if he has sinned, he is secure, since the gifts of God are without repentance, and therefore he is not wont to undo and renew what he has once forgiven, since he himself said: I am, I am he who blots out iniquities, and I will not remember them. But if he is imperfect and unmindful of the heavenly precepts, his grief is restored to him as if the scar had been cut open.

15. My heart grew hot within me, and in my meditation a fire shall blaze out. Graver wounds, as we know, are treated with a caustic medicine or by applying fire. The wise man, then, is himself his own physician; yet, though he is a physician, he burns his own wound, and therefore, if he has not been fully brought to the condition of unimpaired health, his heart steams, he has a fever, he catches fire at the reproaches cast at him and, if he keeps silent, he is chafed even within himself. If stone is rubbed against stone, it strikes out fire. So, then, even a good conscience is kindled with shame if the memory of its own sins is renewed in it, and in that very meditation a fire blazes out. Not evil is the fire that scorches but does not burn up; such was the fire that Moses saw in the bush, when the bush was burning but was not burned up. There is, then, a fire that diminishes sin and devours guilt with its heat. There is also a fire that is kindled by meditations on the heavenly Scriptures, like that of which Jeremiah says: And there was a flaming fire in my bones, or that of which Cleopas and he who was travelling with him, when after the resurrection Christ joined himself to them, conferred in talk with one another, saying: Was not our heart burning within us, when he opened the Scriptures to us? Therefore the Lord also came to send this fire upon the earth, to enlighten by it the souls of all, to kindle zeal, to burn up crime.

16. I spoke with my tongue: Make known to me, O Lord, my end, and the number of my days which is, that I may know what is lacking to me. Good is the fire that made the prophet speak. And see whether it is not the very fire that was seen when, on the day of Pentecost, as the disciples were gathered in one place, the Holy Spirit came down upon them with great force and they spoke in various tongues. For thus it is written: Their tongues were seen dispersed like fire. This, then, is he who said to Jeremiah: Behold, I give my words into your mouth as fire. This tongue of fire, then, David too received, so that, kindled by the ardour of divine knowledge, he might say: Make known to me, O Lord, my end. He is not asking about his death — for that is no end for one who will rise again —, but he asks about that end of which the Apostle said: That is the end, when the Lord Jesus will hand over the kingdom to God and the Father and will make void all principalities and powers, and when death will be destroyed last of all, so that evils may cease and eternal goods succeed. And therefore it was said: Grief, sadness and sighing have fled. This end Paul too was working in men, saying: Or who is it that makes me glad but he who is made sad by me? For by rebuking he stirred up grief, sadness, sighing, so that they might repent, and so deserve an irrevocable pardon and a salvation of which God would not repent. That, then, is the true end, which will be the end not of one but of all. How, then, did he say "my end"? But consider who is speaking. A man, surely: either one of men, sharing the community of substance, or one who is portrayed after the pattern of all and is being trained to the perfection of the consummate man.

17. Hence he also added that the number of days which is should be made known to him, so that he might know what is lacking to him — lacking, surely, for perfection, not for the life of this body, from which the prophet David longed to be released, saying: Woe is me, that my sojourning is prolonged! I have dwelt with the dwellers of Kedar. Indeed, he wished to know the number of days, not of nights as well. For here there are days and nights, but there days alone, there light perpetual and lasting. And therefore he added: which is. "Which is", he says, not "which passes away"; heaven, indeed, and earth shall pass away, but faith abides, and the day of Christ, because he is the same yesterday and today and for ever. And learn what number he seeks: In my Father's house, he says, there are many mansions. The Lord Jesus, then, gives each one a place, that is, a mansion fitted to the merits of each; each one, indeed, in his own order. He went before, then, to prepare a place for those who believe in him; hence he also says: If I go away and prepare a place for you, I come again to you and will summon you to myself, that where I am you also may be. And where I am going you know, and the way you know. There is, then, a way by which Christ, rising again, ascended. And he indeed ascended above all the heavens to the seat of God, but men, those who deserve it, ascend from the first heaven to the second, and then from the second to the third, and from that, through the divisions, almost to the seventh heaven, and into the very vault and summit of heaven. If, then, there is a number of ways and a number of mansions, surely there is a number of days through which one reaches the height of heaven.

18. Behold, he says, you have made my days old. Another version has: You have made my days palaestas [wrestlers / hand-breadths]. If, following the Seventy, we take the "days" as "ueteres" [old], that is: we understand them as spent according to the old man. But our old man has been fastened to the cross; therefore the old day too has passed away, the new has come. For the day of man is one thing, the day of Christ another, which Abraham saw and was glad, of which David also says: This is the day which the Lord has made; let us exult and be glad in it. The holy man, then, exults in the day of the Lord, in the new day in which God the Lord has shone upon us and given to those who are re-formed a new light, a life innocent and whole. Therefore the righteous man, secure of the new light and of the grace of God, says: There will be for me a new heaven and a new earth and a new light. For there neither the lamp nor the light of the sun or of the moon will shine, but the Lord will give light over his people. Those things, then, I have desired, those I have longed for, and therefore I have not sought the day of man.

19. But if we take the "days" as "palaestas", we understand from the name that they are full of contest and toil, since wrestlers in the contest who wrestle for the crown are called palaestae. Therefore, since we too have a wrestling not only against flesh and blood but also against the spiritual forces of wickedness in the heavenly places, our days, that is, the days of this life, are in toil and pain. Hence later on too he says: Our years were meditating like a spider. The days of our years in them are seventy years, but if in the strong, eighty years, and most of them toil and pain. Hence too the Apostle Paul toiled more abundantly than the other apostles, so that by contending lawfully he might attain the crown he desired.

20. But nevertheless, since the measures of architects are also said to be called palaestae, with which buildings are measured, to work out the number of the structure or the extent of the space, and the place in which the buildings are to be set; and since God spoke through Isaiah: Who has measured the water with his hand, and heaven with his palm, and the whole earth with his closed hand? Who has set the mountains in a balance?, let us see whether perhaps this sense is to be gathered from this word: that God has known the days of all, he to whom nothing is immeasurable, who comprehends all things by a certain measure of his knowledge, and to whom nothing is beyond estimation, nothing unexamined and innumerable, who says: But even the hairs of your head are all numbered. This, then, the prophet says: Behold, you have known my days, my daily acts do not escape you, you have set my sins before your eyes, and therefore my substance is as nothing before you. For what is man, but that you are mindful of him? Man has become like vanity; man is in sin, and if he has any uprightness and virtue, he is rightly preferred to the other earthly living creatures and excels in the sight of the animals over which he is set, but in the sight of God his substance is as nothing. Hence elsewhere too he says: For no man living shall be justified in your sight, which Symmachus expressed more plainly, saying: And my life is as nothing before you.

21. There are some, however, who think the days are called short, because Symmachus put σπιθαμὰς. A σπιθαμὴ is a palm, and since God is said to have measured heaven with his palm — the palm or span is the portion of the hand of one who measures and grasps something —, they therefore reckon that the days were called short, as it were, whereas the knowledge of God can be understood through measure, as we said above, and this knowledge is heavenly: that all things have been made according to measure. The prophet's days, then, can be understood not as short but as great, since God measured them with his palm, with which he measured heaven. For he would not call the days of his life short who afterwards says: Woe is me that my sojourning is prolonged, unless perhaps because, on account of men's sins, he made the days of this life shorter, which formerly extended to nine hundred and seven hundred years, so that now it is enclosed within the course of a hundred years. Thus, then, it is to be understood: Behold, you know my days, subject to sin; for the days of this life, which is subject to sin, are short, but the days of eternal life are without end.

22. Or at any rate thus — for it is ours to seek the truth by arguments, yours to choose what you will follow —: Lest sin should increase, he says, you have shortened the days of human life which we live on this earth; and yet, although in this short course of this life the end of sins comes sooner through the swiftness of death, my substance is as nothing before you, because, although we have been made after the image and likeness of God, yet a certain earthly contagion weighs us down while we are placed in this body; and it would not weigh us down if, when we put on grace, we preferred to put off the old man rather than to put the new over it; for the old man stripped off would bring us less toil than the old man clothed over. For what we strip off we cast away; what we clothe over we enclose; and therefore we groan and toil until this mortal garment of the old man, made subject to error, is swallowed up by the newness of its spiritual life — just as, if a great deal of honey is poured over poison, the venom of the poison is destroyed with difficulty and very late, and even much water struggles long with the gall of the poison, if one does not spit it out but swallows it. Therefore penitence is toilsome, grace is sweet, because sin is stripped off where grace is, swallowed up where penitence is; there it is remitted, here it is hidden; and therefore it is written: Blessed are they whose iniquities are remitted and whose sins are covered.

23. And he rested a little while, groaning at the misery of the human condition, because he had said: Nevertheless all things are vanity, every man living, and after the diapsalma, having recovered his breath, he says: Although man walks in the image of God, yet he shall be troubled in vain; he lays up treasure, and knows not for whom he gathers it. See how first he tempered the harshness of his statement, afterwards completed the reasoning of his utterance. He tempered it by saying that man walks in the image of God. Blessed man, you who walk by faith after the likeness of God; blessed because you have faith in you, more blessed if you had no avarice. The eternal good of the mind is one thing, the vain fire of carnal desire another; for faith serves as a soldier of God, avarice of the tempter; the latter heaps up what may profit itself, the former what may benefit others. What, then, is so vain as not to know for what heir one toils? For who knows whether his own son or grandson will survive him? Often the heir written in the tablets of the will goes before the testator to the tomb by an untimely death, or the survivor swallows up the inheritance, or a profligate squanders it, or a very foolish one does not protect it, or one proscribed loses it. If, then, you see anyone eagerly gathering riches, say you too: He is troubled in vain; he lays up treasure, and knows not for whom he gathers it. For he cannot take with him what he possesses, and when he dies he will leave his riches to strangers. Often an enemy and an ungrateful man succeeds him, and the successor insults his predecessor; those whom the one loved the other persecutes, those whom the one nurtured the other sells.

24. The Greek, however, does not have: in the image of God, but only: in the image. In what image, then, does man walk? In that, surely, after whose likeness he was made, that is, after the image of God; and the image of God is Christ, who is the brightness of his glory and the image of his substance. Christ, then, is the image of the Holy One. This image came to earth to the image, so that we might walk no longer in the shadow but in the image; for he who follows the Gospel walks in Christ. Therefore he says to his disciple: Get behind me, that is, that you may follow me. Therefore the people of the Jews went astray, because it walked in the shadow; therefore the Christian people does not go astray, because it walks in the image, having the sun of righteousness shining upon it. A good image, not shaped in the wax of a gleaming painting, but expressed by the fullness of divinity. In this image both the Father and the Son are seen together, because the unity of operation of both of them shines forth. Even though Christ has already risen, yet in the Gospel his image is still shown to us. I read that he raised Lazarus, and I believe it the work of the Father and of the Son. The Son called, the Father heard, Lazarus walked. I read that the demons confessed him; they confessed the Father also, they who besought the Son of God, and they were cast out in him or through him, as it is written: But if I cast out demons by the Spirit of God, by whom do your sons cast them out?

25. First, then, the shadow went before, the image followed, the truth will be; the shadow in the Law, the image in the Gospel, the truth in heavenly things. The shadow of the Gospel and of the gathering of the Church was in the Law, the image of the truth to come in the Gospel, the truth in the judgement of God. Therefore, of the things that are now celebrated in the Church there was a shadow in the words of the prophets: a shadow in the flood, a shadow in the Red Sea, when our fathers were baptized in the cloud and in the sea, a shadow in the rock which poured out water and followed the people. Was not that the sacrament of this most holy mystery in shadow? Was not the water from the rock in shadow, as it were the blood from Christ, who followed the peoples that fled from him, so that they might drink and not thirst, be redeemed and not perish? But now the shadow of the night and darkness of the Jews has departed; the day of the Church has drawn near. We now see good things through the image, and we hold the good things of the image. We have seen the chief of priests coming to us, we have seen and heard him offering his blood for us. We priests follow as we can, so as to offer sacrifice for the people: weak in merit, yet honourable by the sacrifice; because, even if Christ is not now seen to offer, yet he himself is offered on earth, since the body of Christ is offered; indeed he himself is shown to offer in us, he whose word sanctifies the sacrifice that is offered. And he himself stands by as our advocate with the Father, but now we do not see him; then we shall see him, when the image has passed away and the truth has come. Then no longer through a mirror, but face to face, the things that are perfect will be seen.

26. Ascend, then, O man, into heaven, and you will see those things of which there was here the shadow or the image; you will see not in part, not in an enigma, but in their fullness, not under a veil, but in the light. You will see the true light, the eternal and perpetual priest, whose images you saw here: Peter, Paul, John, James, Matthew, Thomas. You will see the perfect man no longer in an image but in truth; as, indeed, is the heavenly, such are they also that are heavenly, surely they too already in glory and beauty, already in the grace of the resurrection, not in the body of death and the deformity of corruption.

27. See that you do not carry the image of the earthly to where the light of heavenly things is. Here, if anyone keeps the images of a tyrant who has already been conquered and perished, he is rightly condemned. How do you bring the image of the enemy and adversary into the city of the true emperor, unless so that he may condemn you? And if you wish to bring in the image of the earthly, the prince of this world will oppose you; he will say: "This image is mine, and the things you offer are mine". What will you do when you are caught, when he says to you: "Mine is avarice, mine is ambition, mine are the riches whose image I recognize in you"? For he is both adversary and defender. But if you are such as Jacob was, you will say to Laban, to him who transforms himself into an angel of light: Search whether you find anything of yours with me. Then he will search and, finding nothing, will go away confounded, because you have hidden and blotted out all the likenesses of the vices. Blessed is he who can say: The prince of this world will come and will find nothing of his in me. For he seeks the things that are his, not those that are Christ's. If, then, you follow Christ, do not strive after riches you do not have, nor increase those you have, but let the poor man feel that you are rich for him. Bring the needy into your house, break bread for the hungry and cover the naked, so that a treasure may be laid up for you in heaven that will pass over with you. Christ became poor, though he was rich, that by his poverty he might make us rich. Do not, then, be troubled in vain on account of riches, be roused from sleep, think how you may keep your money and increase your wealth, go out by night, keep watch at the judge's house so as to plunder others' goods, bring a lawsuit against a poor man, fear the tax-collector. Vain is that trouble.

28. And therefore the righteous man said: And now what is my expectation? Is it not the Lord? And my substance is before you. Our hope and patience is Christ; he himself has become our redemption, he himself is our expectation, so that each of us may say: Waiting, I waited for the Lord, and he regarded me. Regard us, then, in your judgement; let your mercy look upon us, so that we who distrust our own merit may be delivered through your mercy, in whose power is the substance of our soul and life. And therefore let us not fear the death of the body, but him who can either save or destroy our soul, whose substance is virtue, which God poured into human breasts after his own likeness.

29. Whoever, then, has this substance of his soul says: Deliver me from all my iniquities. He who prays to be released from all iniquities does not confess the fall of one crime. For he knows that, unless the Lord pardons, no one can be saved, whoever is born under sin, whom the very inheritance of a guilty condition has bound to guilt.

30. You have given me, he says, as a reproach to the foolish. Who is the foolish man but he who commits sin and chooses evil things instead of good? Therefore he who is the author of sins is himself the foolish one rather than his minister. When we sin, then, we are handed over as a reproach to the adversary, so that he, as an enemy, may accuse and betray us on the day of judgement. Let us take up, then, the reproach of the cross of Christ, which Moses took up and preferred to the royal treasures of Egypt, and therefore he was not handed over as a reproach to the foolish. Other codices have: Do not hand me over as a reproach to the foolish. But David rather has this custom, that he confesses his reproaches and sins instead, and does not blush at his sins and reproaches, for he knows that in confession there is pardon, and in the condemnation of one's own sin righteousness.

31. Indeed, this sense is made clear from what follows, because he added: I was dumb and did not open my mouth, because you made me, that is: "You gave me as a reproach to the foolish; therefore I was dumb and did not open my mouth, lest I should incur greater sins. I recognized your will, that I should blush for a time and afterwards be saved by asking pardon". Sometimes, then, even reproach is profitable, since even the destruction of the flesh is itself profitable, as the Apostle testifies, saying: I have handed him over to the destruction of the flesh, that the spirit may be saved in the day of our Lord Jesus Christ. That reproach too is profitable David himself testified, saying to Abishai: Let the Benjaminite curse me, because the Lord has told him to, that he may see my humility and the Lord may repay me good for this curse. And in truth the Lord saw his reproach, that he went weeping and barefoot with his head covered, and removed his reproach. It is clear, then — since he, given over to reproach, fled and was humbled and made satisfaction to the Lord with bare feet, and did not strike back at the man throwing stones, and did not permit vengeance on the one who cursed him, and after this was restored to his kingdom intact —, that reproach too is from the Lord, and that it is more profitable to bear reproach than to repel it. In the end, victory followed. Reproach, then, is profitable. But what is it that he added: because you made me? Does he not say that he was dumb and did not open his mouth because he recognized the Lord's will, and that he therefore bore up patiently, so that satisfaction might be made to the Lord through reproach for his sin?

32. Hence he also added: Remove my strokes from me. How does this agree with itself? In the preceding psalm he offered himself to the scourges; here he asks that the scourges be removed from him — unless it is because he has already paid off his sins double from the hand of the Lord, by blushing, by bearing reproaches. The compunctions of his heart and of his thoughts are scourges, and so are those of rebukes. Let us see, however, whether he did not say that the substance of his soul is before God for this reason: because the adversary would not attack it unless he had received power from the Lord; for what the Lord has made he also protects. Indeed an angel is round about man, to shield him, lest anyone harm him. The angel does not depart unless ordered by the Lord, so that his athlete may contend. Therefore, as the Lord's substance, he presumes that pardon of his iniquities can be granted him by his creator, and therefore he asks, because he knows his author to be merciful. Next, because he has been given as a reproach, he does not resist, but allows the sinner to stand against him, and he is silent in the consciousness of his substance, which is good and can rein in the ferocity of the body, so that virtue may succeed and guilt be laid aside. Lastly, because he was made by the Lord, he confidently reckons that sin can be forgiven him the more quickly, since he was able to bear the strength of the Lord's hand.

33. And this, surely, belongs to a good substance, even though he says that he has fainted at the strength of the Lord's hand. A strong hand, just as it strikes hard, so heals quickly; what is strong for the wound is strong also for the remedy. Hence he himself says: I will strike and I will heal, so that he who has escaped healed does not remember the wound. Indeed this hand of the Lord took away everything from holy Job and restored everything, nay, increased everything in heaps, so as to double what had been lost. Nor let it move you that he said he had fainted. There is one who faints and rises stronger; for he upholds, the Lord, all who fall and sets upright all who are dashed down; for whoever is set upright rises with strength. Paul fainted at the strength of this hand; the persecutor fell and the preacher of the Gospel rose up. This is what he says: I have fainted at the strength of your hand.

34. And he added: With rebukes over iniquity you have instructed man, and you have made his soul waste away like a spider. The Greek said ὑπὲρ ἀνομίας, that is, "for iniquity", and put the whole verse κατὰ κοινὸν, which is: "with rebukes for iniquity you have instructed man". Therefore the Wise Man said: Who will give me scourges for my thoughts and a seal for my lips?, that he might scourge his heart with his own thoughts and seal his lips, so as both to diminish old sins and not to renew others by speaking, so that, instructed by his own rebukes, he might put on the exercises of virtue. The soul wastes away when sin is diminished, or, when the soul wastes away, guilt is laid down; it grows fat when sins are heaped up. Hence the Lord says of sinners: My spirit shall not remain in them for ever, because they are flesh, and elsewhere Scripture says: The heart of this people has grown fat. Therefore Jeremiah too saw first a rod of walnut, then a boiling pot, so that the soul of the prophet at his beginning might waste away, that guilt might depart and grace come. But why first the rod, afterwards the pot? Because he who is not corrected by the rod is put into the pot, to burn and waste away. And therefore it is said to the prophet Ezekiel too: Take for yourself a frying pan, and you shall take the iniquities of the house of Israel and shall bring them to an end; and they shall waste away, he says, in their iniquities, and elsewhere in the same book the Lord says: But I will make a great firebrand and will multiply the wood and kindle the fire, that the flesh may melt and the broth be diminished. By these testimonies it is signified that the souls of the unbelieving waste away when fire is applied, so that they lay aside a certain moisture of carnal fatness. The souls of sinners, then, are also put into the frying pan, so that a certain flesh of sin may flow away, which had overgrown the vigour of the soul and mind and, as with a kind of broth, had thus steeped the soul in certain allurements of desires. Certain frying pans, then, await us. Therefore let us here grind down our heart, so that it may become fine and lay aside all hardness and a certain fatness of carnal lard. This those will suffer who have lived in luxury in this life and believed, after the Jewish manner, that they should practise the mockeries of disgrace rather than any works of virtue. For Jacob grew fat and became thick and rose up to play, and that play led him away from the truth of faith into the error of unbelief. Rightly, then, did Paul chastise his body, so as not to nourish the flesh of sin; for by living in luxury we make our members members of sin, but by sobriety members of righteousness. It is not nature, then, but guilt that does wrong.

35. Nor again let us follow vain and empty things, lest we be judged to weave a spider's web, since sins can have no substance of perpetuity. Hence, when you see some eager to enlarge their resources, to heap up powers, to strive after splendour, you will say that word of Isaiah: These men weave a spider's web in all things of one day, which cannot be lasting, but is quickly torn apart, and all its work is undone; for it is not set upon any solid foundation, but is hung in the void. Nothing slack, nothing soft befits the true soldier of Christ; behold, indeed, those who are clothed in soft garments are in the houses of kings. All the avaricious seem to themselves clever and industrious. What is cleverer, what more diligent than the spider, which always attends to its work day and night and makes a garment at no cost and no expense? But all that it has made is vain. So is every man who does not set his work on the foundation of Christ; vainly, night and day, he too is troubled, since often, like the spider, the ruin of his own work entangles him even in the very effort of his wickedness.

36. And therefore the wise man, who knows the vanity of this world, does not pray for a long enjoyment of this life, but hastens to those eternal things and longs to be released from the toil and pain of this body, as the prophet is known to have desired, saying: Hear, O Lord, my prayer and my supplication, give ear to my tears. Be not silent toward me, for I am a newcomer with you on the earth, and a sojourner, as all my fathers were. A native inhabitant is one thing, a newcomer another, a settler another: a native inhabitant is one who is from the place and inhabits the place of his birth; a newcomer, one who comes from elsewhere; a settler, one who dwells for a time and changes with the turns of the years. This, then, is what David says: "With you I am a stranger while I am on the earth; I am not now a native inhabitant of your paradise". Hence he himself explained this, adding: and a sojourner, as all my fathers were. This the Apostle Paul too disclosed, asserting that while we are in the body we sojourn away from the Lord, because, even if faith is in us, by faith, however, we walk, not by sight, that is, not face to face as if present; for we still walk in an enigma, not in truth. Indeed he added: We dare, then, and we trust, rather to sojourn away from the body and to be present with the Lord. "We dare" is a word of the boldness of faith in God, not of rashness; and "we trust", that is: we acquiesce willingly and longingly; not resting the authority of fuller knowledge principally on our own judgement, as it were, we wish what we desire to be true: that we may draw near to the Lord, from whom the distance between things to come and things present seems to separate us. We dare, then, to be present, and therefore, he says, we strive. You recognize the word of one who still wishes to arrive, not of one who has arrived; it is the affection, then, not the effect, that is shown in this word. Indeed, question the very affection of his speech, nay, hear him saying it, or rather hear him doubting, when he says: Therefore we strive, whether absent or present, to please him: absent still, through faith; present, through sight. Hence this too can be understood, that both placed here and there we desire to please our judge, who is also the redeemer of our souls. For we must stand before him in sight, so that each may obtain the merits of his works by the examination of a fitting lot, either the righteous man in rest or the ungodly in misery. He sheds tears, then, because when a newcomer has come to the earth, he lingers and is separated from his fatherland, detained by a long exile. Consider for me now that Adam cast out of paradise, banished to a stronghold, gazing at the place from which he was cast down, weeping abundantly, because he remembers what he has lost or whence he has fallen. And therefore let us give thanks to God, who has freed us from perpetual exile, opening the door by which we may return to our fatherland. Hence the Apostle made bold to say: So then you are no longer newcomers and sojourners, but you are fellow citizens of the saints and members of the household of God.

37. Wishing to receive this before Christ came to earth, David said: Remit to me, that I may be refreshed, before I go, and I shall be no more, that is: Remit to me here, where I have sinned. Unless you remit to me here, I shall not be able to find there the rest of remission; for what has remained bound on earth will remain bound in heaven, what has been loosed on earth will be loosed in heaven.
