For the prophet and preacher of the Church that was to be gathered foresaw the indulgences of the Lord by the spirit of the Gospel, and the Lord revealed to him what was to be commanded to the apostles; nay, this was the old sentence, that he who had bound himself on earth should depart from the body in chains. Therefore the Lord gave to the apostles what before had belonged to his own judgement, the equity of remitting sins, so that what is to be loosed should not stay bound long. Indeed, hear him saying: To you I will give the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven also, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven also. To you, he says, I will give the keys of the kingdom of heaven, so that you may both loose and bind. This Novatian did not hear; the Church of God heard it; therefore he is in his fall, we in remission; he in impenitence, we in grace. What is said to Peter is said to the apostles. We do not usurp a power, but we serve a command, lest afterwards, when the Lord comes and finds bound those who ought to have been loosed, he be moved against the steward who kept bound the servants whom the Lord had ordered to be loosed, saying that he who knew his lord's will and did not do according to his will shall be beaten with many stripes; but he who did not know and did things worthy of stripes shall be beaten with fewer.

38. Rightly, then, David asks that it be remitted to him, knowing that this had not been granted to the priests who were after Aaron, but was being kept for the Gospel. He, then, to whom there is no remission is in exile; he remains in the injury of a sojourn away from home. Here, then, pardon must be sought — for here it was said: Blessed are you who weep, for you shall laugh —; here is the place for asking pardon. Let us weep, then, on earth, so that we may deserve indulgence. Unless sin has been remitted here, we shall not have rest; if there is no rest, there will be no eternal life; if there is no eternal life, we shall not be. Therefore he asks that it be remitted to him before he goes, so that he may be. For if it has not been remitted, I shall not be, he says, with those who are, with those who deserve to ascend into paradise. But he who is not will be consumed with those who go down into the pit, he who has been left in chains and prison.

39. He does not, then, say "I shall not be" definitively, for elsewhere he says: I will sing psalms to you on the harp, O God, the holy one of Israel. My lips shall rejoice when I sing to you, and my soul, which you have redeemed; of redemption, then, he is confident. And elsewhere hear him saying: I will please the Lord in the land of the living; he who is confident of pleasing, then, will be. It can also be understood thus, because he said: I am a newcomer with you on the earth, and a sojourner, as all my fathers were, and therefore: Remit to me, so that I may cease to be a sojourner; remit to me the exile to which I have been banished. If you remit it before I depart hence, I shall no longer be an exile and a sojourner; for when you have remitted it, I shall not be a sojourner, but I shall be a fellow citizen of your saints, I shall be with my fathers, who themselves were also sojourners before but now are already citizens. I shall be a member of the household of God, so that I may not fear punishment but may deserve grace, through our Lord Jesus, with whom to you be praise, honour, glory, perpetuity, with the Holy Spirit, from the ages, and now, and always, and unto all the ages of ages. Amen.

On Psalm 39 (40)

1. In the preceding psalms he preached penitence; then he spoke of his own patience and of the disturbance of man. But in Psalm 39 (40) he announces the New Testament. For he who repents of evils waits; but it is not the mark of the perfect to wait, but to have waited; for no one shall be saved except he who has persevered unto the end.

2. Therefore he added: waiting, I waited for the Lord. Where, then, is this rightly said, except in the Gospel? In it he who was awaited has now come to us; in it the shadow of the Law no longer darkens for us, but the truth shines, because Christ has shone forth, who heard the prayer of his own and brought us out of the pit of misery and out of the mire of the dregs, where, already sunk, we were held fast in the whirlpool of our sins, and our whole man stuck fast, and our mind could not tear itself free, overwhelmed by the stain of manifold wickedness. Thanks, then, to the Lord Jesus, the only-begotten Son of God, our Redeemer, who, to remit all our sins, came down from heaven, to snatch us out of the pit and slime of this world and out of a kind of muddy swamp of the earth, and also out of the body of this death, and to set the inner footsteps of our mind upon his own flesh, so that, strengthened in the Word of God and absolved through the cross of the Lord's body, we may walk no longer in the reproach of disgrace but in the remission of sin. Strengthened, then, and rooted in Christ, he records that the footsteps of his feet were set for him upon the rock. Hence the Apostle says: But they drank from the rock that followed, and the rock was Christ. A rock, in that it follows the thirsty and strengthens those who slip, so that the thirsty may not lack water, nor those who slip a firm support.

3. To me, then, as I waited, you came at last, Lord Jesus; you directed my steps in the Gospel; you put into my mouth a new song, which is the New Testament. Rejoicing, we now sing a hymn to our God, because we have come to know the precepts of new virtues: that we should leave all that is ours to follow Christ, and love our enemies. We have also drunk in new ways of life, so that we offer prayer to the Lord for those who persecute us; see, we bless those who curse us; we do not know how to boast of our works or to hide our sins; after the manner of the angels we even refuse marriage.

4. Why say more? From that wretched sinner a blessed man has been made, no longer looking upon the various vanities of this world; for this whole world is vanity of vanities, and all is vanity, as Solomon the wise said. For what is it but vain to follow things which cannot endure and which all pass away like a shadow? Now, false madnesses are declared to be either those contests of wars in which so much blood has been shed for a temporal possession, or the dissensions of theatrical rivalries, or the partisan passions of the circus, full of frenzy, or the deadly doctrine of heretics, or the feigned gestures and contrived words of false prophets in the manner of heavenly oracles. For so the prophet said: Blessed is the man whose hope is the name of the Lord, and who has not looked upon vanities and false madnesses.

5. There are, then, also true madnesses, and perhaps those of the prophets, who, placed in ecstasy of mind, prophesied filled with the Spirit of God, so that to some they seemed to be mad, when, unmindful of their own safety, often naked and barefoot, like holy Isaiah, they ran through the peoples, crying aloud not what they themselves wished, but what they were commanded by the Lord. From these things, then, it is understood that the remedy of salvation has flowed to us, since he who was awaited is present.

6. Adam, cast out of paradise, awaited him, that he might be freed from a long exile. He awaited him in righteous Noah, who was preserved for the seed of things to come, so that from him the seeds of righteousness might sprout among men. He thought him the author of the common salvation, since he had deserved to survive the flood; him the prince of wisdom, since he had cultivated the vine and had been made drunk with its fruit — for what is the vine but wisdom? as David, the interpreter of the heavenly oracle, has disclosed to us, saying: Your wife is like an abundant vine on the sides of your house — because in him was the fullness of wisdom which was awaited in Christ, who, filled with the new sacrament of wisdom which eye has not seen nor ear heard, hastened to bring us an unwonted mystery, so that he might lie naked and pour forth unheard-of cups of overflowing wisdom, so that, forgetting the former things, we might rise again to new ways and to the new sobriety of eternal life, whereby God might be acknowledged through the foolishness of preaching, whom this world did not know through wisdom. Finally, in that figure of the sleeping and uncovered father, he who laughed at the folly was cursed; those who showed reverence obtained the grace of a blessing. And therefore those who have believed that the death of the only-begotten Son of God, the Father and Creator of all things, is to be cast up as a stumbling-block or a reproach to our faith are held bound by the bonds of their sins to the everlasting misery of the curse; but those who accept it as the absolution of the whole human race are upheld by the protection of eternal power. For the cross of Christ is to the Jews a stumbling-block, to the Greeks folly, as the Apostle said, but to us who believe in our Lord it is the power of God and wisdom.

7. He was awaited in Moses, when he had divided and passed through the waves of the sea and opened in the floods a path to travel on foot, impossible to human strength, when the rock followed and poured out waters, to satisfy the inmost bowels of the thirsting fathers in the midst of the desert.

8. He was awaited also in Aaron, the chief of the priests, who stood midway between the living and the dead and halted the creeping plague of death, lest it pass from the corpses of the dead to the destruction of the living. For what is so proper to Christ as to stand before God the Father as the advocate of the peoples, to offer his own death for all, to repel slaughter, to restore life to those about to perish?

9. He was awaited also in Jesus son of Nave [Joshua], and he certainly came, who received both his power and his name, so that he might both lead the people into the land of promise and truly be called Jesus by name. He dried up the streams, turned the waves back to their own source, made the sun stand still until he should complete the victory in power. But that was still a figure, not the truth. Finally, the true captain of the heavenly host shone forth, and Jesus son of Nave worshipped him, although the true one had not yet stood present in the body, but had come as yet in a figure.

10. When, then, did the true one come? Hear him saying: I have announced and have spoken; they are multiplied beyond number. Sacrifice and oblation you did not want, but a body you have perfected for me. Then I said: Behold, I come; at the head of the book it is written of me; to do your will, my God, I have willed. What is more evident than that the true Jesus has come to us with the gift of his Gospel? He himself spoke, and countless peoples of believers were gathered, who before scarcely believed one by one. He himself came in the flesh, and the sacrifice of the Jews ceased, the oblation for sin ceased, because the remitter of sins had come. The remedy of the Law was not needed where the author of the Law stood present.

11. Then I said: Behold, I come; at the head of the book it is written of me. It was certainly written of Christ at the beginning of the Old Testament that he would come to do the will of God the Father in the redemption of men, when it is written that he formed Eve, in the likeness of the Church, as a help for man. For what can be a protection to us in this infirmity of the body and the disturbance of this world except the grace of the Church alone, by which we are redeemed, and our faith, by which we live? At the head of the book it is written: Bone of my bones and flesh of my flesh. For this reason a man shall leave father and mother and shall cleave to his wife, and the two shall be in one flesh. Who it is that spoke, or of whom this sacrament is, hear him saying: This sacrament is great, but I say in Christ and in the Church. Hence he also admonishes that a man should love his wife as Christ loves the Church, because we are members of his body, of his flesh and of his bones. What greater salvation, then, than to be with Christ and to cleave to him in a certain unity of body, in which there is neither any spot nor the filth of sin?

12. It is written at the head of the book that the victim of righteous Abel pleased God, and the oblation of the parricide displeased him. Did not the Lord Jesus openly signify that he himself was to be offered for us, so that in his Passion he might consecrate the grace of a new sacrifice and abolish the rite of a parricidal people? And what is more express than that a son was offered by the holy patriarch and a ram was sacrificed? Did he not openly show that the flesh of man, which is common to all earthly living creatures, and not the divinity of the only-begotten Son of God, would be subject to the wound of the sacred Passion?

13. It is written at the head of the book that a man would come who would rule over the heavenly powers. It was fulfilled when the Lord Jesus came to earth and angels ministered to him, as he himself deigned to say: From now on you shall see the heavens opened and the angels of God ascending and descending to the Son of Man.

14. It is written at the head of the book: A lamb without blemish, clean, full-grown, male, a yearling, shall be yours, and the whole synagogue shall slay it. Who this lamb is, you have heard him say: Behold the Lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sin of the world. This is he who was slain by the whole people of the Jews, whom they still persecute with hostile hatred. And indeed it was fitting that he should die for all, so that in his cross there might be remission of sins and his blood might wash away the defilements of the world. But woe to them who denied the author of their own salvation! You have made, he says, a counsel, but not through me. How could he approve a parricide who did not approve the counsel? And yet how great his loving-kindness! He shrank indeed from the counsel, but if they were converted he did not refuse the remedy.

15. It is written, moreover, not only at the head, but in the whole compass of the Law, that a man would come to preserve the race of men, who would will all that God willed. Hence you understand that he who is of the same will is of the same divinity. Hence also he came willingly to the sacrifice of the Passion, and rightly foretold: Willingly will I sacrifice to you.

16. But perhaps you may say: "How did he say in the Gospel: Father, if it is possible, take this cup from me; but not as I will, but as you will?" And there seems to be a difference of will. But we understand that one thing is expressed according to the unity of the divinity, where he says: I and the Father are one, and another thing he spoke according to the affection of man, whom he taught that he ought to be cautious and not presume what he cannot easily fulfil, lest, while he seeks the prize, he fall into sacrilege. Hence he says: When they persecute you in this city, flee into another. Rightly, then, here too, being about to undergo the passion of the body, he began to be sorrowful and to be sad, and, having taken with him Peter and the two sons of Zebedee, he first said: My soul is sorrowful, and afterwards added: Watch and pray, that you may not enter into temptation; the spirit is ready, but the flesh is weak. You see that everywhere he speaks according to man, prays according to man, is sorrowful according to man, said according to man: My Father, if it is possible. What, indeed, is impossible to God? But it is man's part to doubt, God's to confirm. Therefore he took with him the readier ones, as chosen witnesses of his final judgement, who would recognize the sacrament of his words. Finally, the others were sleeping; these alone were watching with faithful mind. Hence also through Jeremiah, when he had not yet taken a body, he himself says: I was led like a lamb, and I did not know. What is there that the wisdom of God does not know, which is always in the secret of the Father? And therefore the Son of God has declared all things, because he himself is the wisdom of God the Father. How did he foreknow that he was to be led like a lamb, and yet says that he did not know where he was being led? Or how, earlier, through the mouth of David himself, does he say: God, my God, why have you forsaken me? How is the Son forsaken by the Father, he who says: And I am not alone, because the Father is with me, and elsewhere: I was delivered up and I did not go forth, and elsewhere: I have risen and am still with you, unless so that you may observe that he expresses the feeling of men, who think themselves forsaken by their Lord when they are in dangers? Finally, hear what follows: Far, he says, from my salvation are the words of my offences, that is: the words of offences are one thing, the sacraments of eternal salvation another. Words do not prejudice power; the divine substance is upheld by its own strength: man is seen, man is heard, God is recognized in his works. And yet of this man it is said: He is both man, and who shall know him? What is "who"? Not that no one does, but that someone rare does, someone wise, as in that saying: Who shall ascend into the mountain of the Lord? Who is wise, and shall understand these things? Finally, elsewhere: The foolish man shall not know, and the stupid shall not understand these things. You, then, when you read the Gospel, as one wise and not foolish, know what you read; let the brightness of eternal wisdom enlighten you. They are words indeed, but they themselves cleanse, and they themselves enlighten, and they themselves strengthen, and they themselves give life. Hence Peter says: Lord, to whom shall we go? You have the words of eternal life, and we will not leave you.

17. Finally, so that we might observe that he speaks according to man, a little later he said: Behold, the hour draws near that the Son of Man should be delivered into the hands of sinners, and to Judas as he kissed him he said: Judas, do you betray the Son of Man with a kiss?, that is: do you pour poison into the kiss by which the grace of love is poured in, the kiss which is the token of sacred peace, the kiss by which faithful friendship is made firm, the kiss by which holy faith is sealed? With this kiss, then, do you betray to danger him whom you ought to venerate because of the exchange of the kiss, and do you, a man, betray the Son of Man, who came down from heaven for the salvation of men? For so it is written: The Son of Man who is in heaven, he it is who came down from heaven. Do you betray the Son of Man who made you an apostle out of a sinner, do you betray the Son of Man who came to wash away the sins of all in his own blood? I do not refuse the kiss; see to it yourself, you who have violated the sanctity of the kiss. Or have you not read that the wounds of a friend are more useful than the voluntary kisses of an enemy? And not undeservedly was Judas struck by so great a condescension, a heavier weapon than if he had struck him with a thunderbolt. Finally, he who betrayed the majesty could not bear the patience of the Lord, so much so that the guilt of so great a crime could not be washed away from him. And so he went away and gave back the money, that is, he condemned his avarice, the author of so great an ungodliness, and returned to the synagogue the price of unbelief. The traitor, who could find no place for penitence, is not excused, but the sacrilege of the Jews is made more bitter, for they are convicted even by the words of the traitor. He acknowledged his crime, saying that he had betrayed righteous blood; they both took back the price of the crime and persecuted the prince of righteousness with keener zeal. Therefore, to the perpetual condemnation of all these, the crucified thief is absolved, because he acknowledged Christ in his own punishments, whom these did not acknowledge in his benefits, and he confessed his sin to Christ, who knew how to forgive, because on his cross he beheld with his mind the kingdom of the Lord, which Judas could not see at the banquet of Christ. Therefore the thief's prayer was followed by a heavenly voice of this kind: Amen I say to you, today you shall be with me in paradise. You were dancing for joy, dragon, because you had stolen an apostle from Christ: you have lost more than you took, you who see the thief carried over into paradise. There is no one who can be shut out, when the thief, your servant, has been received and has come to that place from which you yourself were cast down.

18. We have added these things on account of that verse, because he said: To do your will, my God, I have willed, that is: the will of the divinity is one for me with you, Father, and because the will is one, the substance is one, and the majesty and power of the Trinity are inseparable. But the voice of the flesh is another, and yet it too consented to the will of God, saying: Not what I will, but what you will, and elsewhere: If this cannot pass unless I drink it, your will be done. To fear death is a common affection; Christ took it on so that he might crucify, as indeed he did crucify, the flesh as well; for he wrestled for me, that he might conquer for me. Although the flesh of Christ was strong and not subject to sin, yet he took on sins; he took on infirmities, although he had nothing to grieve for. Finally, hear him saying: Because he grieves for us, until he brings forth judgement in truth. For he did not take on another flesh, but this one, as the Apostle teaches, saying: For this corruptible must put on incorruption, and this mortal immortality. Although he was born of a virgin and of the Holy Spirit, yet he is the Son of Man, because the virgin is human. And therefore what is born of the flesh is flesh, and he who is born of man is called man, the mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus.

19. For this reason he who was incorruptible took on this flesh, that he might make it incorruptible, and he who was immortal, that he might make it immortal. If anyone, then, follows him, he shall put on immortality; if anyone does his will, he shall not die for ever, but Christ will say to him: Today you shall be with me in paradise. What is "today"? That is: you have passed over from the night, you shall be with me in the light; do not fear the darkness, you whom eternal light has taken up. Beautifully, too, he added: you shall be with me in paradise, that is: do not doubt about the flesh; you have seen me too in the flesh. Do not fear lest you also fall from paradise as Adam fell, but hear that you shall be with me, where, with me present, you cannot fall. The flesh had fallen in paradise before it was taken up by Christ. You shall be taken up there where he also is who took it up, who did not lie when he said: I will that where I am they also may be with me. The shepherd will not withdraw from his flocks, although in paradise you, to whom it was said: I saw Satan like lightning falling from heaven, can no longer fear the wolf. Therefore, before the consummation, the adversary is cast down into the fire and is bound with everlasting chains as well, so that you may now fear no snares.

20. Adam, too, was deceived by the serpent, so that he fell, and was swayed by his wife. Therefore there will now be no marriages in paradise, but all will be without the celebrations of marriages, like the angels in heaven. He had not said to Adam "you shall be with me", because he knew that he would fall, so that he might be redeemed by Christ. Happy ruin, which is repaired for the better! Therefore now the loving shepherd, the good merchant, does not abandon his flock and his own merchandise. There will indeed still be in paradise a fight of the serpent with the angels, until he is cast down; but he who is with Christ will not be able to fear. Adam was not with Christ when he was deceived; for if he had been with Christ and had abided in his commandment, he certainly could not have perished. He did not abide in Christ, because he did not continue in his word. Finally, hear who abides in Christ: If you abide, he says, in me and my words abide in you, you shall ask whatever you will and it shall be done for you, and further on: If you keep my commandments, you shall abide in my love. Do you wish to know that Adam was not with Christ? The Lord said to him: Adam, where are you? He who seeks him as though absent would certainly not say "where are you?", if he knew that he was abiding in him. But let us hear who made him absent: They hid themselves, he says, both, from the face of the Lord. Christ sees all, but he who hides himself is hidden, since he who does not know shall not be known; for the Lord knows those who are his. He wishes all whom he has founded and created to be his own. Would that you, O man, did not flee and hide yourself from Christ! He seeks even those who flee and does not wish the hidden to perish, but calls out, saying: Adam, where are you?, that is: "Man, where are you? I placed you in the light; you have sought the darkness".

21. Finally, in paradise, where it is always the day of morning, evening came, since all sins are dark. Therefore you have it that they heard the voice of the Lord God walking towards evening in paradise. At the same time recognize the mystery. In the morning you went astray; at evening you are set free. For how can there be evening where Christ is? For although his seat is above the heaven of heavens, yet Christ is in his saints. How, then, can there be evening where God is, since God is light? Finally, a light shone round Saul on the way when Christ met him, whence the persecutor was even blinded as the light of God shone forth. Why, then, was he blinded, unless because he had not acknowledged Christ? For if he had known the arbiter of light, he would not have lost the light of his eyes. Finally, when he acknowledged him, he received it back. It is an indication that he did not know Christ that, when he heard the voice: Saul, Saul, why do you persecute me? he answered: Who are you, Lord? He would not have asked who he was if he had known him. Hence the Lord too speaks as to one who does not know: I am Jesus whom you persecute. It is hard for you to kick against the goad. And he said: Lord, what must I do? See the fitting parts each of them plays. The physician threatens so that he may correct, frightens so that he may cure. The sick man first asks about the person of the physician, whether he is fit, one by whom he may presume he can be healed; when he heard the author of the medicine, he asked for the remedy and found health. Finally, from him as he came near he not only drove out the blindness, but he also received a light that he had not had, so that he saw Christ, whom he had not seen before.

22. But let us look at what follows, repeating, however, the verse which seems to have been treated: Behold, I come; to do your will, my God, I have willed, and your law in the midst of my heart. I have well announced your righteousness in the great Church; behold, I will not restrain my lips. Lord, you have known your righteousness; I have not hidden it in my heart. From the former verse, then, we understand: "Behold, I come: not only shall I do your will, but also your law in the midst of my heart". Others have: in the midst of my belly. For there is also a belly of the mind, which conceives the seeds of thoughts, of which Isaiah said: In the womb we have conceived and brought forth the spirit of salvation. With this womb Paul too brought forth, who says: My little children, with whom I am in travail until Christ be formed in you. Christ, then, is formed not in a bodily belly, but in a certain belly of the soul. With this belly the Apostle begot, as it is written: I have begotten you through the Gospel. Therefore some have: in the midst of the heart, others: in the midst of the belly, so that you may understand the belly of the inner man, which is taken to be in the midst of the heart, in which we keep the prescriptions of the natural law, which is not only kept but also done in our hearts. Hence also it is written that not only the hearers of the law are righteous before God, but the doers of the law also shall be justified. For there are those who by nature do the things that are of the law, as the Gentiles, who do not hear the law and are a law to themselves, who keep the precepts of a law unknown to them. Therefore Christ in this place, as the gatherer of the Gentile people, took on our person, so as to say that he does the law in the midst of his heart. For the people of the nations fulfilled the law which it had not heard, so that it acknowledged Christ, whom the people of the Jews, which had received the law, did not receive nor thought worth knowing, because it is written: The ox has known its owner, and the ass the manger of its lord; but Israel has not known me. This, then, is the belly of believers, in which the Holy Spirit works and which he is wont to fill with spiritual seed, as the Lord Jesus testifies in the Gospel, saying: He who believes in me, as the Scripture says, rivers of living water shall flow from his belly. From that river come these rivers, of which we read: The rush of the river makes glad the city of God. Of those, then, from whom such spiritual rivers flow, it is well said: The rivers have lifted up, O Lord, the rivers have lifted up their voice from the voice of waters, of spiritual waters, which praise God above the heavens.

23. He who has lifted up his voice can say: I have well announced your righteousness in the great Church. Why did he add "great", unless because before it was not great? Which is great, unless the one gathered from the parts of the whole world, when the peoples of the nations were called from east and west, from north and south? Yet where we read of the great Church elsewhere: I will confess to you, Lord, in the great Church; in a weighty people I will praise you, there, then, is the great Church where the people is weighty, that is, not restless and fickle, like the one which sat down to eat and drink and rose up to play. A weighty people is one which keeps faith with its God, and which is not changed by any levity nor wavers and fluctuates, but is founded in love in Christ, so that it can say: for I am confident that neither death nor sword nor tribulation can separate me from the love of God which is in Christ Jesus.

24. But he who preaches in the great Church does not hide the righteousness of God in his heart, but lifts up his voice with all his strength and proclaims Christ. He does not fear to preach him before the people, he is not ashamed of his cross, but glories in that Passion of the Lord. He does not follow the letter of the Law, but digs out deep mysteries by which to reveal the glory of the Lord Jesus, who, as the Law signified, was to come to the Church, which was to be gathered as a great one. Righteousness, then, is faith. Finally, Abraham believed God, and it was reckoned to him for righteousness. Behold, I will not restrain my lips. Lord, you have known. So also elsewhere: You have known my sitting down and my rising again.

25. To the Father he says: My righteousness I have not hidden in my heart; your truth and your salvation I have spoken. This whole psalm is spoken in the person of Christ. Therefore he says "my righteousness", although a man too may speak, without arrogance, of his own righteousness, if he believes God and confesses that his faith is reckoned to him for righteousness. He loves truth and mercy who preaches the true faith and forgives sins. For this is why Christ came: to establish faith, to grant the remission of sins.

26. And since we have said that this whole psalm is spoken in the person of the Savior, lest perhaps the Apollinarists say: "See, a body, he said, you have prepared for me, not a soul as well", let them hear him saying in the later part of this psalm: Let them be confounded and put to shame together who seek my soul, to take it away.

27. These are the things in this psalm that we judged difficult. The rest we have considered plain and easy, and in no need of interpretation, since, if they come up for treatment in another psalm in which they seem to be repeated, we shall pursue them more fittingly, with the favour of the Lord Jesus, to whom be praise, honour, glory, perpetuity, from the ages, and now, and always, and unto all the ages of ages. Amen.

On Psalm 40 (41)

1. In good order, he who in the preceding psalm said: behold, I come, in this one now comes and suffers. And therefore the psalm is entitled the fortieth, because the Lord Jesus fasted forty days, so that by that number he might complete the perfection of man, who had perished. For just as through his craving for the forbidden fruit man had lost grace, so his virtue had to be restored through fasting, so that he who had perished in Adam might be restored in Christ. Therefore, then, Unto the end, because he himself is the end of our hope; to him we direct our efforts, to him we bring our vows, because he is the fullness, he is the consummation of all things, he is the sum of all virtues. Therefore to David himself, as to him who alone of all took up our sins in his own flesh, who alone, the Lamb of God, took away the sin of the whole world, who alone blotted out the handwriting of the decree by the shedding of his blood, and took it out of the way and fixed it to the cross; as to wisdom, knowing how to undo the old sin of the world; as to redemption, knowing how to renew man from guilt; as to sanctification, knowing how to sanctify him unto grace. This, then, is the title, this the beginning.

2. Blessed is he who understands concerning the needy and the poor. Open your ears, you who are beginning to know the sacraments of the Lord's Passion. For this reason it is said to you: he who has ears to hear, let him hear. For this reason the Lord in the Gospel touched the ears and the lips of that deaf and dumb man, so that he might know what he was hearing in the mystery, and also what he should speak and when he should speak. For not idly is it written: the Lord has given me a tongue of learning, that I might know when it is fitting to speak a word. You ought, then, to know what you hear, to know what you ought to say, to know also when you ought to speak.

3. Hear him, then, saying: blessed is he who understands concerning the needy and the poor. Blessed indeed is he who suffers with the poor man, who takes his needs into himself, who knows what evil want brings to the needy; but what does this opening mean in the Lord's Passion? It is true indeed that he suffered for the sake of the poor, but yet he himself rebuked Judas when he said of that ointment which Mary had poured over the feet of Christ: this could have been sold for three hundred denarii and given to the poor. The other apostles too had thought the same, but with a different disposition, he through avarice, they through mercy; for Judas was looking to theft, the disciples were taking thought for the feeding of the poor. To them in common he answered: let her alone, so that she may keep it for the day of my burial; for the poor you always have with you, but me you do not always have.

4. Therefore another is to be understood here as the blessed man who understands concerning the poor. Here he speaks of faith, elsewhere of mercy; faith, then, comes first, mercy second. With faith mercy is precious; without faith it is bare, without faith it is unstable; for faith is the stable foundation of all the virtues. Blessed, then, is he who understands concerning the want and poverty of Christ, who for our sakes became poor, though he was rich; rich in the kingdom, poor in the flesh, because he took up this flesh of the poor. For we became exceedingly poor, we who through the serpent's deceit lost the precious vesture of the virtues, shut out of paradise, cast out of our homeland, banished into exile, stripped even of the covering of the body, which the hedges of the virtues once protected and our transgressions afterwards laid bare. If, then, he was needy and poor in the flesh, surely he was needy and poor in the passion of that flesh. Not, then, in his own riches, but in our poverty did he suffer. And therefore it was not the fullness of the divinity, which dwells in him bodily, as Scripture testifies, but the flesh that suffered. Understand this, pursue this, hold to this, lest it be said to you: he would not understand concerning the beggar and the poor man. For if you are in doubt here, surely in that psalm which is spoken in the person of the Savior against the betrayal of Judas you will not be able to be in doubt about that verse in which he says: and he persecuted the poor and beggarly man. Understand, then, the poverty of Christ, that you may be rich; understand his weakness, that you may receive health; understand his cross, that you may not be ashamed; understand his wound, that you may heal your wounds; understand his death, that you may acquire eternal life; understand his burial, that you may find resurrection.

5. But perhaps you may say: "How is Christ rich in poverty?" Even if my own wit fails, yet the aids of the divine readings do not fail, since the Apostle has said: for the Lord Jesus became poor, though he was rich, that you might be enriched by his want. What, then, is this poverty that makes men rich? Let us consider it, let us look closely at the venerable sacrament itself. What can be purer than it, or simpler? No one is drenched with the blood of a bull, as the rites of the Gentiles are said to have it; the sinner is not washed with the gore of goats and rams — for he is not purified by it; the flesh is washed, the guilt is not cleansed away — but: water shall go forth, he says, with delight from the fountains of salvation, and a heavenly table is prepared in your sight, and the cup that inebriates, how glorious it is! These are the riches of simplicity, in which lies the precious poverty of Christ. There is also a good poverty in morals, whence the Lord too said: blessed are the poor in spirit, and in the psalms we find that the Lord will save the humble in spirit. There is also an abounding poverty in a slender contribution, if faith abounds. Whence the Apostle says: and their deep poverty has abounded in the riches of their simplicity.

6. But that we may understand from other words of his as well that he called himself poor, we have it written elsewhere: I am poor and grieving, that is, because he grieves for us, as we read; for he had nothing in himself to grieve over. As, then, he grieves for us, as Isaiah interpreted, who saw him in the spirit, so surely it was for us that he was sorrowful, according to which he called his soul sorrowful even unto death, yet not on account of death; and he called the flesh weak which was subject to the weakness of death, so that he might break the sting of death, and so free us in the day of judgement, which he called evil not lightly.

7. For thus he went on: in the evil day the Lord will deliver him, since that day seems bitter which adjudges many to punishment. For the way of virtue is narrow, the way of transgression broad; therefore fewer walk in virtue, more are engaged in wickedness. Hence it comes about that the number of those who will receive the rewards of recompense is smaller than that of those who, as the price of grave sins, will carry away the adverse lot of the judgement. As one grieving, therefore, he called the day of judgement evil. For if the angels rejoice at the absolving of one sinner, how much more will men be wasted with grief at the condemnation of so many men! And rightly he grieves for that sheep which he carried back on his shoulders and called back, as it wandered, to his fold. The Son of God speaks with our affection, because the day of judgement, which we fear, is bitter to us. To all it seems evil, because it is full of terror, the day on which God judges not only the public deeds of man but also his hidden ones, on which each one is compelled to lay open what he has done or what he has thought. And therefore many are condemned, few are crowned. But even those who are crowned will grieve; for the righteous will be crowned, but the righteous man counts his brother's affliction as his own. And who am I to judge of that day on which man will be brought into the councils of the angels? Let us hear the sentence of the Lord himself: the hour will come, he says, in which all who are in the tombs will hear the voice of the Son of Man, and those who have done good will come forth to the resurrection of life, those who have done evil to the resurrection of judgement. Those, then, who have done evil will be judged, and therefore the day is named evil and harsh by the Judge himself, so that you may know that he suffers with us in the Gospel who suffered with us in the prophets.

8. But we can also take in the evil day in this way, that is, in an evil time; for the world is set in evil, and therefore the days of this world are evil, and the day abounds in its own evil. It is no small thing that we be delivered here from the snares of the sinner. He who has been free from crime here will be free from punishment there, and will not be able to undergo the severity of the judgement.

9. And therefore there is need of vows and daily prayers and of the advocacy of the prince of priests, of whom John the Evangelist says: we have an advocate with the Father, the Lord Jesus, and he is the intercession for our sins. This one, then, our advocate and the intercession for the sins of the whole world, prays for him who understands and believes in Christ, and says: may the Lord preserve him and give him life and make him blessed. May he preserve him from dangers, give him life as one set in the darkness of death, and deign to make him blessed by the increase of good works.

10. And may he cleanse, he says, his life on the earth. Rightly he added "on the earth", because, unless one has been cleansed here, he will not be able to be clean there. Or certainly in this way: "may he cleanse on the earth"; for how is anyone clean in the mud, when the earth smears him? Therefore the hand of the Lord is needed, which made the body from the earth, that it may itself deign to cleanse it.

11. And may he not deliver him into the hands of his enemy. Who is the enemy but that lion who goes about roaring, seeking whom he may devour? Other Greek copies have: and may he not deliver him into the souls of his enemies. He has put souls for hands, because the souls of enemies lie in wait for the good and try to overthrow the righteous man by their thoughts. Souls are also said for men: all the seventy souls who went down into Egypt. Therefore David too preferred to commit himself to the power of God rather than to the snares of enemies, who would not know how to spare of their own will.

12. There follows: may the Lord bring him help upon the bed of his pain; all his bedding you have turned in his weakness. What is the bed of pain but the flesh of weakness? And rightly the bed of pain, because it is the body of death, from which the Apostle prays to be freed, so that he may rise up as from a kind of tomb, in which the soul lies sick and reclines as on a kind of couch. Symmachus said the bed of misery, Aquila the pallet of misery; the Seventy thought it should be said more expressly the bed of pain. These verses, then, are to be used with those who are held for a long time by weakness of body, so that they may have the medicine of God, by which they may deserve to be made whole. The bed of the soul, then, is the weak flesh, with whose sickness our soul suffers; and if it has the Lord as its helper, he says to it: rise, take up your bed and go into your house. Strengthened by this word of God, he who was sick rises and takes up the bed on which he was lying and returns to his former state of health. This is to go back into one's house: to dwell in the dwelling of the most robust health. He who dwells there rules; he who rules has the grace of virtue supporting him. But the Lord also turns all the bedding in the weakness of the one who languishes, to whom he deigns to be present. What is turned changes its state, either from health into sickness or from sickness to health. Here, then, he who is sick turns himself towards the remedy of health.

13. So, then, the Lord Jesus, although he was wounded for our sins, yet did not remain in that weakness, but turned himself for the better, for the salvation of all. Thus weakness was undone by the Passion, death by the resurrection. As, then, he turned himself for the redemption of the peoples and did not feel the sting of death or the weakness of the wound, so he turns to life him who believes in him, even when gravely sick, so as to free him from death. All the bedding, then, that is, the weakness of all men, he turned in his own weakness. For he took up the weaknesses of all in his own flesh, raised himself upon the cross, and turned the weaknesses of all in the weakness of his own body. Whence Isaiah says: by his bruise we have been healed. Wrestlers have this practice: they put themselves underneath those with whom they are engaged and wrestle, so that they seem able to be overpowered, and suddenly, when they are thought to be overcome and about to be thrown down, they turn themselves by a certain skill so as to throw off the one on top; he falls who was being carried, and he who was carrying is found on top, so that he lays low the one pressing upon him. So in the spiritual wrestling-ground the Lord Jesus, taking up our burdens, put himself underneath in that bout of his Passion and, in the semblance of weakness, so that the adversary might judge him a man like the rest, whom he could easily overpower, he laid aside the arms of divinity and put on the covering of humanity. Sure of victory, the tempter came nearer; he wished to wound him in the rib with the soldier's spear, reckoning that he too, like Adam, could be laid low through a rib. But the Lord Jesus, wounded in the side, brought forth life from the wound, emptied out all sin, cast down the adversary, from whom he snatched the death of the thief, and in that death, in that burial of his body, when he was thought overpowered, thrown down, he turned himself by his own power: the adversary fell, the Lord rose again.

14. There follows: I said: Lord, have mercy on me; heal my soul, for I have sinned against you. This can be said in the person of King David, who, seeing in the spirit so great a victory and grace of Christ, asks that in that remission of all sins he may have mercy on him too, since he too, even if as a king he was not bound by human laws, yet acknowledges that he is liable to God for his sins. He confesses his sin, then, so that he may receive remission and obtain the gift of the general pardon. It can also be expressed in the person of the Savior, who asks God's mercy for us and said that he had sinned, because for us he was made sin. At the same time he teaches us, when we are set in some contest against the enemies of God, to implore the help of God, to confess our sins ourselves, and most of all then, when we are pressed hard and worn down, to prostrate ourselves with grief and bitterness, lest we seem to have kept silent, so that medicine may be brought the sooner to our wounds, and that, healed, we may resist the adversary. For how can a wounded man engage? From which we learn that we are wounded by the stings of our sins. A wound seeks a physician; the physician demands confession.

15. From here too the Savior begins more and more to open the sacrament of his Passion, which without doubt agrees with the Gospel. For the Jews were cursing him, seeking his death, lying in wait for him, pressing him hard, not bearing the glory of his work as it grew daily. This, then, the prophecy announces which was fulfilled in the Gospel: my enemies have spoken evil to me: when will he die and his name perish? Madmen, who believed that the author of life could die! But the Church was destroying their wishes, believing that even when dead, according to the condition of the body, he would increase his name. And therefore she sang to him, saying: I will be mindful of your name in generation and generation; she repeated: your name is ointment poured out. Those will die who presume that you can die; I shall not die, who know you as the arbiter of life, the author of salvation. For we know that at the time of the Passion and resurrection the disciples were praying, gathered together, when Magdalene announced to them that the Lord had risen, and when the Lord, the door being shut, showed himself to them in the body of the resurrection, when Thomas saw his wounds and believed. The Jews, then, were mocking him as one dead, but the Lord was revealing himself alive to the apostles. When, then, the Jews said: behold, the whole world has gone after him, when they said that he was leading the people astray by his wonderful works, what else were they saying but: when will he die and his name perish? And therefore at his Passion they cried out: take away, take away from the earth such a one as this, let him be crucified, let him be crucified. What they asked was done: he was taken away from the earth, he rose again, he ascended into heaven, and out of us he re-formed his kingdom in the seats of paradise.

16. Concerning Judas the traitor, too, a clear prophecy is added in the verses which follow: and he came in to see, he spoke vain things; his heart gathered iniquity to itself. And he went outside and spoke. So we find it punctuated in the Greek manuscripts. Judas, then, came in, seeking to betray the Savior, to see what he was doing or what he was saying, so that he might catch him in his speech and report it to the Jews; but he found nothing, who understood nothing. Scripture says well that he was not within with Jesus who had withdrawn from his teaching and abandoned his instructions. He wished to enter, but could not penetrate, because his own unbelief shut him out; for every man who is unchaste or covetous or unclean, which is the service of idols, has no inheritance in the kingdom of Christ and of God. Not only, then, was he striving to enter and did not enter, but he also wished to see and did not see. Indeed, Jesus spoke in parables, but Judas did not hear. Indeed, he did not see the divine mysteries; for not all who seem to themselves to hear hear the word of God. For if all heard, Jesus would not say: he who has ears to hear, let him hear. And therefore, because he did not hear and could not see, he spoke vain things instead of true ones, and thought that he was deceiving Jesus when he said: this could have been sold and given to the poor, whereas he was providing for his own thefts, not for the poor; he called him Rabbi and teacher, from whose teaching he was straying, while the devil was gathering to himself his heart, into which he had thrust himself, and was increasing his iniquities. For he was iniquitous to himself, unjust to himself, he sinned against himself, he who was devising within himself a deceit which could not lie hidden. For Jesus was not being outwitted, who was betrayed willingly and testified: one of you will betray me. Indeed, Judas confessed it, saying: is it I, Rabbi? And the Lord said: you have said it. Not even so did Judas understand that he was known; for if he had understood, he would not have betrayed him.

17. But nonetheless and he went outside and spoke. A fitting verse against those who betray someone's secret. Beautifully he says: he went outside; for outside are the wolves, outside the robbers. Within, Moses in the cloud with Joshua, outside, the Jews; within, Zechariah the priest in the temple, outside, the multitude; within, the Holy Spirit cries to the Father in our hearts, outside, our adversary keeps watch like a lion for prey; outside, the unbelievers, within, the faithful. And therefore those who are outside God will judge, but those who are within Christ will absolve. Judas, therefore, went out and spoke. He went out from the faith, he went out from the council and the number of the apostles, he went out from the banquet of Christ to the brigandage of the devil, he went out from the grace of sanctification to the snare of death, he who spoke vain things to the unbelieving; he went outside, who was abandoning the inner mysteries of life; he went out, who did not know the inner things of Scripture. For if he had known, he would have understood him who says: shall he who sleeps not add so as to rise again? He would have understood this also: but you, man of one mind with me, my guide and my familiar friend, who always took sweet food with me; in the house of the Lord we walked in agreement. He did not understand, then, this psalm, nor Psalm 108 (109), nor others, nor the other testimonies of the Scriptures. Of him, and of the other unbelievers, John the Evangelist rightly says: they went out from us, but they were not of us; for if they had been of us, they would have remained with us.

18. The traitor, then, did not remain with the apostles, he who went outside and whispered with the unbelieving. Divine Scripture used this word, showing that unbelief is without authority, since it fears that what it says will be caught out. But to the faithful man it is said: lift up your voice with strength. See to what the apostle Judas had fallen, he who had been sent to preach the faith to the Gentiles with a proclamation full of authority: to whisper into the ear of his master's plotters what he feared to have heard. Did he not condemn himself by his own judgement? But he blushed that what he did not fear to do should be heard; he blushed, because he was devising evil against the author of his life, of his sanctification and of his honour, and did not repay kindnesses with gratitude, but returned evil for good to a good master, a thing which even men of the world are accustomed to condemn; he blushed to be caught being ungrateful, he blushed to be seen to be avaricious, since he was betraying the salvation of his author for money; he blushed to be seen to be unbelieving. Likewise the Jews also, who in their unbelief suborned false witnesses against the master of the faith, to say that he had preached that the temple of God which Solomon built was to be destroyed, whereas he had signified it of the temple of his body: destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up, were truly outside, being shut out from the knowledge of the heavenly Word.

19. A wicked word, he says, they have set forth against me, that is: they have set before me a speech unlawful and against right and divine law, making bargains over human blood, which Judas most criminally sold, and which the Jews, with no lesser crime, thought fit to buy, most vain men, as if he who sleeps would not add so as to rise again. Let us consider what the addition is.

20. It is natural to all earthly living creatures and to the birds of the sky and to the fishes of the sea to be born and to die; on man, whom God had made more precious than the other living creatures of the earth, he conferred this grace especially, that when dead he should live again. This is called the addition, for the renewal of the former life. Indeed, when the Lord had decreed to King Hezekiah that he had fulfilled the time of his life, and had bidden him through Isaiah to set his house in order, because he was to die, and he had prayed to the Lord and wept with great weeping, the Lord had pity and sent word to him: behold, I add to your time fifteen years. And elsewhere holy David says: days upon the days of the king you will add, his years. Something, then, has been found that may be added to man; but nothing can be added to God, because it is written: there is no adding or diminishing in him in whom all things are perfect and full. Even though Christ rose again, surely he rose again in that which he had assumed, in which nothing could either be taken from the fullness of his divinity or be added to it. For God is incorporeal and immortal, whose supreme nature and fullness of powers is designated without any addition of flesh. But the flesh in Christ belongs to the dispensation, which he took up by title of compassion and by right of loving-kindness, so that he might redeem it when captive, wash it when defiled, raise it up when dead. And beautifully he says: shall he who sleeps not add so as to rise again? He did not say "it is not added", but "shall he not add so as to rise again", because the Son of God raised himself up, as we said above that he had said to the Jews: destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up. The flesh received the addition as taken from man, received from the Virgin, but as God he wrought the addition himself, who rose again by his own power, so as to be the worker of his own resurrection. When you read that the Father raised the Son and that the Son raised himself, recognize that that working was of the divine power, because the Father and the Son have one power, a common working, since the substance of the Trinity is one and the same.

21. "But why do I accuse the others?" says the Lord Jesus; "what wonder if the people did not know me and adjudged me to the cross, when my own apostle exacted the price of betrayal, when my own table-companion sold my death? For indeed the man of my peace, in whom I hoped, who ate my bread, has enlarged the supplanting over me". Some think that Judas should be excused, because he paid back the money which he had received as the price of the purchase. But see, he is condemned beyond the unbelief of the Jews; for from him to whom more has been given more is required. "Why", he says, "should I complain of the others? The man of my peace, to whom I had given my peace, to whom I had wished to leave my peace, led the persecutors to me and handed me over, and he deceived me, in whom I hoped". Was he unaware that he would be his betrayer, he who had foretold of him: one of you will betray me? Surely not. But to condemn him the more, he said that he had hoped in his faith. Aquila said I had trusted, Symmachus put I was trusting. For we bear more heavily upon one in whom we say we had very great hope or confidence, if afterwards he has disappointed our hope and our good opinion.

22. At the same time he shows how great is the force of divine sanctification, which shuts out malice and amends a depraved character, if the form of a better purpose is joined to it. How many we know to have been converted! That Zacchaeus was changed, the publican intent on gain with an avaricious disposition, who afterwards paid for his error with a fourfold restitution, condemned avarice in himself, forswore the gains of his former time, and made it good to the poor if the claimant was lacking. The thief himself changed his wickedness by a better purpose, recognized Christ on the cross, confessed the Son of God, called him king with his own voice. At the time when very many righteous men were wavering, when the apostles themselves were certainly in hiding, the centurion, the very executioner, did not deny him. Why do I speak of the time of the Gospel? Rahab was a harlot, who fed herself on the wages of unbelief and incontinence. Yet when she saw the spies whom Joshua son of Nun had sent, she put on faith, and with danger hanging over her she kept righteousness, and did not betray those she had taken in, but protected them with faithful kindness. Therefore, then, he says that he had hoped, as though he had rightly presumed that the apostle would lay aside his former ways and follow better ones, and that he who had received the office of sanctifying others would himself guard in himself the grace of holiness and cleave to the duties of sincerity. Rightly he says I hoped, because he gave man the free choice of what to follow. I have set, he says, before you good and evil. If you choose evil, it is not nature that sins, but the disposition of the one who chooses. If every man who has chosen evil is condemned, what do we say of Judas, who betrayed him to whom he was joined in the sweetness of the banquet?

23. Who ate, he says, my bread, has enlarged the supplanting over me. Why is it that he added "my bread"? He could have said: "who ate bread with me", unless you understand that nourishment which is peculiar to Christ, that is, the nourishment of the heavenly Word; not indeed by bread alone does man live, but by every word of God. Indeed, elsewhere he says: who always took sweet food with me. We rightly use these verses against those who have thought that men should be killed with whom they are seen to have feasted. The Gentiles are recorded to observe this, enemies for the most part, robbers even, that they spare those with whom they recline at table. Hence, because of a disposition of this kind belonging to the grace of the banquet, if we consider the words, not idly did he speak of sweet food. For how are they sweet which quickly spoil? But see whether he did not call the words of life sweet food; for no bitterness spoils this food, no corruption of death defiles it. Whence he also said "always". For no one can always be eating, but we can always cleave to the word of God, by night and by day, if we meditate on the law of God day and night.

24. What, too, is "has enlarged"? The Greek said ἐμεγάλυνεν, that is, "has magnified". The Lord set out for us both points that might move us, saying in the Gospel: he who eats bread with me has lifted up his heel over me. Let us consider whence this expression has been taken: we were, he says, unbelieving, erring, serving desires and pleasures. Let us confess our error, that we may be washed clean. I too, as a boy, saw an athlete who, when he had thrown his adversary, struck him on the forehead with his heel, which was a sign that he was insulting the vanquished. This is what he says: has magnified the supplanting over me, by which word he declared the boastfulness of one who insults. Judas too, then, lifted his heel over Christ when he betrayed him, but he did not lift it with impunity. Adam still lifts the heel which was wounded by the serpent. And surely Christ had washed his feet, he had heard him saying: he who is washed needs only to wash his feet, but is clean all over. But what grace had washed, unbelief had defiled. Judas, then, lifted his heel to the wound. Truly he did not hold the head who lifted his heel over Christ. Adam lifted it over himself, this man over Christ, and therefore the serpent wounded him more grievously than the rest. He lifted his heel who offered the deceitful kiss, by which to supplant his author, and therefore it is written in the prophet: he has enlarged the supplanting over me. Esau too says of his brother: he has now supplanted me twice. He who supplants practises a deceit by which he throws down or wounds his adversary. Therefore Judas too is said to have supplanted, because by his kiss he inflicted a wound, by which he showed the persecutors the sign for seizing the Savior. He supplanted, then, like a serpent, which pours poison from its mouth and wounds the heel with its tooth; for this man too wounded not the divinity of Christ, but the heel, the extremity of the body. Judas also lifted his heel like an insolent and proud wrestler, with which to strike the head of the Savior. But he could not strike the head of Christ, because the head of Christ is God. His own head, indeed, he bound with the knot of a hideous noose, so as to take away from himself the remedy of salvation.

25. See whether you can devoutly apply that which the holy patriarch Jacob said in Genesis, when he was prophesying of his sons: Dan shall judge his people as one tribe in Israel, and: let Dan be as a serpent sitting in the way and in the path, biting the heel of the horse, and the rider shall fall backward, awaiting salvation from the Lord. We read indeed that Samson, of the tribe of Dan, was a judge in Israel, but another is signified here, one to come, who, proceeding from that tribe, will afflict the people with the punishments of sacrilegious ungodliness, that Antichrist who will sit in the temple as though he were God, truly judgement, which is what the interpretation holds; for Dan signifies judgement. And beautifully he said that he will judge his own people, he who will become liable to perpetual punishment; for he who does not believe is already judged, but he who believes will not be judged, because he believes in the Son of God, the Redeemer of his soul. And therefore, as one free of judgement, he says: he who judges me is the Lord. He will judge, then, the people of ungodliness, not the folk of faith; for those are judged who fall by the ruin of unbelief, not those who rise again by the freedom of faith.

26. He will sit, therefore, in the way and lie in wait in the path like a serpent, to wound those who pass by and to bite the heel of our horse; for the soul of the faithful he will not be able to wound. But in what way will he sit and in what path, if not those from which, as we read, Christ the Lord called the people of the nations to his supper, saying: go out into the ways and round the hedges and compel them to come in, that my house may be filled? And since Judas likewise is read to have amplified the supplanting over Christ, he himself seems to be signified in this place, in that he was to bite the heel of the horse on which Christ the Lord was carried. Whence it was said: your riding is salvation, because his flesh was to take up the sin of the whole world, so that salvation might succeed in place of destruction and eternal life in place of death. Wounded, then, was the horse that alone is not false for salvation; wounded was the horse which took up the sins of the whole world and bore our burdens and was not wearied; wounded was the horse which did not know how to neigh in the frenzy of lust. And therefore perhaps to John the Evangelist heaven was shown opened and a white horse, upon which sat one having diadems on his head and a name written on his thigh: King of kings and Lord of lords. White was he who was immaculate, who had known no defilements of sin. For if lust is dark, rightly holiness shines and gleams. This horse, then, was wounded, but not held back. Indeed, with the very wound he rose again from the tomb, ran to heaven above all the angels and archangels, and was found swifter than all the cavalry of the heavenly host, above even those fiery horses by which Elijah was carried off. And deservedly swifter, he who ascended above all the heavens and above the heaven of heavens to the seat of God almighty, and sits there, as Scripture has taught us, saying: hereafter you will see the Son of Man sitting at the right hand of power.

27. This horse, then, was shown beforehand too, in a figure, in the time of the Maccabees, the horse which that captain of the heavenly host sat, gleaming in golden armour, who laid low, struck down Heliodorus, that plunderer of the widows' deposit and of the other things which had been committed to sacred keeping, and afterwards, won over by the prayers of Onias the priest, restored him to the use of life. Hence golden armour truly gleamed on him, because he alone sat this horse and conquered, on which no one came through free from guilt: not Enoch, who was carried off lest malice should change his heart, not Abraham, not Isaac, not Jacob, not Moses. For all are under sin, all under death. Indeed, death reigned from Adam to Moses. He alone it is who did no sin; Christ alone it is who took away the sin of the world, who said to death: where, O death, is your victory? where, O death, is your sting?

28. How, then, did Judas amplify his supplanting over him, and how did the rider fall backward? As to what is in Judas, he practised deceit; as to what is in Christ, he suffered willingly. We often see the stronger man wish to be struck, so that, provoked, he may rise up more fiercely and be roused more vehemently. The serpent, then, bit the heel of the horse and spewed out the venom of his deceit; the rider willingly threw himself down, willingly fell, so as to raise us up. Yet what is it that Scripture says: and the rider shall fall backward? Why backward, except that he might guide from behind those who follow him? Peter was behind, to whom he said: go back behind me; behind were the apostles and the other disciples. Therefore he fell backward, because those upon whom he falls he saves. Everyone indeed who falls upon this stone, he says, will be shattered; but upon whomever it falls, it will cleanse him. Others have: it will winnow him, others: it will crush him. For he who winnows the grain cleanses it of chaff; he who crushes something reduces it to powder, so that the softness of the powder may be of use either in a healthful drink or in the rest of medicine. Hence we gather that we must take care lest anyone, once received by Christ through baptism, stray from his body, that is, be shaken out of the Church; for that is the ruin of perpetual death. But him who comes to believe in Christ, so that his death may find him, his cross take him up, his nail fix him, his blood pour over him, his burial too wrap him round, the grace of his resurrection lift him up, rouse him from sleep, give him life from death, him, I say, he frees from the world, cleanses from sin, heals from his wound, bends from the hardness of ungodliness, softens to the gentleness of devotion, so that with a circumcised heart he may draw in that sweet and incomprehensible Spirit and drink the cup of eternal salvation. The rider, then, fell backward.

29. Take another reason too why he fell backward. All are behind him, no one before him. He fell, then, not for himself but for all, so that by falling upon us he might soften the hardness of our heart. He fell upon Adam, whom he was seeking, that he might find him. He sought him behind, so that as the good shepherd he might place him upon his shoulders and carry him with himself into paradise in the redemption of that thief.

30. But perhaps you may say: "The persecutors too fell backward; for so it is written, that the chief priests, whom Judas the traitor had brought, and the others who had come to seize the Lord Jesus, went backward and fell to the ground". This is what I too say, that he wished to fall upon sinners, not upon the righteous; for the righteous man has no need of a physician, but sinners have. If the shadow of the apostles healed, how much more does the flesh of Christ defend from death those whom it touches! Indeed, he touched a leper and cleansed him without delay. But that you who read may know that he wished to free even his persecutors, question the witness of Christ. For when Peter was inveighing against the Jews because they had killed the prince of life, he showed them the remedy, that they should repent and deserve to have the sin which they had committed remitted to them. Why, then, do you doubt that he fell for this reason, that he might raise up, since he himself said: I will strike and I will heal? Yet see the difference in the words. In Genesis it has: the rider shall fall backward, awaiting salvation from the Lord, that is, so that he may not turn his eyes away from God, may not bend back his own mind, may claim the prerogative of his own resurrection and raise himself up by divine power. But here it has: they went backward and fell to the ground. He who is earthly falls to the earth; he who is heavenly cleaves to heaven. Even if he falls in body, he does not fall in power, but always aims at the things that are above; for he always looks to the Father, so as to fulfil his will concerning us. Would that these too had not gone backward, but had received Christ, as Joseph received him and buried him in a new tomb! He who is faithful does not fall to the earth; he who is unbelieving falls and goes down into the lower parts of the earth, as it is written: let them go down alive into hell. Therefore the persecutor falls upon the earth and into hell; Christ falls upon those who are to rise again, Christ upon the rock, Christ upon the Church. Hear how he falls upon the Church. Peter was behind and was following him when he was being led by the Jews to the house of Caiaphas, the ruler of the synagogue. He is that Peter to whom he said: you are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my Church. Where Peter is, therefore, there is the Church; where the Church is, there is no death, but life eternal. And therefore he added: and the gates of the underworld shall not prevail against it, and to you I will give the keys of the kingdom of heaven. Blessed Peter, against whom the gate of the underworld did not prevail, against whom the gate of heaven did not shut itself, but who on the contrary destroyed the entrance-halls of hell and threw open the heavenly places. Set on earth, therefore, he opened heaven and closed the underworld.

31. This too may fit this place: that the Samaritan did not pass by that man who was going down from Jericho, wounded by robbers, but took him up and tended his wounds, pouring in oil and wine, and set him upon his own beast and brought him to the inn. Why do you doubt about the good horse, when you have read of a good beast too, when you have read that many horsemen, and very many with four-horse teams and chariots, enter into that Jerusalem? And let it not trouble you that you have read: some in chariots and some on horses, but we will be magnified in the name of the Lord our God. They have been bound and have fallen. But Elijah did not fall, who entered in a chariot; for where the name of God is, there no one is bound, but all are loosed. In the name of our God the poor man is safe and the rich, the noble and the base-born, the weak and the mighty. Riches do not prejudice faith, if only we know how to use riches.

32. And therefore, having the name of God in himself, he says: but you, O Lord, have mercy on me and raise me up, and I will repay them. This he surely does not say as though doubting of the resurrection, he who had it in his power to say: destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up, but he gives a pattern to man, that he may hope for mercy from God and resurrection from God. Next, he does not ask to be raised up himself, but asks that Matthias be raised up for him in the place of Judas, so that the apostolic number might be filled. For indeed, since the son of perdition had of necessity to perish, that the Scripture might be fulfilled, it was needful that a son of salvation be put in his place. Take a third sense. He asks that he be raised up, that is, his body; but the body of Christ is the Church. And because Judas was a figure of the unbelieving people of the Jews, which either bought or sold Christ for money — as those do who count money of more value than religion —, he signifies that in return for the destruction of the faithless people the grace of the Church will rise again for him. Whence she too says in the Song of Songs: if you stir up and raise up love, when she was seeking Christ from the young maidens, because Christ is love.

33. But as to his saying: I will repay them, we understand this, that they will be tormented by the advances of the flourishing Church, and will then know what the punishment of unbelief is, when they observe what the splendour of faith and of grace is. Or, because the Lord is good, who could say: they repaid me evil for good, and the word of repayment is a middle word, used either for good or for evil, he says that he will repay for good, because, although blindness will befall Israel in part, yet, when the fullness of the Gentiles has come in, then all Israel will be saved by the compassion of Christ.

34. And he added: by this I knew that you have desired me, because my enemy will not rejoice over me. Above he spoke of his Passion as a man; from here he now reveals his majesty: that he alone is without sin, he alone over whom the sinner could not ride, he alone whom the Father loved as his only Son. For what reason, then, did the enemy not rejoice over him? Because, although he took on death for us, yet he rose again, and himself insulted the enemy, whose victory he destroyed and whose sting of death he broke. And we, although we are sorrowful in this world, and the enemy rejoices in our grief and in the contrition of our heart and mind, yet by rising again we destroy his joy. Whence Micah too said: do not rejoice over me, O my enemy, because I have fallen, for I shall rise again. It is the resurrection, then, by which all the chains of the enemy are loosed and his every triumph is abolished.

35. There follows: but because of my innocence you have received me and have confirmed me in your sight for ever. Blessed be the Lord God of Israel for ever, from age to age; so be it, so be it. The innocent man harms no one, sins against no one. Therefore Christ is designated by the authority of divine Scripture as without sin, without guile; this, then, is declared here too, where he says: but because of my innocence you have received me. But what receiving is signified? For elsewhere too we read: you received me from my mother's womb. Since, then, he who was born of the Spirit and the Virgin was born clean of every concretion of human generation, the Father received him for this reason, because no customary concretion of bodily generation darkened his birth. He received him rising again, because no offence of a lapse had bespattered him. Immaculate he returned to the Father, from whom immaculate he went forth when he descended to earth. Whence the Father says to him: you are my Son, today I have begotten you, that is: I recognize in you the privilege of my generation, you whom no stain of sin could defile. You lived among sinners, you took up the sins of all, you were made sin for all, you were made a curse for all, but no practice of sin could pass over to you. So you lived among sinners as though you were living among angels. You made the earth to be what heaven is, so that there too you might take away sin. Today I have begotten you, in whom I am well pleased. You have proved yourself the Son, you who have fulfilled the Father's will in all things. Today I have begotten you, in whom I recognize the light of an immaculate offspring. Yesterday and today you are the same to me, and for ever. There is no night in you, because you are wholly day. Rightly is he said to be confirmed in the sight of the Father for ever, because he is always with the Father and truly in the sight of him whose brightness of glory and image of substance he is. But just as the Father is well pleased in the Son, so the Son too blesses the Father; for the Father honours the Son and the Son honours the Father.

36. Blessed, he says, be the Lord God of Israel, that is, the God of the people that sees God, which believes him to be both its Lord and its God, from age to age; so be it, so be it. In the Hebrew it has amen, amen, as those have asserted who have read the book written in Hebrew letters. The Greek in this place said γενέσθω, γενέσθω, which is "fiat, fiat" [let it be, let it be]. This word has different meanings, so that it is sometimes the word of one commanding, sometimes of one praying, sometimes of one confirming something. It is of one commanding when a superior lays down for an inferior what is to be done or followed. It is of one praying, as in that: let your will be done; for no one commands God, but pours out a prayer. It is of one confirming when a prophet or a priest or some holy man blesses and the people answer: "fiat, fiat".
