Here, then, it seems to me rather a confirmation of the blessing than a prayer or entreaty, especially because a repetition of the word itself has been made. And the Hebrew word seems indeed to have been changed, but the same sense expressed. For just as, when the priest blesses, the people answer "amen", confirming for themselves the blessing which the priest asks of the Lord for the people, so in the psalm the answer is "fiat, fiat", as if "amen, amen". And amen is clearly shown to be a word of confirmation in the Gospel, where the Lord, confirming his speech, says: amen I say to you. But the force is greater where the word is repeated, which we find quite often in the book of the Gospel according to John, because he spoke most of all of heavenly and deep mysteries, as it is written: amen, amen I say to you, unless one has been reborn of water and the Holy Spirit, he cannot enter into the kingdom of God, and elsewhere: amen, amen I say to you, if you ask anything of the Father in my name, he will give it to you. Two examples are enough; the rest, if anyone seeks, he will find.

37. "Fiat, fiat" is also a sign of a finished book. For the Psalter is seen to be divided into five books. The first book ends with this psalm, that is, the fortieth. And beautifully the fortieth psalm, reaching as far as the Passion of the Savior, was included to give the book its end, since the Lord's Passion is the end of the Forty Days, so that the second book might begin from the mysteries of renewal, a book which, as belonging to the Forty Days, embraces more perfect sacraments. For it foretells the sacraments of baptism, when it says: as the hart longs for the springs of waters, and the rest of the saints who have come to the heavenly tabernacle, and the descent of the Holy Spirit, when the spiritual grace was poured out in a kind of voice of the heavenly cataracts, because the Spirit was borne along with great power, as we read in the Acts of the Apostles, and the entrance of the renewed man to the altar, and the ascension of the Savior, and the sanctification of the virginal purpose. This book ends in Psalm 71 (72), in which the peaceful kingdom of Christ, spread over the whole world, is announced in prophetic speech, and the remission of sins; where, having first set down the blessing of the Lord, he added: and the whole earth shall be filled with his majesty. So be it, so be it. The third book, too, ends with Psalm 88 (89), and there it has: blessed be the Lord for ever. So be it, so be it. The fourth book ends with Psalm 105 (106), and there it has: blessed be the Lord God of Israel from age to age; and all the people shall say: so be it, so be it. The fifth book runs to the end, where in place of this word the prophet says: let every spirit praise the Lord.

38. But perhaps it may again trouble someone how I assert five books, when the Psalter is one. But the Gospel too is one, and we cannot deny that there are four books. That the Gospel is one we have also indicated elsewhere, if I am not mistaken, and, if it is asked again, we can easily show it, since the Savior said: amen I say to you, wherever the Gospel shall be preached, and the Apostle: I wonder that you are so soon being transferred to another gospel, which is not another, except that there are some who pervert you, and elsewhere: for I make known to you, brethren, the Gospel; and nowhere do you find this body of four books signified otherwise than as the Gospel. Indeed, because the psalms too are one body, Scripture said: on a ten-stringed psaltery, with a song on the lyre. But again, because there are five books, it said elsewhere: on the vessels of psalms.

39. Beautifully, then, there are five books and one Psalter, since the outer man is quickened by five senses, and again by five spiritual senses that hidden man of the heart, our inner man, is perfected, who alone is found rich before God, on the testimony of the apostle Peter. He who has, he says, ears to hear, let him hear. You have one sense. And just as there are those who have eyes and do not see, so there are those who, with eyes that do not see, are believed to see more. Whence the prophets too were called "seers", even those who did not see with their eyes. Good too is that odour of which the Apostle said: we are the good odour of Christ to God, whence Job too says: and the divine spirit which is in my nostrils. The same, then, is the odour of Christ and of the Holy Spirit to God, because the odour of the Trinity is one. There is also an inner food, of which the Lord said: my food is to do the will of my Father who is in heaven. There is also an inner touch, by which she touched Christ in the Gospel who for twelve years had suffered a flow of blood and had not received healing from physicians, but received it from Christ. It is the touch of faith by which Christ is touched. Indeed, men did not see it, but Jesus saw it, and testified that he had seen it, saying: someone has touched me; for I too perceived that power had gone out from me. The knowledge of Christ is the bestowal of health. Since, then, it is plain that there are ten senses in man, therefore David, who sang both outwardly and inwardly, outwardly with the body, inwardly with the heart, says in the psalm: on a ten-stringed psaltery I will sing psalms to you.

40. The psaltery, then, is the man perfected in Christ, in whom, just as the strings of the chords resound by the art of those who play in concert, so the tuneful works of the virtues in accord resound, so that he can say: I will sing psalms to you, O God, on the lyre, Holy One of Israel. My lips shall rejoice when I sing to you, and my soul, which you have redeemed. Paul says of himself: without were fightings, within were fears. This man rejoices both within and without, but that one was still in the contest, this one at rest, because he had known that he had merited the redemption of his soul. He who sings psalms is in gladness; he who wrestles is in anxiety. Yet Paul too knew how to sing psalms, but he was still reserving himself for further advances, until, when he had been more fully approved, he might sing psalms. And therefore he said: I will pray with the spirit, I will pray with the mind also; I will sing psalms with the spirit, I will sing psalms with the mind also.

41. And since he has shown a twofold office of singing psalms, Scripture has also taught that there is a twofold voice, the prophet saying: with my voice I cried to the Lord, and my voice to God, and he attended to me. What is "my"? For he could have said: "I cried to the Lord". But what voice is ours if not that which is better, which knows not how to err? For from much speaking we incur sin and cannot avoid it. That is the prophet's voice which reaches God, the voice which cried to the Lord while Moses was silent, with which Hannah too cried in her prayers, when she did not move the lips of her mouth, and obtained the son whom for a long time before she had not obtained when she cried with the voice of the body. Of this voice the Lord said: but you, when you pray, go into your chamber and, having shut the door, pray to your Father who is in secret; and your Father who sees in secret will repay you. What is "will repay"? It is that which you have entreated, that which you have prayed for. For just as sinners receive from the hand of the Lord their sins double, so on the contrary the righteous receive with effect the very things they asked. Hear how they receive them. Your prayer ascends to God and descends to you. Whence in the psalm too we read: and my prayer shall return into my bosom, that is, so that it may bring back fruit to the one who asks. Blessed be the Lord God of Israel, who has opened to us such great mysteries, from age to age, that is, from the infinite to the infinite. So be it, so be it.

On Psalm 43 (44)

1. The title of the psalm is: Unto the end; for the sons of Korah, of understanding. Now that the mysteries of the Lord's Passion, of baptism and of the approach to the most holy altar have been gone through, the holy prophet David teaches how Christ trains his athlete, whose name is to be enrolled in the contest of the saints. For just as, without labour of ours, not from works but from faith, through his grace, he forgave us our sins, and most indulgently and kindly admitted us to seek the crown, and did not let our former crimes stand against us, by which we would be judged unworthy of so great a contest — for even in those who undergo this lustral contest a discipline of living is also required —, so in turn, lest, slackened by so great a forgiveness of sins, we return to our former crimes or to sloth, this psalm warns us that many and heavy contests are set before us, so that no one is crowned except he who has contended lawfully. We must wrestle, then, against the enticements of this flesh and the heat of the blood; we must wrestle against spiritual wickednesses. No one is more grievous than an adversary of one's own household, no one more harmful than that enemy whom the power of a heavenly substance supports. When I say 'no one', remember the divine saying in which the Lord said that among those born of woman there is no one greater than John the Baptist; for he who was born of a virgin is himself above all. And therefore let us, who have Christ, fear no one, since, if Christ is for us, who is against us? Let us contend, then, unto the end, so that, when all the things which formerly fought in our body against Jesus and the Lord's will have been made void, we may, intent on the exercises of true virtue, serve our God and Lord alone. For this is the end, as it is written: Christ the firstfruits, then the end, when he shall have delivered up the kingdom to God and the Father, when he shall have made void every principality and power and virtue, so that death may be destroyed last, and we may begin to be subject to God, keeping within us the heavenly kingdom where before there was the cesspool of sins.

2. Rightly, then, it is inscribed Unto the end; for the sons of Korah, of understanding, so that in coming to know it not common hearing but spiritual understanding may be at work. Indeed Aquila has put 'a psalm of discipline'. And therefore Scripture says to you: Give your heart to discipline, and prepare your ears for the words of understanding, and elsewhere: Love wisdom and discipline. Wisdom is that which wells up from the understanding; discipline is the form of the virtues grown together into a kind of habit of nature, and the judgement of the mind strengthened by the disciplines of knowledge. Scripture has also taught us to understand obscure speech, the prudence of sayings and the riddles of Proverbs; and on its authority we gather that these are the principal virtues of prudence, in that wisdom, like a fruitful mother, is always bringing forth, discipline, like a sterner mistress, holds in check, and understanding, like a diligent searcher, finds and probes out true righteousness and judgement.

3. Let us show these things by testimonies of the sacred reading. Wisdom says: I, like a fountain from paradise, said: I will water my garden, and further I will pour out doctrine like prophecy; for like a devoted mother she pours her offspring and good thoughts into the mind of man. Take also the testimonies of discipline: My son, do not neglect the discipline of God, nor faint when you are rebuked by him; for whom God loves he rebukes. Discipline is indeed severe in rebuking, but sweet in correcting, so that we may not drift and wander astray, but be received by Christ; for discipline, as it is written, when untested, wanders. But here he has put discipline for manner of life. Yet as with wisdom, so with discipline: both the perfect and the imperfect are called by the same name; but where discipline or wisdom is named without any addition, they take the definition of perfection. Untested discipline, then, drifts, but tested discipline does not drift. Of understanding too the same Scripture gives a definition, that understanding is good to all who practise it. And since understanding holds in itself both the grace of wisdom and the order of discipline, we are surely taught that both wisdom and discipline are good to those who carry out their precepts in their own ministries. We have spoken of the title; let us take up the psalm.

4. O God, with our ears we have heard. So Symmachus. Theodotion said in our ears, as did the Seventy also. What does he mean in saying 'with our ears'? If 'we have heard' was not enough, surely, if that was what he wished, 'with ears we have heard' was complete. Since, then, 'our' has been added, what does it mean, unless that you should understand that those things are ours which belong to the mind, and that these are better than those which belong to this body? And therefore this same prophet says here, as though of another: I will not fear what the flesh may do to me, and elsewhere: With my voice I cried to the Lord, and my voice to God, and he regarded me. That is my voice which is heard by Christ, not that which resounds in public. He does not wish, then, that what is corruptible and earthly be his own, who remembers that he was created in the image and likeness of God. Indeed Scripture teaches us that man was first formed in the image of God, and afterwards made from clay. As the superior, then — for he is superior who is older —, the vigour of the soul, both pilot and ruler of this body, says: with our ears we have heard. Who is this, unless the one to whom it is said: He who has ears to hear, let him hear? For there are those who have ears and cannot hear the mysteries, of whom Jesus says in the Revelation of John: Outside with the dogs and the sorcerers and the unchaste, whose ear for hearing the lust for crimes and the rust of money has stopped up. Let us consider, then, what they say they have heard, or from whom they recall having heard it.

5. Our fathers, he says, have declared to us. Who are these fathers? The title holds that the psalm was written for the sons of Korah, to be sung by them, who had received the office of singing psalms and had been appointed to a duty of this kind. Now Korah and Dathan and Abiram, with others who had risen up against Moses and Aaron in stubborn zeal, were swallowed by a chasm of the earth in the desert and killed. When they were dead, what other fathers could have declared to their sons the wonderful works of the Lord? For into a malicious soul wisdom does not enter. If, then, they did not declare it to their own sons, much less to strangers, who would have shunned the offspring of the unbelieving. Who, then, are these fathers? See whether they are not those of whom it was said: Ask your father, and he will tell you. You ask when you read Paul; or, if you do not read him and something troubles you, inquire of him. For he is a good father, who can teach and form the Lord Jesus in us, as he himself testified, saying: My little children, with whom I am in labour until Christ be formed in you. The Evangelist John shows you these fathers, who says: I say to you, fathers, who have known what is from the beginning. These are the fathers whose old age is a spotless life. Therefore in the gathering of the elders put your finger to your mouth, so that you may hear what will profit you and come to know the sacraments of eternal life, lest, an insolent recruit, you make a din against your teachers and presume to speak when you ought still to be learning. Let us hear, then, what these fathers declared to the sons of Korah.

6. The work, he says, which you wrought in the days of old. Let us inquire what these days are in which God wrought great and wonderful things. Scripture teaches us that there are certain glorious days in which divine deeds have shone forth, since it is read that the sun shall be turned into darkness and the moon into blood, before the great and illustrious day of the Lord comes; and it shall be that whoever calls upon the name of the Lord shall be saved. For on that very day Christ rose again for men, and therefore it was said specially of it: This is the day which the Lord has made; let us exult and rejoice in it. Though God made all days, yet to this day above the rest the prerogative of the divine work has been given, the day on which all sin was taken away; but the other days are days of sins. This, then, is the day which that sun of righteousness has lit up; for righteousness itself is also wont to have its risings and its settings, whence it is also written: In his days righteousness shall arise. There are, then, days of Christ in which righteousness has arisen, there are days of Christ in which abundance of peace has arisen, there are days of Christ in which wisdom has arisen. Hear how wisdom arises: If anyone among you seems to be wise in this world, let him become a fool, that he may be wise. And therefore Wisdom, which has chosen the foolish things of this world, says: The Lord created me the beginning of his ways for his works. For as in the days of King Uzziah, in the days of Nebuchadnezzar, unbelief arose and captivity held sway, so in the days of the Lord Jesus faith arose, which has spread the splendour of its brightness and light over the whole world. For what days are better than those in which the vision of God shone upon us, as Jacob said? For thus it is written: I saw God face to face, and my soul was saved. And at once the sun rose upon him. Who is this sun, unless he who made the days of righteousness shine, in which Christ was born on earth of a virgin? Jacob saw in figure and said that he had seen God; the Jews saw in truth and did not believe. Therefore for them the days were shortened, for us lit up, since for them the day was failing, for us it was drawing near. Hence holy David, fleeing the blindness of the Jews, says: Do not lead me off at the half of my days. For the day of the Lord is great and illustrious in this way: surely not by length of time, but by the brightness of righteousness or of grace. Half-days, then, are those which are shortened by the darkness of ungodliness and the squalor of unbelief, those in which the sun sets upon the prophets, as it is written. For as the sun of righteousness rises for the righteous, so it sets for unbelievers.

7. But that saying meets us in which the Lord says: For the sake of the elect the days shall be shortened, and it seems contrary, unless you pay careful attention and go back over what precedes. For the Lord says: Then, if anyone says to you: Look, here is the Christ, or: Look, there, do not believe it; for false christs and false prophets shall arise and shall give great signs and wonders, so that even the elect, if it can be, are led into error. And therefore, so that the elect may not be deceived, the Lord admonishes what is to be followed: that we may not be caught by the speech of false prophets nor be deceived by any portentous deeds of theirs, but may believe that Christ is to come at that time when days full of righteousness begin to shine. For Christ will be revealed in the full light of his majesty, and, as the lightning goes out from the east and spreads its light over the whole world even to the west, so too the Son of Man, coming with his angels, will light up this world, so that every man may believe and all flesh be saved. Let us not, then, believe the antichrist, of whom the false prophets will say: This is the Christ; for the days of the antichrist will be days of unbelief. Let us not believe those who will say: 'Christ is in the desert, Christ is in the inner rooms'; for when Christ begins to draw near, all things are already full of Christ. But when we see the things which Christ foretold beforehand in his Gospel, then let us believe in his coming, lest, while we seek the true light, we fall into the darkness of unbelief.

8. The signs of unbelief, then, are to be guarded against, by which the days are shortened and lessened. For the sun will be darkened, the stars will fall, that is, the righteous of God; for there are those who shine like stars in heaven. When you see these things, believe that Christ is still delaying; for where Christ is, there faith is brighter; where the antichrist is, there are half-days. Of these the prophet David would surely not complain, if it were to be referred to shortness of time, since he himself said elsewhere: Woe is me, that my sojourning is prolonged! How could he grieve either that that heavenly sojourning was put off or that this earthly one was drawn out, he who had earlier begged not to be led off at the half of his days, if shortness of days seemed to bring a shortcut for hastening this course? Hence it is also gathered how we ought to understand that saying: Honour your father and mother, that you may be long-lived upon the earth. For many who honour them are often snatched away quickly, so that they die at an unripe age; many too who show less deference to their parents enjoy the wages of a long-lived old age; and unless we take that long life as the life eternal, Scripture is found to be left without the support of truth. Hence the Greek has said more expressly: that you may be of many days, that is, πολυήμερος; for whoever keeps the devout duties of worship with diligent service, being a stranger to the darkness of night, dwells in the light of days. And therefore of him who reads Deuteronomy, he shall read, it says, it all the days of his life, surely not by nights, but by days, because the day shines for one who reads the mysteries of truth and the oracles of divine loving-kindness. Let us consider what follows.

9. Your hand utterly destroyed nations, and you planted them; you afflicted peoples and drove them out. We know indeed that the Lord uprooted and overthrew many nations, so that the people of the Jews might find a possession for itself, the lands of which nations the Lord declared he would give to the seed of Abraham. But since this psalm proclaims the Gospel of the Lord and the times of his coming, it seems to me not to recount what was done earlier by the Jews, but to mark out what was to come: how the people of the nations would believe. It confirms, then, the faith of the Church before it announces it, and recounts the victories of its devotion, which drove out savage nations not by its own arm nor by its own sword, and put hostile squadrons to flight not by warlike combat, but by gentleness and faith possessed the lands of its enemies without any bloodshed; for faith alone fought. And therefore it earned triumphs which unbelief could not reverse but only increase, because the Church of the Lord is not conquered by its persecutions, but proved. Let us learn, then, what these nations are which the Church has conquered. I speak old names, but new mysteries: they are the Canaanites, the Hittites and the Amorites, the Perizzites, and the rest whose names we will set out below. These are names of peoples, but they are the infirmities of the passions and the incitements and reproaches of human sins. What comes first, then, is this: man in Christ tamed himself and conquered himself, so as to live for himself; for he lives for himself who lives for God, so as to live the eternal life of Christ. The people of the Church, then, did not fight with military arms and iron weapons, as the people of the Jews fought. That people fought in figure, we fight in the spirit; that people fought against foreigners, we have a war within ourselves, and therefore the passions of our own body must first be conquered by us.

10. Hear the apostle Paul fighting against the nation of the Canaanites: I see, he says, a law of my flesh fighting against the law of my mind and making me captive in the law of sin. Now our flesh raises itself up, now it casts itself down; it raises itself against the mind, it casts itself down before power, and, when it sees itself pressed, it abandons its constancy and, ready for subjection, is led along in the law of sin, so as to abandon faith, succumb to unbelief and, under the sway of Hittite weakness, destitute of the truth, yield to falsehood, serve crime, acquiesce in error and, like an Amorite, speak bitter things instead of sweet; for faith is sweet, unbelief bitter. Hence Peter excellently expressed what faith is, saying: Lord, to whom shall we go? You have the words of eternal life, and we believe. How then do you bid us depart from you? For, captivated by the sweetness of the heavenly words, he did not wish to depart from Christ; but he who denies Christ separates himself, or is separated, from Christ. But neither the crafty disputation of the Amorites, which through philosophy leads some away from the truth, nor the base abjectness of the Jebusites, which subjects itself and lies down beneath those who run the worldly course, a course which cannot be perpetual but fails at the gates of death, from which the prophet David rejoices that he has been lifted up, nor the Kenite, who is possessed through his money, as the avaricious man is through his insatiable craving, nor the Kenizzite, who thinks that there is a perpetual possession in riches, sets a vain hope in perishable things and boasts himself in an empty opinion, nor the Rephaim, who professes to bring medicine to others when he cannot cure his own wounds. And therefore, if anyone takes refuge with a physician of this kind, he must first consume his whole patrimony before receiving any progress toward health, like that woman in the Gospel who for twelve years could not stanch the flowing blood of bodily pleasures, until after she took refuge with Christ. For she understood that no one is a perfect physician except him who had come down from heaven, of whom she had learned that it was said: He sent his word and healed them, and she noticed that it was said of him: Behold, I have given my words into your mouth and set you today over nations and kingdoms, to root up and to destroy and to ruin and to pull down and to plant. For although God has frequently spoken in the prophets, yet in whom has he spoken more expressly than in his own Son, who, expressing all the power of the Father, says: My doctrine is not mine, but his who sent me? Not Jeremiah, then, who underwent exile in the time of the captivity, but the Lord Jesus by his words rooted out the vices of the Gentiles from their inmost hearts, destroyed the unbelief of the nations and the depraved inventions of crafty thoughts, and abolished every trace of iniquity. Then afterwards he poured in faith and the discipline of continence, lest the sacraments of the virtues should turn sour in a confusion of vices, as in a tainted vessel.

11. Hence the Apostle says to you beautifully: But death reigned from Adam to Moses. What is Moses but the Law, since he himself is the interpreter of the Law? But the end of the Law is Christ Jesus. Sin, then, reigned in this world, and in sin death, cruel and intolerable, a kind of punishment of sin. Moses indeed taught us to lift up our hands to the Lord, establishing the discipline of devout worship. He taught how Amalek the crafty-tongued was to be conquered: that we should lift up our conduct and our works to Christ; that thus unbelief could be destroyed; but if we were to cast down our soul, bend our affection and turn our mind away from the pursuit of continence, so that vain persuasion overcame us, there would be no remedy, unless Jesus raised up the already lowered arms of Moses, that is, the weakness of the Law, and sustained them by his mercy. But the help of the Law would still have been weak, had not Jesus himself come to earth, to take our infirmities upon himself, he alone whom our sins could not weigh down, nor could they bend his hands, who bent himself even unto death, and the death of the cross, on which, spreading out his hands, he raised up the whole world that was about to perish, lifted up those who lay low, and drew to himself the faith of all nations, saying to a man: Today you shall be with me in paradise. This, then, is to destroy and to plant: that vicious things are rooted out and better things planted in the hearts of each. Of this Moses says excellently in the song of Exodus: Bringing them in, plant them in the mountain of your inheritance, in your prepared dwelling, asking that the Lord would bring his people into those nurseries of lofty virtue and wisdom, and that there it should be planted for his works and instructed in the disciplines of the heavenly precepts, and that in it he should prepare for himself the dwelling of his sanctification, which the Lord deigns to confer surely not by hereditary right nor in consideration of our merits, but according to his grace. For how could we return to the place where we were unable to remain, unless upheld by the privilege of eternal redemption?

12. And therefore the prophet said beautifully: for not by their own sword did they possess the land, and their own arm did not deliver them, but your right hand and your arm and the light of your countenance, because it was well pleasing to you in them. What, then, is the land which is not taken by the arm, not possessed by the sword, unless the land of promise, of which this same prophet said: I believe that I shall see the good things of the Lord in the land of the living? Not undeservedly, then, is its possession so glorious, in which not the adversities of the world hold sway, but the eternal good things of the Lord bear fruit. And therefore it is not acquired by the sword but possessed by meekness, as the Lord Jesus testified, saying: Blessed are the meek, for they shall possess the land. Let no one, then, be unpeaceable, no one arrogant or proud; but let him be meek and humble of heart, claiming nothing for himself but referring all to grace; let him not glory in his own strength, but believe that he is protected by the right hand of God, saying: The right hand of the Lord has wrought power, the right hand of the Lord has exalted me; let him remember too that he has been preserved by the sword of God, and enlightened also by the countenance of almighty God; in all of which Christ is protector, right hand, defender and sword. Hence Simeon also said to Mary: And a sword shall pierce through your own soul too. For the sword of God is the Word, which, sharper than any sword, pierces even to the divisions of the soul and its thoughts, so that no hidden things escape its knowledge. Christ is also the splendour of the Father's countenance, and therefore he says: He who sees me sees the Father also, being the splendour of his glory and the image of his substance. To him, then, all things are rightly to be referred. To him Moses, to him Aaron, to him also the fathers consecrated all that they had done, so as to triumph over their enemies. For it was not in his own confidence that Joshua son of Nun said: Let the sun stand still over Gibeon, but because he presumed on Christ, whom he recognized as the leader of the heavenly host and suppliantly adored. And therefore he merited to root out savage nations and to bring the people of the fathers into the land of promise, because he ascribed nothing to his own works, believing that the works of men are unworthy of so great a reward of heavenly glory, which the Lord has determined to confer on those who believe in him out of his own mercy rather than in consideration of our deeds. Hence Abraham too believed God unhesitatingly, so as to find with him the grace of justification rather than to lay up the wage of his own work; for the gifts of the giver are richer than the wage of the worker. For this reason too the Lord replied to him who had begrudged the equality of the wage: If I am good, why is your eye evil? Our fathers, then, as being near to the patriarchs and their heirs, when planted in the land of promise did not claim this for their own merits. Therefore neither did Moses bring them in, lest this be thought to belong to the Law, and not to grace; for the Law examines merits, grace looks to faith. Hence the Apostle, following the faith of the fathers, says excellently: But neither is he who plants anything, nor he who waters, but God who gives the increase. For not Joshua son of Nun, because he brought them in or because he planted them, but God, who gave the increase to the people, holds the primacy of glory.

13. Nor let it trouble you that he said above: Your hand utterly destroyed nations, and you planted them. For from this you gather that not everyone who plants or who waters gives the increase, but he who can give the increase can himself plant, as the Lord is said to have planted nations. For he planted who gave the planting its effect, yet in those who have merited to please the Lord through faith in Christ, to whom alone it is said by God the Father: You are my Son, in you I am well pleased. Those, then, who are partakers of Christ obtain from him the grace of pleasing. And he says beautifully: it was well pleasing to you in them, so that the distinction may be seen to be rightly kept. For God is rightly well pleased in the Son, because he is equal to the Father and is found unlike in nothing, because he is well pleased by right of the divine nature and by unity of substance. And rightly the Father is well pleased in the Son, because the Father is honoured in the Son as the Son is in the Father. But in us it was well pleasing to God, because he himself granted us to be able to please him. And Scripture has devoutly taught us that this too was granted specially to man, not arrogantly usurped. For it is fitting that it should be well pleasing to God in those whom he made after his likeness and whom he willed, through his image, to have the prerogative of heavenly grace. In his image, then, God is well pleased; but in those who are according to his image it is well pleasing to God. And in them is his own boon and his own gift, which will be revealed when that which is perfect has come, because, when it has appeared what we are, we shall be like him, as it is written.

14. He, then, who presumes not on his own arm, that is, on his own working, but on the grace of God, believing that it is not their own deeds that justify each man but a ready faith, says to the Lord: You are yourself my king and my God, who command the salvations of Jacob. It is no perfunctory word, that you should say: You are yourself my king. Not just anyone says this, but he in whom is the kingdom of God; and in whom God reigns, surely in him sins do not reign; for God's kingdom has nothing in common with crime. And who calls God his own, except him who shows him a disposition full of reverence and devotion? For to the righteous man it is said: I am your God. Indeed Thomas too, when he touched the side of Christ with his own hand and found the evident signs of an undoubted resurrection, answered: My Lord and my God: Lord, because he redeemed his little servants; God, because he not only rose again, but also raised himself up. And he commanded the salvations of Jacob, because in each of the oracles there is salvation, in each of the mansions too there is salvation, salvation in the ministries of the angels who are appointed for the protection of men. Salvation, then, is conferred on man by the command of God, not by his own working. For God preferred that salvation should be sought by man by faith rather than by works, lest anyone should glory in his own deeds and incur sin. But he who glories in the Lord acquires the fruit of devotion and escapes the crime of boasting.

15. This, then, is he who can say: In you we will toss our enemies. What is 'to toss'? The Lord gave horns to many animals, so that they might defend themselves from the attack of wild beasts. And therefore the ox often resists the lion and tramples the bear; the stag too, though it is timid, yet also protects itself with its horns; the ram too butts the wolf. The animals, then, which have horns are said to toss. But man has no horns: how then does he toss? Hence see what Scripture says: In you, it says, we will toss our enemies. You are our horn, Lord Jesus, and therefore, as we do not presume on our own arm, so we have our defence for tossing not in our own horns, but in Christ. For faith has its own horns, which it borrows from Christ. Indeed not idly do we read in the blessings of Moses: May he who was seen in the bush come upon the head of Joseph and upon his crown, the firstborn glorified among his brothers. The beauty of a bull is his, the horns of the unicorn are his horns; with them he will toss the nations together even to the end of the earth. These are the ten thousands of Ephraim and these the thousands of Manasseh. Who was seen by Moses in the bush, unless the firstborn Son of God, who says: I am the God of your fathers, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob? He showed himself to human sight for this reason, that he was to come so as to be seen by all. The bush burned and was not burnt up for this reason, that he was minded rather to burn this earth, which sprouted thorns and briars for us, through the discipline of continence, than to consume it through the hardship of death. Hence the prophet says: Burn my reins and my heart. He revealed, then, a certain sign of a bodily splendour to come, by which this flesh would shine through the resurrection. For what did the harmless fire signify but the lights of those who rise again? Hence the Apostle presumed to say that, as star differs from star in brightness, so also is the resurrection of the dead. May he, then, he says, come upon the head of the tribe of Joseph, to exalt the head of his people; may he come also upon the crown, to pass over the crown of the hair, so as to cut off the superfluities of sins and to sanctify the ornaments of the virtues.

16. This is he who is glorified among his brothers, as he himself says: I will declare your name to my brothers. What greater glory is there than to pour the knowledge of the divinity into the hearts of the nations? He it is whom Joseph saw in a vision, in that the sheaves of his brothers adored his sheaf, and the sun and the moon with the stars adored him. That sheaf is taken as the figure of the flesh; for all flesh is grass. He took up this grass of ours, so as to make his own wheat sprout and bring forth the fruit of the resurrection. Do you seek a testimony? Hear him saying of himself: Unless a grain of wheat falls into the ground and dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it will bring forth much fruit. Indeed, so that you may know that Moses speaks of the Son of God, he says: the firstborn glorified among his brothers. Surely Joseph was not the firstborn among the sons of Jacob, but Reuben; for Joseph came after very many. But he is called the firstborn who was to come to gather together the peoples of the nations.

17. Hence Scripture says beautifully: The beauty of a bull is his. For as the bull leads the herd, so Christ led the people of the Gentiles to the Church, led them into the pastures, so that it could say: In a place of pasture, there he has set me; by the water of refreshment he has established me. The herds, according to the capacity of our frailty, have followed the beauty of this bull going before them, so as to gain eternal life. This bull bellowed, and death fled. The lion roars, and who does not fear? Truly the bull is the greatest victim, to take testimonies from our adversaries; for what greater victim is there than that which cleansed the sin of the whole world by its own blood? Hear that the holy prophet Jacob too calls him a bull, when he revealed the Passion of the Lord, whom the Jews afterwards persecuted: In their fury, he says, they hamstrung the bull. And to make plain that he was speaking of the Jews, he added: I will divide them in Jacob and scatter them in Israel. This is the man who wrestled with Jacob and touched his thigh, at which touch the patriarch's sinew grew numb, signifying that he would come from his line according to the flesh, he who was to be hamstrung by the people of the Jews in the passion of his body. Not understanding this mystery, they decreed that the sons of Israel should not eat the sinew. And therefore they cheated themselves of the redemption of the sacred blood, and renounced the benefits of the saving Passion too, so that they do not merit eternal life, because it is written: Unless you eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink his blood, you will not have life in yourselves. But he who eats my flesh and drinks my blood will have eternal life. Take another point. What is the sinew but that by which the whole body is bound together? The body of Christ is the Church, which is bound by the bond of charity. What else did the Jews loose in Christ but every bond of charity? Rightly is Peter chosen as priest, who bound himself with a threefold spirit of divine charity.

18. Take this further, that Moses signified him. He added: The horns of the unicorn are his horns; with them he will toss the nations. But do not fear because he tosses; for he has said: I will strike and I will heal. Good horns, with which he has enclosed the world; good horns, with which he tossed that lion, our adversary; good horns, by which he has brought it about that we cannot fear the horns of the adversary; for Satan too has his horns, as Daniel testified, saying: I saw also that horn which made war with the saints and prevailed against them, until the Ancient of Days came. This is the Ancient of Days. And who is the one-horned but the only-begotten Son of God and the only Word of God, which was in the beginning with the Father? This Word with its horns put to death and brought to life the peoples of the nations, so that they became the ten thousands of Ephraim even to the ends of the earth, and the thousands of Manasseh, because the people of the nations was to believe in him, which would fill the whole world, and afterwards the people of the Jews too was to believe, turned from forgetfulness to grace; for it forgot its own salvation, since it was to turn to Christ so late. Therefore Saul is among the thousands, David among the ten thousands, because the one is harsh among few, the other gentle among many.

19. Yet I do not refuse if anyone thinks that The firstborn, the beauty of a bull is his was said as of Joseph, and thinks it should be punctuated so, because he gave horns to his saints too. For he exalted the horns of his people, and Hannah, the mother of the prophet Samuel, says: My heart has exulted in the Lord, my horn is exalted in my God. Joseph too surely had spiritual horns in the Lord. Let Joseph, then, be understood, so long as there is in him the figure of Christ, to whom it is said: Joseph, my son increased, my younger son; return to me. For it is not Joseph who returns to Jacob, but Christ, raised from the dead, who returns to God the Father, as it is written: His going forth is from the highest heaven, and his meeting even to its height. Yet it may be taken of Joseph: return to me, because the people returns to the land of the Canaanites, among whom the patriarchs had dwelt before. Hence Isaac says to Jacob: You shall not take a wife from the daughters of the Canaanites, and sends him into Mesopotamia, to find himself a wife there. Did the lords of the arrows lie in wait for him? Was it he who, because of the blessing of his mother's womb, was blessed in all the world, and not the Lord Jesus, whom a virgin bore? And therefore he was blessed not only above men but also above angels and archangels and above all the sublimity of the heavenly powers, as Scripture testifies, saying: Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord; the Lord is God, and he has shone upon us. For the Lord God himself is King of kings and Lord of lords among the things that are in heaven. Because of these horns of the Lord Jesus, David too, singing with the voice of the horn trumpet, was heard by Christ, and Moses taught that the rites of the sacrifices were to be celebrated with the sound of the trumpet, as it is written: Sound the trumpet at the beginning of the month. For whom was this written but for us? For the ancients were wont to reckon the months from the beginning of the moon. But the moon is the Church, which is lifted up by spiritual and evangelical preaching in abundance of peace, as the prophet said. In Christ, then, we will toss our enemies.

20. And in his name we will spurn those who rise up against us. What is a name but that by which each one is properly signified, which he does not have in common with others? For the word man is a common term; unless you add what he is called, he cannot be made known. A name, then, is the property of each one, by which he can be understood. Hence I think that Moses too, wishing to know what is proper to God and something special about him which was not common to the heavenly powers, asked: What is your name? Indeed God, knowing his mind, answered not with a name but with the reality, that is, he expressed the thing, not the appellation, saying: I am who am, because nothing is so proper to God as always to be. Let those, then, who deny that Christ is coeternal with the Father see that they deny God, whose property it is always to be and never at any time not to have been. By this knowledge of the divine property, then, Moses wished to spurn those who rose up against him, or, as Aquila or Symmachus said, to trample on those who resist him, like serpents and scorpions, which the Lord also says in the Gospel are to be trodden down with spiritual footsteps, so that they cannot obstruct our way, which leads to the secret places of paradise. We have, then, in ourselves the zeal for divine knowledge, we have the Word of God, which is the name of the Father. This truly is the property of God, that he is the Father of Christ, and therefore he who came to do the will of the Father came in the name of the Father. And therefore he says: I have come in the name of the Father, and you have not received me; if another comes in his own name, him you will receive, signifying that the Jews, who would not believe in Christ, would believe in the antichrist. He, then, who believes in Christ does not hope in his own bow nor in his own sword, but places the hope of victory in his name.

21. And therefore he says: for I will not hope in my bow, and my sword will not save me, although we must hope in a bow, but in the bow of God which he set in the cloud, so that the human race might cease to fear a flood. With this bow he protects us, the bow with which he also shoots the arrows with which he strikes our enemies and adversaries.

22. Hence he also says: For you have delivered us from those who afflict us, and you have confounded those who hate us; in God we shall be praised all the day, and in your name we shall confess for ever. Hear how he delivers us: the inner parts of our earth are pierced by the arrows which he shoots from heaven, so that every earthly motion may die away and be still, as he says in a later passage: From heaven you shot judgement; the earth trembled and was still. So, then, we are delivered, while the hostile and adverse powers are confounded, or the wisdom of this flesh, blunted by the heavenly commands, blushes at its own crimes and is confounded with shame at its own misdeeds: the adulterer in the flames of his lust and the fire of his insane love, the avaricious man in the heat of his immoderate greed, who, in seizing what belongs to others, desires the more the more he has invaded, the drunkard in his drunkenness, the criminal in his crime. These are confounded most grievously even here, but they will be confounded much more when they see the saints of the Lord rising again and that Gospel saying fulfilled which the Lord spoke: Many of those who sleep in the tombs of the earth shall rise again, these to eternal life, and those to reproach and everlasting confusion.

23. When, then, they begin to be raised up to everlasting confusion, the saint of the Lord will say: In God we shall be praised all the day, and in your name we shall confess for ever. The rich man is praised in his riches, the voluptuary in his banquets, the fornicator in the night, the powerful man in this life, which has nights; but the saint will be praised not in this life but in God, he who has longed to please the Lord in all things, who can say: The Lord is my strength and my praise. He will be praised all the day, because he acted honourably and did not hide his works and his crimes, but laid them open to the eternal King, walking in the day, not in darkness and hidden places. Hence the Apostle too says: Let us walk honourably as in the day, that is, not in taverns and hovels, not in chambering and impurities, in which the shameless man thinks he is covered by walls so as to be able to escape the knowledge of God, whereas the crimes of all are unveiled before God, and the sins of men cannot be hidden from the divine knowledge. The saint, then, will be praised in the time to come, because he sought not the praise of present things, but the grace of things to come.

24. See, however, the difference. While he is here, he glories in the Lord; there he will be praised in the Lord; in return for a short time he will receive the reward of eternity. Aquila, however, put: In the Lord we shall glory all the day; Symmachus says: To God we shall sing a hymn through every day; the sense of all agrees, that we should not cease from the praises of God, whether by singing a hymn or by singing together the divine glory at every moment. For he who sings a hymn sings with a pure heart and spiritually, and shuts out every kind of human passion, so that his office may be hindered by no grief, by the bitterness of no pain, nor his disposition be exasperated, but that, singing a hymn to God, he may persevere unmoved and unshaken, like David himself, who says: I will bless the Lord at all times; his praise is always in my mouth, like Job, who, when his sons were lost, all his household slain and his means plundered, said: The Lord gave, the Lord has taken away; blessed be the name of the Lord.

25. But now you have cast us off and confounded us, and you will not go forth, O God, in our powers. Since holy David himself has said in another place: For the Lord will not cast off his people, how does he say in this place: You have cast us off, especially when he says this of the people for whom he recounts no ordinary marks of their own virtue? For it is no ordinary thing to say that storms and various temptations came upon them and they did not forget the Lord their God and did not deal unjustly in his covenant, and the rest that follows. Hence the holy Apostle too interprets it and says that the Lord did not cast off his people, so much so that he set him down as saying: All the day long I have stretched out my hands to an unbelieving and contradicting people. Supported by the authority of this saying, he says: I say then: has God cast off his inheritance? By no means; for I too am an Israelite, of the seed of Abraham, of the tribe of Benjamin. God has not cast off his people whom he foreknew. But it can be said in this place that he did not cast off the people for this reason, that the remnant according to the election of grace has been saved. And he seems indeed not to have cast off the people in the few, yet to have cast off, if not the whole people, still very many of it. Hence he added, setting down the answer of God, that he not only preserved his people in a few, but even left for himself seven thousand men who had not bowed the knee before Baal. Granted that this was said by the Apostle according to the Gospel, since the Lord had passed over to the Gentiles; but let the time of the divine answer be referred to a zeal of faith still fervent, when the grace of the prophets was still overflowing to the people of the Jews. Hence, if in the time of Elijah the Lord denied that his people had been cast off by him, much more in the time of David or of the sons of Korah, when the faith of the chosen people still shone brightly.

26. Yet let us question holy David himself too, so that we may gather how the Lord did not cast off his people. For since God is one who draws near, not one who withdraws far off, as the Scripture of the Old Testament has it, surely he who draws near does not cast off. For it belongs to the divine mercy to be a cause of death to no one and to think that all are to be redeemed. But see whether it is not he who is cast off who withdraws himself far from the Lord, as David says: For behold, those who withdraw themselves far from you shall perish; you have destroyed all who commit fornication away from you. What is 'commit fornication away from you'? That with the mockeries of their own shamefulness they leave you and depart from you and stray from your precepts. Indeed elsewhere you have it written: But adulterers God judges, destroying them from himself, that is: by estranging them from you, you have destroyed them. He, then, who separates himself from the Lord is himself separated from the Lord, and he who does not know the Lord is not known by the Lord, and he who ignores is ignored, as the Lord himself said: Depart from me, all you workers of iniquity. I do not know you; for he does not deign to know the ministers of injustice and the authors of iniquity. The sinner, then, is not cast off, because he casts himself off. In this sense, then, he says: But now you have cast us off and confounded us.

27. How God casts off, however, the following verse explains: you will not go forth, it says, O God, in our powers. When God does not help, he seems to cast off; for each one who does not feel the aids of the divinity conferred on him by its assistance thinks himself cast off and deserted. But what does he mean in saying: you will not go forth, O God, and what does it mean that God is sometimes said to go forth or to go out or to arise or to come down? For God, who is above all things, is not moved bodily, nor goes out from some place, nor passes to another, nor truly arises, as though he were lying on some couch or sitting bodily on some seat; but these things are said so that you may understand when, and to whom, he is said to go out, and to whom to arise. For we read in this very psalm: Arise; why do you sleep, O Lord? And we must consider whether he does not sometimes seem to fall asleep because of our blameworthy acts, as we read in the Gospel that, when the apostles were still imperfect, Jesus Christ was asleep to them. But when they were struck with fear and dread of shipwreck, they woke the sleeping Christ. To the unbelieving, then, the Lord Jesus sleeps; to the faithful he keeps watch.

28. Indeed he who is faithful both enters in to God, and from the negligent God seems to go out and depart. Certainly the Lord draws to himself the imperfect and those still weak, as you have read the young maidens saying: Draw us; let us run after the fragrance of your ointments. But Moses, who was already stronger and chosen by the Lord, is read both to ascend and to enter in to the Lord his God, as it is written: For Moses went up into the mountain, and light surrounded the mountain. And the majesty of the Lord came down upon Mount Sinai, and the cloud covered it six days; and the Lord called Moses on the seventh day from the midst of the cloud. And the appearance of the majesty of the Lord was like a burning fire on the top of the mountain before the sons of Israel. And Moses entered into the midst of the cloud. As, then, Moses here entered into the cloud when he was first called to the mountain by the Lord, so later, being more perfect, when he entered into his tabernacle, the pillar of cloud came down to him and stood at the door of the tabernacle. And all the people, standing, adored, each one at the door of the tabernacle. But the Lord is in the cloud; for he has made darkness his hiding place. And so that you may know that he is speaking of the Lord: And there spoke, it says, the Lord to Moses. As for the record that the tabernacle of Moses was set outside the camp, so that he might meet God, this does not seem to be contrary; for he who seeks God, as it were, enters in to God, and with his whole mind goes in to the knowledge of him.

29. The righteous man, then, enters in to the Lord, as Moses entered into the cloud; and God is in the cloud. Indeed, behold, the Lord comes on a light cloud, and in the Gospel you have it written: But when you pray, enter into your chamber, and, having shut the door, pray to your Father in secret; and your Father who sees in secret will reward you. Within, then, as in your chamber, is God. Hear also elsewhere that God is within: Behold, he says, the Lord will go forth from his place and will come down and will ascend upon the high places of the earth, and the mountains shall be moved beneath him and the valleys shall melt. Scripture says this, and already then it signified that he would forsake the people of the Jews and pass over to the Gentiles. Indeed we find this expressed in a psalm too, when David says: And he, like a bridegroom going forth from his bridal chamber, exulted like a giant to run the way.

30. In these ways, then, we have found the going out of God or his going in, as the Lord Jesus too says that he comes to the man who fears him: Behold, he says, I stand at the door and knock; and if anyone hears my voice and opens the door, I will come in to him and will sup with him, and he with me. We have said these things, then, because the prophet says: You have cast us off, and you will not go forth, O God, in our powers.

31. In like manner let us also take this: that God went forth in the powers of the Jews when Joshua son of Nun adored the leader of the heavenly host, and therefore conquered without iron and destroyed without any siege the city of Jericho, which had very strong walls. For when God went forth in the powers of his servants, victory followed; for the angel went forth at the prayers of Hezekiah and laid low the countless forces of the Assyrians. But sometimes the Lord, as it were, abandons for a time even those whom he wishes to be crowned in the contest, so that they may conquer by faith, and their devotion may not grow slack through favourable and prosperous circumstances. Indeed, those who have fallen through a course of prosperity and the enjoyment of success are often corrected by adversity. Therefore we frequently find the Jews fallen after victory and corrected after servitude, because by prayers and lamentations they made God return to them.

32. Hence that saying too, which was spoken according to man and the affection of man, does not conflict with this view, when the Lord said: O God, my God, look upon me; why have you forsaken me? Not that the Lord was forsaken, who says elsewhere: For I am not alone, because the Father is with me; but because, according to the flesh and affection of a man who is set in a heavy contest, he seems forsaken by the Lord. Indeed not idly does Scripture say that after the first temptation the devil departed from him until a time, that is, until the contest; for when the great contest of the sacred Passion arrived, the adversary came forward again to his temptations. But man, when he is set, as it were, in uncertainty, thinks that he has been deserted by his God. But just as we take this according to the affection of the flesh, that the man whose advance Christ wrought in his own body seemed to himself forsaken, so too by his divinity he knew that he was never forsaken by the Father, for he himself said: I am in the Father and the Father in me, and indicated that he was not alone.

33. He also adds this verse: You have turned us back before our enemies. It is not well with him who is turned back, because no one who puts his hand to the plough and looks back is fit for the kingdom of God. Indeed Lot's wife, because she looked back, could not reach the top of the mountain, that is, the higher things, but was turned into salt, a substance which quickly dissolves and cannot last long in use. So are those who follow temporal things and do not follow eternal ones: both here they are made foolish by their own folly, and they cannot have the grace of things to come. Yet there are those who, though placed behind, forget the former things and stretch themselves out to those that are before, so as to be able to reach the prize. And to Peter it is said: Get behind me, Satan, so that he may be called forth to better things; and in the reckoning he is first who is after Christ. Therefore, then, 'before our enemies': because they afflict us and they pursue us, you willed that we should be behind, so that we may stretch ourselves out to higher things, so that we may always attend to what is before us, pursuing our enemies and longing to pass beyond their place.

34. Indeed Theodotion has it as the Seventy have it, that is, as this verse has been explained. Aquila has: You turned us back from the one who afflicts; Symmachus: You have ranked us last from every adversary. What are the things contrary to us but the pleasures of this world, full of delights and wantonness, and the things which are incitements to luxury? Lot's wife was deceived because she had behind her the luxury of the Sodomites and the enticements of that region. Whence was she deceived? Because she looked back behind her. And so, that you too may not look back to your enemies, God has set you last in the world; or else: he who makes himself last by humility does not turn back, but always looks before him. And so that you may know who your enemies are, hear him saying: Hear, O Israel; why is it that you are in the land of enemies? You are defiled among the dead. Yet who is so much your enemy and foe as the prince of this world and his allies, who persecuted the Lord Jesus even to the cross, not knowing his power? For if they had known, they would never have crucified the Lord of majesty. You, then, though you are last, do not follow your enemies, but follow the Lord Jesus, lift up his cross and press on in his footsteps. For he who follows Christ does not look back behind him, that is, to the luxuries and enticements of sins, and therefore he can say with the saints: Our heart has not turned back. Hence it is understood that he too who is ahead of his enemies becomes last and hindmost in heart, if he looks back to them and attends with the affection of his mind to carnal rather than spiritual things. He too who is last can be first in heart, if he considers the things which are in heaven and not those which are on earth. Hear that he who is last in the world is among the first with Christ: I think that God has shown us apostles as last, as men appointed to death, that we may be a spectacle to this world, both to angels and to men. He called himself last, and yet the same man showed that he was first, when he says: But our way of life is in heaven.

35. There follows: And those who hated us plundered for themselves; you have given us like sheep for food, and among the nations you have scattered us. Perhaps you wonder that, since in what follows, as we have said, those who speak have the highest constancy of faith, here they say that they were plundered by those who hated them; but do not let these things alarm you; for many are plundered here, and yet their merits cannot be separated from Christ. The apostles were dragged away, beaten with rods, thrust into prison, separated from one another, and yet they remained with Christ; nay, by the very fact that they were dragged away by the unbelieving, their merits grew and their grace in heaven was increased; for he who is plundered by men is not at once also conquered. See the apostle Paul rejoicing in his dangers, see him glorying that he was let down through a window in a basket. See how holy Jeremiah, holy Ezekiel, holy Daniel, though led into captivity and plundered by the Assyrians, yet suffered no captivity of their own faith, nor transgressed against the covenant of the Lord; for even in captivity they kept the precepts of the divine Law, and did not think that they should take any of the forbidden foods against the institution of their forefathers.

36. Hence Theodotion said well: And those who hated us exulted over us; for the insult of enemies does no prejudice to good minds, nor does the plundering of enemies. And therefore the saint, who is sure that plundering cannot harm him, says: Who shall separate us from the charity of Christ? Tribulation or distress? and the rest. But in all these things we overcome and more than conquer through him who loved us, knowing that neither death nor life nor angel nor powers nor things present nor things to come can separate us from the charity of God which is in Christ Jesus our Lord. He, then, who is such is not separated from Christ. Hence, even if he is plundered bodily, yet spiritually he is free, and not only is he not liable to plunder, but he even seeks for himself a praiseworthy plunder, like those of whom it is written: From the days of John the Baptist the kingdom of heaven is taken by force, and those who use force plunder it. He, then, is the victor who, though set among earthly things, knows not how to be the captive of unbelief; and not only is what is glorious in him not plundered, but rather he himself plunders what is eternal.

37. There are also those who become sheep for food. Good is our Lord Jesus Christ, because he became the sheep of our feasting. Do you ask how he became so? Hear the one who says: Christ our passover has been sacrificed, and consider how our forefathers, in figure, tearing the lamb apart, ate it, signifying the Passion of the Lord Jesus, whose sacrament we feed on daily. Through that sheep, then, they became flocks of food, as Aquila said, or flocks for food, as Theodotion put it, or the pasture of those who eat, as Symmachus said. But a good feasting not only is not to be feared by the saints, but is even to be desired. For otherwise the kingdom of heaven cannot be reached, since the Lord himself has said: Unless you eat my flesh, you will not have eternal life. It is proved, then, that our Lord is the feasting or the nourishment of those who eat, as he himself says: I am the living bread who came down from heaven.

38. And so that you may know that all these things were done for our sake, from the time when he came down, from that time the saint says: We all are one bread. Let us not fear, then, because we have become sheep for food. For as the flesh of the Lord and his blood redeemed us, so Peter too endured much for the Church; much too did the holy apostle Paul and the other apostles suffer, when they were beaten with rods, when they were stoned, when they were thrust into prison. For by that endurance of injuries and experience of dangers the people of the Lord was founded, and the Church gained its increase, while the rest hastened to martyrdom, seeing that through those sufferings nothing had been taken away from the virtues of the apostles, but rather that in return for this short life immortality had been gained for them.

39. This the following verse also shows, for they said: Among the nations you have scattered us. Theodotion said the same; but Aquila and Symmachus: in the nations or among the nations you have winnowed us. For the holy apostles were sent to the nations and scattered among the nations, as were the holy prophets too, of whom we spoke above, so that in that scattering very much fruit might spring up. For as our Lord Jesus Christ fell into the earth like a grain and died, so as to bring forth much fruit, so too the holy apostles were scattered, so as to bring good seed among the nations, by which the fruit of the nations might sprout after their likeness. Indeed Scripture says that the Lord said this: For this reason I have sent you, that you may go and bring forth very much fruit, and that your fruit may remain. The Lord, then, became like a seed, according to what was said to Abraham: and to your seed, which is Christ. Christ, then, is the seed of all, and therefore he suffered himself to fall and be scattered, so as to transfigure the body of our lowliness into conformity with his own body. This saving seed, then, sprouted for all men, and from it the holy apostles, formed after his image like seeds, were sent through various places and scattered, so that the nations, gathered into the field of the Church, might shine with various fruits throughout the whole world. These, then, are called the scatterings, as the same David also says in a later passage: The Lord building Jerusalem and gathering the scatterings of Israel. For they were scattered so as to produce new fruits, and afterwards to be carried, like new wheat, into the granaries of the Church.

40. Now this scattering takes place not in the lower regions, that is, not on earth, but in heaven. Indeed the precepts of the Law confirm this, through which the Lord says: If your scattering be from the height of heaven even to the height of heaven, from there I will gather you, says the Lord. What is the scattering from the height of heaven even to the height of heaven? Who is this so great one, who could be sown in this way? If a man is born on earth, he began from the lowest, not from the highest. If these things trouble and disturb you, go back to the earlier words of this holy prophet and hear who that great one is who made harvests of this kind fruitful on earth, so that their fruit reached the heavenly storehouses. It is that bridegroom who like a giant ran through this whole way, impassable to others, passable to himself; and from him it began to be passable now to mortals, yet so that, while these were to ascend, he himself alone came down first, so that afterwards his saints might merit to ascend. Hear, then, how the sowing was from the height of heaven to the height of heaven: From the highest, he says, heaven is his going forth, and his meeting even to its height. To what man could this be attributed? Moses scarcely ascended to the top of Mount Sinai, and that because he was called by the Lord and urged on by a heavenly voice. Who would come down from the highest heaven but Christ, who, even when he came down to earth, remained in heaven? For no one has ascended into heaven but he who came down from heaven, the Son of Man who is in heaven. You see, then, that both when he came down and when he ascended, he himself was in heaven and remained there. For he himself said: I fill heaven and earth, says the Lord; he it is who is in heaven. Let this suffice to have been said about the scattering.

41. As for his saying: you have winnowed us, though it differs in the word, it agrees in the sense. For as those who were scattered were proved, so too those who were winnowed merited to come to approval. For as wheat is winnowed and separated from the chaff and is clean, but if it has not been winnowed it cannot be clean, but stands caked and mixed with chaff, so too man, unless he has been winnowed by temptations, cannot separate from himself whatever is frail, or the chaff. Hence too it was said to Peter: Simon, behold, Satan has demanded to winnow you like wheat; but I have asked my Father that your faith may not fail. See what he says and understand: Peter is winnowed, so that he is driven to deny Christ. He fell into temptations; he spoke certain words as if full of chaff, but he spoke them with his word, so as to be more firmly founded in his affection. Indeed he wept and washed his chaff, and by those temptations he merited that Christ should intercede for him. How much greater is the patronage than the trial of that disturbance! And therefore he gained more than he suffered; for he gained Christ as his patron. But the adversary is driven to tempt the saints of the Lord to his own loss; for while he tempts, he makes them better, so that he who is tempted, who seemed weak in himself, can instruct others too. Indeed Peter is set over the Church after he was tempted by the devil. And therefore the Lord signifies beforehand what it means that afterwards he chose him as shepherd of the Lord's flock. For here he said: But you, when you have turned, confirm your brothers. The holy apostle Peter, then, turned to good fruit and was winnowed like wheat, so as to be himself, with the saints of the Lord, one bread, to be nourishment for us. For while we read the acts of Peter and learn the precepts of Peter, there comes to be for us a nourishment of eternal life and salvation.

42. You have sold, he says, your people without price. The contract of buying and selling seems to be a kind of equal one; but yet, if you consider the disposition of the buyer and the seller, each sells what is of less value to himself and buys what pleases him. For, to speak apart from that class of men who carry on a trade in selling slaves, no one readily sells anyone except one who displeases him and whom he thinks unfit for his use, and again each one desires to buy him whom he has judged suited to his advantage or his service. But even those who lay out their own expense in selling slaves and pursue gain surely hold those whom they sell so cheap that they are cheaper to them than money; and often too they prefer to keep rather than to sell those whom they have judged more fit for their trade.

43. So, then, God too sold the cheaper and bought the better. He sold the people of the Jews, not through the harshness of God but through their own fault, to whom it is rightly said: Behold, for your sins you have been sold, and for your iniquities I have put away your mother. In this way, then, the people of the Jews was sold and the Christian people bought; that one sold by sin, this one bought with blood. Hence Peter says: Not with corruptible things, silver or gold, were you bought, but with precious blood. What is the precious blood, unless that of that spotless lamb, that is, of our Lord Jesus Christ? The people of the Jews, then, is without price, but the Christian people is precious; that one has no price, because it has sin; this one is of price, because its sin has been forgiven. Hence it is rightly said to the sons of the Church: You were bought with a price; do not become the servants of men. If it is said to you that you should not become a servant of men and lose your own freedom, much more is it said to you that you should not become a servant of sin, and again, that you should not become a servant of the serpent, the enemy and adversary, but serve the Lord alone, who has redeemed you by his own charity, because he himself is the redemption of his little servants.

44. The Lord, then, sells only a bad servant; surely he does not sell a good one. For although Joseph was sold, yet the prophet did not say: 'Joseph was sold by the Lord', but: Joseph was sold for a servant, he whom his own brothers sold, but whom the Lord, in a figure of his own Passion, redeemed from servitude and renewed to grace. So his brothers sold him and received money, but the Lord not only restored him to freedom, but also heaped him with dignity. And that there was in him a figure of the Lord Jesus, Scripture shows, which says: My son, sought with jealousy, return to me, and elsewhere: The firstborn, the beauty of a bull is his, in that God the Father exalted the horn of his anointed.

45. He, then, who is sold is worthless and without price, as one who rather is not than is. Hence Aquila too said: You have handed over your people, so that it should not be, and Symmachus: You have handed over your people without substance. For those who are rejected by Christ are not; but those are who are chosen by the Lord, since the Lord called the things that are not as though they were, and the peoples of the nations were chosen so that the unbelief of the Jews might be destroyed. That people, then, was handed over to nothingness, because it was sold by its own sin; for guilt has no substance.

46. Hence I think it was said: and there was no multitude in their exchanges, that is: you have scattered us among the nations for nothing. For you do not receive anything in exchange for handing over the soul of a man; for nothing is more pleasing to you than the conversion of a soul. But if it refuses to be converted, it hands itself over to nothingness, so as to be made the chattel of sin and crime. Therefore you have sold a degenerate people without price and without exchange. Hence in a later passage he says: For there is no exchange for them. Why is there no exchange for them? Because they did not fear, he says, God.

47. There is a twofold understanding of why the people of error had no exchange. One is that man cannot redeem his soul with silver or gold or possessions. For what exchange will he give for his soul, since those things are temporal or perishable, but the soul endures either for reward or for punishment? But that exchange too which is in the resurrection did not exist in the ungodly, of which the Apostle says: We shall all rise again; we shall not all be changed. It can also be understood thus: that, although a few who were redeemed merited to be changed, like Abraham, Isaac, Jacob and Moses, yet the exchange itself did not reach all. There is also this: that there is no multitude in the exchanges of grave sins, according to what is written, that the sins of some men are manifest, going before them to judgement, and some they also follow after. Those which are grave and manifest go before and are sunk without any doubt, but those which are lighter are often relieved by good works; for blessed are they whose iniquities are forgiven and whose sins are covered. But where iniquity weighs the scale down, it is not called back by any exchange of good works.

48. And so, even if anyone of the people of the Jews has been redeemed, he was not redeemed because he was valued at a great price, nor because he had a multitude in his exchange, but he was redeemed freely, as the Apostle teaches you, saying: The remnant according to the election of grace has been saved. And where there is grace, it is surely not by the merit of works nor by the justification of virtues, but by the liberality of the giver, by the choice of the redeemer. This Scripture has equally taught you, when the apostle Paul says: But if by grace, it is no longer of works; otherwise grace is no longer grace. What then? What Israel sought, this it has not obtained. Recognize why it did not obtain it: because it desired to justify itself, because it sought to boast of its works, because it did not offer faith nor acknowledge grace. And therefore the election obtained it, which heard him who called and received him who came.

49. Rightly, then, it is said in the person of that people: You have made us a reproach to our neighbours, a mockery and a derision to those who are round about us. Who these neighbours are, and how they appear to have belonged to the neighbourhood of the Israelite people, is not easy to discern. If a spiritual neighbourhood is sought, some light of knowledge is lacking for searching out the mystery of Christ, especially since we have learned in that very book of Solomon which is entitled Ecclesiastes that the more wisdom is sought, the deeper it is judged to be. For thus it is written: I said: I will seek wisdom, and it went far from me, much farther than it was, and in its depth who shall find its height? Hence diffidence rises up in me at searching the mysteries of the divinely breathing Scripture and examining its words, which he could worthily do who spoke wisdom among the perfect.
