I see the perfect, and not undeservedly would I call you perfect, since I see that you have your senses trained for the discernment of good and evil; but even he, who knew how to take more solid food, sought aid, so that the word of the Lord might run in him. Nor could this man either claim this without prayer, that his praise should become pleasing to God; for the assertion is not of one who claims, but of one who prays.

50. Yet, so that we may not seem to have shrunk back entirely, let us consider who were the neighbours of the tribes of the Jews. For, to review old matters, the Philistines were held to be neighbours of the tribe of Judah; on another side the same people bordered on the tribe of Reuben. The Ammonites too were in the desert, and those who were in the territories of Tyre and Sidon were joined to Galilee, that is, to the tribe of Zebulun and the tribe of Naphtali, which were in Galilee, as Isaiah said: The land of Zebulun and the land of Naphtali, the way of the sea beyond the Jordan. From this one can gather what the particular character of each of the several tribes was, that they were joined to certain particular neighbours, so that the knowledge of the heavenly sacraments might gradually be poured into the nations as well. This can surely be understood of that Canaanite woman who went out from her territories to meet the Lord Jesus as he passed by and, setting aside her idols, to adore the true author of salvation; to her our Lord Jesus Christ gave testimony that her faith was great. The grace of the Gospel and the light of his august presence were indeed already shining forth; but yet, because she went out of her own accord and begged for mercy and confessed the Lord with a devout voice and, though treated as it were with disdain, persevered in crying out her petition, this woman received no ordinary prerogative, she who was the first to go out from the peoples of the Gentiles and merited by persevering supplication to carry off so great a testimony from the Lord.

51. In this passage indeed I have learned that certain people have, as it were, been in doubt as to what it means that he says: I was not sent except to the sheep that have perished of the house of Israel, as though the Lord, who came so that all might be redeemed, seemed to have sought the nobility of a chosen race for saving souls. But he deigned to show his purpose: that he by no means wished to reject those whom he had first chosen, and that, though they were often straying and wandering, he yet sought to call them back with a kind of fatherly affection. But when they themselves forsook their own Author, he, knowing that he was the God of the mountains, not of the valleys, and that the kingdom of heaven is plundered not by those who disdain it but by those who use force, turned little by little to the Gentiles, so that both the affection of the nations might be the more approved and the obstinacy of those others deservedly condemned.

52. Then what does he mean in saying: It is not lawful to take the children's bread and throw it to the whelps? Surely he called the Israelites, that is, the people of the Jews, children, and the people of the nations whelps. What then? Is it for the rich or the more noble to receive the heavenly sacraments, and not for those who press on more with devout fervour of mind toward the knowledge of the heavenly mystery? And if some Israelite is slack and negligent or obstinate, casting the words of God behind him, but a Canaanite wishes unhesitatingly to bend his mind to acquiring the sacraments, is he who is indifferent to be preferred rather than he who offers zeal? Surely not. And therefore we must attend carefully to what he expressed, who, when he had said: It is not lawful to take the children's bread and throw it to the whelps, and the Canaanite woman answered: For even the whelps of dogs eat of the crumbs which fall from their masters' table, praised her zeal and her humility, since she did not claim for herself the depth of wisdom from the start, although she had already gone out from the territories of Tyre and Sidon, but believed that certain crumbs of the saving word, falling from her masters' table, were to be gathered up. For he who diligently and minutely searches out the words of Scripture is wont to reach the height of the mysteries. Hence it is said to her too: Great is your faith. Be it done to you as you will, that is: may the door of the word be opened to you and the saving mystery of eternal life shine forth.

53. Let him, then, who is a neighbour go out and desire to see Christ and cry out in prayer, as this Canaanite woman went out from her territories and saw Christ and cried out with unceasing voice, so as to carry off his mercy. So, while the Canaanites believe, let the Jew be in reproach, because he himself did not believe, and let him grieve that he is mocked, and groan that he is derided by those who are round about.

54. And this prophecy seems to be about the nations who were to believe, especially as what follows shows it, for he says: You have made us a likeness to the nations, a shaking of the head among the peoples, because we have learned on the authority of the Apostle that through the fault of the Jews salvation came to the Gentiles, and that what was done among them in figure has served for our knowledge. And truly they were made a reproach to the Gentiles, because the Gentiles preferred the reproach of the Lord's cross to all the riches of the world. But he who thought that cross a reproach and fled from it as a reproach has truly remained in long-lasting reproach. Yet since those who could say: For we have not dealt unjustly in the covenant of the Lord, and whose heart did not turn back from the Lord, seem not to be Israelites according to the flesh, see whether they do not recall that they were a mockery and a derision to those set round about them, of whom the Lord says: Many calves have surrounded me; for the persecutors surrounded him when, according to the counsel of his own will, he underwent the Passion. Hence elsewhere too he says: Surrounding, they surrounded me, and in the name of the Lord I have taken vengeance on them. Therefore it is better to hold to the straight way, on which the wicked cannot walk; they quickly fall, because they do not walk in uprightness. Hence those men too, when they wished to seize the Lord Jesus, fell as soon as he looked at them. Because of these, then, who are wont to walk round about, the Lord has set an angel to drive back the enemies who lie in wait behind. The angel of the Lord, then, stretches himself round about those who fear him, and protects and guards them; for round about the ungodly walk, as it is written. See, then, the difference. The ungodly walk round about, but the angel stretches himself round about those who do not walk round about, and he stretches himself out for this reason, to rescue the righteous from the snares of their persecutors. Those, then, who speak these things seem to be righteous spiritual Israelites, in that they were subject to the temptations of the world and were a reproach to their neighbours, that is, to those who did not dwell in Jerusalem their homeland, and were a mockery also to the spiritual wickednesses and powers against which the righteous are wont to have their wrestling. Indeed let history teach you that the Jews carried off to Babylon, although they seemed to be subjected because of their sins, were yet held to be much better than the Gentiles. For those round about them who insulted them were worse, since they themselves were bowed down by the weight of grave sins.

55. The first understanding, then, is to be applied to our captive fathers, whom the Assyrians insulted, mocking them in Babylon, and over whom that most wicked king Nebuchadnezzar also ruled. The second understanding is to be referred to higher things: that the most savage king is the devil. He is the Assyrian Nebuchadnezzar, who is wont to lie in wait for the good, so as to draw them into sin through the enticement of the flesh. Mark this king, then, in that place where the Apostle says: I see a law of my flesh fighting against the law of my mind and leading me captive in the law of sin, that is, in that law of carnal enticement. The adversary is a lion who presses hard on the guilty and wears out the righteous. Hence Aquila put it beautifully: You have made us a scorn and a pageant to those who are round about us. For so they were reckoned contemptible who were made a theatre to this world, both to angels and to men, because, even if they do not sin against the covenant of God nor are turned away from the Lord by the enticements of the flesh, yet they seem to be carried about as in a kind of pageant and despised, since sinners are wont to insult the humility of the righteous, so as to count the cross of the Lord and Savior and the humility of the righteous a reproach. Indeed the Apostle, set as it were in a kind of pageant of the theatre, says: I am well pleased in injuries, in necessities, in persecutions, in distresses. In these he is well pleased who counts all things of the world as loss, so as to gain Christ, seeking him alone through life and death, he for whom to live is Christ and to die is gain, so that he may be found in Christ.

56. These Israelites add further, saying: You have made us a parable to the nations, a shaking of the head among the peoples. To what nations were they made so, unless those which were to believe? For the things which befell the Jews were done as a likeness, so that the destruction of the Jews should become for us a type and figure, and that from that likeness we should seek through the parable a solution for present things, by which we may guard against their examples; next, because all the affairs of the Jews are like parables, that is, like figures needing a solution. Indeed the Lord spoke to the Jews in parables, so that the unskilled might not understand and the wise might understand, as the apostles understood, and in what troubled them they questioned the Lord; but the Jews neither understood nor, again, desired to learn what they had not understood.

57. Learn what a parable is: Abraham, then, had two sons, one by the bondmaid and one by the free woman. The Jew did not understand this except according to the letter; he did not know which of the two sons received the father's inheritance, because he leaned towards carnal things. The parable needed a solution. The teacher of the Gentiles came, the faith of the nations, and understood that those two women are the two testaments: one from Mount Sinai, which bore the people of the Jews into servitude, which is Hagar; and therefore Sinai, which by interpretation signifies 'its measure'; for measure belongs to the Law, but grace is superabundant. Sinai is also called 'wage', and it suits the people of the Jews, who preferred to be justified by the wage of works rather than by the grace of faith. The Jerusalem, then, which was under the Law was in servitude with her children, but the Jerusalem which is above is free. And therefore Sarah, who is called 'princely', has freedom, because he who is righteous and upright is master of himself. From the two testaments, then, come two peoples, one of the Jews, the other of the nations, which believed in Christ. That people of the Jews which did not believe is in servitude; it is born according to the flesh, because it interpreted divine Scripture according to the flesh and according to the letter, not according to the spirit. But he who is of the free woman is born through the promise, that is, he to whom the promise comes from faith. He is the son of the Church, holding the freedom of grace, on whose account the Church says: Cast out the bondmaid and her son; for the son of the bondmaid shall not be heir with my son Isaac, and the Apostle says: So then, brothers, we are not children of the bondmaid, but of the free woman. This, then, is the solution: what the Jew did not know, the faith of the Gentiles has solved, poured into the minds of the nations by Paul the teacher. Therefore, then, their deeds are for us in a parable.

58. But the Lord too said: I became a parable to them. Those who sat in the gate exercised themselves against me; and he became a parable because, he says, you say: Physician, heal yourself. He became a parable because he rebuked those sitting in the gate; for at the going out we ought to be not idle but exercised. And therefore it is written: See that your flight be not in winter or on the sabbath, that is, not in idleness but in business. The Jews, then, sat sluggish, because those could not stand who did not know the courts of the true Jerusalem; for if they had known, they would have sought that Jerusalem. These, then, sat wearied in the gate. And he did not only say 'they sat', but also that they hated the Lord who reproved them in the gates. For he was in the gate who was about to undergo the passion of his body, through which he would depart from the earth and return to heaven. He was in the gate when he said: Walk while you have the light. What, then, is I became a parable to them, unless that Christ is the end of the Law, and his death solved the riddles of the prophets, and the things which before were unknown to the Jews when they were prophesied were afterwards made manifest by the effect of the Lord's Passion? He became a parable to them, because he offered himself as if worthless, thrusting himself upon them and not fleeing their seditions, and leading them on to the time of his good pleasure, so as to die for all men, that by his blood the race of men might be cleansed.

59. Likewise it is written in a parable: You shall not bind the mouth of the ox that treads out the corn. The Apostle solves this parable, saying: Is it for oxen that God has care? Or does he say it surely for our sake? For they were written for our sake. Who is it that ploughs, unless he who ploughs in hope, so as to reap in the judgement of the Lord? And therefore, like good husbandmen, when our earth has been ploughed for the sake of hope by bodily continence, let us sow not carnal but spiritual things, since from carnal things corruption is gathered, from spiritual things eternal life is gained. Let the deeds of our forefathers, then, be to us a type and figure; let the Law written in our hearts be a type, so that in it there may be for us a certain figure of things to come, not the truth. Let the Law, then, be to us as a figure, the Gospel as the light and seal of the truth.

60. In a parable, then, the Lord showed himself to the Jews, but they did not understand the parable, because they did not receive the physician who would heal the souls of the suffering and free them from every sickness and unevenness of ailment. He became a parable also because he took up flesh which the Jews crucified, not understanding the spiritual parable. For they understood so little that, when they were crucifying the Author of life and saw the saving flesh of the Lord hanging on the cross, they shook the head; for if they had understood, they surely would not have insulted, but would have groaned. And yet that very shaking of the head turned out for them as a likeness, so that it signified not what they meant, but what the mysteries declared. For they, according to the custom of the common people, shook their heads in insult to the Lord, as those who desire to insult the righteous shake the head if they happen to see them wearied in this world.

61. This shaking of the head has a twofold understanding. For those who shake the head seem blameworthy according to the authority of Scripture, since Matthew too says: Those who passed by blasphemed the Lord Jesus, shaking their heads, and Mark: Those who went past blasphemed him, shaking their heads. And that passers-by are reckoned blameworthy this same prophet teaches, who says of the vineyard of Sabaoth: And all who pass along the way gather its vintage. Indeed the verse that follows also marks this: The boar from the wood has laid it waste, and the solitary wild beast has fed upon it; and in a later passage he says: And those who passed along the way did not say: The blessing of the Lord be upon you, because he who passes by and does not stand in the courts of the Lord does not give a blessing. So, then, the Jews shook the head over the Lord Jesus so as to pass by, being men who could not have a firm and solid footing of faith. And they shook their own head, those whose head Christ ought to have been, whom the unbelief of the Jews ought not to have shaken off and thrust away. But because they did not suffer themselves to be members of Christ, therefore Christ was not their head. Nor was the Law their head either, because they were being stripped even of the Law by the people of the nations. The Word of God was not their head, because they had lost the prophets and did not have the apostles. Let this be said of the blameworthy shaking of the head. Yet there is also a praiseworthy shaking of the head: blameworthy for the Jews, praiseworthy for the people of the nations. For what is the head but that in which the senses are? For the eyes of the wise man are in his head; and these eyes, that is, the senses of your mind, must be moved, so that they may not grow sluggish through any torpor. Move, then, your prudence, move your wisdom, move your thoughts by comparing the heavenly sayings and discussing the prophetic words, so that you may not sin against the covenant of God, but your faith may be firm. For there are heretics who move their senses, yet so as to stir up unbelief, not faith.

62. But now we must speak more expressly of that shaking of the head itself, which has been set as a likeness of the people. Who is the head of the peoples but Christ? For the head of the woman is the man, the head of the man is Christ; but the Law too is the head of the intelligible woman. Indeed the people of the Jews is under the Law as under a husband, because law is called νόμος in Greek, and therefore the synagogue was joined to the νόμῳ as to a male, but to the bodily law, not the spiritual, that is, to the Jewish rite of the Law, by whose bonds she has been bound, not knowing the sacraments of lawful marriage. But if the Law has died, that is, the bodily interpretation of the Law, then the people, as though her former husband were dead, will lawfully marry a second husband, who rose from the dead. The Gospel is also the head of this woman. Hence certain people, interpreting mystically, have held that, when the Jewish rite was put to death, this woman married according to the Law the Gospel, as the brother of the deceased, because the Law announced the Gospel. Therefore the Law was the former husband; afterwards comes the marriage of the second husband, that is, the sacraments of the Gospel; for the two testaments are as it were two marriages. One marriage is the Old Testament, which was dissolved by the death of the former husband. And therefore that woman to whom the Law is dead rightly enters a second marriage, that is, the New Testament. So one woman is joined to two brothers, a woman for whom her former sins die and better sacraments are afterwards renewed. The Apostle has plainly set this out for us, saying: For the woman who is under a husband is bound to the law while the husband lives, that is, to the law of her husband, so as to owe him chastity. But if the husband is dead, she is loosed from the law of the husband, that is, so as to marry whom she will, provided she marries when the husband is dead, because while the husband lives she will be called, he says, an adulteress, if she is joined to another man; but if her husband is dead, she is freed from the law of the husband, so that she is not an adulteress if she is with another man. That this sacrament also pertains to Christ and the Church he showed elsewhere too, because it is a great sacrament. Here too he did not pass it over, saying: So, my brothers, you too have been put to death to the Law through the body of Christ, so that you may belong to another, who rose from the dead. How have you been put to death to the Law, unless the shadow of the Law dies for you when you approach the heavenly sacraments? For you must not approach the Gospel otherwise than loosed from the hindrances of bodily interpretation. Otherwise it is adultery to mix false things with true; but it is spiritual chastity if your soul, stripped of all the hindrances of wayward knowledge, approaches the Word of God without wrinkle. As, then, Christ is the head of the Church, so the Law is the head of the synagogue, that is, of those who are under the Law, for whose sake the Apostle made himself to be under the Law, though he was not under the Law, so as to gain those who are under the Law. And again, as teacher of the Gentiles, he became as though he himself were without law, so as to win for Christ those who were without law. And he says beautifully that he was not without the law of God and remained in the law of Christ.

63. But the Law was not the head of the synagogue from the beginning, because Christ was the head of the old synagogue which Moses founded and established. Indeed, hear one who says that it was founded by the word of the Lord: By faith, he says, Moses, when he was grown, denied that he was the grandson of King Pharaoh; for he chose to suffer with the afflicted rather than to delight in temporal things, and he esteemed the reproach of Christ greater riches and preferred it to the treasures of Egypt; for he looked to the reward of God's promises, which is the grace of the resurrection. But when, because of the unbelief of the Jews, the Word of God died to the unbelieving and fell from the hearts of the Jews, the spiritual understanding of the Law perished, and the synagogue married the bodily rite and the observance of unbelief. But that was not the sobriety of a spiritual marriage, but adultery against chastity. Therefore the synagogue could not be free, since she had married badly and had not believed that the resurrection of Christ would come. Hence the Church was rightly married to him, she for whom, according to the sacred dispensation, Christ died according to the flesh, so that that Jewish soul, which before had been bound by sin, might through the death of Christ be loosed from the bond of sins. A woman, then, does not marry another husband unless her husband has died, that is, unless she has been loosed from the bond of lawful love; but if her husband has died, she is freed.

64. She was indeed bound by the Law to be joined to the brother of her own husband, if her husband had left her no seed. This bond was tied more by the interpretation of the letter than by spiritual grace. Indeed the brother of the deceased had it in his will, if he was unwilling, not to take his brother's wife to raise up seed for the deceased. Hence he was ordered to loose his shoe, so that he might be freed from the tie of union with her whom he had shown to be estranged from his favour. For thus it is written, that he loosed his shoe and gave it to his neighbour, saying: This shall be for a testimony in Israel. And the kinsman said: Take it and acquire it for yourself. And he loosed his shoe. And the bystanders also said: May the Lord make the wife who enters your house like Rachel and like Leah, who both built the house of Israel and wrought power in Ephrathah. But this bound the Jew according to the interpretation of the letter; for the rest, it was a figure that Christ would come to earth, to raise up the seed of the dead people by right of a brother, because it was said: I will declare your name to my brothers, and: whose are the fathers, and of whom is Christ according to the flesh; or else because the Gospel would work the restoration of the dead Law in the descendants, so that the course of the Gospel might raise up the seed which the Law had not left.

65. It is no wonder, then, if they shook their head by perverse interpretation, so as themselves to thrust away from themselves Wisdom, the Author of their salvation, or at least, by shaking their own head, to become the cause of a shaking of the head not to a few but to peoples. This shaking of the head took place, since their sound went out into all the earth, so that all the earth might become the Lord's and the kingdom of the Lord be among the nations. Hence in a later passage too he says: The Lord has reigned, let the peoples be angry; you who sit upon the Cherubim, let the earth be moved. This Aquila beautifully interpreted by saying μετάστασιν, that is, a transmutation from place to place, from Judaea to the peoples of the nations. How the Law was moved can be shown clearly from that reading which is in the Gospel, where Scripture says: The scribes and Pharisees have sat upon the chair of Moses. All things, then, which they tell you, observe and do; but do not do according to their works; for what they say they do not do. They bind heavy burdens and lay them upon men's shoulders, but they themselves will not move them with their finger. And they do all things to be seen by men.

66. What is 'the scribes have sat', unless that the letter is written? Hence the scribes are also called γραμματεῖς in Greek, following the interpretation of the letter, not the understanding of the spirit; but the letter kills. Those, then, who follow the killing letter and echo it in their very name are scribes and Pharisees, that is, those who are divided from the unity of truth; for phares is interpreted 'division'. And the Pharisees divide the words of the Law in this way: they echo them and meditate on them, but do not see the mysteries and sacraments of the spiritual Law. For if the Law is spiritual, surely the precepts of the Law are spiritual and its works are spiritual. Saying, then, what Moses wrote, they bind heavy burdens with the bodily things of the Law — for those things are heavy which are contrary to the yoke of Christ — and through their wayward doctrine they lay heavy burdens on the ears of their hearers, which they themselves will not move even so far as to stir them from the bodily things of the Law to the spiritual words of Scripture. For so it is to be understood that they will not move them with their finger, that is, not even for a tiny point and moment do they carry themselves over to spiritual understanding. There is also this, that it is written: But if I cast out demons by the finger of God. What the finger is, Scripture itself interprets, which says in another book of the Gospel: But if I cast out demons by the Spirit of God. The finger of God, then, is the Spirit of God; for God is spirit and has nothing bodily in his substance, but is wholly spirit. He, then, casts out the demon by the Spirit of God who casts it out by the finger of God. Hence holy David too says: For I will see your heavens, the works of your fingers, that is: the heavens are the works of your Spirit, as it is also written: By the word of the Lord the heavens were established, and all their power by the spirit of his mouth.

67. To conclude the discussion, then: the scribes and Pharisees refer nothing to the inner man, who, as he himself was made in the image and likeness of God, so in the things which he thinks and meditates ought to think of things that are spiritual rather than those that are carnal. They strive, then, to refer everything to that outer, that earthly man, and therefore all their works are such as to be seen by men, not so as to please the Father who sees in secret; for they fear to reveal their works.

68. This, then, is the shaking of the head which took place among the peoples, or, as Aquila and Symmachus said, among the nations; for all the nations shook their head so as to interpret the Law spiritually. Let no one think that he is to be saved according to the letter of the Law, because the letter of the Law holds a curse, the spirit brings a blessing. No one can be justified by the works of the Law, because all who are under the letter are under a curse. Let all, then, flee the curse of the Law and take refuge in the grace of blessing, so as to have a blessing in the heavenly places in Christ Jesus, who died for us so that faith might pass over to the Gentiles, and who, though he was formerly the husband of the synagogue, did not desert her either, and redeemed us. For he did not desert her, so that a remnant might be saved; he redeemed us by choosing the peoples of the nations. This election of the Gentiles, then, is salvation for the Jews; for the remnant has been saved not through its own works but through the election of grace. It is, then, the grace of Christ which both redeemed us and gave life to the remnant. The shaking of the head took place among the Gentiles, but from this shaking the Jews, whose remnant has been saved, received the grace of no ordinary change.

69. There follows: All the day my shame is against me, and the confusion of my face has covered me. What does it mean that this psalm is spoken sometimes in the person of one, sometimes in the person of many? For from the beginning it started with many, as in: O God, with our ears we have heard, and the rest that follows; then he says: You are yourself my king and my God; again: In you we will toss our enemies; and afterwards: I will not hope in my bow, and: In God we shall be praised all the day, and: All the day my shame is against me. What does this change mean, which is frequently repeated in the psalms, as also in Psalm 108 (109) he says: O God, do not keep silent about my praise, for the mouth of the sinner and the mouth of the deceitful is opened against me? This is of one; there follows of many: They have spoken against me with a deceitful tongue, and hatred in return for my love. Then of one: Set a sinner over him. In Psalm 108 (109) one part seems to be referred to the people of the Jews, another to Judas the betrayer: when things are said in the plural, to the people; when in the singular, to Judas. Here too we can say that what is said in the plural is referred to the saints, what in the singular, to our Lord Jesus Christ, because it can be thought that he spoke in the prophet both for many and for himself, and therefore said: My shame is against me, which is called ἐντροπή. Now ἐντροπὴ is when someone wishes to strike shame into another, so as to bend him from his purpose, as in: Let him come down now from the cross, and we believe him. He trusts in God; let him deliver him now. And he says beautifully 'all the day', because the incarnation of our Lord Jesus Christ brought light to the world. It is, then, the day of the incarnation, of which the same Lord says: Walk while you have the light, and: Abraham saw my day. This shame, then, has confusion in it; hence Symmachus too: All the day, he says, my confusion is against me. And what is the confusion of Christ but the cross, in that he hung naked on it? And well 'all the day', because from when he was fixed to the cross until the ninth hour there was darkness, and after the ninth hour the light shone again until evening. This day is the first of the three. Yet the choir of the saints too can speak of itself both in the plural and in the singular. For a choir has, as it were, a single person, but it is a number of many, as in that saying: This people honours me with their lips, but their heart is far from me.

70. But what is the confusion which covered the Lord Jesus, since sins cause confusion — for it is they which cover the sinner —, whereas he committed no sin? But just as there is a confusion which brings sin, so there is a confusion which abolishes sin, like the confusion of the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, through which sins are cleansed. Every confusion too is good which you undergo for the name of Christ, and exceedingly glorious, if, for example, in a persecution there is a law that a Christian be scourged, that he lose his rank, that he be deprived of his privilege, that he be led into chains. This confusion surely, even if it has no privilege, has the glory of a devout confession. There is also the confusion of Christ, as he himself says: Whoever shall have been confounded at me before men, I too will be confounded at him before my Father who is in heaven. Good, then, is this confusion; yet to many this confusion of Christ is bitter, that is, to unbelievers, but pleasant to believers.

71. Whether, then, you wish to understand the confusion of Christ as his cross, which seemed to be a confusion to the Jews, who insulted him as though Christ blushed at that work in which he was working the common salvation, or also as this confusion which seemed to be heaped upon him by the voice of him who reproaches and reviles, by the face of the enemy and the persecutor. For he blushed that his chosen people had so fallen, and he was confounded before the Father about those whose end had come. For the Father was present to the Son, from whom the Son can never be separated, and therefore he heard him saying to the Jews: Behold, your house shall be left to you desolate.

72. But there are also those who glory in their confusion, like those of whom the Apostle says that their glory is in their confusion. To them the glory of Christ is contrary, who took up the cross and did not flee confusion, which we too ought by no means to fear, nor to shun through dread or disdain, lest that which can bring the grace of life should bring sin upon us. For there is also a glory of confusion, as when you fall among many Gentiles or philosophers, and they cast the cross of Christ in your face, and you cannot answer with a word, and no one listens to you as you relate the words of salvation. It is confusion, but by the voice of him who reproaches and reviles, by the face of the enemy and the persecutor. And therefore do not answer a fool according to his folly; be like a man dumb and silent, and as though not hearing the words of those who insult and revile, not because you cannot answer, but because you ought not to answer. For there is a time for everything, a time to keep silence and a time to speak. You must keep silence when you do not find a ready hearer; you must speak when the Lord grants a tongue of learning, so that your speech may work upon the affections of the hearers. But how should he listen to you who reviles you for this very reason, that he does not wish to hear your wholesome speech, or how should he acquiesce in you who is a persecutor and wishes to lay claim to you for himself, or, as Aquila or Symmachus said, to avenge and punish in you the fact that the inheritance of Christ seems to have been transferred to the Gentiles? Far removed, then, is this confusion from the confusion and reproach of the sinner. For the conscience of the one who has not forgotten his God is enlightened; that of the others is confounded, whose mind is darkened and disturbed by the memory of their sins.

73. And therefore, well conscious of themselves, they say: All these things have come upon us, and we have not forgotten you, and we have not dealt unjustly in your covenant; and our heart has not turned back, and you have turned aside our paths from your way. The last verse seems not to agree with the preceding ones. For how have these turned aside, upon whom no forgetfulness of the divinity has crept, nor the consciousness of any iniquity, nor any sloth? But if you consider that the righteous man is scarcely saved, you will surely understand that even his footsteps sometimes stagger, and that God seems to be the author of their turning aside, because he too allows the righteous often to be tempted, so that, schooled by temptations, they may be the more proved. And who is so strong as by no means to be moved in temptation, unless the Lord stands by him as his helper? Indeed the prophet David himself was troubled, because he said in his abundance: I shall not be moved for ever. Or who is he whom the place of affliction and the corruption of the earth does not move, since the divine sentence has excepted no one as immune from the toil and weariness of this earth, to which it was said: Cursed is the earth in all your works? Thorns, he says, and thistles it shall bring forth. Indeed Paul himself, the Lord's vessel of election, though absolved from the curse of the old sentence by the Passion of the Lord Jesus, yet most vehemently lamented that he was being led captive in the law of sin, and found no one but Christ by whom he might be freed, when the law of his body and the shadow of death pressed upon him.

74. We have given the sense. But it is fitting for us to consider more expressly what the place of affliction is: whether it is this world, which is set in wickedness, or rather our flesh, in which our soul is humbled when it comes down into fellowship with this flesh, or the adversities by which we are exercised while set in this body, in which we groan, being burdened, because we do not wish to be stripped, but to be clothed over. For it is a heavy labour while we wait for this mortal thing, of which we ought to strip ourselves, to be swallowed up by life; for it is stripped off with less labour than it is swallowed up. And see whether this is not the difference between the one who strips himself and the one who waits for it to be swallowed up by life: grace strips off, penitence swallows up; loftiness of mind strips off, diligence swallows up. But how can you doubt that this is the place of affliction, when it is the body of death, in which the shadow of death also covers us? And therefore we must always attend to that lamp which is the Word of God, so that we may know how to direct the paths of our mind and the inner footsteps of our thought, so that the light of souls may dispel bodily darkness.

75. And the interpretation of Aquila too has beautifully expressed how we ought to understand the place of affliction, by saying: For you have humbled us in the place of the sirens, so that the fault lies not in the flesh or in nature, but in those things which have made the flesh corruptible. Indeed the sirens, of whom divine Scripture has made mention a second and a third time, pagan history has related to be certain maidens who, singing with the sweetness of their voice, provoked sailors by their enticements, out of eagerness to hear, to bring their ship to the shore; and ancient history has handed down to posterity that, when they followed the charm of the voice, they suffered shipwreck in a rocky place. Their interpretation is this: the pleasure of the voice and a kind of flattery. So, then, the pleasure of the world delights us with a kind of carnal flattery, so as to deceive. As, then, there the fault lay not in the shore but in the melodious sweetness, which caused the roughness of the shore not to be guarded against, so the fault lies not in the flesh but in those things by which this flesh is solicited and tossed. Indeed the sea, when there is no storm, is calm; if a tempest rages, it is dangerous.

76. And the view of the Seventy seems to agree with these, when they say: for you have humbled us. Testimonies of this interpretation must be fetched for us from the divine Scriptures. For he says in Deuteronomy: If you go out, he says, to battle against your enemies, and the Lord your God delivers them into your hands, and you take their spoils as plunder, and you see in that plunder a woman of good appearance and live with her, and below: If afterwards you do not want her, you shall let her go free, because you have humbled her. You see, then, that it is not her own nature, but he who robbed her of the integrity of her modesty, who is said to have humbled her. Humiliation, then, belongs to the deed through which this flesh has been made a body of sin and a place of affliction. Again, elsewhere: If a virgin is betrothed to a man, and a man, finding her in the city, lies with her, both shall be brought out to the gate of their city and shall be stoned, and the girl shall die because she did not cry out in the city nor call on a man to help her, and the man, he says, because he humbled his neighbour's wife. Here too the reason for this language is the same: she is said to have been humbled by her corrupter. You also have a third testimony on this matter, and more if you seek them; yet hear one from the same book: If anyone finds a virgin who is not betrothed, and, doing violence to her, lies with her, and is found, he who lay with her shall give the girl's father fifty denarii, and she shall be his wife, because he humbled her; nor shall he be able to put her away at any time, because he humbled her. The same expression is repeated, so that you may understand that it is not the condition of the flesh that seems to humble us, but guilt.

77. Yet we cannot deny that the flesh too is humbled by many things: by the place itself, by the enticements themselves, and also by the frailty through which it lay open to error. And although it was deceived by no ordinary adversary, the serpent, yet it had no ordinary grace of its own before sin crept in: Adam was in the sight of God, he flourished in paradise, he shone with heavenly grace, he spoke with God. Did you read that he was humbled before, except after his own transgression humbled him? He surely passed on to us the inheritance of this fault, so that, set in this body, we are unwilling to sojourn away from the body and to be present with God. And therefore we humble our soul, which longs to lift itself up to God; but the corruptible body weighs down the soul, and the earthly dwellings weigh heavier, so that the mind devoted to God is frequently bent down and cannot subject itself to God, since the wisdom of the flesh, which entangles our affection, knows nothing of subjection.

78. Saying these things about ourselves, what do we say about the flesh of our Lord Jesus Christ? He surely took up the truth of this flesh, and therefore he humbled himself even unto death, and the death of the cross. Pay careful attention and consider each point. See that he willingly took up this form of our body and the obedience of your servitude, made in the likeness of men, not in the likeness of flesh, but in the likeness of sinful men; for every man is under sin. Therefore, and being found in appearance as a man — as it is written of him: He is both a man, and who shall know him? a man according to the flesh, but beyond man according to his working —, as a man, he says, he humbled himself, because God came to free those who had been humbled. He humbled himself, then, for our sake. His body, then, is not a body of death, since it is the body of life, nor is his flesh the shadow of death, since it is the brightness of glory, nor is there in him a place of affliction, since in that body there is the grace of consolation for all. He humbled himself so that you might learn what humility is. Indeed hear him saying: Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart. He humbled himself so that you might be exalted; for he who humbles himself shall be exalted. But not all who are humbled shall be exalted; for crime humbles many to their ruin. But the Lord was humbled even unto death, so as to be exalted from the gates of death.

79. Behold, see the grace of Christ, see his benefits. After Christ came, this flesh which was the shadow of death began through the grace of the Lord to shine and to have a light of its own; hence also it was said: The lamp of your body is your eye. But see what eye he speaks of: not the outer, but the inner. If your eye, he says, is single, your whole body will be full of light. Surely it is the inner eye which enlightens the whole body of a man; it is the inner eye too which brings blindness to the whole body, because it is written: But if your eye is evil, your whole body will be dark. You see in one man that from the place where darkness is born, from there light arises. You yourself, then, are your own darkness or light: the darkness of iniquity and the shadows of sins, the light of innocence. You yourself, then, are the author either of your injury or of your grace; the divine working errs in nothing. The light is in yourself, because your infancy is innocence, the pure mind of man before the slipperiness of adolescence and the error of youth. You, then, have made for yourself darkness out of what is light. Indeed the same eye, that is, the sense of man, is dark in the sinner, bright in the innocent.

80. Nor let us pass over what Symmachus says: You have afflicted us in a desert place. We are afflicted for this reason, that at times we are left destitute of heavenly protection, and this region is under a curse or in worldly slipperiness. Also, our Lord Jesus Christ had not yet blessed his earth, and therefore they laboured, and the gaze of the spiritual eyes was not directed to the light of his loving-kindness. Next, because the pleasure and delight of the world frequently creeps in. Hence we ought not to bind and tie ourselves with the bonds of that man, nor to shut our ears with wax, but to turn away the ear whenever anyone thinks fit to speak things foreign to the faith and contrary or adverse to our profit, lest in that speech the shadow of death cover us. And if anything of the kind is uttered, we ought nevertheless to put in the Word of God, which may enlighten the eyes of our mind with the brightness of its discussion.

81. There follows: If we have forgotten the name of our God, and if we have spread out our hands to strange gods, will not God require these things? — Aquila: he will search out these things? Symmachus: he will find these things? — for he knows the hidden things of the heart. For for your sake we are put to death all the day; we are counted as sheep for slaughter. What does he mean by 'God will require'? As if, unless he requires, he does not know; and God will so require your forgetfulness, which has crept in, as to take away every trace of it. Hence Aquila too said: he will search out. For surely he who knows the hidden things of the heart, how does he seek, he to whom even the things within are manifest? For before God the conscience of each betrays itself, and the thought of man before him either condemns or clears itself. Indeed Aquila said the visions of the heart, because he sees our heart, and every thought of our heart is laid open before God, and the image of our thoughts shines back to him. Aquila said that well: in you we are put to death, since it is glorious to die in Christ, and he dies in him who for his name has been crowned by a sacred passion. But why do you wonder that this verse declares that God requires the hidden things of the heart, when elsewhere you have read that God is the searcher of the heart? Why do you wonder too if the majesty of the divine substance cannot be unfolded in our words, when the Apostle says: Do not judge anything before the time, until the Lord comes, who will bring to light the hidden things of darkness and will make manifest the counsels of the hearts; and then each one will have praise from God? We often read of the darkness of the heart in place of crime, indeed almost everywhere. See, then, whether these hidden things of darkness are not the hidden things of the heart, and whether they have not received the name of darkness because they are not detected by others but lie hidden. And therefore the Lord is declared to be about to bring to light on the day of judgement the hidden things of darkness, because the heart of each, which before was hidden, will be made manifest, known indeed to Christ alone, who, knowing the grace of his judgement, says: There is nothing hidden that will not be made manifest.

82. There are also many things which we suppress out of modesty and, to fulfil the heavenly command, keep to ourselves in silent affection, so that we may not seem to glory in what is ours and, by publishing them, begin to lose their fruit. There are also the hidden things of wisdom which the apostle Paul heard when caught up into paradise; and because it was not lawful for a man to speak them, he kept them covered within himself, not because he begrudged others their use or grace, but because what it was not lawful for a man to speak perhaps also did not profit a man to hear; these the Lord will bring to light when the time for bringing secrets to light has come. See whether you may not find some difference in the Gospel too, where he said: There is nothing hidden that will not be made manifest, nor covered that will not be revealed. Yet this supports the former interpretation, so that we may believe both to be about good things, because it is written: The Lord knows those who are his. He knows his own, then, and he knows those who know God, but he ignores those who ignore God; for he who ignores will be ignored. The Lord, then, recognizes what is good; the things full of divine knowledge, the hidden things of wisdom, the Lord knows; but he turns away from the things full of iniquity and sin.

83. Indeed, if you consider the person of these men, whom no forgetfulness of divine worship separated so that they spread out their hands to a strange god, and who were unwilling to lay open their souls, you will understand that in their hidden things there were no crimes, but they held the things that belong to innocence, since they were put to death for the worship of God and all the day long were dwelling in death, as Paul dwelt, who says: I die daily, by your glory. And truly Paul died daily according to the flesh, set in fastings, in shipwrecks, in various dangers, in miseries; but he did not die wholly. His outer man died, but the inner was renewed, and therefore he did not fail when he was pressed by so many dangers, because, though his outer man was being corrupted, the inner was renewed from day to day, since the faith and grace of working of each one lights up the day for him. Hence the apostle Paul did not know night either, because he who is renewed is always in the light. Or does he not seem to you to be in the light who holds within himself the charity of Christ? And therefore, secure about being sacrificed, he does not flee hard things, nor can any persecution or famine or sword separate from Christ the Lord him whom the bonds of divine charity bind fast.

84. Meanwhile, however, even in good works the human mind is troubled and shaken by temptations. Yet this too belongs to the righteous: that, when they see themselves tempted, they ask for help, as the apostles in the Gospel, when Christ was sleeping in the boat, dreading the storm of the waves, woke the Lord Jesus, so that he might drive the dread away from them. Do not the holy apostles seem to you to have spoken these verses: Arise; why do you sleep, O Lord? Arise, and do not cast off unto the end? Certainly, even if the letter seems to differ, the sense agrees. Nor, indeed, even when set in the body, did our Lord Jesus Christ so sleep as to be unaware of the storm of the sea, or that the rising tempest of winds escaped his knowledge through the depth of his sleep. He slept in body, but he kept watch in power. And this was the arrangement: that the Son of God, the Word of God, should go up into the boat, and that his disciples should go up with him; and he, not suffering them to be slack and negligent, as though secure in the Lord's presence and favour, composed himself in bodily sleep. By that appearance of sleep, then, the Word of God showed the apostles that he was asleep to this world. Yet his power and providence kept watch, which raised the storm on the sea, so that the boat was almost covered by the rising waves, that it might be known whether the disciples, troubled by the storm, would support themselves by the provision of saving counsel, or would rather pass beyond the remedy. But in those for whom Jesus was not asleep, faith was awake, and therefore, rising, they woke the Lord Jesus, saying: Lord, save us, we are perishing.

85. See whether what we read of Jonah also differs from the Gospel: he slept in the belly of the ship and snored, and in him a figure of the sacred Passion went before. For as Jonah slept in the ship and snored securely, as if not fearing to be caught, so our Lord Jesus Christ, who fulfilled that figure by the sacrament of his death, slept in the boat in the time of the Gospel; and as Jonah was three days and three nights in the belly of the whale, so the Son of Man was three days and three nights in the heart of the earth in the passion of his body. When he had roused himself from death and released the sleep of his body, so as to rise again for the salvation of all, he visited his disciples. This, then, is the true Jonah, who offered his soul as a redemption for us. For this reason, then, he was taken up and sent into the sea, to be received and devoured by the whale, so that, set within the whale's belly, he might empty its innards. Of what whale this was said, hear Job saying: He who has, he says, to render the great whale captive. Who this is you surely recognize when you read that our Lord Jesus Christ led captivity captive; for when the adversary and enemy was vanquished, we who were captives began to possess freedom through Christ.

86. Indeed the very prayer of holy Jonah teaches that these are the mysteries of the Lord's Passion: And he said: I cried, he says, to the Lord my God in my tribulation, and he heard me from the belly of the underworld. You see that he said the belly of the underworld, not of the whale. And the Lord went down not into a whale but into the underworld, so that those too who were in the underworld might be loosed from everlasting bonds. Indeed very many even rose again. But what rivers surrounded Jonah, or of what abyss did Jonah say: The uttermost abyss surrounded me, it plunged my head into the clefts of the mountains, I went down into the earth whose bars are everlasting restraints? Surely this does not fit and suit the person of Jonah. But the Son of God went down into the clefts of the mountains when he went down into the tomb. For Joseph, as the Gospel teaches, laid him in his new tomb, which he had hewn in the rock, and rolled a great stone to the door of the tomb. Indeed in another book too: He laid, it says, him in a tomb hewn out, in which no one had yet been laid.

87. And who is it that sacrificed with the voice of praise and of confession of the Lord, unless the prince of all priests, who both vowed vows for all and paid them? For he alone had power over his own fulfilment. As, then, Jonah was sent into the sea and the sea ceased from its commotion, so our Lord Jesus Christ came into this world, so as to gain the world and by his blood to make peace for all things, whether earthly or heavenly. By his coming, then, he redeemed all men and called them forth to the worship of God by his works; when he raised the dead and healed the sick, he poured the fear of God into human affections. He it is who sacrificed to the Lord a saving sacrifice for us and offered to God victims worthy of our conversion; he it is who slept and awoke.

88. We must beware, then, lest he fall asleep to sinners; for he seems to fall asleep to those for whom he has not risen again and whom he has cast off unto the end. And who is it that is cast off, unless he who sojourns away from the Lord, because he does not sojourn away from the body? For how can those who are in the flesh please God? And therefore let us not be in the flesh, but in spirit cleave to the Lord Jesus, who in Psalm 88 (89) says that he was cast off for us: But you have cast off for nothing, you have put off your anointed; you have overturned the covenant of your servant. And so, cast off for us, he became a reproach to his neighbours; for he took up the reproach of the cross, so as to clothe us with the glory of his resurrection. He seems cast off as one dead, and drenched with confusion as one crucified; but blessed for ever is he who took away the curse and procured the grace of blessing.

89. There follows: Why do you turn away your face? We think that God turns his face away from us when we are set in some afflictions, so that darkness is poured over our affection, by which we are hindered and cannot drink in the brightness of truth with our eyes. For if God attends to our understanding and deigns to visit our mind, we are sure that nothing can draw a mist over us. For if the face of man shines more than the other parts of the limbs, and whomever we look upon we either come to know if he is unknown or recognize if he is known, he whom our gaze does not suffer to lie hidden, how much more does the face of God enlighten him upon whom it looks! On this, as with the rest of his sayings, so this too is splendid, from the holy Apostle, who is truly the interpreter of Christ, so that he might pour him into our minds with apter sense and speech. Hence he says: For God, who said that light should shine out of darkness, has shone in our hearts to the enlightenment of the knowledge of the brightness of the Lord in the face of Christ Jesus. We have heard, then, where Christ shines in us. For he is the eternal brightness of souls, whom the Father sent to earth for this purpose, that, enlightened in his face, we who before were held in earthly darkness might be able to behold eternal and heavenly things. Why should I speak of Christ, when even the apostle Peter said to that man lame from his mother's womb: Look upon us? He looked upon Peter and was enlightened by the grace of faith; for he would not have received the remedy of health had he not believed faithfully. Hence, when there was such glory in the apostles, Zacchaeus, hearing that the Lord Jesus was passing by, climbed into a tree, because, being small and little, he could not see him in the crowd. He saw Christ and found light; he saw him, and he who before had seized others' goods gave away his own. Rightly, then, these men say: Why do you turn away your face?

90. Or perhaps it is said for this reason, that the coming of Christ was delayed for those who longed for it; for he is the face of the Father, he who is the image and glory of God. Certainly, if we do not take this of the sacrament of his incarnation, the saints rightly make use of that saying too: Why do you turn away your face?, that is: even if you turn away your face from us, O Lord, yet the light of your countenance, O Lord, is sealed upon us. We hold it in our hearts and it shines in our inmost affection; for no one can subsist if you turn away your face. For all things wait upon you, that you may give them food in due season; when you give it to them, they will gather it; when you open your hand, all things will be filled with goodness. But when you turn away your face, they will be troubled. You will take away their spirit, and they will fail and return to their dust. You will send forth your spirit, and they will be created, and you will renew the face of the earth. Do you recognize what the food is which God supplies to men? It is the food on which his only-begotten Son, our Lord, feasts according to the will of the Father, as he himself says: My food is to do the will of my Father who is in heaven. This food is saving for us, and all the things of God are saving.

91. You hear of certain members of God; recognize the powers: his hand is the fullness of goodness, his face is the enlightenment of the mind. And therefore let us always hope from him, direct our prayers and all our endeavours to him, and not despair even if we cannot see him bodily. For neither did Moses despair, to whom it was said: You cannot see my face; for man shall not see my face and live; and he added: Behold, there is a place beside me; you shall stand on the rock; and when my glory passes by, I will put you in a cleft of the rock and cover you with my hand until I pass by; and I will take away my hand, and you shall see my back parts, but my face you shall not be able to see. The face of God, then, is not seen by man, yet there is a place in which God shows himself to man by faith. This place is beside God, and therefore, if we stand on the rock, that is, in the consciousness of this flesh and the firmness of faith, even if we cannot see his fullness, yet as far as we can we shall drink in, as it were, the remnants of his light. For Moses did not see the whole fullness of his divinity which dwells in Christ bodily, but he saw the back parts of Christ; he saw his brightness as a man, he saw the glory of his Passion, through which he unlocked for us the heavenly kingdom.

92. Aquila, however, put: Why do you hide your face?, and so did the others too; which seems to agree with the words of Moses, who says: Show yourself to me, as if he said: do not hide your face from me, but rather show it to one who longs to see.

93. And he added: Do you forget, he says, our want and our tribulation? Of what want does the holy prophet say that it has come into God's forgetfulness, or what is this want which he complains has slipped from our God and Lord? No want is at ease, unless perhaps that which is wont to have God as protector and rewarder. For if there is a want to which the kingdom of heaven lies open, these men rightly thought that their want had slipped from the divine mind, since they lamented that their soul was humbled into the dust and said that their belly cleaved to the earth; and those who cleave to the earth seem to be deprived of the divine blessing. Hear, then, what this want is: for blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven. If, then, that poverty is blessed to which heaven lies open, let us neglect these earthly riches, let us shun bodily abundance. Let us follow the acts of the apostles, before whose feet each one laid what money he had from the price of his fields, to be distributed to the poor. These holy men, then, had begun to practise this evangelical want long before, and therefore they said: Do you forget our want? This is like that saying of the apostles: Behold, we have left all things and followed you. Good, then, is the poverty which proceeds from devotion and is undertaken by the exercise of the virtues.

94. And not only their want, but also their tribulation, they lament, has come into God's forgetfulness. Good too are the tribulations which are proper to the righteous, who endure them not for their own deserts but for devotion; in them the righteous man is not crushed but enlarged, as the holy prophet declares, saying: In tribulation you have enlarged me, and the Lord Jesus himself says: In tribulation I called upon the Lord, and he heard me into a broad place. He enlarged the holy apostle Paul when he took from him the light of his eyes; for so he confessed Christ, whom he had denied before. Therefore he merited to be a vessel of election. Indeed, so that you may know that the Lord Jesus enlarged Paul, hear the Apostle himself saying: Our mouth is open to you, O Corinthians, our heart is enlarged. Open, then, your mouth, confess Christ, because he is fullness; where there is fullness, there is breadth also. And like a good master, who wished to have disciples equal to himself, he exhorted us too to be enlarged, saying: Be enlarged, you also.

95. And when God somewhere enlarges in tribulation, then there will be a breadth of heart like the innumerable sand of the sea. What this breadth is, hear from holy Solomon, as he asks to receive wisdom from God. He asked not for riches, not for nobility, not for power, but for wisdom, and in it he found all things, even those which he did not ask for. Hence Scripture says that the breadth of his heart was like the innumerable sand of the sea. Whence you may obtain this breadth he says, knowing it from himself: In the breadth of your heart write it down. And therefore let him who has wisdom not keep it hidden, nor proclaim it only for a moment, but let him sing it at the going out, and everywhere proclaim with authority what he perceives.

96. Symmachus, however, put affliction in place of tribulation. But whether it be affliction or tribulation, we need to remain in the Lord and not withdraw from him, since with the Lord as our guardian and standing by us we can bravely endure every contest. But if we neglect the Lord and make ourselves far from the Lord, we make our adversary stronger. [The commentary breaks off here.]

On Psalm 45 (46)

1. The title of this psalm is inscribed thus: Unto the end; for the sons of Korah, for the hidden things, a psalm to David himself. What is "for the hidden things"? That he had knowledge of hidden things through the revelation of the Lord Jesus, the holy prophet also records in Psalm 50 (51), saying: The uncertain and hidden things of your wisdom you have made manifest to me. And what those hidden things are, we are taught in the holy Gospel, where the Son of God says that the mysteries of wisdom are hidden from the wise and revealed to little ones. These are, unless I am mistaken, the things which eye has not seen nor ear heard, nor have they entered into the heart of man, which the Lord has prepared for those who love him. Let us therefore store away the hidden things of wisdom in the hidden place of our heart, and not rashly divulge them to any except those who share in the sacraments, whom the Lord has called to his grace, he who wishes to be entreated in secret, since he alone is the one who knows things secret and hidden.

2. In fact the Lord Jesus himself willed that Moses alone, though not without Jesus [Joshua], should go up the mountain to receive the Law; in the Gospel too he revealed the glory of his resurrection to Peter, John and James alone out of the whole number of the disciples. So hidden did he wish his mystery to be, and he warned them often not to announce easily to anyone what they had seen, lest someone weaker, who could not drink in the force of the sacraments with a wavering mind, should meet a stumbling block. In fact Peter himself did not know what he was saying, since he believed that three tabernacles were to be made for the Lord and his servants. Then he could not bear the brightness of the glory of the Lord as he was transfigured; he fell to the ground; the sons of thunder, James and John, fell too, and a cloud covered them, and they could not rise until Jesus came and touched them and bade them rise and put away their fear. They entered, then, into the cloud, that they might come to know things secret and hidden, and there they heard the voice of God saying: This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased; hear him. What is "This is my beloved Son"? That is: "Do not err, Simon, by thinking that the Son of God is to be compared with servants. This is my Son; Moses is not the Son, Elijah is not the Son, although the one opened the sea and the other shut the heaven. For at the word of the Lord each of them overcame the nature of an element; but they furnished a ministry, while this is he who made the waters solid, shut the heaven with drought, and when he willed released it into showers. Where testimony to the resurrection is called in, let the ministries of servants bear their witness in support; where the glory of the Lord rising again is shown, the splendour of servants is hidden. For the risen sun veils the very globes of the stars, and all their lights vanish under the sun of this world. How then could fleshly stars be seen under the eternal Sun of righteousness and that divine brightness? Where, then, are those lights which by a kind of miracle shone before your eyes? All things are darkness in comparison with the eternal light. Let others hasten to please God by their ministries; this one alone is the true and eternal light, in whom the Father is well pleased, or in whom it has well pleased me, so that whatever he has done is mine, and whatever I have done may rightly be believed to be the work of the Son. Hear him saying: I and the Father are one. He did not say: 'I and Moses are one'; he did not say that he and Elijah had any partnership in the divine glory. Why do you prepare three tabernacles? This one has his tabernacle not on earth but in heaven." The apostles heard and fell down, shaken with dread. The Lord came and raised them up and bade them tell no one what they had seen.

3. To him, then, keep your hidden things, to whom David committed his hidden things, saying: From my hidden things cleanse me, O Lord; for clean must be the receptacles in which the hidden mysteries of devotion are kept. And therefore it is said to you: Drink water from your own vessels and from the springs of your wells, and let your waters overflow in the streets, so that you may be cleansed by a watering spring. Therefore it is said to you: Let your presses overflow with wine; therefore it is said: In tribulation you called upon me and I delivered you, and I heard you in the hidden place of the tempest; I proved you at the water of contradiction. For there is in tribulations a certain savage tempest within the breast, when there are fightings without, fears within. There is a water of contradiction, when our thoughts toss as with a kind of surge of the salt sea, and are calmed by no stability of resolve in the depths of our minds. Then there is a tempest in a hidden place, then a water of contradiction, which can be stilled only by the word of Christ, by which he forgives us our sins, according to what he himself deigned to say: Now you are clean because of the word which I have spoken to you. Speak, Lord Jesus: your word is medicine, your word is light, your word is the washing away of our filth, your word is a fountain; you speak, and guilt is washed away.

4. And since our discourse has been led to the hidden place of the tempest and to the water of contradiction, the Word of God has become all things for us. It became flesh, to save the flesh; it became water, to temper by its grace the water of contradiction and to take away the bitterness of ungodliness. And so it is written: Deep water is the word in the heart of a man; in one place "word", in another again "counsel"; for there is good counsel where the word of God is good. And therefore the Law says to you: Take heed to yourself, lest an evil word arise in your heart, and lest Jesus say: Why do you think evil things in your hearts? For God sent his Word to heal man, not to destroy him. This Word is medicine, not punishment. Let this Word be in your heart and in your mouth; let this Word be in the hidden places, where the devil lies in wait; let the Word enter, let the devil go out. For if the devil creeps in, Christ withdraws, as he withdrew from Judas after the devil had plunged himself into his heart. You too, then, say: From my hidden things cleanse me, O Lord, lest the devil lie in wait in hidden places, like a lion in his lair. But if you keep the hidden things of Christ devoutly and faithfully, there can be no place for the devil to lie in wait. And so in Psalm 9 (9-10), where David writes of the hidden sacraments of the Lord, that is, the conversion of sinners, the calling of the Gentiles, the kingdom of God, the preparation of the heart, the judgement of the poor, the refreshment of the needy, he first says that the adversary must be driven out of the hidden and secret places of your breast, lest he sit in hidden places, desiring to kill the innocent. And so it is said to you: Enter into your chamber and, having shut the door, pray to your Father in secret. Let your prayer go down to that place where he who searches hidden things may hear it; there let him find your patience, whether in covering an injury or in avoiding boasting; for the wise man hides his injury. There is the hiding place of wisdom; for he made darkness his hiding place; the wicked shall seek me and shall not find me.

5. That is why Abraham dug a well, so that whoever sought water might find it; that is why Isaac dug a well; that is why the Lord Jesus sat at a well — now the fountain of Jacob was there — he sat at a well, so that you might seek. The fountain there for you was Christ. He is a fountain, so that waters may overflow for those who seek them, by which all the shameful deeds of the flesh may be washed away and its fires quenched. This, then, is the depth of the wisdom and knowledge of God, that the wicked do not find it, while the good seek it and are occupied in the zeal and meditation of acquiring it. Do you want to know how true this is? Hear the words of Wisdom herself: Ask, and it shall be given to you; seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened to you. Knock, then, at the gate, for Christ is the gate; knock at the door of the word, that it may be opened to you to speak the mystery of Christ, and that you may be able to find the treasures of wisdom hidden in Christ. In him our wisdom, in him our life is hidden, as Paul declared, who was caught up even to the third heaven, that he might hear the things which it is not lawful for man to speak. How great is the discipline of hidden things! It is lawful for man to know, and not lawful to speak. To know is life, to speak is death.

6. Knowing, then, that the end of the hidden things, which in Greek is called σκοπὸς, is this, and this the sum of it: that the unworthy should not grasp the discipline of wisdom, and that those who have the desire to take in the knowledge of the divine sacraments, but with the merits of life and faith supporting them, and who labour and sweat in daily exercises, should deserve to obtain the fruit of their striving, let us read Psalm 9 (9-10) and Psalm 45 (46) more carefully, so that we may be able to grasp with a faithful mind the wisdom hidden in a mystery; for these alone bear at their head the title concerning the hidden things. But now let us set about the psalm.

7. God our refuge and power. What is "our"? That is: of us who believe in you. So also elsewhere: Lord, you have become a refuge for us; "for us", he says, who believe, not for unbelievers; for us who seek the truth, not for those who flee from it. For he can say this who has placed the hope of his salvation in the protection of God alone. For us, then, you have become a refuge, both our refuge and power, we who are from the peoples of the nations. The Jews denied you, the Jews did not receive you, and therefore you turned to the Gentiles; you called us, and you have become our refuge, and you are power for us. Do you want to know how truly this was said of us who are from the Gentiles? The cross of the Lord is to the Jews a stumbling block, to the Greeks foolishness, but to us the power and wisdom of God, and below: But of him you are in Christ Jesus, who has been made wisdom for us from God. Certainly Paul spoke not of the Jews nor of the Gentiles, but of those who, converted to the Lord Jesus, proclaimed him the author of their salvation. You have fled for refuge, then, to Christ: do not desert the foundation, do not forsake the one who strengthens you, so that he who took you up may himself protect you, and the loving merchant of our souls may recognize his own merchandise.

8. A helper in the tribulations which have found us exceedingly. "Exceedingly" fits both well, so that it can be understood both as "in the tribulations which have found us exceedingly" and as "a helper exceedingly". For the greater the tribulations are, the greater and the stronger the helps we need. And therefore it often happens that we are crowned in the heavier and harsher contests, because by tears and groans and more frequent prayers we call to our side the aid of the eternal divinity.

9. So it is that we do not fear when our earth is troubled and the mountains are carried over, because if you are for us, who is against us? We cannot fear the frailties of this flesh, which is broken by fear and dread, inflamed by desires, softened by luxury, enervated by delights, heated by fevers, vexed by pain, made lifeless by distress; or when those mountains are carried into the heart of the sea, of which the Savior said: If you have faith like a grain of mustard seed, you shall not only do this to the fig tree, but even if you say to this mountain: Be taken up and cast into the sea, nothing shall be impossible to you. This mountain cast itself into the heart of Judas and troubled his mind with a heavy tempest, tossing it with the waves of various passions. For as the soul that is fruitful in good works and in the fruits of devotion and faith is compared to the fertility of the lands, so that the smell of Jacob is like the smell of a full field, so too the soul of one who does not know how to be quiet and stirs up heavy waves with bitter thoughts is figured by the restlessness of the stormy sea. In fact, not even by his kiss did the Lord Jesus temper the bitterness of Judas the traitor. The traitor received the kiss, poured in poison, inflicted death, whose sting Christ indeed blunted, but the traitor alone did not escape. And to show us plainly a mountain of this kind, elsewhere too, when he had commanded a demon to go out of a man and the apostles asked for what reason they themselves had not been able to cast it out, he says: This kind is not cast out except by prayer and fasting, and he added: If you have faith like a grain of mustard seed, you shall say to this mountain: Pass from here to there, indicating with his hand which mountain it was that perfect faith could move. For perfect faith is compared to a grain of mustard seed, as the Apostle says: If I have all faith so that I move mountains, but have not charity, it profits me nothing.

10. There are also some who punctuate thus: and the mountains shall be carried over, so that there follows: in the hearts of the sea its waters have sounded and been troubled, the mountains have been troubled in his strength. Not good are the waters which sound and are troubled and cannot give drink; better are those which pass through, as it is written: Between the midst of the mountains waters shall pass through, and all the beasts of the field shall drink; the wild asses shall take them for their thirst. In fact, when the legion of demons asked and received power to enter into the swine, it both cast itself headlong with a great rush into the sea, and those waters were troubled by the headlong fall of its herd. Elsewhere too the same prophet David put it thus: The waters saw you, O God, the waters saw you and were afraid, that is, the good powers, which are calm through knowledge of the heavenly Word, not terrible with sound and crashing. But what does he say of the evil powers? And the abysses were troubled; a multitude of the sound of waters. The abysses do not see God, and therefore they are always subject to troubling. And he explained what the abysses were: a multitude, he says, of the sound of waters. For that beast too, the antichrist, ascends from the abyss to make war against Elijah and Enoch and John, who for the testimony of the Lord Jesus have been given back to the earth, as we read in the Apocalypse of John. To him a mouth was given speaking great things, so that he might sound forth against God insults full of fury and of sacrilege.

11. The waters therefore sounded, that is, the unclean spirits, when they were troubled at seeing the works of God, dreading at the coming of the saving Lord that judgement was drawing near to them. The waters sounded and were troubled at the Passion of the Lord. Hence it was also said: Why have the nations raged, and the peoples meditated vain things? And elsewhere Scripture says: Their memory has perished with a noise. The devil seeks noise, Christ silence. In fact, as a lamb before its shearer, so he opened not his mouth, and elsewhere: He shall not cry out nor strive, nor shall anyone hear his voice in the street, until he casts out judgement unto victory.
