What is this victory but the victory over the serpent? In this strength of the Son of God the mountains were troubled, when the devil and his ministers saw the dead rising again. These are the mountains which are troubled; those are the mountains which are carried over into paradise: those to whom it is said: Today you shall be with me in paradise, and that other, to whom it is said: If I will that he remain until I come, what is that to you? Follow me, and Enoch, who was caught up lest he be changed by the malice of the world.

12. After the Passion of the Lord, what else had to follow but that a river went out from the body of the Lord, when water and blood flowed from his side, by which he gladdened the souls of all, because with that river he washed away the sin of the whole world? There is also the river which goes out from Eden and encircles the whole earth: the Word of God, by which the intelligible paradise is watered and every soul is called to the grace of Christ, as the Word of God himself says: If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink. Whoever I give water to, rivers of living water shall flow from his belly. For from that one Word of God flowed the streams of the four rivers, of prudence and fortitude, of temperance also and justice. For to one is given through the Spirit the word of wisdom, to another faith in the same Spirit, to another the grace of healings in the one Spirit, to another the working of powers. All these, indeed, one and the same Spirit works, dividing to each as he wills. These are the rushings of the river, when by a spontaneous division it pours itself out into the grace of ministries, of working and of power.

13. By these courses of the heavenly river, then, that city in which God dwells is watered, and the tabernacle of the Most High is sanctified: every soul that is indwelt by Christ, or that body which the virgin brought forth, of which the angel Gabriel said: And that which shall be born of you shall be called holy, the Son of the living God. A good tabernacle, which no sins could overshadow, a wonderful temple of God and a heavenly court, which, when it was dissolved, could not be corrupted, but within three days rose again, raised up by him who seemed dead. Nor is it any wonder, since God was in the midst of his city and was not moved: whether because the Son of God was moved by no lapse into any sin, nor stumbled by striking against any error, nor wavered at the push of bodily allurement; or because the grace of God did not leave his soul but remained inseparable in it, since it continued unstained by all the filth of sin. Hence the Son of God also said to the Father: Nor will you leave my soul in the underworld. Therefore, because it lay within him not to sin even in the flesh, he says divinely: I am in the Father and the Father in me, and elsewhere: I was delivered up and I did not go out. From him, surely, he did not go out to whom he said afterwards: I have risen, and I am still with you. But where anyone sins more gravely, God is moved in him, so as to pass away from him. Hence too he seemed to Adam, when he sinned, to be walking about, and to Stephen to be standing, because the one fell in transgression, the other was crowned in martyrdom. And in the book of Zechariah it is written thus: The Lord shall walk in the commotion of the threatening of his wrath. Something similar was said to the Jews in the Gospel: Behold, your house shall be left to you desolate; for the Lord forsook the Jews, and at once ruin followed the synagogue. But if Christ had not deserted it, final destruction would never have followed it. We have opened, then, what was said: God is in the midst of her; he shall not be moved.

14. There follows: God shall help her at daybreak, by which it is signified that the morning resurrection confers on us the support of heavenly helps, for it drove back the night and poured the day back again, as Scripture says: Rise, you who sleep, and arise from the dead, and Christ shall shine upon you. But see the mystery. Christ suffered in the evening; therefore according to the Law too the lamb is slain towards evening. He rose in the morning; for thus it is written: On the first of the sabbath Mary Magdalene came at first light to the tomb, while it was still dark, and saw the stone removed from the door. He is slain at the evening of the world, when the light was already failing, because this whole world was in darkness, and it would have been wrapped in a greater squalor of darkness had not Christ come to us from heaven as eternal light, to restore to the human race the times of innocence. The Lord Jesus suffered, then, and by his blood forgave our sins; the light of a purer conscience shone out again, and the day of spiritual grace gleamed forth; hence the Apostle also says: The night has gone before, the day has drawn near. We have awakened, therefore; let us sleep no longer. We have put on the garment of light; let us not go back to the wraps and the cast-off skins of a dark way of life. We have bidden farewell to revellings and delights, which belong to the time of night; we have chosen sobriety, by grace of which Jacob found the primacy which he did not have.

15. In other copies too is found: God helps her with his countenance. What this too signifies, let the prophet himself teach us; for he himself said in a later place: Show your face, and we shall be saved. The look of God, then, is our salvation, and in his countenance is our help. How great a force there is in it divine Scripture declares, which not idly added that God looked upon the gifts of Abel, but upon Cain he did not look; and therefore Cain was saddened, because he knew that his sacrifice had displeased God and his brother's had pleased him. Hence that too which was sung today in the response of the psalm greatly supports our assertion: Waiting I waited for the Lord, and he looked upon me. Whom the Lord looks upon, he saves. In fact, at the Passion of the Lord, when Peter faltered in speech, not in mind — although the very speech of Peter, even faltering, is more faithful than the teaching of many —, Christ looked at him, and Peter wept, and by this he washed away his own error. So him whom he seemed to deny with his voice he confessed with his tears.

16. The nations were troubled, the kingdoms were bowed down; the Most High gave his voice, and the earth was moved. At the coming of the Lord the Savior the adverse powers with their legion were troubled, when they were ordered to go out of human bodies and begged to be sent into the swine. When the most wicked powers had been troubled, the worshippers of idols had to be troubled, and the kingdoms of sin had to be bowed down as well. For it was a heavy kingdom, which had subjugated to itself in heavy servitude the minds of all sinners; for he who commits sin is the slave of sin. And the kingdom of sin is the kingdom of death, which long prevailed over the whole world. Hence the Apostle also says: But death reigned from Adam to Moses even over those who did not sin after the likeness of the transgression of Adam, who is the form of the one to come. Truth came, the figure ceased; life came, the kingdom of death vanished; the remission of sins came, and the chains of sin were loosed. Before, even lighter offences were bound by the bonds of death; but after the coming of the saving Lord even graver crimes were remitted. The kingdoms of the spiritual wickednesses which are in the heavenly places were bowed down, then, because the worship of idols and the allurement of sin began to be diminished by the preaching of the Gospel teaching. Unbelief was bowed down after faith began to reign in the hearts of the nations. The kingdoms of sin are bowed down when it is read: Let not sin reign in your mortal body. The Lord bowed down all the kingdoms of unbelief with his voice, saying: Come to me, all you who labour and are burdened, and I will refresh you.

17. This voice the Most High gave, and all the peoples of the nations followed, fleeing the harsh kingdoms of sin, the cruelty of perpetual death and the intolerable servitude of all crimes, since he promised rest to the weary, release to captives, freedom to those in servitude, and, having shaken off the iron yoke of the Babylonian king, put his own sweet yoke in its place upon the necks of the faithful, lest the enemy should again bind the empty necks of the Gentiles with the chains of his iniquity. For those whom Christ binds he sets free, and those whom he ties he releases. In fact, he who has been called by the Lord as a slave is the Lord's freedman, and he who has been called free is the slave of Christ. And therefore Paul glories in his chains, saying: Paul, a prisoner of Jesus. What under others is an insult, under Christ is glory. The Lord gave his voice in the Passion, and all the elements trembled, the whole earth was moved, so that the rites of the Gentiles might be dissolved, and there might be the earth of the Lord and its fullness, as it is written; that the auguries of the unbelieving might cease, and the knowledge of faith and the zeal of devotion might abolish the sacrifice of ungodliness. The Lord gives his voice daily, and it resounds in the hearts of each, so that everything earthly may fall away from the affection of him who believes well, and every sense of the inner minds may be moved by devout confession from error, from the corruption and dissolution of luxury, to the knowledge of the heavenly sacraments and zeal for chastity, from wickedness to virtue.

18. He says, then, that those who are on the earth were moved; or else because all men in whom there was earthly corruption were earth, since they did not know how to raise and lift the eyes of their minds to the height of heavenly grace, nor could they say: the God of powers is with us, for there was none with them but the instigator of the fall and the author of error. But after the voice of the Lord resounded in the Gospel, the prophet dares to say: The God of powers is with us. Well does he say: The God of powers is with us, who believed that a virgin conceived in her womb and brought forth. We too dare to say: The God of powers is with us, after Emmanuel, that is, God, began to be with us. Where, then, Emmanuel is, there the God of powers is with us.

19. Our upholder, the God of Jacob. Let us imitate, then, the patriarch Jacob, his fragrance, his virtue, his labour. For just as we ought to do the works of Abraham, so as to deserve rightly to be called his sons, so also we ought to undergo the exercises and contests of holy Jacob, so that the God of Jacob may be believed to be our God. For if he does not recognize in us the works of Jacob or, since we cannot equal him, the zeal for his work and faith, how will he deign to help us as the God of Jacob?

20. As he says these things, behold, through the Holy Spirit a present image of things to come was poured into him, and seeing, after the heavy turmoil of wars, peace spread over the whole world at the coming of the saving Lord, he cries out to all, saying: Come and see the works of the Lord, which he has set as prodigies upon the earth. "Come" looks to assembling, "see" to attention and zeal for knowing the truth. See, he says, the works of the Lord full of miracles, in which are the signs of great powers. In fact τέρατα in Greek are called in Latin things to be wondered at. Do not, then, take the works of the Lord as prodigies of horror, but as full of wonder. Excellently is it said: "he has set upon the earth", that is, into the earth.

21. Taking away wars to the ends of the earth, he will crush the bow and break the weapons and burn the shields with fire. And truly, before the Roman empire was spread abroad, not only did the kings of individual cities fight against one another, but even the Romans themselves were frequently worn down by civil wars. Marius fought against Cinna; on both sides Roman blood was shed. Sulla rose up and stirred up civil wars again against Marius the victor. Lepidus and Sertorius showed themselves rebels against the Roman empire. Caesar pursued Pompey and roused the fury of the Gauls against Roman arms. Having defeated the elder Pompey, he vanquished the younger in the regions of the Spains. Why should I speak of the triumvirs, how from enemies they became friends, and from friends went on again into hostile tumults, and of how the seas too were stained with Roman blood in the war at Actium? Hence it came about that, from weariness of civil wars, the Roman empire was conferred on Julius Augustus, and so the internal battles were calmed. And this served to the end that the apostles might rightly be sent through the whole world, as the Lord Jesus says: Go, teach all nations. To them indeed even kingdoms shut off by barbarian upheavals lay open, as India to Thomas, Persia to Matthew. But yet, so that they might traverse more expanses of the lands, at the rise of the Church he spread the power of the Roman empire over the whole world and, by the gift of peace, settled the minds of those at variance and the divisions of the lands. All men, living under one empire over the lands, learned to confess with faithful speech the empire of the one almighty God.

22. He took away wars, then, to the ends of the earth, and there is no doubt that this was done. But see whether he has not taken away the wars of weakness, which are in the hearts of those who waver. For whoever has passed beyond earthly things no longer says: Fightings without, fears within, but says: But our citizenship is in heaven. Pass over the world, then, so that you may begin to be above the world; for περᾶσαι is "to pass over" or, to put it more expressly, "to cross the sea". The Lord, then, took away the wars which spiritual wickedness was stirring up against us, and, having vanquished the devil, he left us his peace. He broke the bow from which the fiery darts of the adversary were scattered, and burned the shields of unbelief with fire, so that the shield of faith might remain inviolable.

23. Be at leisure, he says, and see that I am the Lord. The voice of the Lord after the wars have been calmed is "be at leisure", so that there may be no preoccupation of souls, lest the overflowing of worldly disturbances should cloud the eye of the inner mind. Be at leisure, he says, from errors; be at leisure from disturbances; be at leisure from sins, because everyone who sins has not seen God nor known him, as John the Evangelist said in his epistle. Be at leisure, then, for the study of knowing the divinity; do not be occupied with earthly works. That is why the king of the Egyptians ordered the number of bricks and the other burdens of the Jews to be increased, so that they might not know God and be at leisure for sacred worship, offended by the voice of the holy men Moses and Aaron, who said: Let the people of God go, that we may go and serve the Lord our God.

24. I shall be exalted among the nations, and I shall be exalted on the earth. The Lord was exalted in the heights, that is, among the angels and archangels, among the patriarchs and prophets, who thought lofty things of him and did not compare him with earthly images and rough-hewn idols; but he was not exalted among the peoples of the nations, who seemed to seek their god in grottoes and in dwellings in caves. In fact, the Magdalene is reproved, who wept for Christ as though he were dead and sought him as though buried in the tomb. Hence Christ says to her: Mary, why do you weep? Whom do you seek? Surely not one who could be taken away? She heard his voice and recognized him and wished to touch him, and the Lord said: Do not touch me, whom you think dead; I am going to the Father. Do you wonder that you did not find in the tomb him whom heavenly things await? Or perhaps she is reproved because nothing but perfect faith touches Christ, as the woman with the issue of blood touched him with full faith. Hence the Lord says: Someone has touched me; I perceive that power has gone out from me. At this word she recognized in herself that she had been cured by the power going out, and at once, conscious of what had happened to her, she confessed that it was she who had touched the Lord. Hence the Lord says: Your faith has made you whole; go in peace. But the Lord was exalted on the earth, according to what he himself says: When I am exalted, I will draw all things to myself, or because his will was done as in heaven so also on earth.

25. And he repeats, saying: The God of powers is with us, our upholder, the God of Jacob. Repetition is confirmation. Or else it is because God was made with us not only when he was born of the virgin, but also after the resurrection he himself said: Behold, I am with you even to the consummation of the world, to whom be honour and glory, perpetuity, from the ages, and now, and always, and unto all the ages of ages. Amen.

On Psalm 47 (48)

1. Its title is this: A psalm of a song for the sons of Korah, on the second of the sabbath. Good are the sons of Korah, and already approved in the earlier psalms, because with fitting thoughts and reflections suited to the pursuit of the virtues they composed a spiritual psaltery within themselves, and, intent upon the knowledge of the divinity from the wakeful lookout of the mind, they were worthy to draw out the secrets of the heavenly mysteries. And therefore, since they had received the duty and office of the psalms to be sung daily before the ark of the Lord, they seem to have been chosen to receive in particular the psalm of a song on the second of the sabbath, to be sung together. For those to whom it was rightly seen to have been entrusted to sing with their voice the ascent of the Lord rising from the dead, to them surely it was due that they should celebrate by their duty and ministry the very day of the resurrection. For what is the second of the sabbath but the Lord's day, which followed the sabbath? Now the sabbath day was later in the order of days, but earlier by the sanctification of the Law. But when the end of the Law came, who is Christ Jesus — for Christ is the end of the Law unto righteousness for everyone who believes — and by his resurrection sanctified the eighth day, the same day began to be first which is the eighth, and the eighth which is the first, having its precedence from the order of number and its holiness from the resurrection of the Lord. Hence we also read in the Gospel of the δευτερόπρωτον sabbath, which in Latin is called "secundoprimum" [second-first]. For when the Lord's day, on which the Lord rose again, began to excel, the sabbath which had been first began to be reckoned second after the first; for the first rest ceased, and the second succeeded it. Hence the Apostle too, writing to the Hebrews, says: On a later day, therefore, there remains a rest for the people of God. Let us hasten, then, to enter into that rest.

2. The true rest, then, lies no longer in the ceasing from work but in the time of the resurrection. And so those who kept the solemnity of the sabbath according to the Law called it the one of the sabbath, as though there were one sabbath and not another. We, who have put on a spiritual observance and desire to shape our warfare after the likeness and the rest of God, and to render our service in that heavenly city where there is no copy, no shadow, but truth, say the first of the sabbath, as it is written: But in the evening of the sabbath, which dawns into the first of the sabbath. Nothing is more beautiful, nothing more explicit. Before the resurrection he said 'sabbath'; after the resurrection he called it 'the first of the sabbath'. John the Evangelist also says: On the first of the sabbath Mary Magdalene came in the morning, while it was still dark: darkness for the time, morning for grace. But these spoke more explicitly, according to the Gospel, as though the veil had been drawn back; David, on the other hand, placed under the Law, both kept the moderation of due caution and did not suppress the mystery, so that he declared the Lord's day as the second of the sabbath, whereby he might both reckon the order of the number to be kept for the Jews and recognize the spiritual sacrament as revealed. Let us hear, then, what he says who sings psalms to God on the second of the sabbath.

3. Great, he says, is the Lord and exceedingly to be praised in the city of our God, in his holy place. Is God not, then, great and to be praised everywhere? He is indeed great and mighty everywhere, but a narrow mind does not grasp the greatness of his divine power and grace. Yet the nearer our knowledge draws to God, the more surpassing his majesty seems to us. Thus Thomas, who before was saying: Unless I see in his hands the print of the nails and put my hand into his side, I will not believe, when he put in his finger and recognized the evident signs of the resurrection, marvelling within himself at the grace of God: My Lord, he says, and my God. Then, therefore, he most fully confessed his divinity and lordship, when he did not doubt that he had risen from the dead. Thus a hymn befits God in Zion, and a vow is paid to him in Jerusalem. What wonder, then, if in the heavenly city and in that supernal and ethereal place his might is proclaimed more mightily?

4. He added: spreading wide the exultation of the whole earth. And truly is he called great who has poured out over the whole world the exultation of this earth, in which before the shoots of intolerable sins sprouted, where there was grief, sadness and groaning. And so the conscience of men, which before mourned, sunk in the whirlpools of its own shameful deeds, now rejoices, released from fear through the pardon of Christ and the remission of all sins. It fits, then, even if we understand it as we have said. But since in some old copies we have found εὐρίζῳ ἀγαλλιάματι πάσης τῆς γῆς, which in Latin means "bonaradice exultatione uniuersae terrae" [with the good-rooted exultation of the whole earth], we notice that the sense is indeed the same, in that the divine gifts of God the Father in us are praised, who has given us an exultation founded on a good root, namely God Jesus, the true and most fruitful vine, from which the heavenly sacraments have flowed, in which there is perpetual and lasting joy, because by them every sin is washed away and the inner conscience is cleansed; and we recognize that the wording also fits, because we who exult in Christ rejoice with a well-rooted exultation. For the joy of the world is temporal, but perpetual is the joy of him who rejoices in the Lord.

5. Mount Zion, the sides of the north, the city of the great king. God is known in her steps, when he shall take her up. He has plainly expressed why there is exultation of the whole earth: because the Lord Jesus has gathered for himself a Church out of sinners. And so those who before were the sides of the north, that is, the companions and adherents of the devil, to whom it is said: Arise, north wind, and let the south wind come, have become faithful to Christ. For they are the ones of whom it is said: Those who trust in the Lord are like Mount Zion. So they have become Mount Zion through the grace of Christ and the sacrament of baptism. Heavy were the blasts of the intelligible north wind, with which he stirred up the harshest tempests and storms in human affections. He who harassed others began to lose his own. He had overthrown the whole nation of the Jews, he held all the nations subdued under his rule; they were his sides, that is, they breathed in concert with him. As we call the bodyguards and companions of a prince his sides, as a woman is the side of her husband because she is joined to him in a compliant partnership, so those who did his will were the sides of the devil. These, then, are now Mount Zion, who contemplate the eternal God and are intent upon him night and day. See, I pray you, how Paul, when he was persecuting the Church of the Lord, was a side of the north; see now, when he is read in the Church, how he is a mountain of watching, through whom we know and see the glory of Christ. And since the Greek said mountains, that is ὄρη, see how the little servants of Christ are mountains, mountains round about it, in which is the Church of the Lord, which is the city of the great king. At the same time, because the Lord, according to the tract of the sky, suffered ἐν Κρανίῳ [at the Skull], which was a place on the side of the north. And there also is Mount Zion, there the Jerusalem which is on earth.

6. See whether this too may not seem to be expressed: that the congregation of the people of God, which was joined to the north wind through guilt, has become the Church of Christ through grace, so that it is now called not the synagogue but the Church of Christ. And since the remnant of the Jews has been saved through the election of grace, in them God is known, in Peter, Paul, John, James, who, weighty and lofty men, are as it were the foundations and summits of the Church: like weighty foundations, since no heavy blasts of the north wind have shaken them, but unmoved they have kept the foundation of faith, so that the whole Church should not totter; most lofty like summits, since they have raised up its peak with the towers of their virtue. For just as a founder is recognized in his works and a builder in his buildings, so that, even if you do not hear the name, you nevertheless discover the craftsman from the quality of the works — and what wonder in human things, when of the eternal Creator himself, who is not seen by us, we yet understand the invisible things through the things that are made? — so in lofty and great houses, which house we are, if we hold the foundation of freedom and glory firm to the end, God the indweller is recognized, as the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob. For he is the true God who founded such men by the instruction of his precepts. Therefore, just as in them he showed himself the eternal God, so, he says, when he shall take up the city of the great king, he will pour into her steps the reception of the knowledge of himself, so that those may become taught of God who have both heard his commandment and judged that it must be carried out, lest they fall away from the firmness of that house built upon the rock.

7. Seeing this, he says, the kings of the earth were gathered together and passed over into one; they themselves, seeing, then marvelled. Good kings are those who can show themselves rulers of their own body, flowing from the very origin and teaching of the patriarch Abraham, who taught that sin can be overcome; therefore he was a true king, for he knew not how to be subject to shameful deeds, he in whom the allurement of guilt and error could not reign. Hence also it was said to such a man by the sons of Heth: You are a king from God among us. Such kings of the earth, then, who command the flesh, were gathered together, so as to be of one heart and one spirit, as we read that the multitude of believers had one soul and one heart and there was no division among them. These are they who passed over into one, not so much into one place as into the same disposition and purpose; for to the one who is approved it is said: Pass over, sit down at table. For it is no small virtue to pass over from wicked things to honourable ones, from earthly things to eternal ones, and to change the habit of carnal life and abolish all its practice, to take up new ways and to turn aside the whole state of the old manner of life; and rightly it is said to him: If you pass through the water, I am with you. God is with the one who passes over, not with the one who stops.

8. These, then, who before were at discord within themselves, when they came together into one in mind and in zeal, having received the peace which made both one and dissolved the enmities in his flesh by destroying the dividing wall, marvelled either at him who is known in her steps, or that men were so converted that those who before served the devil are now bound over to the Lord Jesus in the devotion of a righteous service. And since, as kings of their own body, they had experienced in themselves no small labour — for it is neither easy nor a small thing to conquer the mind, to cut back the desires, to tame the wantonness of the flesh, to ward off lust, which fought even against Paul, to bind him captive to itself by the law of sin — just as they marvelled at grace in those who had advanced in virtue, so too they were disturbed lest anyone should slip back into vices, cast down from the higher summit of the virtues. For the human condition is prone and changeable either way, so that wherever it is intent, there it inclines and tends, either to the pursuit of the virtues or to the allurement of sins.

9. And therefore they were shaken by no small feeling, so that out of dread trembling seized them, as they considered what is written, that in the places of the ungodly the righteous groan. Thus see, I pray you, Peter weeping most bitterly because error had stolen upon him and had bent the virtue of his mind through the lapse of bodily frailty, and rightly rebuked by Christ in a low voice because he had dared to lay claim to steadfastness and had not considered that his nature was liable to fall. And he indeed by grace amended his error, but Judas remained in his crime and persisted in groaning. He was righteous when he was joined to the number of the apostles; but since he was tripped up by the devil, he now laments and groans in the places of the ungodly that he has fallen back from grace into punishment. When, therefore, such men fell, as was Judas the betrayer, or Saul, who had been called to the kingdom by the Lord his God, even strong men trembled.

10. Then, comparing with how great a contest the end is reached, how great are the afflictions of the righteous, how great the anxieties, what an adversary, how great a struggle in long-continued labour, how great the bitternesses of a stricter purpose, what bonds of continence, what scourges of conscience, they were not a little troubled lest anyone should yield either to such great labours of a more austere life or to its pains. For there are pains there, bitter and heavy; that is, in the Church, in that city of the great king, there are pains as of a woman in labour, until Christ be formed in us. For Paul was not without pain when he saw among the foolish Galatians the too slow progress of his teaching, and therefore he said: My little children, of whom I am in labour again, until Christ be formed in you. The Lord too testifies in the Gospel that the pains of a woman in labour are heavy, when he says: A woman, when she is in labour, has sorrow, because her day has come; but when she has borne the child, she no longer remembers the sorrow for joy, because a man is born into this world. Great, then, is the labour for one to acquire Christ for himself, lest without a helmsman he toss like a ship on the sea of this world. Thus the apostles were sad when Christ told them that he would return to the Father, because they thought that, with their guide absent, they would be forsaken. Christ is the joy; he is also the child with whom he is in labour who has received in the womb of his mind the spirit of salvation. He who has brought forth and nourished exults; he who is in labour is shaken and pierced.

11. It is good that you should both bring him forth and nourish him. You shall not cook a lamb in its mother's milk, lest it be said to you: Woe to those who are with child and to those who give suck. For Christ was born of Mary in such a way that you should acknowledge him, as the ox acknowledged its owner, and know that he himself created you and he himself possessed your forefathers. Do not nourish him as a little child, but know that he is to be adored as true and perfect God from true and perfect God. For so the magus adored him, that he did not cook a lamb in its mother's milk, but venerated him as the eternal God. Thus, to find him, he looked up at the sky; he therefore did not seek on earth him whom a star shining from heaven was pointing out. Therefore Mary was not in labour with him but brought him forth, because she knew that both Lord and Saviour was to be begotten of her, as she herself testifies, saying: My soul magnifies the Lord, and my spirit has rejoiced in God my Saviour. There, then, are pains as of a woman in labour. It is pain to be in labour, it is pain to nourish him whom from the beginning you ought to hold as perfect.

12. And so very many are heavily shattered, as the verse that follows signifies: With a vehement spirit you shall shatter the ships of Tarshish. For while the servants of God desire to reach the perfection of faith, they wear down their body, chastising it more severely and bringing it into the service of slavery, lest those who had already been approved be rejected. And they do this in the Holy Spirit, strong and vehement. For he is the spirit of counsel and of power, so that by great abstinence they dissolve their body, whereby they may empty out all its pleasures, so that it may be said to them: Grow strong, you slackened hands and loosened knees. For when someone has been loosed from this earthly frame, then he rises stronger to eternal life. I lie, unless Scripture bears me out, which says: to be dissolved and to be with Christ is much better — he who is dissolved begins to be with Christ — and elsewhere: the time of my dissolution is at hand. He, then, who has practised dissolving himself through abstinence will not be able to fear the day of his passion.

13. And well does he say 'a vehement spirit' when he is about to speak of ships. Now he spoke of ships for bodies, which are moved by the wandering surge of diverse passions and quickly sink unless they have a helmsman. Hence he also says elsewhere: Those who go down to the sea in ships. But they sail well who in their ships carry before them the cross of Christ as a mast, and from it search out the blasts of the winds, so as to steer their bodies towards the grace of the Holy Spirit, safe and secure on the wood of the Lord; nor do they let their ships stray over the seas with a wandering surge, but by the direction of their course they press on towards the harbour of salvation and the consummation of grace, that they may gain a faithful anchorage, where, though dissolved, they may restore their courses in the resurrection, where they cannot fear shipwreck.

14. Let us too, then, look carefully about this grace, let us be its watchmen, let us search it out, so that our bodies may become ships of Tarshish. For then the flesh is brought into servitude, when we are intent upon the grace of God, so that we follow what is bitter for a time, as fasting is, not what is sweet and pleasurable. Tarshish, then, is the watchtower of grace, an intelligible city, like the higher Zion. To it Jonah was hastening. Of it Scripture said that Jehoshaphat allied himself with King Uzziah3, and it so pleased him that they should make ships and that the ships should go to him to Tarshish. But because he joined himself to an ungodly man, therefore a prophet was sent to him to say to him: The Lord has destroyed your work, and your ships have been shattered, and they could not go to Tarshish. By this it is signified that the bodies of those who transgress the commandment of God cannot reach the consummation of grace. But to Solomon, as being a wise and peaceable man, ships came from Tarshish with gold and silver and ivory tusks without any mishap, and he himself sent ships there. For he had with King Hiram a spiritual exchange of sacred trade and a disposition of mutual grace vying with each other. These ships, then, those who possess a royal authority within themselves to restrain the fierceness of the body, seeing them become incorruptible from corruptible, shining with the highest light of grace from stained, renewed from dissolved and worn down, living from dead, rising from buried, recount in celebrated discourse.

15. As we have heard, so also have we seen in the city of the Lord of powers, in the city of our God; God has founded it for ever. We have heard outside the city, we have seen in the city, for which God is the eternal light, where the day shines without the sun of the world, the moon is not sought; its foundation is not temporal but eternal.

16. We have received, he says, O God, your mercy in the midst of your temple. If it is said to the Son of God, we understand the temple in the way he himself spoke of his body: Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up. Truly the temple of God is the body of Christ, in which is the purification of our sins. Truly the temple of God is that flesh in which there could be no contagion of sin, but which itself was a sacrifice for the transgression of the whole world. Truly the temple of God is that flesh in which the image of God shone, in which the fullness of the divinity dwelt bodily, since Christ himself is the fullness. Therefore it is said to him: We have received, O God, your mercy in the midst of your temple. What is this, but what he says: In the midst of you stands one whom you do not know, that is, he is in your midst and is not seen? But if it is referred to the Father, what is "in the midst of your temple", but that God was in Christ reconciling the world to himself?

17. In that temple, then, we have received, he says, your mercy, that is, the Word which was made flesh and dwelt in us. For as Christ is redemption, so also is he mercy. And what greater mercy than that for our shameful deeds he offered himself to be sacrificed, to wash with his blood the world, whose sin could have been abolished in no other way? For if the Apostle said of holy men: You are the temple of God, and the Holy Spirit dwells in you, how much more should I call the flesh of the Lord Jesus the temple of God, who is read to have been always full of the Holy Spirit, and himself testifies, saying: I perceive that power has gone out from me, the power which healed the bitter wounds of all! This too can be understood: that he said he had received with the people the mercy of God in the midst of his temple, since he himself founded his Church and spread it abroad for ever, since truly he himself, with his only-begotten Son, bestowed this grace on his people, and showed him also to be its builder, saying: He shall build me a city, which, spread over the whole world, has brought it about that the whole earth should be full of his praise and of his name. For as it is written: The earth is full of his praise, so it is written: A name has been given him above every name, that at the name of Jesus every knee should bend, of things in heaven and on earth and under the earth, and every tongue confess that the Lord Jesus is in the glory of God the Father.

18. Hence the prophet said fittingly: According to your name, O God, so also is your praise unto the ends of the earth; your right hand is full of your righteousness. It is no wonder if he who is the right hand of God is full of the righteousness of God. And the same one is the power of God and the righteousness of God, so that, inasmuch as he is full of the righteousness of God, you may know that in him is that fullness of righteousness which the Father has; and inasmuch as he is read to be the power and righteousness and wisdom of God the Father, you may understand that nothing of the divine majesty is divided in him, but may recognize that the Son has received all things from the Father, and that the Son is the radiance of all the Father's glory and the stamp of his substance, who has so expressed the Father that the whole Father is in him, as the whole Son is in the Father.

19. And since the prophet saw in the spirit that the praises of Christ were to be proclaimed in all lands, making, as it were by a kind of epilogue, a departure from the discourse he had undertaken, he exhorts us to gladness and to the worship of his name, saying: Let Mount Zion be glad, that is, those who trust in the Lord, as he himself afterwards interpreted it, saying: Those who trust in the Lord are like Mount Zion, and so on. Let the Lord Jesus himself, who is the mountain and height of his Church, which contemplates its Christ with all its affection, be glad in its faith and devotion, which has pleased God, since the unbelieving one has been rejected. This too, then, can be devoutly understood: that he himself, being higher than all, is also the mountain up which he who preaches the faith is bidden to ascend, as Scripture says: Go up into a high mountain, you who bring good tidings to Zion.

20. Let the souls that confess to you exult, he says. These are the daughters of that Judaea in which God is known. For this Judaea which is in the flesh or in the letter did not acknowledge the Lord; for if it had acknowledged him, it would also have received him. But it did not acknowledge him whom it did not receive, and it fled from his judgements, by which he declared that they sprang not from father Abraham but from the seed of the devil, since every sin is the seed of the enemy. But the daughters of confession do not commit sin; rather they overshadow it, and therefore blessed are they whose sins are covered. And rightly will the daughters of confession exult because of the judgements of the Lord, when they come to know his judgements and his ways, which in this world are unsearchable and untraceable for men; but when they begin to behold the truth face to face, then they will grasp the things that now seem unsearchable and will rejoice in the knowledge of them.

21. Surround Zion, he says, and embrace her; tell in her towers. Those who understand Zion surround her and embrace her with their minds, so as to hold her and not let her go, lest the watchful discipline of virtue be taken from them. And so she says in the Song of Songs: I held him and I will not let him go, she who was seeking the wisdom of God and embracing the righteousness of God with the arms of charity and, as it were, with the arms of faith and devotion; for this is the embrace of a devout mind. Let us too, then, grasp, as far as we can, the inner mysteries of truth, and write them down and fasten them to our minds, and the power of the heavenly oracle, to attain which that Moses, learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, went up the mountain and entered into the cloud, to come to know things covered and hidden, with Jesus as his companion cleaving to him, because no one can grasp the uncertain and hidden things of wisdom without the true Jesus. And therefore, in the figure of Jesus son of Nave [Joshua], the presence of the true Saviour that would breathe upon him was signified to him, through whom all would become taught of God, who would open the Law and reveal the Gospel.

22. Therefore those who have surrounded Zion with their prudence and embraced her with their understanding go up upon her towers and tell from above to him who has not been able to go up into her towers what must be done or what must be guarded against. For the city of God is walled, and therefore elsewhere the Lord says: Behold, Jerusalem, I have painted your walls; it is set with towers, from which a lurking enemy can be detected if he begins to approach. He has his armies with which he is wont to besiege the souls of God, and to bring up siege engines so that he may storm the fortified towers. But you, he says, proclaim from the towers; for in the towers of Jerusalem there is abundance. Proclaim righteousness, urge watchfulness; for wisdom is sung in the going out and is proclaimed on the highest of the walls, and elsewhere again he says: Upon your walls I have set watchmen day and night; to the end they shall not be silent. Tell, therefore, without intermission, without end. The adversary is awake, he goes about and with a wild beast's rage seeks whom he may find to devour. Therefore the Lord must always be sung, who crushes the teeth of the lions and breaks their mouths, so that they cannot harm us.

23. Set, he says, your hearts on his power, that is, so that you may understand his power, and distribute her steps, that you may tell it to another generation, for this is our God for ever and unto the age of the age. He will rule us unto the ages. The Greek says βάρεις, that is, lofty and towered houses. Hence the prophet, when he was speaking of the city of God and of those who dwell in it: distribute, he says, her steps, that is, her high and lofty dwellings in the heavenly places, whereby he teaches that the order of merits is to be kept; for each one, indeed, in his own order. Hence the Lord too says that he goes and prepares dwellings for his disciples, so that each one may obtain fitting abodes in proportion to his merits, in which he may enjoy that eternal rest.

24. There are also sublime and lofty precepts, in which are the hidden mysteries of devotion and the contemplations of heavenly discourses. These, he says, distribute, and divide them according to the capacity of each, as much as each can grasp with his own understanding. For Paul did not discuss the same things with all. To some he preached nothing but Christ Jesus and him crucified, not in the persuasiveness of the wisdom of the word, but in the demonstration of the Spirit and of power; but among the perfect he spoke wisdom, because the whole people was not capable of receiving the wisdom which is hidden in a mystery.

25. Christ hid it for this reason: that the foolish may not understand it, that the prudent may seek it more eagerly and tell it to another generation, one that would be more perfect by the power of the Spirit, since the former generation, through weakness of faith, could not know the sacraments of perfect wisdom, and therefore sought a sign and rejected the truth, although surely there is not a truth of the sign, but a sign of the truth. Hence the Lord answered it: An evil and adulterous generation seeks a sign, and no sign shall be given it but that of Jonah the prophet. And so, to convict the unbelief of the Jews, the example of Jonah is summoned, because the Ninevites believed the sign and thought the preaching of Jonah, who had been in the belly of the whale, must be followed, whereas the Jews did not believe even after the resurrection of the Lord Jesus, who after three days rose from the heart of the earth and lives and reigns through all ages. Amen.

On Psalm 48 (49)

Its title is: Unto the end; for the sons of Korah, a psalm of David.

1. Hear these things, all nations, perceive with your ears, all you who inhabit the circle of the earth. At the very beginning we recognize the voice of the saving Lord calling the nations to the Church, that they may renounce error, follow the truth and acknowledge the gift of devout worship. But because the hearts of the human succession had been infected with the serpent's poisons, and the conscience, liable to sins, could not call itself back while it despaired of pardon, he promises a remedy, he promises forgiveness freely, by the bounty of his loving-kindness, so that the guilty man may not fear, and the man of good conscience may rejoice that he renders the ministry of service to a good Lord, who knows how to forgive sins and to honour virtues. Then, in the same sequence of calling, the Lord also exhorts us in the Gospel, saying: Come to me, all you who labour and are burdened, and I will refresh you. Take my yoke upon you and learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart. Next he records that his yoke is sweet and his burden light, since he who has despaired of the medicine will not be able to be healthy. And therefore gentleness is welcome to all, for it heals the wounds of the inner minds.

2. Being about to come, then, to the sick, about to come as a physician to heal our grievous sores, he sets forth medicines, so that men may hear and run together with faithful diligence to the remedy of salvation. So that woman in the Gospel earned healing who had been suffering from a flow of blood for twelve years. She heard that the physician had come, she hastened to touch him, saying: If I touch his fringe, I shall be saved. She said this within herself, and Christ heard; and she touched the fringe, and power went out from the Savior, which healed the sick woman, strengthened the feeble, stopped the flow, approved her devotion. In like manner, therefore, here too he calls all nations to the fount of wisdom and prudence, he promises redemption to all, so that no one may tremble, no one despair, where no one is excepted, but every soul is invited to grace, that it may be redeemed from guilt without price and obtain the fruit of eternal life.

3. This is the sense of the psalm, which we shall set out in its order once we have explained the force and character of the words above. For the nations are commanded to hear, and those who inhabit the circle of the earth to perceive with their ears; that circle of the lands, surely, of which he spoke above: The earth is the Lord's and the fullness of it, the circle of the lands and those who dwell in it. He has founded it upon the seas and prepared it upon the rivers. This the Greeks call οἰκουμένην, because it is inhabited by Christ, as he himself said: For I will dwell in them; for he dwells in it to fill what seemed empty. What, then, is the οἰκουμένη but the holy Church, the temple of God and the dwelling-place of Christ? Nor was it unfitting that the Latins, with a word of different sound indeed but of equal sense, named it the circle of the earth, because the life of the saints, who are inhabited by the Spirit, rolls round in a circle like a wheel, without any stumbling in its course.

4. In tribulations, then, the Church is founded, in tempests and storms, in anxieties and grief; in adversities and in rivers it is prepared. In what rivers? Hear him who says: The rivers have lifted up their voice. Hear also in the Gospel: He who believes in me, as Scripture has said, rivers of living water shall flow from his belly. But this he said of the Spirit, which those who believed in him were receiving. In these rivers, therefore, the Church is prepared, in which the grace of God flows forth. These are the streams which perceive the word of God with the ear and speak, so as to pour the word into the hearts of each one. And therefore Scripture made a distinction by saying: Hear these things, all nations, perceive with your ears, all you who inhabit the circle of the earth. All can hear, yet not all can perceive with their ears, except the elect of God. And therefore the Savior says: He who has ears to hear, let him hear. He means those spiritual ears of the inner man, whose nostrils too we read of, as Job says: The divine spirit which is in my nostrils. Not all men have these ears and these nostrils; for the form of the body is one thing, that of spiritual grace another. He who is spiritual hears all things, judges all things, but the natural man does not perceive the things that are of the Spirit of God; for this one is earth-born, that one heavenly.

5. And therefore he added: all you earth-born and sons of men, together in one, rich and poor, that he might call all. Who is the earth-born but a son of man? Who is the spiritual but a son of God? The one formed from blood and begotten of the pleasure of the flesh and of man, the other born of God; one rich in every word and in knowledge, another poor, yet rich in grace, since the poor man cried out, and the Lord heard him. On the contrary, there is also the rich man in pride and the poor man in humility. All are called to the Church, so that all may be redeemed by Christ. He who is sick finds a physician, he who is well acquires wisdom, the captive a Redeemer, the free man a rewarder. Divine Scripture builds up all; in it each one finds the means either to heal his wounds or to confirm his merits. And the call of the rich and the poor, made together in one, summons us to a certain humility and equality, so that neither the rich man may disdain the needy nor the poor man envy the rich, but one grace may bind both to itself, since the Lord too became poor though he was rich, so that he might be the Savior both of the poor and of the rich.

6. And he went on: My mouth shall speak wisdom, and the meditation of my heart prudence. Rightly are all called, since the fount of wisdom abounds for all; nor is she compared to a treasure of money, who is declared more precious than all treasures. And therefore neither is the rich man turned away nor the poor man shut out, since wisdom discriminates not between means but between wills; he is the fitter who is foremost in affection and nearest in discipline. But if the meditation of the heart speaks prudence, how much more does perfect teaching! Next we are admonished not to utter hasty speech, but to weigh what is to be said by a certain exercise of meditation and on the balance of the inner mind, since it is written elsewhere: A deceitful balance is not good.

7. I will incline my ear to a parable, I will open my proposition on the psaltery. The wise hearer attends and inclines his ear, that he may understand the figures of parables. The Savior calls him his own ear who has the ear for hearing. The Lord also opens his closed proposition when he has found a fit instrument for himself and a vessel of election, which he calls a psaltery; like such a psaltery, Paul made the song of sweet grace resound, all his strings sounding in harmony with him, striking the inner and the outer string with the plectrum of the Holy Spirit, so that he prayed both with the tongue and with the mind. And therefore he says: I will pray with the spirit, I will pray also with the mind; I will sing with the spirit, I will sing also with the mind. Such a man, then, Christ promises long before, through whom he would make manifest hidden things and open what was wrapped in the obscurity of prophetic speech. A good psaltery it is when life agrees with faith and the flesh with the soul, and the will breathes in accord with virtue. This is the sweet psaltery, where the discipline of living is melodious, so that what is written may be fulfilled: The tongue of the dumb shall be clear.

8. There follows: Why shall I fear in the evil day? The iniquity of my heel shall compass me about; that is: since my mouth speaks wisdom and the meditation of my heart prudence, what can I fear in the day of judgement, unless perhaps the iniquity of my heel must be washed away from me? Our iniquity is one thing, that of our heel another, in which Adam was wounded by the serpent's tooth and left the inheritance of the human succession liable to his wound, so that we all limp from that wound. Hence the Lord washed his disciples' feet, to wash away the serpent's poisons, and Peter is rebuked because he was excusing himself, so that the Lord should not wash his feet. And therefore it was said to him: Unless I wash your feet, you will have no part with me. On hearing this he offered not only his feet but also his hands to be washed. To him the Lord answered: He who has been washed has no need to wash again, except to wash his feet, but he is wholly clean.

9. Therefore David says: My iniquities have gone over my head, he who knew that he had been conceived in iniquity and brought forth by his mother in sins. But the Lord, who had no sins of his own and knew no iniquities of his own, says: The iniquity of my heel shall compass me about, that is, the iniquity of Adam, not my own. But it cannot be a terror to me; for in the day of judgement it is our own crimes in us that are punished, not those of another's iniquity. Hence I think that the iniquity of the heel is rather the slipperiness of offending than any guilt of our own offence. And rightly the Lord, who took up all things for us, says: Let us wash the feet too, so that we may take away the slipperiness of the heel, whereby there may be a sure standing of virtues and no one may slip through his father's error; he who is ready to stand by his purpose, let him not fear the slipperiness of the inheritance, he who desires to keep the footstep of virtue. The iniquity of our heel, then, is the transgression of Adam, through which he slid into contempt and neglect of the heavenly commandments.

10. The iniquity of the heel is also the detestable betrayal of Judas. "Of the heel" for this reason, because it is written: He who eats my bread has lifted up his heel against me. Well did he say "his" there and "my" here: his, because Christ is without iniquity, while this is the iniquity of the traitor alone; but Christ calls Judas "my" "heel" as being the last part of his body, Christ whose body and members we are. The apostles are the more excellent and active members of Christ, Judas the heel, as being the extreme part of the body, exposed to the serpent and open to a wound. The first heel, then, is Adam, the second heel Judas; the one is the fall of the whole inheritance, the other of the traitor alone, who could not entangle the inheritance, since we are no longer carnal but the inheritance of the Redeemer, as Scripture says: There is an inheritance for those who believe in the Lord.

11. There is also that iniquity of his heel which compassed Christ: Compassing, he says, they compassed me, and in the name of the Lord I have taken vengeance on them. His heel is the synagogue; the beauty and glory of his countenance is the Church, as it is written: The Lord has reigned, he has put on beauty.

12. Those, then, are the heel of whom he says: who trust in their own power and glory in the abundance of their riches; for the rich, indeed, have been in want and have hungered. This was said of the Jews; but all those too who live luxuriously and do not hold the head are the heel of Christ. But these are general things, which extend widely; specifically, however, he spoke of Judas the traitor, who seemed to himself strong and rich in the glory of the apostleship.

13. Then the discourse is woven on concerning the Lord's Passion: A brother does not redeem; a man shall redeem. Nor shall he give to God his propitiation and the price of the redemption of his soul. That is: Why shall I fear in the evil day? For what can harm me, who not only have no need of a redeemer, but am myself the Redeemer of all? Shall I make others free, and myself tremble for myself? Behold, I will make all things new, I who am above the very affection and dutiful love of brotherhood. Him whom a brother, poured forth into the light from the same mother's womb, cannot redeem, because he is held fast by the weakness of a like nature, a man shall redeem; but that man of whom it is written that he will send them, the Lord, a man who will save them, who said of himself: You seek to kill me, a man who have spoken the truth to you. But although he is a man, who shall know him? Why shall no one know him? Because, as there is one God, so there is also one mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus. He it is alone who shall redeem man, surpassing brothers in loving-kindness, since for strangers he shed his blood, which no one can offer for a brother. And so he did not spare his own body, that he might redeem us from sin, and gave himself as a redemption for all, as his true witness, the Apostle Paul, asserted, who says: I speak the truth, I do not lie. But why shall he alone redeem? Because no one can equal him in loving-kindness, so as to lay down his life for his little servants, no one in integrity; for all are under sin, all lie subject to that fall of Adam; the Redeemer alone is chosen who could not be liable to the old sin. Therefore by the man let us understand the Lord Jesus, who took up the condition of man, that in his flesh he might crucify the sin of all and blot out the bond of all with his blood.

14. But perhaps you may say: "How is it denied that a brother will redeem, when he himself said: I will declare your name to my brothers?" But not as a brother, but as man, Christ Jesus, in whom God was, forgave us our sins. For so it is written: God was in Christ, reconciling the world to himself, in that Christ Jesus of whom alone it was said that the Word was made flesh and dwelt in us. Not as a brother, then, but as Lord he dwelt in us, when he dwelt in the flesh. Since, then, he reconciled the world to God, he himself certainly had no need of reconciliation; for for what sin of his own would he propitiate God, he who knew no sins? Then, when the Jews demanded the didrachma, which was given for sin according to the Law, he said to Peter: Simon, from whom do the kings of the earth receive tribute or census? From their own sons or from strangers? Peter answered: From strangers. The Lord said to him: Then the sons are free. But so that we may not give them offence, cast a hook and take the fish that comes up first, and when its mouth is opened you will find a stater; take that and give it for me and for you. He shows that he does not owe a propitiation for sins for himself, since he was not the slave of sin, but was free from every error, the Son of God. For the Son sets free; the slave is in guilt. Therefore he who is free from all does not give the price of the redemption of his own soul, he the price of whose blood could suffice to redeem all the sins of the whole world. Rightly, then, does he set others free who owes nothing for himself.

15. I add more. Not only does Christ not owe for himself the price of his redemption or a propitiation for sin, but even if you take it of any man whatever, it may be understood that individuals do not owe their own propitiation, since Christ is the propitiation of all and he himself is the redemption of all. For whose blood is now fit for his own redemption, when Christ has shed his blood for the redemption of all? Is there, then, anyone's blood that could be compared with the blood of Christ? Or what man is so powerful that he could give his own propitiation for himself beyond that propitiation which Christ offered in himself, who alone reconciled the world to God through his blood? What greater victim, what more excellent sacrifice, what better advocate than he who was made an entreaty for the sins of all and gave his life as a redemption for us? No propitiation or redemption of individuals, then, is sought, since the price of all is the blood of Christ, with which the Lord Jesus redeemed us, who alone reconciled the Father and laboured unto the end, since he himself took up our labour, saying: Come to me, all you who labour, and I will refresh you. See him labouring: I have laboured crying out, my throat has become hoarse, and elsewhere: Issachar desired what is good, resting among the lots, and below: He put his shoulder to labour and became a man of the soil. Therefore man will no longer give his own propitiation or redemption, since he has once been washed from sin through the blood of Christ; yet he will labour to keep the precepts of living and not depart from the heavenly commandments. While he lives, let him be in labour and persevere in it, so that he may live unto the end, lest he too should die by death when he has already been redeemed from death.

16. But he who has kept the commandments of life shall not see destruction, when he sees those die who seemed to themselves most wise and most prudent in this world. This applies specially to the scribes and Pharisees, who claim for themselves the chairs in the synagogues as though they were the primacy of wisdom. They truly labour in vain, since they think that they are set free by the didrachma, while they have despised the spiritual price of their soul and that unique sacrifice of the Lord's body through some empty interpretation of the Law, shunning the sacrament of baptism. For the blood of goats and bulls redeems no one, but the one blood of Christ frees all, of which in Leviticus there is the figure, not the truth. For it is not by the didrachma, but by the price of that blood alone, that we are set free.

17. Let us, then, be taught of God, so that none of us may see destruction on that day when the wise of this world, or the princes of the Jews, will be condemned to perpetual death, when the unwise and the foolish shall perish together. The two are not the same: the foolish is he who has no wisdom and understands foolish things; the unwise he who is wise amiss: The unwise man has said that there is no God; just as the iniquitous man is so called, not because he does not know equity, but because through the wickedness of his heart he commits iniquitous things, so the dishonest man too is so called because he wishes to destroy honesty by his malice, not because he is ignorant of honesty. When, then, the unwise and the foolish themselves perish, they leave their riches to strangers; for they cannot find their own lawful heir of folly and dishonesty. For the lawful ones say: That we are sons of God. And if sons, heirs also; heirs indeed of God, and co-heirs of Christ, provided that we suffer with him, so that we may also be glorified together. The unwise, then, do not have riches, since they do not have an inheritance either. The faithless do not have riches, nor a possession; for to the faithful man the whole world is a possession. They do not have an inheritance, since there is an inheritance only for those who believe in the Lord. But they have their own riches, and so the covetous man says: I have nowhere to gather my fruits; for money is not the true fruit, but the fruit of the covetous. And the Lord says to the covetous man: Go, sell your goods, and come, follow me; for a patrimony is not the perfect good, but these are the goods of the covetous, which consist in some gain. Sell, he says, what you think to be yours, though they are not yours, since they pass to others like a river, and follow me, so that you may know the immortal good. The wise, then, are led into destruction, from whom God has hidden what he has revealed to little ones. The unwise and the foolish will perish together and will be in one and the same place, and they will leave their riches to strangers, since wickedness does not find a lawful successor.

18. And their sepulchres are their houses for ever, their tabernacles in progeny and generation; they have called their names in their own lands. Everywhere Scripture reckons sepulchres among base things, to which the degenerate and those greedy for this life are compared: Their throat is an open sepulchre. For these, then, who live from day to day, sepulchres serve as houses. For the house of the righteous is not on earth but in heaven, as the Apostle taught, saying: But our citizenship is in heaven. That is our house which is the house of Christ, whence Scripture says: And Moses indeed was faithful in all his house as a servant, for a testimony of the things which were to be spoken, but Christ as a son in his own house. For the devout, then, the house is the Church, for the devout the fatherland is heaven, and therefore the righteous man says: I am a newcomer with you on the earth, and a sojourner, like all my fathers. For the luxurious the house is a sepulchre; indeed, they live as in a sepulchre who can say: Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we shall die, not having the resurrection before their eyes. And therefore, fixed to their tombs and entombed in them, they cling to them, since they did not believe that they could be raised again. Their dwelling-place, then, is in their own grave, and their tabernacle is in an earthly progeny and generation, for they leave worms as heirs of their body, so that their memory may be here only and cannot pass into eternity.

19. Then their names in their own lands, since their works are corruptible and earthly, and therefore their names are written there where they preferred to live. But to those who have lifted up their works it is said: Do not rejoice that the demons are subject to you, but rejoice that your names are written in heaven. From this, then, let us understand that it was not idly that the Lord rebuked those who built sepulchres for themselves, or the tombs of the prophets, and adorned them. For the Jews are condemned, who offered to the prophets whom they had slain earthly consolations, not eternal rewards. And therefore he forbids his disciple to bury his father, since he must be intent without ceasing upon the eternal Father. And in the book of Isaiah it is written: What, he says, that you have built and hewn out a sepulchre for yourself? And Mary is rebuked, who was seeking Christ in the tomb. Leave the sepulchres, which those left who, on hearing the voice of the Lord in his Passion, rose from the tombs; do not dwell among the dead, you who live. Hear the voice of Jesus raising you up: Rise, you who sleep, and arise from the dead, so that the light of Christ may be poured into you for ever. His light is the Holy Spirit, who shone upon the holy apostles like fire in the dividing of the tongues.

20. There follows: Man, when he was in honour, did not understand; he has been compared to the beasts which are without sense and has become like them. This their way is a stumbling-block to them, and afterwards they shall take pleasure in their mouth. Because man did not understand when he was in honour — and it is an honour that he was made in the image of God, was made capable of reason — he has been compared to the beasts which have no sense; but he who has understood is compared to the angels. And therefore the latter is magnificent in the brightness of good works, while the former is reckoned like a neighing horse for the filth of his living, which brings a stumbling-block on the way to those who live in baseness and obscenity; the folly, indeed, of a man defiles his ways. Men of this kind live according to their appetite, which creates a stumbling-block, and after the stumbling-block they will take pleasure in their mouth, but not in their heart; for they are not good, of whom it is said: With their mouth they blessed, and with their heart they cursed. By this an answer is also given to those who have it so written in their codices: And afterwards they will bless with their mouth. For let your word be near, both in your mouth and in your heart.

21. Like sheep they shall be led down into hell, death shall feed upon them; and the righteous shall have dominion over them in the morning, and their help shall grow old in hell, away from their glory. Those who did not wish Christ to feed them, death shall feed upon. Who, then, would forsake the good shepherd, who lays down his life for his sheep, since the care of his flock belongs to him? Or who would choose the hireling death, which is paid back as the fitting wage of the worst works? Know at the same time, O man, that Christ is the true shepherd, who feeds his own unto life. Death crept in, which leads strangers to destruction and devours those over whom it can prevail because of their crimes. Though in this life they had a zeal for power and riches, so as to rule over others, yet in the resurrection there will be servitude, when the morning splendour has shone upon the righteous; of this it is a figure that Jacob is set as lord over his brother. A wretched servitude, then, that at the time when others are called into the glory of splendour and light, their glory should grow old and be consumed in the darkness of the underworld; and rightly it is said of them: they have been driven out from their honour and from their glory, since this glory is temporal.

22. But God, he says, shall deliver my soul from the hand of hell, when he has received it. Well does he say this who knew that his soul was not to be left in hell. For he descended to set the captives free from the jaws of the underworld, not to remain a captive among them.

23. Since, then, the things that are in this world cannot pass over with us, therefore it is said to you: Do not fear when a man has become rich and when the glory of his house has been multiplied, for when he dies he shall not take all things, nor shall the glory of his house descend with him. Do not, he says, be dismayed at riches and the power of the rich man and the glory of the world, since those things are perishable and depart more quickly than they came. This treasure is a dream; you wake, and it is gone, since he who has been able to sleep off the drunkenness of this world and to take on the sobriety of virtue despises these things and counts money as nothing. Nor should you fear when you hear of the multiplied glory of some powerful man's house. Look closely, and it is empty, since it has nothing of the fullness of faith. And what shall I say of a house? This whole world was empty before Christ filled it with the number of believers, and therefore it is written: He shall judge among the nations, he shall fill up the ruins. For what else was there before in this world but the ruin of unbelief? What is ruin? Hear him who says: Where there is ruin, there also are the eagles, that is: where there is ruin, there also is resurrection. Ruin is empty of firmness; resurrection is the fullness of living. And therefore the ruin of that Adam emptied us, but the grace of Christ filled us again. Therefore he emptied himself, that he might fill us and that the fullness of power might dwell in the flesh of man. Learn what ruin is. While a man lives, he is filled with spirit; when he has died, he is surely empty of this vital spirit. Much more does the loss of spiritual grace make him empty whom grace was wont to fill, so that we may say: For we have all received of his fullness, and grace for grace. Of another it is said: I have laboured for what is empty, but the righteous man is like the scent of a full field.

24. But what is not empty that is of this age and of the world, when he who despised this world with sober virtue rightly said: Naked was I born, naked also shall I die? Can anyone take with him what he possesses? Here he leaves all things behind, he enters the tomb alone, and empty is the sepulchre of that rich man whom the earth itself could not contain. Hence it was said: Shall you alone dwell upon the earth? Does the glory of his house, or his wealth, descend into that sepulchre? See the force of Scripture. The glory of the world will not descend with the sinner, but the glory of virtue will ascend with the innocent; and, to put it more briefly, the glory of a man will ascend with him who ascends, and will not descend with him who descends. What belongs to grace and virtue will ascend. Into paradise one ascends, into hell one descends. Let them descend, he says, alive into hell; and therefore Lazarus the poor man was carried up by angels into Abraham's bosom. The luxurious rich man, lifting up his eyes in hell, asks Abraham to send the poor man to touch his tongue with his finger, so that, set in the greatest burning, he might obtain some rest. See that poor man abounding in all good things, whom the blessed rest of the holy patriarch surrounded; see this one empty, who seemed to himself to abound in all things in this world, whose soul was blessed in his lifetime, but who now, helpless, is tormented by death.

25. Hence there fittingly follows: For his soul shall be blessed in his lifetime; he will confess to you when you have done well to him. Foolish men, he says, will bless him with an earthly blessing, men who consider present things, not future ones; his hangers-on and parasites will bless him. He himself too thinks himself blessed if he has seized some field that he desired. He confesses to God then, when things go favourably for him in this world, but when adversities befall, he curses with an ungodly mouth. This, then, is what he says: that the worldly man does not give thanks when placed in poverty and adversity; but the righteous man in straits blesses the Lord all the more with grateful affection. Then the devil says to God: What wonder if Job blesses you? All things abound to him. But put forth, he says, your hand and touch the things that he possesses; let us see if he blesses you to your face. And the Lord gave him power to take away the things that were of this world and to drench his flesh with the cruelty of a sore. Yet Job blessed, saying: The Lord gave, the Lord has taken away; blessed be the name of the Lord.

26. He shall go in even to the progeny of his fathers; even for ever he shall not see light. Man, when he was in honour, did not understand; he has been compared to the beasts which have no sense, and has become like them. Therefore, he says, this man shall go in to the progeny of his fathers. Who is the father of the ungodly, the Lord has declared, saying: You are of your father the devil. There are also those fathers of whom it is said: Leave the dead to bury their dead. Therefore, too, the Moabites were forbidden to enter into the church of the Lord unto the third and fourth generation, so that their sons, because of the sins of their fathers, might enter into the generation of those whose deeds and morals they imitated. Hence it is also written: The children of adulterers shall be imperfect. He, then, who follows not that Father who is in heaven, but the one who lives in earthly filth, enters into the progeny of the earthly father. Such, indeed, as is the earthly man, such also are the earthly, so that his life is earthly, there is no rest for him after death, and he does not see light for ever, since he has sought what is temporal. But he who has followed the glory of Christ, who has desired that light which enlightens every man coming into this world, shall find and see the eternal light, since Christ has redeemed him from death.

27. Then, therefore, he will reflect within himself that when he was in honour he did not understand, and therefore he will be like the beasts, he who ought to have been like the angels through the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, to whom be honour, glory, perpetuity, from the ages, and now, and always, and unto all the ages of ages. Amen.

On Psalm 61 (62)

Title: Unto the end, for Jeduthun; a psalm to David himself.

1. It is the undoubted habit of all of us to hasten to the end, and to wish to know the sum of the things we have read or seen and heard. Hence the title itself does not allow us to be idle, nor to pass over with ears on holiday this sign of a common desire. For above the others (though it shines out in the others too), yet in a psalm of this kind the divine oracle gleams forth. Hence let us consider more carefully what the end is; for the end is called σκοπὸς, and the sum of the matter we wish to set forth. Christ is called the end, because he himself is the end of the law, as it is written: For Christ is the end of the law unto righteousness for everyone who believes. He is called the end also because he himself is the beginning and the end. Therefore the psalms which are entitled Unto the end are either about Christ or of Christ himself: about Christ, when he is announced; of Christ himself, when he announces himself and promises that he will come to the earth, and deigns to reveal to us the coming passion of his own body.

2. And so they are inscribed in the title to David himself, as though the ministry of the prophetic mouth were assigned to the true David, to the Lord himself, through whom the heavenly power sounded forth by the office of a human voice and the divine sentence rang out.

3. And rightly for Jeduthun, who was a prophet and used to sing psalms with his six sons, stands as a kind of preface, because leave to prophesy was given to those who, appointed in the ministry of singing psalms, spoke divine oracles with a human voice.
