And since the Passion of the Lord's body was to be undertaken to abolish the error of the whole world, he set forth, under the name, that is, of this man whose diligence was proven, an instruction of the people the more commended. So let us hear him speaking.

4. Shall not my soul be subject to God? When our Lord Jesus was about to take up the flesh of man, so as to cleanse it himself in himself, what ought he to have abolished first, before all else, if not the contagion of the old sin? For since guilt had crept in through disobedience, when the divine commands were violated, he ought above all to restore obedience, so as to shut out the seedbed of error. For the root-fibre of sin had spread from there, and therefore, like a good physician, he ought first to cut away the roots of the ulcer, so that the lips of the wound might feel the healthful remedy of the medicines. For in vain will you have treated the scar if the contagion creeps on within; nay, the wound is made worse if it is closed outside while the poison seethes within. For what did it profit to have forgiven sin, if the affection for sinning remained? That was not to heal the scar but to close it. So he willed to cleanse the wound, so as to heal the affection, lest any lot of disobedience should remain. He himself took up obedience, so as to pour it over into us. For so it was fitting that, since through the disobedience of one man very many had been made sinners, through the obedience of one, in turn, many should be made righteous.

5. Hence the fact shows that those err greatly who say that the flesh of man was taken up by Christ but deny the affection, and that they go against the counsel of the Lord Jesus himself, they who take man away from man, since man cannot exist without the affection of man; for flesh without affection is free both of reward and of guilt. Therefore he ought to take up and heal that from which guilt had flowed, so as to close the origin of error and, as it were, the doors of the sin that was bursting forth. From what would I recognize the Lord Jesus as man today, whose flesh I do not see but whose affection I read of; from what, I say, would I recognize him as man, had he not hungered, had he not thirsted, had he not wept, had he not said: My soul is sorrowful even unto death? Then it is written: And he is a man, and who shall know him? But he is known as man through these things, he who through his divine works is reckoned above men. So he himself, though he was God, so far wished to be believed a man that he called himself a man, saying: Why do you seek to kill me, a man who has spoken the truth to you?, and called himself not only a man but also the Son of Man, as he says: Who do men say that I, the Son of Man, am? Hence he was declared to hold the sum of the faith who both recognized the Son of God and did not deny the man. He himself, then, is one and both, inseparable in number, and to be recognized by the distinction of his works, not by a difference of person. For it is not one from the Father and another from Mary; but he who was from the Father took flesh from the Virgin, assumed affection from his mother, so that he himself might take up our infirmities. Hence the prophet says: And he grieves for us. How did he grieve with my grief, if he did not bear my affection? A man, he says, under the stroke and knowing how to bear infirmity.

6. So as a man he was made weak, as a man he grieved, and we reckoned him to be as a man in sorrows; but as the victor over infirmities, not one conquered by infirmities, he grieved for us, not for himself, and was made weak not on account of his own sins but of ours, so as to heal us by his bruise. What indeed is a man under the stroke and knowing how to bear infirmity, if not that he felt compassion in the stroke? Or how did he know how to bear infirmity, if he had shut out the feeling of infirmity? For what we carry, we sustain as a burden. So he took up our sins in order to carry them; he took them up also in order to cleanse them. Then it is written: And he himself cleansed their sins. Therefore he himself shall possess many as an inheritance and shall divide the spoils of the strong. For there the triumph is greater where the inner mind is cleansed. What he carries, then, belongs to remission; what he cleanses, to correction. So he took up our compassion; he took up also subjection. For that he subjected all things to himself is his own; that he was subjected is ours. And therefore he says: Shall not my soul be subject to God? The soul, he says, is subject, not the divinity; the soul is subjected, not the power of God; for the power of God is subject not to a power but to unity, and enjoys partnership in its power. That is subjected which is very often changed by the frailty of the human condition, not that which cannot be changed. The soul suffers with the flesh, and the flesh with the soul, for they are joined to each other in a kind of common dwelling. That is subjected which is sorrowful, for it is written: My soul is sorrowful even unto death; that is subjected which was taken up, not born of God the Father. And yet this subjection itself is not in the guise of infirmity but in the working of power, rather the arbiter of a temporary dispensation than enslaved to a perpetual servitude. Hence he also says: Shall not my soul be subject to God?

7. Why did he say "shall be", if the subjection is everlasting? But because the Son of God who was speaking is eternal, while he took up the soul in time, he therefore speaks of the subjection of his soul as future. Through it, then, came obedience, through it humility; and yet these were taken up not to the weakening of his power but for the teaching of discipline. For the Lord Jesus himself, when he was in the form of God, did not think it robbery to be equal with God, but emptied himself, taking the form of a servant, made in the likeness of men; found in appearance as a man, he humbled himself, becoming obedient even unto death, as the Apostle says, wishing to declare a subjection not continual and perpetual but temporal, such as was taken up with the flesh and laid down with the servitude of that body. So too it is written to the Hebrews. For when he had set forth an example, saying: You are a priest for ever after the order of Melchizedek, the same Paul added: Who in the days of his flesh offered prayers and supplications, with a loud cry and tears, to him who was able to free him from death, and, being heard by him from his fear, although he was the Son, learned obedience from the things which he suffered, and being perfected became to those who obey him the cause of eternal salvation, called by God a priest after the order of Melchizedek. Did not the Apostle, then, cry out plainly, saying that that obedience and humility were not of the divinity but of the flesh? For what is learned is temporal. As a man, therefore, he learned obedience from the things which he suffered, so as to be perfected in the flesh and, through the succession of obedience poured over into us, to become the cause of eternal salvation for us, to whom formerly that first Adam, through the inheritance of disobedience, had become the cause of death.

8. Subjection, then, is the teaching of human virtue, not a lessening of divine power. For if they call the Son less than and unequal to the Father because he was subject to God the Father, is he then also less than his mother because he was subject to his mother? For it is read of Joseph and Mary: And he was subject to them. But dutiful love is not a loss to us all but a gain, the dutiful love through which the Lord Jesus poured faith and grace into us all, so as to make us subject to God the Father with a faithful spirit. And therefore by a new and deep counsel the Apostle says that he himself will be subject to the Father in us, when there is in all men the fullness of faith and a kind of unity of devotion. For now, as long as we disagree in our opinions, we in a manner lessen the kingdom of Christ, because not yet are all things subjected to him whose kingdom is unity; but when all things have been subjected to him, then he himself also shall be subject to him who subjected all things to himself, that God may be all in all, as it is written. For now he is above all by power, but there is need that he be in all by will; and he will so will when he knows that all things in us are full of him and empty of sins. Not yet, then, is he subject to the Father, because not yet is Christ all and in all; but when Christ is all and in all, God will be all and in all. Hence it is gathered that the kingdom of the Father and of the Son and also of the Holy Spirit is one, because he who has received the Son receives also the Father, receives also the Holy Spirit, because the power of the Trinity is one, its grace one, its working one.

9. And rightly the Lord added: for from him is my salvation, as if he said: "Do not be troubled because I said that my soul would be subject to God. The soul will be subject, which is your portion; but my salvation is from him, that is, because I am always from the Father and in the Father. For I came from the Father into this world, and if you see a man, believe the Son of God; but when the Paraclete comes, whom I shall send you from my Father, the Spirit of truth who proceeds from my Father, he will bear witness of me". Both the Son proceeds from the Father and the Spirit proceeds from him; so concerning the unity of the divinity there is nothing doubtful. And therefore David longed for this salvation of God to be given to us, because it is itself eternal life, if we know both the Father as true God and the Son, who it is certain came for our salvation, but did not think it robbery to be equal with God. He sets aside the assertion of his divinity and displays the office of the human dispensation.

10. And therefore he added at once: For he himself is my God, my helper, my defender; I shall be moved no more. These things he says, of course, as a man, because we ought to place our hope in God, so as not to slip easily; and yet in saying I shall be moved no more he shows that he has the marks of divine power. For you have him saying elsewhere: Yet once more I will move the earth. He moved it by the flood, when all flesh perished except the ark of Noah; he moved it when Sodom and Gomorrah were consumed by sacred fire. These are signs of the indignation from on high. But since the Lord chose rather to save the human race than to destroy it, he will be moved no more to indignation, he who came for mercy; he came to redeem us with his blood, not to shed ours; he came to offer himself for us and, like a good merchant, to preserve his merchandise by the passion of his own body.

11. And as he said this, he lifted up his eyes and saw the persecutors on one side, the spiritual wickednesses on the other, gathering together, and said: How long will you rush upon a man, slaying all?, that is: "Why do you hasten to blot out the human race? Do you not know that I have come for the redemption of all? I have offered myself for all, so as to protect all by my offering". "A man", then, we take either for the whole multitude of mankind or, if we take it of one, we understand it as said of himself: you rush, but upon a man, because you cannot rush upon God, as you have elsewhere: Why do you seek to kill me, a man? For the divinity could not be subject to death, but the human nature he had taken up could. If, then, you rush upon me and wish to hold me, why do you wish to kill those who are with me? It is enough for you to have him whom you seek. I do not seek a partner in my passion, I who need no helper for the salvation of all. I do not ask for an envoy, I send no messenger ahead, but I myself, the messenger, offered myself to those who were not seeking me, gave myself up to those who were not seizing me, so as to free those who had been seized in the snares of death. You rush, then, as upon a leaning wall and a battered enclosure, not knowing that I came, not to make the wall lean and to batter the enclosure, but to dissolve it. For I came as peace, to make both one, and dissolving the middle wall of the enclosure, which divided flesh and soul so that they could not be of one mind. And therefore the flesh fought against the soul and could not be subject to its rule, because, through the obstacle of the wall set in the way, it could not obey its steering.

12. The law of the flesh, then, was opposed to the law of the mind. This partition of old enmities the Lord Jesus took away like a wall, and made the meeting of mind and flesh open to passage, so that, coming together into one, they might follow what was healthful to both. The wall, then, is a kind of rising-up of sins; hence Paul says to the high priest of the Jews: God will begin to strike you, you whitewashed wall; for a wall is built of mud and rubble. From this the Egyptians built cities, when the Jews were forced to make bricks. In Egypt mud was prepared, and the people of God wrought sin there; therefore they groaned, therefore they were heard and freed from sin; therefore the law was given and grace promised, so that the law might cut back sin in part, and grace might forgive it all.

13. And while he was bestowing these things on us, while he was undergoing these things for us, we were rejecting his price; hence he says: But they thought to reject my price, when, as Judas the traitor brought back the price, the Jews would not put it into the treasury, saying: It is not lawful for us to put it into the treasury, because it is the price of blood. Here the traitor is condemned by his own judgement, he who gave back the price he had received, weak as a witness to the sacred gift but strong as a witness to his own crime; for each man is a heavier accuser of himself, and himself pronounces against himself a sentence without excuse. The rulers of the synagogue condemn themselves by their own judgement; for they confess that what they had given was the price of blood, while they reproach the one who gives it back. For if it was not to be received back, neither was it to be paid. And would that they had either not given it or, since they had given it, had not taken it back! For they spurned what they would not put into the treasury, and with it they bought a field for the burial of sojourners. Rightly, then, the Gentiles, for whom the burial in that field which was bought by the death of Christ has been of profit, are no longer newcomers and sojourners, but have become fellow citizens of the saints and members of the household of God, because they are buried together with the Son of God; but the Jews, who spurned the price of his Passion, sojourn away from Christ. More tolerable than they is the traitor, at least in this, that he confesses his error, while they make their crime worse by excusing it; for confession after a sin is the more modest. Shamelessly, then, they rebuffed him as he confessed the sacrilege they shared, saying: What is that to us? See to it yourself; for what was the traitor saying but this: I have sinned in that I have betrayed righteous blood? What was the crime of the one who betrayed, how could it not be the sin of those who received, since crime was joined to crime, so that they slaughtered the more criminally him whom they had criminally bought?

14. I ran, he says, in thirst. It is true indeed that our Lord Jesus thirsted. Blessed is the Lord's thirst, because he thirsted for us, and most of all in his Passion; then he said: I thirst. He thirsted then, therefore, when from his side he was pouring out streams of living water to quench the thirst of all. Then it is written: Rivers of living water shall flow from his belly. But in the Greek it is put ambiguously, because ἔδραμον signifies both the singular and the plural number. Therefore, since according to the Greek we can understand both "I ran" and "they ran" (and we have said what "I ran" expresses, that is, I hastened to take up the thirst of all, so as to satisfy all with the abundance of the perpetual fountain; for he to whom I give water shall not thirst, neither here nor in the future, as was said to the Samaritan woman), let us now say what They ran in thirst signifies, that is, they hastened, so that, denying the living fountain in the heat of their unbelief, they might dry up their throats with an eternal drought, as the Lord himself says: They have forsaken me, the fountain of living water, and have made for themselves broken cisterns. Nor is it any wonder if they thirsted who could not hold the water of the eternal fountain, leaking as they were through the cracks of their unbelief. They thirsted, then, because they deprived themselves of the spiritual drink which they had drawn from the rock that followed. Hence Symmachus too says: συνεδόκει, that is, it pleased them in falsehood, when they brought false charges for true. Every falsehood has thirst; truth has an abundance that endures for ever.

15. And how quickly he proved that they ran in falsehood: With their mouth, he says, they blessed, and with their heart they cursed. Wretched is the thirst of the unbelieving, who held one thing in their heart and spoke another with their mouth. But where the abundance of faith overflows, with the heart one believes unto righteousness, with the mouth confession is made unto salvation. And how they blessed with the mouth and cursed with the heart, the course of the Passion shows, when they said: Let him come down from the cross and we believe him — fools! He rose from death and they did not believe: how would they have believed if he had come down from the cross? —, He hoped in the Lord, let him rescue him; let him deliver him now, since he wills him; for they said these things mocking rather than wishing it, and they asked whether he was the Son of God, speaking peaceable things in words while in their heart they were devising a charge of sacrilege.

16. But these are mystical things; let us come to moral ones. For since Christ is attacked not once — he was attacked once in his own body which he took up from the Virgin; he is attacked often in that body which is the Church, for we are the body and members of Christ; he is attacked also in each of his saints and in the innocent who have dedicated themselves to the Lord —

17. let us remember someone lately attacked by all, abandoned and betrayed by his own; who, lately set on the dais of empire, suddenly destitute of everything, began to be pressed by those very men whose allegiance he had received by inheritance, as they rushed upon him to his ruin, as they brought death upon him, with no helper, with no longer any ally of his own, with no companion. What better could he have said than what he had received from him to whom he had devoted himself: Shall not my soul be subject to God? That is: why do you persecute, why do you rage, why do you insult? You can kill the flesh; the soul you cannot. You can snatch away this life of the body; its merit you cannot extinguish; for it is written: Do not fear those who can kill the flesh but cannot kill the soul; but rather fear him who can destroy both soul and body in Gehenna. Therefore the soul that is subject to God is not subject to human power; for from him it hopes for the fruit of eternal life and the help of perpetual salvation. He, then, to whom I have given myself up will both protect me when slain and raise me up when dead and avenge me when killed. The righteous man was snatched away lest wickedness should change his heart. This death, then, is rather a flight from sin than a loss to the one who dies.

18. For he himself is my God, my helper, my defender; I shall be moved no more. He who slips is moved, because he glides down from a certain station of his integrity and innocence. For one who has once been set on the rock of Christ ought not to have a slippery footing, but to hold the firm ground of his own standing; hence he is called immovable who keeps his will immovable and pursues some purpose with the intent of a faithful mind. For we read also of the immovable will of God, as you have it in Hebrews: In which God, wishing to show more abundantly to the heirs of the promise the immovability of his will, interposed an oath. Rightly, then, is the will of God immovable, for it is moved by the allurements of no sin; men are movable, slippery for sinning, easily led to stray. Then it was said of the Jews: But those passing by blasphemed him, moving their heads, and the Lord himself says in the psalm: All who saw me scorned me, and spoke with their lips and moved their head. Christ is the head of all; it is better, then, that Christ remain in us than that he be moved by any movement of ours. Hence the Jews, moving their heads, said: Away from the earth with such a one; the Gerasenes asked him to depart from their borders; but the Church held him and did not let him go. Therefore she who held him possesses him; those who moved cannot hold him. And finely does the Evangelist say that those passing by moved their heads: passing by, not standing. We stand in the holy courts; we pass by in the streets. The priest passed by, the Levite went past; he stood who tended the wounds that had been inflicted. And therefore those who passed along the way did not say: The blessing of the Lord be upon you; we bless you from the house of the Lord; for the feet of those who bless are standing, not passing by. Then too it is those passing by, not those who stay, who strip the vineyard whose fence has been broken down; those passing by hiss, as we read in the Lamentations of Jeremiah: All who pass by the way have held their hands over you; they have hissed and moved their head over the daughter of Jerusalem. Therefore the priest could not be crowned, nor could the Levite, because they passed by and went past like a shadow; but Stephen was crowned with martyrdom, because he saw the Lord Jesus standing immovable, not passing by. Immovable in faith, then, Stephen saw Christ immovable, and was moved by no fear of death; Stephen did not move himself, nor did Christ move. The sons of the traitor were moved, of whom it is said: Let his sons be shaken and removed, and let them beg; for he who is moved needs a physician. The sinner is needy, the righteous man is rich, because he lacks no good who always seeks the Lord.

19. And since the discourse has been drawn from there, because he said: I shall be moved no more, David himself shows that to be moved is to be moved to sin, saying: Who has set my soul unto life and has not allowed my feet to be moved. Hence it is also said to a holy man by the Lord our God: But you, stand here with me, since he who is nearest to God cannot be near to a fall. For every sin is wont, as with an adversary's thrust, to throw the mind down from its place and to move it from its own standing; which seems to be drawn either from an enemy or from wrestling. Anger disturbs, lust inflames, envy goads, avarice tortures, fear casts down, grief afflicts. And to Peter it was said by the Lord Jesus: Come behind, after me. He did not say: "Come behind" (for this he said to the devil alone), but: Come behind, after me; for he who is after Christ is not behind. But since Peter was in error, as the Lord himself says to him: For you do not savour, he says, the things that are of God but those that are of men, therefore come after me, so that you may begin to mind not human things but divine. Therefore this righteous man too, who was to be snatched from the earth, says: I shall be moved no more; to be dissolved, indeed, and to be with Christ is far better. For he will not be able to slip who has begun to be with Christ, because death is for the righteous man the end not of nature but of sin.

20. Amid these words he suddenly sees the troops of his pursuers and, set in the midst of dangers, anxious not for himself but for those whom he desired to free, he says: How long will you rush upon a man, slaying all? If you seek me, why do you wish to kill others? I offer myself for many, because for all only he could offer himself who is the author of all. So, as an imitator of him whom he followed, he says: If you seek me, let these go, that the word which Jesus spoke might be fulfilled: that I have not lost any of them "except one" (for the Lord's saying includes this in another book), who nevertheless perished by his own will rather than, he says, being punished by my severity. And though I offer myself freely, you rush upon me and bear down on me as upon a leaning wall and a battered enclosure, that is: I, lately the summit of the noble imperial throne and once the strong wall of an empire spread around the whole Roman world, collapse like a leaning wall or a battered enclosure.

21. But be it so: they attacked me even unto death, they sought me to kill me, saying: "Let us take away the righteous man, let us take away the merciful man, for he is burdensome to us even to look upon"; but they thought also that my reputation, my chastity, was to be wounded by false slanders. For this is: They thought to reject my price, because our price is chastity, which separates us from the beasts and joins us to the angels; our price is mercy, which, when it is bestowed on the needy, redeems us from death; our price is faith, which has won for Christ all men who were pressed down by Gentile error and servitude; our price is a good reputation, by which the course of each one's merits is reckoned; our price is purity and simplicity, for it is written: A pure man is a precious possession; for nothing is more precious than a simple man. Hence the Apostle says finely: He has abounded, he says, in the riches of his simplicity. For what is richer than simplicity, which, like a good father of a household, abounds enough for itself and, content with its own purity, does not seek what is another's nor gnaw it away, but pours out of itself to others? The innocent man indeed believes every word, nor does he often change himself into various arts, as cunning does, which, to be wary, fears everything and does not trust itself to its own counsels, but turns its own opinions over and over; but simplicity knows nothing of fear. In this way, then, they tried to reject the price of the righteous man; although this too can be understood, that they received his gold with their hands while in their inmost heart they rejected him, holding the plunder, denying their faith.

22. Hence he also says: I ran in thirst, because he was seeking faith and could not find it, abandoned by his companions, forsaken by his own. So we read in the Gospel that the Lord Jesus both hungered and thirsted, when he hungered for our faith, thirsted for our works, and seemed to seek what belonged to others. Or, to refer it to the thirst of the body, with what indignity he says: I ran in thirst! That is: I was thirsting while others belched my wines, and from that high dais of sublime empire and from royal opulence I was brought down to the extremes of thirst with a kind of plebeian meanness.

23. And since the Greek has ἔδραμον, which signifies both "I ran" in the singular and "they ran" in the plural, it can also be understood thus: They ran in thirst, those whose throats were parched with an excessive heat to shed my blood. For as there is a thirst of faith, so there is also a thirst of unbelief. The former thirst the righteous man thirsts, who says: My soul has thirsted for you, O God; the latter the unbelieving thirst, whose tongue was parched for thirst; and with this thirst he thirsted who, set amid the dishes and cups of a banquet, was plotting the death of an innocent fellow guest, an Augustus. Did it not seem to you, ungodly man, while you ate and prepared the slaughter, that human bones were crackling under your teeth? While you drank wine and devised the parricide, that in those cups you were pouring the blood of the innocent into yourself? So utterly a stranger were you not only to the imitation but even to divine reading that there did not come to your mind that verse of the psalms: But you, a man of one mind with me, my guide and my acquaintance, who took sweet food together with me, and that other, which the Lord Jesus himself recalls in the Gospel: He who ate bread with me has lifted up his heel against me?

24. What shall I do to you, Ephraim? What shall I do to you, Judah? You have left us many heirs of your betrayal and fraud; for this can also be turned by apostrophe to Judas the traitor. You betrayed your lord's life, his kingdom. But the Lord Jesus is betrayed once in himself, often in his little servants; for what you did to one of these least, he says, you did to me. You received the purse entrusted to you and the money of the poor, this man the provinces committed to him; you the honour of an apostle, he the dignity of military command, the insignia of office. I count up his honours in order to magnify his crime. Each of you violated the fellowship of a banquet; yet you rose from the banquet to betrayal, this man to murder; that is, you, though in crime, were the more modest, you who denied having believed that death would be inflicted on the Lord whom you were betraying, who gave back the price, lest the wages of parricide should seem to remain with you; this man not only kept the wages of betrayal once received, but even extorted them when they were not offered; this man was not only not deceived by another's lie, but deceived others by his own, and deceived in order to kill. And when the other refused the banquet, seeing that he was to be put to death, this man offered an oath, lest any part of heaping up the crime should be lost to him. Lastly, the money of the earlier traitor was of profit for the burial of sojourners; this man denied his prince a burial intact.

25. Nor was there lacking one to wash his hands, saying: I am innocent of the blood of this righteous man; in which Pilate did not cleanse himself but defiled and entangled himself, and did not get himself free. For although there is no weight in the judgements of the lost, yet there is a greater bond in their confessions. For the righteous are acquitted not by another's sentence but by their own life; the unrighteous bind themselves more strongly by their own words, they who cannot bind others. Yet, lest this man too should seem to lack his own confession, he swore in order to perjure himself; he washed his hands while touching the Gospel, so that nothing should be lacking to the pattern; he washed his hands with water so as to defile them the more criminally with the blood of an innocent man. Nor was Herod lacking, whom a second Pilate believed he would please if he sent the captured prince to him. What shall I say of this too, that he also was clothed in a white garment for mockery, and afterwards was presented with a royal garment for dying, lest he should seem to have lost the honour that was his due? For although he perished by a crime, yet even in death he kept the right of the power conferred on him, by the service or the assent of the very men by whom it was being denied.

26. To so great a crime a funeral pageant would have been added, had not he who was lamenting these things without restraint, on learning what was being prepared, moved by a just grief, rebuked the proud victor, saying that such things were customarily inflicted on tyrants, not on kings. So, roused by horror at the outrage, he laid aside the preparations of that most foul crime. Nor, then, was one lacking to ask for the burial, although that Joseph who was called righteous was lacking. But Maximus, far more savage, was denying what Pilate himself could not take away. In this the parricide lacked humanity; yet the innocent man did not lack grace, and, patience being assumed for a time, vengeance has been deferred a little.

27. And therefore he says: But my soul is subject to God, since from him is my patience. For he himself is my God and my saviour, my upholder; I shall not depart. That is: has the burial denied to my body taken from me the mansion of perpetual rest? I have my dwelling in the tabernacle of the Lord, and I shall rest on his holy mountain. I shall not depart through human crime, because I have been upheld by the Lord's favour, because neither death nor sword nor tribulation will be able to separate me from the love of Christ. For it is not the innocent man but the sinner who departs from the tabernacles of the righteous. The heirs of Judas the traitor depart, of whom and of their like it is rightly said: Let his sons be shaken and removed, and let them beg; let them be cast out of their dwellings.

28. This is well referred also to the Lord Jesus Christ, who, when he had descended to the earth, was crucified and buried, did not depart from the Father but remained in the Father: I was delivered up and I did not go forth. Nor did he lay aside what he was, but kept it; nor did he cease to be in the form of God, but continued; the glory of God was not lessened by his taking up a body, but while it remained he won a triumph and did not lose his might. Hence he himself says: From now on you shall see the Son of Man sitting at the right hand of power.

29. Hence he exhorts to faith the peoples of the Gentiles whom he has called together and won for his Father by his blood, saying: Hope in him, all the assembly of my people, that is, not Israel alone, but "all", he says; nor the remnant alone, but also the fullness of the Gentiles; nor the Gentiles alone, but Israel; for when the fullness of the Gentiles has come in, then all Israel shall be saved. Who can comprehend the divine mysteries? Their multitude is so great that rightly Paul marvels and rightly has said: O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God! The Gentiles were rejected so that Israel might be chosen; afterwards Israel was blinded on account of the calling of the Gentiles, and the remnant of blinded Israel was saved according to the election of grace, after the fullness of the Gentiles had come in. So the fault of Israel profited the Gentiles, and the faith of the Gentiles set the Israelite people free.

30. Pour out, he says, your hearts before him. He who puts on faith ought first to put off unbelief and empty his heart of every stain of malice, so that his heart may become capable of spiritual grace. And therefore the Apostle says: Be renewed in the spirit of your mind; for when the old malice is poured out, a new grace is taken in, by which each one is renewed. So the Son of God says: Pour out your hearts before him, because he knew that the Father would say: I will pour out of my Spirit upon all flesh. There is also that manner of speaking, that we seem to pour out our heart when we open all the thoughts and wishes of our heart to someone. Let us, then, cast our thoughts upon God and not believe that anything is hidden from him. We pour out our heart before him when we empty out human disputings.

31. But vain are the sons of men, lying are the sons of men in the scales, so as to deceive. If the sons of men are vain and the sons of men are lying, the sons of God can be not vain, they who were born not of blood nor of the will of the flesh nor of the will of man, but of God. For the sons of men seem to themselves to weigh out equity and to test justice by the weight of a severer judgement, hard on others, lenient to themselves, unjust in their works, censors in their speech. Hence Solomon says: A double weight is abominable to the Lord, and a deceitful balance is not good. By this he signifies those who plan plunder with an insatiable heart and put forward sobriety on their brow. Such are the scribes of the Jews, who lay on others heavy burdens of a harsher observance, not having the yoke of their own soul straight but bent by a leaning toward iniquity, while they themselves are runaways from their own precepts. So it is found in avarice, as in the root of all vices, what is the head and the kindling of iniquity, which the man who renounces the world and is buried together with Christ through the sacrament of baptism, as one dead to the world, ought to turn aside from and flee.

32. Do not, he says, hope in iniquity, and do not covet plunder; if riches flow, do not set your heart on them. You see that they flow in; do you not see that they flow past? Naked you were born, naked you will die. Why do you seek to take in what you cannot take away with you? What you admire are streams; as they come, so they pass and recede. The Jordan alone was turned back, the Jordan into which you went down, which admonishes you to return to the very fountain and origin of nature, which brought you forth naked, not clothed in a garment, so that you may learn not to seek superfluous things. That you blush to be naked, sin taught you, not nature; for there would be no shame about the genitals if there were no sin. For neither are the other animals clothed so as to be covered, nor are they clothed so as to be protected; all are protected enough by the covering of their own nature. We, while we seek superfluous things, have lost the covering of nature, have forfeited the common right. And therefore a healing teaching is ascribed in this psalm, because by spiritual exercise he trained either Jeduthun, or himself under his name, for undergoing temptations, so that one who is set in straits may subject himself to God and, if he has sinned before, may cease to sin and not be moved by a relapse into error.

33. Once has God spoken; these two things I have heard. God has spoken once, and many things have been heard, because he did not speak through letters and syllables. He spoke through riddles, he spoke through visions, he spoke through the distributions of graces, he spoke through the spirit of each one; but we speak many things and are scarcely heard. He spoke once in the Law; the same things he spoke again in the Gospel. Or perhaps, as I think, since he spoke in many ways in the prophets, but last of all spoke in his Son, he spoke once when he spoke in the Son, and there were heard also those things which before had not been heard by those to whom he had spoken through the prophets. He spoke once, then, in the New Testament, and the Old Testament, which was not being heard, was heard, because the Lord himself said of the people of the Jews: They shall see and shall not see, they shall hear and shall not hear. They shall hear with the bodily ear, but they shall not hear with the hearing of the mind.

34. To us, then, that is, to the Church, he has spoken once and two things have been heard, because in her we have heard and understood what those who read did not understand, what those who heard did not hear. For Christ alone opened the human ear to know the mysteries; he alone loosed the seal of the book and unlocked the riddles of the prophets.

35. And therefore he himself will render to each according to his works; in this he includes also the quality of the works, so that either he may render to the merits of each the rewards of many and better works, or, on the contrary, we may be weighed down both by the number and by the bitterness of our deserts.

1 Translator's note: the passage cited is Ecclesiastes 10:4-5, not the Song of Songs; the Latin reads in Canticis.
2 Translator's note: the editor, Petschenig, judges this sentence spurious but prints it; one group of manuscripts has a different sentence here, urging us above all not to accept the sign that the enemy sets, such as the sign the Antichrist will set.
3 Translator's note: Scripture (2 Chronicles 20:35-37) names Jehoshaphat's partner as Ahaziah; Ambrose writes Ozias (Uzziah), and all the manuscripts agree.
